**Chapter written:** [Ch 12 — Dynamic blocks & complex types](book/ch12-dynamic-blocks-complex-types.md) (2026-08-05).

---

### ✅ I4 — Using modules

**What it is:** Consuming modules — the registry, source addresses (registry/git/local), version constraints, passing inputs, and reading module outputs.

**Why you need it:** Almost no production config is flat; you compose from modules, and the exam tests module sourcing/versioning.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Interactive — HCTut ["Modules overview"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/module)** (~7 min) — the conceptual opener to the Modules collection; no code. Notes: [[tut-module]]. Take three things from it: the definition is broad enough that **every configuration is already a module** (the directory you run commands in is the *root module*, one called by a `module` block is a *child module*), the strongest argument for modules is a **security** one rather than a tidiness one (encode the correct S3/GCS settings once, since misconfigured object storage is a recurring incident class), and Terraform reads only one directory's files — a `module` block is the only thing that makes it read another. ⚠️ Its best-practices list says *"Name your **provider** `terraform-<PROVIDER>-<NAME>`"* — that convention names a **module repository**; providers use `terraform-provider-<NAME>` (verified against [Publishing Modules](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/registry/modules/publish), 2026-08-08).
2. **Interactive — HCTut ["Use registry modules in configuration"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/module-use)** (~12 min) — consume the AWS `vpc` and `ec2-instance` registry modules to stand up a VPC plus two EC2 instances. Notes: [[tut-module-use]]. (A first taste is also in [[tf-aws-manage]] — the `terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws` module block, `module.<name>.<output>` references, re-`init` to install it, and `module.vpc.*` state addressing.) Four things to take from it beyond the syntax: **22 resources come out of two `module` blocks**, which is the encapsulation benefit and the review problem in one number; **module outputs are not inherited** — a child's outputs are readable in config but invisible at the CLI until the root module re-exports them; **`count` is legal on a `module` block** (the mechanism [[tut-for-each]] uses to multiply on two axes); and **local modules are referenced in place**, so edits to them take effect with no re-`init` and no `terraform get`. (The page says "symlinked"; measured on 1.15.8, `.terraform/modules/` holds only `modules.json` and its `Dir` points back at the relative source path — no symlink, no copy. The edit-loop consequence is unchanged, and both callers of a shared local directory move together. Book Ch 13 §7, `labs/chapter13/lab1`.) The page also states the negative case for variables well — *"if your organization requires NAT gateway enabled for all VPCs, you should not use a variable to set the `enable_nat_gateway` argument"* — a variable is a permission to vary, and declaring one hands the decision to the caller. (Its own config then does exactly that.) ⚠️ Every pin is years stale (provider `~> 4.49.0`, vpc 3.18.1, ec2-instance 4.3.0) and the `ami` is a hardcoded region-locked ID — but **every argument and output it uses still exists at vpc 6.6.1 and ec2-instance 6.4.0** (verified 2026-08-08), so the pins are a version exercise, not a rewrite. The rendered page and its example repo also disagree on `name`; here the *page* is the better one (`my-ec2-cluster-${count.index}` vs. the repo's un-indexed name, which tags both instances identically).
3. **Reference — [HCDocs "Modules" section index](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules)** (~10 min) — read this first for orientation; it is short. Notes: [[tf-modules]]. It gives the **Develop → Distribute → Provision** split the whole docs section is organized around, and a definition that complements the tutorials': a module is *"a collection of resources that Terraform manages together"* (semantic) where [[tut-module]] says *"a set of Terraform configuration files in a single directory"* (structural). The second makes "every configuration is already a module" true; the first is what makes the scoping question in I5 answerable at all.
4. **Reference — [HCDocs "Use modules in your configuration"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/configuration)** (~30 min) — the consumer-side reference, and the one that covers what [[tut-module-use]] leaves out. Notes: [[tf-modules-configuration]]. Git sources default to **whatever `HEAD` points at today** unless you add `?ref=`, which takes a branch, tag, or SHA; `?depth=1` makes a shallow clone. The `const = true` requirement on variables feeding `source`/`version` is a **timing** argument, not a safety one — modules install at `init`, before plan-time evaluation exists — and the constraint is on the whole dependency chain, so a `local` composed from const variables is legal in the source position. Two operational facts worth holding: **`init` honours what is already installed while `-upgrade` re-resolves the constraint**, which with no module lock file is exactly where an untested version arrives; and **`-replace` takes individual instance addresses only**, including full nested paths like `module.example.module.from-child2.aws_instance.child2-inst`, with no module-wide form and no wildcards. ⚠️ Its removal section is garbled — a version note stranded mid-paragraph, a stray "And", an unclosed backtick, and a closing link to the **`moved`** block reference at the end of a section about **`removed`**.
5. **Reference — [HCDocs "`module` block reference"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/block/module)** (~35 min) — the argument spec, and the exhaustive `source` catalogue (local, registry, GitHub, Git, BitBucket, Mercurial, HTTP, S3, GCS). Captured as [[tf-block-module]]. Things that live only here: **`//` marks a sub-directory within a package**, with query parameters *after* it (`<package>//<subdir>?ref=<x>`), and because Terraform extracts the **whole** package to disk, sibling modules inside it can reference each other by local path — which is what makes A8's module-split shim resolve. **Same `source`, different labels** is legal and is the multi-region workaround forced by the static provider association in I8. **Absolute local paths are copied into the module cache** and are discouraged for coupling a config to one machine's filesystem. **`localterraform.com/<NS>/<NAME>/<PROVIDER>`** resolves against whichever HCP/TFE instance runs the config — portable where a hard-coded hostname is not. **HCP Terraform can authenticate to Git sources only with SSH keys**, stated four times but never prominently. And **`ignore_nested_deprecations`** (1.15) is the consumer's opt-out from a module author's `deprecated` warnings (I5). ⚠️ The page **contradicts itself on `source` ten lines apart** — "must specify a literal string… does not support… arbitrary expressions" against "can reference constant input variables and local values"; the second is current, the first is stale pre-1.15 text. Its `depth` "default is 1" also cannot be right, since that would make every Git source a shallow clone and break the SHA refs the same page documents.
6. **Reference — [HCDocs "Module Sources"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/sources) + [version constraint syntax](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/expressions/version-constraints)** (~30 min) — `~>`, git refs, local paths. The version-constraints page is captured as [[tf-expr-version-constraints]] — the operators (`=`/`!=`/`>`/`>=`/`<`/`<=`/`~>`, with the `~>` right-most-increment rule), that constraints apply to modules, provider requirements, and `required_version`, the "newest installed that qualifies, else download" resolution, pre-release matching only via `=`, and the reusable-module (min-only `>=`) vs root-module (`~>` upper+lower bound) best-practice split.
7. **Book chapter — TUR Ch 4** (~1.5 hrs) — the classic module treatment; still the best explanation of inputs/outputs/composition. **Captured: [[04-reusable-modules]].** Read it for *why* each feature exists — it arrives at inputs, locals and outputs because its running example broke without them, which no reference page can do. Three lines worth memorizing: **"the input variables are the API of the module"**; a variable is **permission to change a value**, which is why the port/protocol constants became `locals` (callers could otherwise override them accidentally); and **prefer separate resources to inline blocks in a module**, because a separate resource "can be added anywhere" while an inline block can only be added by the module that owns it. It is also the only source here that walks the **promotion workflow** end to end — tag `v0.0.2`, point staging at it, leave production on `v0.0.1`, roll forward once proven. ⚠️ Its `aws_launch_configuration` example is unrunnable in a recent AWS account, its `aws_security_group_rule` advice now points at a superseded resource (`aws_vpc_security_group_ingress_rule` is current), and its *"Git tags are as stable as a commit"* needs the correction in the callout below — tags move.

!!! note "📌 Already captured — [[modules]]"
    The cross-source synthesis, blending HCDocs, TID Ch 2 §2.8 and Ch 3, TUR Ch 4 and the AWS get-started tutorial, and it splits along the same seam this path does: **consuming** modules is I4, **authoring** them is **I5**. Three framings worth having before the reference reading. A module behaves **like a function** — `variable` blocks are the parameters, `output` blocks the return values, and `locals` are internal-only, so from outside you can set inputs and read outputs and nothing else. A module is **not a provider**: it is pure HCL with no Go plugin, it **cannot define new resource types**, and it can mix resources from several providers, which is why modules abstract *systems* rather than objects. And the three flavours matter for where a `provider` block is legal — only in a **root module**, the directory you actually run `init` in.

!!! note "📌 Dynamic module sources (Terraform 1.15)"
    Terraform **1.15** added **dynamic module sources**: `source` and `version` can now be variables, not just string literals — so one module block can point different environments at different registries/pins instead of duplicating the block. Any variable feeding a module source must be marked `const = true` (module sources resolve at `terraform init`). OpenTofu offered this earlier; this is one of the 1.15 gap-closers. Note the exam (004, Terraform 1.12) still assumes literal sources. (See [[tf115-ot112-features]].)

!!! note "📌 `terraform modules` (TF 1.10)"
    **`terraform modules`** (TF 1.10+) prints every module declared in the config — key, source, and version — for the whole tree. Use it to audit what you depend on and to drive policy on module consumption. (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 Governing module versions — no lock file to lean on"
    `.terraform.lock.hcl` records **providers only**. Module selections live in uncommitted `.terraform/modules/modules.json`, so a fresh clone or a clean CI runner re-resolves the constraint and can land on a version nobody tested. `terraform init -lockfile=readonly` has nothing to check. Ch3 states the hazard; the controls belong here, in three layers.

    The in-scope control here is the **exact pin**: `version = "6.6.1"` for a registry module. Enforcing that pin is **A5**; keeping pinned versions current is **A3**.

    Don't try to commit `modules.json` as a substitute. `Dir` is repo-relative so it looks portable, but it's an undocumented internal snapshot that `init` rewrites, with no readonly enforcement.

!!! warning "📌 Git module sources: immutable pin *or* automated updates, not both"
    A registry module gets both properties at once — `version = "6.6.1"` is exact, and both update bots understand it. A **Git** source forces a real choice, and this is the reason to prefer registry sources where you have one.

    **`ref=<tag>`** is what the bots can move, but a Git tag is mutable: whoever controls the source repo can force-move `v1.2.0` to different code and your "pin" follows it silently. Mitigate by trusting the publisher, or by requiring tag-protection rules on the source repo.

    **`ref=<full commit SHA>`** cannot be moved by anyone, which is the supply-chain-safe answer and mirrors the advice to SHA-pin GitHub Actions. The cost is that **no update bot will move it for you**, confirmed on both:

    - **Renovate** — not supported natively. The maintainer's reason in [discussion #31006](https://github.com/renovatebot/renovate/discussions/31006) is architectural: Renovate's HCL parser returns an AST with comments stripped, so it has nowhere to keep the version↔SHA annotation its Docker/Actions digest pinning relies on. Open request, no timeline. Worse, a global `pinDigests: true` *breaks* on Git-sourced Terraform modules ([#14790](https://github.com/renovatebot/renovate/issues/14790)); set `"terraform": { "pinDigests": false }`. A custom regex manager is the community workaround.
    - **Dependabot** — same gap, tracked at [#10787](https://github.com/dependabot/dependabot-core/issues/10787) (SHA-pinned source reports "no update needed" even when newer commits exist) and [#10926](https://github.com/dependabot/dependabot-core/issues/10926). It considers semantic versions and skips SHA refs.

    **How to choose.** Third-party module you don't control: SHA-pin and accept manual upgrades, because the mutable-tag risk is a real supply-chain exposure. Module in your own org with protected tags: pin the tag and keep the automation, since you control whether the tag can move. Either way this is a deliberate trade, not an oversight — verified 2026-07-20.

    **A third thing a SHA pin costs you: shallow clones** (added 2026-08-08). `?depth=1` on a Git source is the documented way to stop cloning a large repository's whole history — and it **rejects a SHA `ref` outright**. The [Use modules](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/configuration) page and [Module Sources](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/sources) both describe `depth` with no caveat; the [`module` block reference](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/block/module) does carry it — *"You cannot use raw commit IDs"* — but **no page connects the restriction to the SHA-pinning advice**, so the cost only surfaces when the pin fails. `go-getter` passes `--depth` together with `--branch <ref>`, and `git clone --branch` takes only a branch or tag; a commit-shaped ref is caught by a regex and fails with *"note that setting 'depth' requires 'ref' to be a branch or tag name"*. Version-gated: `go-getter` **v1.8.6**, the release Terraform **1.15.8** vendors. **Immutable pin or shallow clone — not both.** Evidence: `cache/search/git-module-shallow-clone-vs-sha-pin.md`, [[tf-modules-configuration]].

!!! warning "🧪 Lab — **KL has no module lab at all** (corrected 2026-08-15)"
    This slot previously cited KL's Lab 06 as a "reusable-module section". That repository contains **zero** `module "…"` blocks, and the word *module* does not appear anywhere in Lab 06's instructions — "reusable" there means parameterized, not modularized. KL stops before modules entirely, so authoring them needs the other sources in this topic. See [[krausen-labs-facts]].

**Milestone:** You can consume a versioned registry module, pass it inputs, and reference its outputs in your own resources.

---

### ✅ I5 — Authoring modules

**What it is:** Writing your own modules — standard file layout, input validation, sensible outputs, composition over inheritance, and module design principles.

**Why you need it:** Reusable, testable modules are the core skill of both certs and of any real Terraform codebase.

**How to learn it:** *(hands-on first, then the design reading, then the six **Develop modules** reference pages in the docs' own sidebar order, then a module of your own. Entries name each other by page title rather than by number, because this list has been renumbered twice and the numeric cross-references rotted both times.)*

1. **Interactive — HCTut ["Build and use a local module"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/module-create)** (~15 min) — author a local `modules/aws-s3-static-website-bucket` submodule and call it from the previous tutorial's root config. Notes: [[tut-module-create]]. This is the authoring counterpart to I4's consumption tutorials, and its content is the two interface-design questions rather than the syntax: **which arguments become variables** (its answer: not the ACL, because the module's purpose fixes it to `public-read` — a value your purpose decides is not a variable) and **which values become outputs** (*"outputs are the only supported way for users to get information about resources configured by the module"*). Also states the provider rule — no `provider` block in a module, inherit from the caller — which [[tut-for-each]] shows is a hard constraint, not a recommendation, once `count`/`for_each` is on the module block. ⚠️ **The rendered module `main.tf` cannot apply on a current AWS account:** new buckets default to Bucket-owner-enforced (ACLs disabled, `AccessControlListNotSupported`) with Block Public Access on, so both its `aws_s3_bucket_acl` and its public `aws_s3_bucket_policy` are rejected. **The example repo is already fixed** — six resources with `aws_s3_bucket_ownership_controls`, `aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block`, and `depends_on` chains — so clone it rather than copying the page. Evidence: `cache/search/s3-acl-bpa-defaults.md`.
2. **Interactive — HCTut ["Customize modules with object attributes"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/module-object-attributes)** (~17 min, Terraform 1.3+) — refactor a module's four flat file-related variables into one `object` variable with `optional()` attributes, then add a `list(object(...))` for CORS. Notes: [[tut-module-object-attributes]]. This is the clearest interface-design exercise in the collection, and the argument for it is API evolution, in the page's words: optional attributes *"make it easier for you to ship new module versions without changing the variables that module users need to define"* — the additive counterpart to the `deprecated` mechanism below. Two things to take beyond the syntax: **a qualifier baked into a flat variable name is a namespace you are hand-rolling** (`terraform_managed_files` → `files.terraform_managed`, where the suffix existed only because a flat namespace had nowhere to say which group the variable belonged to), and **`path.module` vs `path.root`** — the module's fallback resolves against its own directory while the caller's value resolves against theirs, which is the whole reason the path is worth exposing. ⚠️ The page has real defects: its transcripts predate its own repo (printed counts are 6 where a current clone gives 8), the "Clean up" section shows an apply that *adds* a resource under a `terraform destroy` heading, and the delete-these-variables snippet lists five. Its repo also omits the `depends_on` that the sibling `learn-terraform-modules-create` repo uses to order the bucket policy after the public-access block.
3. **Interactive — HCTut ["Write Terraform tests"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/test)** (~1.5 hrs) — **A2 owns this tutorial**, and the framework, the `validation`/`precondition`/`check` distinction and Terratest all belong there. Do the module half here, because a module is not finished until something asserts its interface. TUR Ch 8's structural rule below is that every module gets an example and every example gets a test, and `.tftest.hcl` is what makes that rule executable: a `run` block applies the configuration with a chosen set of inputs and asserts on the result, so the interface you designed in the two exercises above acquires a regression test **before** it has consumers rather than after. Sequence it here for the same reason Ch 8 writes `examples/` first — a test is the second caller your module ever has, and the second caller is what exposes an interface that only works once.
4. **Reference — HCTut ["Module creation — recommended pattern"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/pattern-module-creation)** (~17 min, no code) — HashiCorp's *Recommended Enterprise Patterns* guide to module **scoping**, which is the question every other module resource skips. Notes: [[tut-pattern-module-creation]]. **Learn the three axes:** *Encapsulation* (group what always deploys together), *Privileges* (never span a segregation-of-duties boundary), *Volatility* (keep long-lived infrastructure out of a module that ships daily). The last two are blast-radius arguments, which is why they also decide **state** boundaries and not just module boundaries — the same argument A7 makes one granularity up. Its heuristic for "too big" is a good one: *"If a module's function or purpose is hard to explain, the module is probably too complex."* **MVP rules:** works for 80% of use cases, never code for edge cases, avoid conditionals in an MVP, expose only the most commonly modified arguments — but **maximize outputs**, because one module's output is the next module's input. And the default test worth carrying: `var.vpc_id` should never have a default, because *if the right value differs on every use, a default is a trap*. **Nesting:** don't go more than two deep (utility modules like tagging excepted), and know the trade — an external child module can change behavior *"with no changes to the parent's calling code or version"*, while an embedded submodule is locked to its parent's version but cannot be reused elsewhere. ⚠️ Its naming table gives `terraform-azure-database`, but the provider is **`azurerm`**; the same table also mixes underscores and hyphens. Third page in the collection to fumble that convention.
5. **Book chapter — TUR Ch 4 + Ch 8** (~2.5 hrs) — module design and "production-grade" module conventions. Ch 4 captured as [[04-reusable-modules]]; read it against [[03-variables-modules]] (TID Ch3), which covers the ground TUR Ch4 skips — module flavors, the type system, `validation`, `sensitive`, and registry publishing. The comparison is written up on the [Modules](topics/modules.md) topic page.

    **Ch 8 is now captured as [[08-production-grade]], and it is the design half of this topic.** Four rules — small, composable, testable, versioned — plus the practice worth adopting immediately: **write the `examples/` code before any module code**, so the API is designed from the caller's side. Its structural rule is that every module gets an example and every example gets a test, which is what turns `modules/`+`examples/`+`test/` into a convention rather than a folder tree. It also closes the registry gap Ch 4 leaves: the four Public Registry requirements, and the `<OWNER>/<REPO>/<PROVIDER>` + `version` source form. ⚠️ Its self-validation section is stale — see the correction under **A2**.

6. **Reference — [HCDocs "Module Development"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/develop) standards** (~40 min) — the standard module structure and publishing rules. The section index itself is captured as [[tf-modules-develop]], and its **"when *not* to write a module"** section is the part to read twice: *"over-using modules can make your overall Terraform configuration harder to understand and maintain"*, a good module *"raise[s] the level of abstraction by describing a new concept in your architecture"*, and the naming test — *"If you have trouble finding a name for your module that isn't the same as the main resource type inside it, that may be a sign that your module is not creating any new abstraction."* Pair it with the recommended-pattern guide's complexity test above and the target sharpens: **a module should be nameable in a phrase that is neither a paragraph nor a resource type.** It also restates the two-deep nesting rule from a different angle — keep the tree flat and compose, because a flat set of modules can be assembled in more combinations than a deep one.

    ⚠️ **The requirements are not on the page the sidebar calls "Publish modules."** That page ([[tf-modules-publish]]) is thin — publish to the registry, it versions and documents your module — and the repository-naming, semver-tag and structure rules live under the separate **Registry Publishing** section instead. It does supply the cleanest one-line case for a registry source over a Git one: *"The alternative sources do not support the first-class versioning mechanism."* And note its own example omits the `version` argument on the same page that sells version constraints.

7. **Reference — [HCDocs "Standard Module Structure"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/develop/structure)** (~25 min) — the layout half of the overview above, and the page the registry's tooling actually depends on: it parses submodules, examples and documentation out of this structure. Notes: [[tf-modules-structure]].

    Four of its rules are not guessable from the file tree. **A submodule's `README.md` is its public/private marker** — *"Any nested module with a `README.md` is considered usable by an external user. If a README doesn't exist, it is considered for internal use only"* — and it is *"purely advisory"*, so a private submodule stays private by staying undocumented. **The two source-address rules point opposite ways:** a root calling its own nested module uses a **relative** path so it isn't downloaded twice, while an `examples/` block uses **the external address**, because examples are expected to be copied out. **Resources may be split across files but `module` blocks must stay in `main.tf`.** ⚠️ **It also contradicts the recommended-pattern guide above on READMEs, and this path cites both sides.** This page says the README *"doesn't need to document inputs or outputs of the module because tooling will automatically generate this"*; the recommended-pattern guide says to document required inputs, optional inputs and outputs in the README. The reconciliation is that the registry generates those tables **from the `description` arguments**, so writing good descriptions *is* documenting the interface, and a hand-written table beside it is a copy that goes stale. Read that guide as "the interface must be documented" and this page as "here is where that documentation comes from". And the root module *"is expected to be opinionated"*, with nested modules as the escape hatch — the structural form of the recommended-pattern guide's 80%-of-use-cases rule. Also: use **commit-specific absolute URLs** for links and images in a module README, or the registry renders them against the wrong version. The registry's own bar is short and worth memorizing: public GitHub repo named **`terraform-<PROVIDER>-<NAME>`** (`<NAME>` = infrastructure type, `<PROVIDER>` = the provider creating it), adherence to the standard module structure so the registry can generate docs and parse submodules/examples, and at least one semver release tag `x.y.z` (optional `v` prefix). ([Publishing Modules](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/registry/modules/publish), verified 2026-08-08; the minimal file set — `LICENSE`, `README.md`, `main.tf`, `variables.tf`, `outputs.tf` — and the best-practice framing are in [[tut-module]], which misnames this convention as a *provider* rule.)

8. **Reference — [HCDocs "Providers Within Modules"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/develop/providers)** (~35 min) — the third page in the **Develop modules** group, and the one that states the authoring rule the other two assume. Notes: [[tf-modules-providers]]. **A reusable module must not contain its own `provider` block**, and the reason is a lifecycle one rather than the `count`/`for_each` conflict every tutorial cites: a provider configuration is needed to *destroy* a resource as well as create it, and state records which configuration last applied each object, so *"a provider configuration must always stay present in the overall Terraform configuration for longer than all of the resources it manages."* A module holding both removes them at the same moment. The authoring consequence is `configuration_aliases` in `required_providers`, plus the inheritance asymmetry to design around: default provider configurations inherit into child modules, **aliased ones never do, and `source`/`version` requirements never do either** — every child declares its own. The caller-side mechanics of the same contract belong to **I8**, which cites this page too; read it there if you are configuring providers rather than authoring a module.
9. **Reference — [HCDocs "Best practices for composing modules"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/develop/composition)** (~40 min) — the design reference, and the most substantial page in the Modules section. Notes: [[tf-modules-composition]]. It is stricter than the recommended-pattern guide's two-deep rule: keep the tree **flat, one level of child modules**, and wire them with expressions, because *"instead of a module embedding its dependencies… the module receives its dependencies from the root module, which can therefore connect the same modules in different ways."* **Three patterns to take away.** *Conditional creation* — never write a module that detects whether something exists; declare an `object(...)` input listing only the attributes you need, and let the caller pass either a `resource` or a `data` source. *Assumptions versus guarantees* — an assumption is what must hold for a resource to be usable, a guarantee is what the rest of the configuration may rely on, and preconditions on **outputs** are how you assert the latter (A2). *Data-only modules* — a module of pure data sources, whose real trick is that **a `join-network` module with the same outputs as a `create-network` module makes the two swappable**, so promoting an environment to owning its infrastructure is a one-line `source` change (relevant to A7, and neither module tutorial mentions it). Also worth reading for the flat statement that Terraform *"intentionally does not attempt to abstract over similar services offered by different vendors"* — multi-cloud abstractions are yours to build, out of shared object types and matching output names. ⚠️ Two of its examples would fail `validate`: `aws_ami` takes **`owners`** (a list), not `owner`, and **`aws_subnet_ids` no longer exists** in the AWS provider (`aws_subnets` replaced it).
10. **Reference — [HCDocs "Publishing Modules"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/develop/publish)** (~10 min) — short, and worth the ten minutes for one sentence: *"The alternative sources do not support the first-class versioning mechanism."* That is the argument for publishing at all rather than pinning a Git `?ref=`. Notes: [[tf-modules-publish]]. ⚠️ **The publishing *requirements* are not on this page** — repository naming, semver tags and the standard structure live under [Registry Publishing](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/registry/modules/publish), summarised under Standard Module Structure above. Note also that its own example omits the `version` argument on the page that sells version constraints. **Actually getting a module into a registry is A4**, which covers the HCP private registry hands-on and, since 2026-08-16, the open-source alternative.
11. **Reference — [HCDocs "Refactor modules"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/develop/refactoring)** (~20 min for the author's half) — the last page in the **Develop modules** group, and the one that covers how a module's *internal structure* changes once it has consumers. Notes: [[tf-modules-refactoring]]. **The deep `moved` reference is A8** — addressing rules, instance keys, `count` ↔ `for_each` migrations, the cross-type and managed-to-data limits. What belongs here is the part addressed to an author. **Scoping is what makes refactoring an author's privilege:** addresses resolve relative to the module the block is written in, and *"a module may only make `moved` statements about its own objects and objects of its child modules"*, so you can rename and re-shape internals across a version bump and every consumer gets the migration in their next plan without touching their own code. **`moved` blocks are the upgrade path, not a courtesy changelog.** Chain them (`a→b`, then `b→c`) when the same object moves twice, because each block covers consumers arriving from a different starting version, and keep them — removal is safe only for a *private* module, once you are certain every consumer has applied. **The section to read as an author is splitting a published module.** The original module is not deleted; it becomes a **shim** that calls the new modules and carries the `moved` blocks, so new users take the pieces directly and existing users upgrade in place. HashiCorp names the compromise rather than hiding it: the shim's blocks address resources *inside* child modules, which *"violates the typical rule that a parent module sees its child module as a 'closed box'"*, and it attaches a precondition — *"all three of these modules are maintained by the same people and distributed together in a single module package."* That licence covers your own package and never someone else's module. Read with the `deprecated` callout below, this is the full toolkit for evolving a published interface: `moved` re-shapes structure, `deprecated` retires API surface.
12. **Interactive — HCTut ["Create and use no-code modules"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/no-code-provisioning)** (~14 min, HCP Terraform Standard edition + an AWS account) — **E6 owns this tutorial** for the self-service platform half; read it here for the author half, because it is the one page that documents where two of the rules above stop applying. Notes: [[tut-no-code-provisioning]]. A no-code module **declares its own `provider` blocks**, and the page gives the cleanest statement of why the prohibition exists at all: *“Since users will not reference no-code modules in written configuration, there is no risk of this conflict.”* So the ban was never about providers, it was about `count`/`for_each`/`depends_on` on a `module` block — remove the possibility of that block and the ban evaporates, at the price of the organization having to arrange provider credentials for every workspace HCP launches per provision. It **tightens** the standard structure rather than relaxing it: *“No-code modules must follow standard module structure and define all resources in the root repository of the directory.”* And it is the only page in the Modules collection that has been kept current — ephemeral resources (**1.10**) and write-only arguments (**1.11**) — where eight of the other nine still pin 2022 provider versions, so it is the one whose configuration is worth copying as written.
13. **Interactive — refactor** (~1.5 hrs) — extract your Beginner project into a reusable module with a clean input/output surface.

!!! tip "📌 Type your module's outputs, for the same reason you type its inputs"
    A module's **outputs** deserve `type` constraints as much as its inputs do (TF **1.15+**, B6): the constraint is checked, and a consumer reads your interface without reading your implementation. That is the case, and it's enough. A visible side effect is that a fully-typed output set makes `module.<NAME>` a `list`/`map` under `count`/`for_each` rather than a `tuple`/`object` (verified 1.15.8; Ch 7 §3 has the machinery) — but treat that as a curiosity, **not** a reason: auto-conversion means callers almost never notice, since a tuple splats, converts, and satisfies a `list(object(...))` constraint exactly as a list does. **OpenTofu has no typed outputs** as of 1.12.4, so a module written for both can't rely on the argument at all. Evidence: [[conditional-branch-evaluation]].

!!! note "📌 Module API deprecation (Terraform 1.15)"
    Terraform **1.15** added a **deprecation mechanism** for a module's public API: put `deprecated = "message"` on a `variable` or `output` and callers get a warning at `terraform validate`. This is how you evolve a published module's interface without breaking consumers overnight — deprecate, warn, then remove a version later. Pairs with the `moved` blocks in the Refactor modules reference above — `deprecated` retires interface surface, `moved` re-shapes the structure behind it — and with the refactoring-at-scale workflow in A8. (See [[tf115-ot112-features]]; argument specs in [[tf-block-variable]] and [[tf-block-output]]. Output-specifics: `deprecated` is **child-modules-only**, only *consumers* see the warning (not the defining module), and `ignore_nested_deprecations` on the `module` block suppresses nested warnings.)

!!! note "📌 Publishing is the step after authoring — and the workflow choice is not cosmetic"
    Writing the module is this topic; getting it into a registry is **A4**, captured in [[tut-module-private-registry-share]]. Two things belong in an author's head, though. **Your repository name is your module's identity** — `terraform-<PROVIDER>-<NAME>`, required for the public registry and recommended for HCP's private one — and **your git tags are your version numbers**, semver `x.y.z` with an optional `v`, with non-matching tags ignored and at least one tag needed to publish at all. So versioning is a release-process decision made in the repo, not an argument you write in HCL.

    HCP's **branch-based** publishing is the one to know about, because it is the only workflow that supports **module testing** — relevant the moment `terraform test` (A2) is part of how you ship a module.

!!! tip "📌 Generate the README's input/output tables — and make its links absolute"
    A module README goes stale the moment a variable is added, which is why [[code-styling]] treats module docs as a **generated artifact**: run [`terraform-docs`](https://github.com/terraform-docs/terraform-docs) manually, or wire it into `pre-commit` so the inputs/outputs tables refresh on every commit. The reference implementation the book points at is [`terraform-aws-vpc`](https://github.com/terraform-aws-modules/terraform-aws-vpc), which uses exactly this setup.

    **The registry-specific trap:** *"All links in `README.md` files should be absolute"*. The Terraform Registry renders your README off-repo, so a relative link that works fine on GitHub resolves to nothing there. This is a publishing constraint rather than a style preference, and it belongs with the publishing rules above. The rest of the toolchain is in **B4**.

**Milestone:** You can package infrastructure into a module with validated inputs, documented outputs, and a README, and consume it from two callers. **Written up as [Chapter 14 — Authoring modules](book/ch14-authoring-modules.md)**, whose lab measures typed outputs against both engines, the `deprecated` warnings each engine emits and when, and a module split that leaves the consumer's plan at `0 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy`.

---

### ✅ I6 — Remote state & backends

**What it is:** Where Terraform keeps state, taught on three backends and one idea. **`local`** is where you already are. **`s3`** and **`gcs`** are the two object-store backends worth knowing properly — bootstrap, migration, locking, IAM, encryption — and reading them side by side is what turns "the S3 backend" into a general model of what a backend does. Then `terraform_remote_state` for sharing outputs, and enough of the rest of the catalogue to recognise it.

**Why you need it:** Teams cannot share local state; remote state with locking is the baseline for any collaboration and is exam-critical. Two backends rather than one, because everything that matters here — the bucket you must create first, the lock that is really a conditional write, the plaintext secrets you are now storing centrally — is a property of the *pattern*, not of AWS.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Video — [TF2026 "Remote state & backends" (Lecture 07)](https://youtu.be/l5qtFBsxZdk)** (~35 min) — the only video coverage this topic has, and the one that shows the bootstrap as a sequence rather than a paragraph: build the S3 bucket with server-side encryption, versioning and a public-access block **first**, then add the `backend "s3"` block and re-`init`. Companion code is `Lecture07/` in the course repo. ⚠️ **Its backend block locks with `dynamodb_table`**, which the S3 backend reference now deprecates in favour of `use_lockfile` — see the S3 reference below and the native-locking callout further down. The same lecture also predates the `endpoints {}` block, using the deprecated `endpoint` and `force_path_style` arguments. The bucket setup it teaches is still right; the locking argument is not. (Verified 2026-08-15 against the local checkout — see `docs/research-cache/tf2026-course-facts.md`.)
2. **🧪 Lab — the `local` backend, said out loud** (~20 min, nothing required) — start where every earlier chapter already was, and make it explicit. `backend "local" { path = "state/dev.tfstate" }` moves the file; deleting the block and re-running `init` migrates it back, which is the whole of “removing a backend” — there is no separate command for it. **Measured 2026-08-19 on 1.15.8, and not what the docs imply:** only a *first* backend adoption prompts on a plain `init`. Once a backend is recorded in `.terraform/`, changing or deleting the block **errors** with `Backend configuration changed` and makes you choose `-migrate-state` or `-reconfigure`. And the migration **copies without cleaning up** — the old state file survives complete, so a plaintext snapshot stays on the disk you just moved it off (Book Ch 15 §6). Reference: [[tf-backend-local]], including the legacy `-state` / `-backup` flags that **switch off workspace-aware state filenames**, which HashiCorp recommends against “even if you are running Terraform in automation.” Lab: `labs/chapter15/lab1/`.
3. **🧪 Lab — the `s3` backend: bootstrap, migrate, lock** (~1.5 hrs, **no cloud account**) — the core exercise of this topic, run against the book's AWS emulator. Three things in one sitting. **The two-step bootstrap**: a local-backend configuration creates the bucket with versioning and a public-access block, because the bucket holding your state cannot be tracked in that state before it exists. **The migration**: apply with local state, add an empty `backend "s3" {}` plus a `config.s3.tfbackend` partial configuration, then `init -migrate-state` and answer the copy prompt. **The lock**: `use_lockfile = true`, an apply held open, and a second command refused with **`412 PreconditionFailed`** on the `<key>.tflock` object. Verified transcripts in the callout below. Lab: `labs/chapter15/lab2/`.
4. **🧪 Lab — the `gcs` backend** (~45 min, **no cloud account**) — the same shape against a second cloud, which is what turns one backend into a general idea. It runs on a local GCP emulator behind a compose profile, because the AWS one cannot stand in: `gcs` speaks the Google JSON API at `/storage/v1/b`. Two differences to carry away. **There is no `key`** — state is `<prefix>/<workspace>.tfstate`, so the workspace name is always in the path, where S3 prefixes only non-default workspaces, with the literal `env:`. And **locking is on by default**, with no `use_lockfile` equivalent to forget. The refusal names the mechanism: `googleapi: Error 412: ifGenerationMatch: … conditionNotMet`. Lab: `labs/chapter15/lab3/`.
5. **Reference — [HCDocs “Backends”](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend) + [“local”](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend/local)** (~40 min) — what a backend *is*, before which one you pick. Captured as [[tf-backend-configure]] and [[tf-backend-local]]: the three limitations (one block per configuration, **no named values**, nothing declared inside it referenceable elsewhere), `backend` and `cloud` being mutually exclusive, the full partial-configuration surface, and the documented **`*.backendname.tfbackend`** filename convention the labs use. The State-section framing of the same ground is [[tf-state-backends]]: a backend's two responsibilities — storage, plus an **optional** locking API — and the guarantee that a non-local backend never writes state to local disk **except** when persisting to the backend fails. The dedicated locking page is [[tf-state-locking]], which is where the `force-unlock` rules below come from.
6. **Reference — [HCDocs “S3 backend”](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend/s3)** (~35 min) — the one backend worth reading argument by argument, captured as [[tf-backend-s3]]. **Locking is opt-in and the old way is going out**: `use_lockfile` defaults to `false`, while `dynamodb_table` and `dynamodb_endpoint` are deprecated and *“will be removed in a future minor version”*; both may be set at once purely as a migration path. **Workspaces change the object path** to `<workspace_key_prefix>/<workspace_name>/<key>`, default prefix the literal **`env:`** (**A7**). **The IAM set is more specific than “read and write the bucket”**: `s3:DeleteObject` is needed on the **`.tflock`** object but explicitly not on the state object, *“as Terraform does not delete it.”* **Encryption has three shapes**, and the third bites: `sse_customer_key` set in a `.tf` file *“will cause it to be persisted to disk in `terraform.tfstate`.”* The page also carries `assume_role_with_web_identity`, which is how a backend authenticates from CI with no stored key (**A6**).
7. **Reference — [HCDocs “gcs backend”](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend/gcs)** (~30 min) — the Google counterpart, captured as [[tf-backend-gcs]], and the page that makes the S3 one legible by contrast. Locking needs no argument at all. Addressing is `prefix` plus workspace, never a `key`. Authentication is organised by *where Terraform runs*: workstation ADC, an attached service account on Google Cloud, a key file outside it, or `impersonate_service_account`. **The encryption asymmetry is the part to plan around** — a customer-**supplied** key (CSEK) cannot be migrated automatically and needs an out-of-band `rewrite` first, while a **KMS** key can, but only takes effect on the *first write after* migration, so the old key has to outlive it. And a 403 immediately after granting IAM may be nothing: *“Terraform will return 403 errors till it is eventually consistent.”* **The argument that decides whether the backend and your resources share an identity** is the environment variable you export: every option here reads both a backend-scoped `GOOGLE_BACKEND_*` name and a shared `GOOGLE_*` one, and the page warns that *“if using the Google Cloud Platform provider as well, it will also pick up the `GOOGLE_CREDENTIALS` environment variable.”* Exporting the shared name authenticates the backend and every `google` provider as the same principal, which is the collapse of exactly the split the S3 page’s multi-account architecture is built on (**A7**). Its IAM story is correspondingly coarse: one line asking for **Storage Object Admin** on the bucket, against S3’s four-statement policy with a separate `.tflock` statement.
8. **Book chapter — TUR Ch 3** (~1.5 hrs) — the canonical remote-state + isolation discussion, now captured as [[03-manage-state]]. What it gives this topic that the references do not: the **two-step bootstrap** (apply the bucket with a local backend, then add the backend block and `init` to migrate, and the same in reverse to tear it down), **partial configuration** as the answer to `key` being the one argument you must not copy-paste, and the honest list of what the backend block cannot do. ⚠️ Two of its limitations are now engine-specific — OpenTofu **1.7** state encryption and **1.8** early variable evaluation in `backend` blocks both close gaps the chapter presents as inherent (notes: [[ot-early-eval-backend]], and the encryption reference below) — and its DynamoDB lock table is superseded by `use_lockfile` (see the callouts below). Cross-source synthesis: [State](topics/state.md).
9. **Book chapter — [TID Ch 6 §6.4 "Storing state"](books/tid/chapters/06-state-management.md)** (~1 hr) — the reference-shaped counterpart to TUR Ch 3’s narrative, and the treatment that enumerates where TUR argues: **§6.4.1** the possible backends, **§6.4.3** the `backend` block, **§6.4.4** alternative authentication methods, **§6.4.5** the `cloud` block as the other way to do the same job, **§6.4.6** migrating between backends and **§6.4.8** upgrading one. Ch 6 is already cited from **I7** for its state-surgery half (§6.5) and from **B9** for §6.1–§6.3; **§6.4 belongs to this topic**, and it is the section that turns the backend catalogue from a list of names into something you can configure.
10. **🧪 Lab — pipeline on the forge, state in the bucket** (~1.5 hrs) — the arrangement most teams actually run, and this topic's answer to “where does state live when our code is on GitLab, GitHub or Bitbucket?” A self-hosted GitLab runs `validate` → `plan` → **manual** `apply` against the **same `s3` backend as lab 2**, with the job container on the emulator's network. It ends with two objects in one bucket — `app/terraform.tfstate` written from a workstation, `ci/terraform.tfstate` written by a runner — and no Terraform state on the forge at all. Lab: `labs/chapter15/gitlab/`. The credential half is **OIDC**: [[gha-oidc]] for GitHub, [[bitbucket-pipelines-oidc]] for Bitbucket, feeding the `assume_role_with_web_identity` block above so no long-lived key sits in CI (**A6**). ⚠️ Two traps the lab records because each cost a failed pipeline: `gitlab/gitlab-runner:latest` is a **pre-release** whose helper image does not exist, so every job dies in `prepare_executor`; and the runner needs an explicit `--clone-url`, because the URL GitLab advertises to a browser is unreachable from a job container.
11. **Reference — the backends that are not object stores** (~40 min, read once) — enough to recognise the rest of the catalogue rather than adopt it. **`http`** is a protocol rather than a vendor ([[tf-backend-http]]): GET/POST/DELETE plus `LOCK`/`UNLOCK`, **423** when the lock is held, and lock addresses that **default to disabled**, so a minimal block is silently unlocked. **GitLab-managed state** is that protocol implemented inside GitLab ([[gitlab-tf-state]]) — an empty `backend "http" {}` against `/api/v4/projects/<ID>/terraform/state/<NAME>`, where project roles replace bucket IAM and, the fact that decides it, *“any user with the Developer role or higher can download and view Terraform state files.”* **HCP Terraform** is a `cloud` block rather than a backend type, and **Terrakube** ([[terrakube-migrating]]) speaks the same API self-hosted, needing only an extra `hostname`. The remainder — `azurerm`, `consul`, `kubernetes`, `pg`, `oci` (1.12), `oss`, `cos` — is chosen by the platform you are already on.
12. **Interactive — the managed-platform comparison** (~1.5 hrs total, optional) — three HashiCorp tutorials, kept because they show what a managed backend does that a bucket does not, not because this topic depends on them. [“Migrate state to HCP Terraform”](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/state/cloud-migrate) is the cheapest — a `random_pet`, no cloud account — and is captured as [[tut-cloud-migrate]]: the `cloud` block, `terraform login` storing its token *“in plain text”*, and an `init` prompt whose **no** branch adopts the workspace's existing state rather than cancelling. [“Version remote state with the HCP Terraform API”](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/state/cloud-state-api) is the disaster-recovery half, captured as [[tut-cloud-state-api]]: download, edit, **increment `serial` by hand**, and upload a new state version, with the workspace lock a precondition of the upload. [“Migrate state from S3 to HCP Terraform”](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/cloud/migrate-remote-s3-backend-hcp-terraform) is the long one, and lab 2 already covers the migration mechanics it teaches.
13. **Reference — [HCDocs "`terraform_remote_state` data source"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/state/remote-state-data)** (~25 min) — reading another config's **root** outputs, the `config` object (nested backend blocks become `=` attributes), `defaults`, and — most of the page — HashiCorp's case *against* using it. Notes: [[tf-remote-state-data]].
14. **Reference — [OTDocs "State and Plan Encryption"](https://opentofu.org/docs/language/state/encryption/)** (~40 min, **OpenTofu only**) — the direct answer to the hardening callout below, and a capability Terraform has no equivalent of. An `encryption {}` block inside `terraform` pairs a `key_provider` with a `method` and applies it to `state {}`, `plan {}` and — the part that bears on the `terraform_remote_state` warning further down — `remote_state_data_sources {}`. The whole configuration can also arrive through **`TF_ENCRYPTION`**, with the environment overriding code. **Read its threat model before adopting it, because the page is unusually honest about the limits:** encryption protects state *at rest*, and *“does not protect against data loss … and it also does not protect against replay attack”*, nor against *“the person running the tofu command.”* **Two operational facts to plan around.** Migration is deliberately not seamless — turning encryption on over an existing state is not enough, because OpenTofu *“will refuse to read plain text data”*, described as *“a protection mechanism to prevent OpenTofu from reading manipulated, unencrypted data”*, so there is a documented initial-setup path to follow. And the compatibility promise is narrow: key providers and methods are supported for **+1 minor version**, with a console warning at `plan`/`apply` before removal, so an encrypted state is something you keep current rather than set and forget. Rotate keys where the method can saturate — AES-GCM is named. Engine history and the rest of the divergences are **E3**. Now captured as [[ot-state-encryption]], which adds four things this entry did not carry. **The `unencrypted` method is the documented migration path** in both directions, temporarily named in a `fallback` block, and `fallback` is the general rollover mechanism rather than a migration-only tool: OpenTofu reads with either method and *“will always use the new method and not the fallback”* when writing. **`enforced = true` is the guard for environment-driven configuration**, because an unset `TF_ENCRYPTION` otherwise means plaintext written silently — the same shape of failure as `use_lockfile` defaulting to `false`. **Renaming a key provider breaks decryption**, since the encrypted data carries metadata keyed to the block name, and `encrypted_metadata_alias` is what makes a rename or a cross-project name mismatch survivable. **Key-provider authentication is inherited from the matching backend**, so `aws_kms` and `gcp_kms` reuse the S3 and GCS credential chains and add nothing new to learn.

!!! note "📌 `s3` versus `gcs` — the same job, and where they genuinely differ"
    Read the two reference pages against each other; this is the summary the chapter is built on. Both verified in `labs/chapter15/`.

    | | `s3` | `gcs` |
    | --- | --- | --- |
    | Object path | `key`, verbatim, for the default workspace | **no `key`** — always `<prefix>/<workspace>.tfstate` |
    | Other workspaces | `<workspace_key_prefix>/<name>/<key>`, prefix defaults to the literal **`env:`** | same rule as the default one; the name is already in the path |
    | Locking | **opt-in**: `use_lockfile = true`, defaults to `false` | **on by default**, and no argument exists to set *or* unset it |
    | Lock mechanism | `<key>.tflock` object, conditional PUT → **412 PreconditionFailed** | `<prefix>/<name>.tflock`, `ifGenerationMatch: 0` → **412 conditionNotMet** |
    | Bucket | must pre-exist | must pre-exist — *"prior to configuring the backend"* |
    | Versioning | recommended, not automatic | recommended, not automatic |
    | Encryption | `encrypt`, `kms_key_id`, `sse_customer_key` | `kms_encryption_key` (migratable) or `encryption_key` (**not** migratable) |
    | CI credentials | `assume_role_with_web_identity` | ADC, attached service account, or `impersonate_service_account` |
    | IAM granularity | four statements, `s3:DeleteObject` on the `.tflock` but **not** on state | one line: **Storage Object Admin** on the bucket |
    | Backend/provider identity | separable by design — the multi-account pattern depends on it (**A7**) | `GOOGLE_CREDENTIALS` is read by **both**; use `GOOGLE_BACKEND_CREDENTIALS` to split them |

    **The lock is the same idea twice.** Neither cloud runs a lock service: both write one object that must not already exist, and both surface the failure as a **412**. Once you have seen that, "does this backend support locking" becomes a question about whether the store offers a conditional write.

    **The differences that will actually catch you** are `use_lockfile` defaulting to `false` — a valid S3 backend block can be silently unlocked — and GCS refusing to migrate a customer-supplied encryption key, which needs an out-of-band rewrite first.

!!! example "🧪 Verified — a GitLab pipeline writing to the `s3` backend"
    Run 2026-08-19 on **Terraform 1.15.8**, **`gitlab/gitlab-ce:19.2.4-ce.0`** and **`gitlab-runner:v19.2.2`**, tuned per [[gitlab-memory-constrained]]. Walkthrough: `labs/chapter15/gitlab/`.

    - **The pipeline is the point.** `validate` → `plan` → manual `apply`, the job container on the emulator's network, `backend "s3"` initialising inside CI, and the bucket ending with `app/terraform.tfstate` from a workstation beside `ci/terraform.tfstate` from the runner. The forge held the code and ran the job; the object store held the state.
    - **`gitlab/gitlab-runner:latest` breaks every job.** It was a pre-release (`19.3.0~pre.1819`), and the runner derives its helper image tag from its own version, so `prepare_executor` failed on `gitlab-runner-helper:x86_64-v19.3.0` — *manifest unknown*. Pin the runner to a released tag on GitLab's own line.
    - **`--clone-url` is not optional here.** GitLab advertises `external_url`, which must be `127.0.0.1` for a browser and is unreachable from a job container.

    - **It fits.** 3.54 GB image on disk, **2.3 GB** resident at steady state with swap untouched, ~7 minutes from `up -d` to serving. GitLab's 16 GB headline is the untuned figure and is not what this needs.
    - **`healthy` is not ready.** The container reported healthy after ~1 minute while `gitlab-ctl status` still listed only `gitaly`, `postgresql`, `redis`, `logrotate` and `sshd` — no `puma`, no `nginx`. HTTP answered six minutes later. Wait on `/users/sign_in`, not on the health status.
    - **`127.0.0.1`, not `localhost`.** On Windows, `localhost` resolves to `::1` first and the published IPv6 mapping gave an empty reply (curl exit 52) while `127.0.0.1` returned 200 — and the same request succeeded *inside* the container. `external_url` has to be the IPv4 literal or every link GitLab generates is unreachable.
    - **The backend works exactly as documented.** Empty `backend "http" {}` plus the eight `-backend-config` flags, `terraform_data` only, no provider plugin: `init` succeeded, `apply` wrote `"serial": 1`, and the state read back over `/api/v4/projects/1/terraform/state/lab`.
    - **A lock taken by hand blocks the apply**, with `HTTP remote state already locked: ID=manual-holder`. The interesting part is the `Lock Info` block: the request claimed `Who: lab@host` and Terraform printed **`Who: root`**. GitLab overwrites the holder with the token's own identity and stamps its own `Created` time — the lock belongs to a GitLab user, not to whatever the client asserted.
    - **Versions are addressable by serial.** After the second apply, `/versions/1` and `/versions/2` both return 200 and `/versions/3` returns 404 — the counter [[ch09-state-fundamentals]] measured, doubling as version history.

!!! example "🧪 Verified — the `gcs` backend against a local GCP emulator"
    Run 2026-08-19 on **Terraform 1.15.8** against **floci-gcp** (MIT, port 4588), which the labs start behind a `--profile gcp`. The book's AWS emulator cannot serve this backend at all: `gcs` talks to the Google JSON API at `/storage/v1/b`, and a source grep of the AWS emulator finds no Google surface anywhere. Lab: `labs/chapter15/lab3/`.

    - **State landed at `terraform/state/default.tfstate`** from `prefix = "terraform/state"`, confirming that the workspace name is part of every object path here rather than only of non-default ones.
    - **Locking needed no argument** and refused a concurrent run with `googleapi: Error 412: ifGenerationMatch: 1787133258314 != 0, conditionNotMet` against `gs://tf-state-lab/terraform/state/default.tflock`. Same conditional-write idea as S3's 412, in Google's spelling.
    - **`storage_custom_endpoint` is being used off-label.** HashiCorp documents it for Private Service Connect — "the protocol, the DNS name … and the path for the Cloud Storage API (`/storage/v1/b`)" — and it happens to be a plain URL override. That is this project's finding, not a documented promise.

!!! example "🧪 Verified — the `s3` backend against the lab emulator"
    Run on **Terraform 1.15.8** against **Floci 1.5.33** on `:4566`, so this whole topic is exercisable with no AWS account. Full transcripts in [[tf-backend-configure]] and [[tf-state-locking]].

    - **`endpoints` is an attribute, not a block.** `endpoints { s3 = … }` fails to parse; write `endpoints = { s3 = "http://localhost:4566" }`, plus `use_path_style` and the `skip_*` flags.
    - **The documented leak is real.** `.terraform/terraform.tfstate` holds the resolved backend config as JSON *including `access_key`/`secret_key`*, and the same values are embedded in the msgpack `tfplan` entry inside a saved plan file.
    - **`use_lockfile` locking is a `<key>.tflock` sibling object**, present only while the operation runs. A concurrent command fails with `PutObject … 412 PreconditionFailed` — the conditional write *is* the lock — and prints the `Lock Info` block with the ID, holder, operation, and start time.
    - **A partial block can be completely empty.** `backend "s3" {}` plus `-backend-config="./config.s3.tfbackend"` initializes fine; the "keys set to empty values" the docs show is a style, not a requirement.

!!! note "📌 `init` backend/provider flags"
    `terraform init` isn't just first-run setup — its flags manage the backend/providers over a project's life: **`-backend-config=…`** (partial/dynamic backend config), **`-migrate-state`** (move state to a new backend), **`-reconfigure`** (ignore existing state when switching), and **`-upgrade`** (re-resolve providers and refresh the lock file within constraints). (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 Four properties of a hardened state backend"
    Ephemeral values don't cover every case — some secrets land in state even when you're doing it right (a data-source read you chose because the value must persist, or a resource that returns a credential on creation, like an IAM access key). When a secret **has** to be in the file, protect the file. Four properties matter: **encrypted, versioned, locked, and readable only by the identities that run Terraform.** On S3 the backend block carries two of them (`encrypt = true`, `kms_key_id` with *your* key rather than the S3 default, and `use_lockfile = true`); the other two live on the bucket (enable **S3 Versioning** so a corrupted state is recoverable, and scope the bucket IAM to the run identities). GCS uses `kms_encryption_key` plus Object Versioning; Azure Blob authenticates via `use_oidc`.

    **Backend config leaks harder than provider config:** it is copied into `.terraform/` *and captured in plan files*. Backend credentials come from the environment — never inline, and never as `-backend-config` values. (See [[infisical-terraform-secrets]]; the state-encryption backend list is in [[tf-manage-sensitive-data]]; cross-links **A6**.)

    HashiCorp documents this directly ([[tf-backend-configure]]), and names the two plaintext files: **`.terraform/terraform.tfstate`** (the backend config for the working directory) and **every plan file**, each capturing that config as it stood at plan time. The consequence to plan around: **a saved plan applies with its own frozen backend config**, not current settings, so time-limited credentials baked into it can expire before the apply runs. Environment variables are read fresh at apply and are the documented fix.

!!! danger "📌 `terraform_remote_state` grants access to the *whole* state, not just outputs"
    The data source exposes only root outputs to your configuration — but "the state snapshot data is a single object, and so any user or server which has enough access to read the root module output values **will also always have access to the full state snapshot data by direct network requests**." State stores resource attributes in plaintext, secrets included. HashiCorp: "Don't use `terraform_remote_state` if any of the resources in your configuration work with data that you consider sensitive."

    Two recommended alternatives:

    - **On HCP Terraform / Enterprise — use the `tfe_outputs` data source**, which "does not require full access to workspace state to fetch outputs."
    - **Elsewhere — publish shared data explicitly** to a store with its own access controls (SSM Parameter Store, Consul KV, a Kubernetes ConfigMap, DNS records, an S3 object + `jsonencode`/`jsondecode`). Bonus: non-Terraform systems can read it too. Encapsulate the choice in a **data-only module** so you can swap strategies later.

    Also: **only root-level outputs are readable.** Nested-module outputs need an explicit passthrough `output` block in the producing config's root. (See [[tf-remote-state-data]]; cross-links **A6 (secrets)**.)

!!! warning "📌 `force-unlock` — use with care"
    When a run crashes mid-apply the state lock can be left held; **`terraform force-unlock LOCK_ID`** releases it manually (the lock ID is printed in the error). Use only after confirming no run is actually in progress — force-unlocking a live run corrupts state. (See [[feature-history]].)

    HashiCorp scopes it tighter than "no run in progress": force-unlock is for **your own lock, when automatic unlocking failed** — not a tool for clearing a colleague's stuck run. The lock ID is a **nonce** identifying one acquisition, so you cannot unlock blind and a stale ID will not release the current lock. (See [[tf-state-locking]].)

    **The command reference adds three things the concept page leaves out** ([[tf-cmd-force-unlock]]). Its single option, `-force`, only suppresses the confirmation prompt: `LOCK_ID` stays a required positional argument either way, so there is no blind-unlock form of this command. It *“does not modify your infrastructure”*, which is worth knowing before running it during an incident, since the damage it can do is the second writer it permits rather than anything it touches itself. And *“local state files cannot be unlocked by another process”* — the `local` backend locks through system APIs, so a stuck local lock is a process to deal with, not a lock to break. This is a remote-backend command.

!!! note "📌 S3 native state locking"
    The S3 backend now has **native state locking** via a lock file in the bucket (`use_lockfile = true`) — the separate **DynamoDB table is no longer required**. Older tutorials still show the S3+DynamoDB pairing; prefer native locking on new setups. (See [[feature-coverage-matrix]].)

    **Corrected 2026-08-13 — Terraform got here first, not OpenTofu.** This callout used to read "Terraform 1.11+; OpenTofu shipped native S3 locking in 1.10", which invites the wrong conclusion because both projects number releases `1.x` on different schedules. By date: Terraform **1.10 (2024-11-27)** introduced it as opt-in alongside DynamoDB, Terraform **1.11 (2025-02-27)** made it GA and deprecated the DynamoDB arguments, and OpenTofu **1.10 (June 2025)** shipped it about seven months after Terraform's first. Terraform 1.10 takes both locks during the transition. (See [[release-feature-map]], [[opentofu-release-feature-map]].)

!!! note "📌 OCI Object Storage backend (Terraform 1.12)"
    Terraform **1.12** added a native **OCI Object Storage backend** (Oracle Cloud), joining S3/GCS/azurerm/HCP as a first-class remote-state store. (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! info "📌 Coming: pluggable state stores (`state_store`) — watch, don't adopt"
    The backend list is currently **fixed and built in**, which is why the catalogue above is finite. Terraform has been building a way out: a **`state_store` block** inside `terraform`, letting a *provider* supply state storage the way it supplies resources.

    It has been in Terraform's config parser since **1.13** and is still gated behind `AllowExperimentalFeatures` in **1.16.0-rc1**, so it is alpha-build-only and unusable in a normal release. It appears in **no changelog** — not even the EXPERIMENTS sections that do list deferred actions and `test cleanup` — so there is no release note to watch. Found by diffing config schemas across tags. **OpenTofu declares no `state_store` block** — `internal/configs` has nothing corresponding — so this is the one place Terraform has in-flight architectural work the fork does not. One nuance worth stating precisely: OpenTofu *does* carry `ValidateStateStoreConfig` and `ConfigureStateStore` in its vendored `tfplugin6` protobuf, because both tools share that plugin protocol. The wire surface exists on both sides; only Terraform exposes a configuration block for it. Nothing to do today; know the name so you recognise it when it lands. (See [[release-feature-map]].)

**Milestone:** You can bootstrap a state bucket and migrate a project onto **both** the `s3` and `gcs` backends on open-source Terraform or OpenTofu, explain where each one puts the object and why the workspace name appears in one path and not the other, and demonstrate a lock being refused on each — naming the conditional write behind both 412s. You can read another configuration's outputs with `terraform_remote_state` and say who else can read that state as a result. And you can run the whole thing from a pipeline on a forge, with the state in the bucket rather than on the forge, and no long-lived cloud credential in CI.

---

### ⬜ I7 — State management operations

**What it is:** The state-surgery toolkit — `import` (+ `import` blocks), `state mv`/`rm`, `refresh`, detecting and reconciling drift, and `moved`/`removed` config blocks.

**Why you need it:** Real infrastructure predates your config, gets renamed, and drifts; you must bring it under management without destroying it.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Interactive — HCTut ["Import"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/state/state-import) + ["Manage resource drift"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/state/resource-drift)** (~1.5 hrs) — import a manually-created resource and reconcile a deliberate drift. The import half is captured in [[tut-state-import]] and costs nothing to run, since the resource it adopts is a **local Docker container** rather than anything in a cloud account. It is the whole config-driven workflow end to end: the `import` block's two arguments (`id`, provider-specific; `to`, the state address), `terraform plan -generate-config-out=gen.tf` for best-guess HCL (TF 1.5+), the **pruning** step that draft requires, and the apply that lands `1 to import, 0 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy`. The drift half is [[tut-resource-drift]], and it does **cost money** — the drift is manufactured with three `aws ec2` calls against a real EC2 instance, so there is no local-emulator version of it. Findings from both are below.

    **Then run the toolkit end to end — HCTut ["Manage resource state"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/state/state-cli)** (~24 min, needs Terraform **1.7+** and an AWS account). This is the page that puts every operation in this topic into one narrative: read the raw state JSON, `apply -replace`, `terraform state mv` between two state files, a `removed` block, `terraform import` back, reconcile an out-of-band delete, destroy. Captured as [[tut-state-cli]]. Two cautions before running it, both below.

2. **Reference — HCDocs [import resources](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/import) ([[tf-import]], with [[tf-import-single]], [[tf-import-bulk]], [[tf-import-generate]], [[tf-block-import]] and [[tf-block-list]] behind it), [moved](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/block/moved) ([[tf-block-moved]]), [removed](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/block/removed) ([[tf-block-removed]]), [`state` subcommands](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cli/commands/state) ([[tf-cmd-state]], with [[tf-cmd-state-list]] and [[tf-cmd-state-show]] behind it), [manually update state](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cli/state) ([[tf-cli-state]], the section index for everything that *writes* state) and its [resource addressing](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cli/state/resource-addressing) reference ([[tf-resource-addressing]], the grammar all of the above parse)** (~45 min) — prefer config-driven `import`/`moved` over CLI surgery. Know the full `state` toolkit: **`list`** / **`show`** (inspect), **`mv`** / **`rm`** (surgery), **`pull`** / **`push`** (read/write raw state — dangerous, backup first), and **`replace-provider`** (rewrite a provider source address in state, e.g. after a registry namespace change or a Terraform→OpenTofu move). The forget-without-destroying procedure has its own page, captured in [[tf-state-remove]]: the five steps, why HashiCorp recommends the `removed` block over `terraform state rm` (the block goes through plan, so you preview the result), and the fact that re-adopting the resource later requires an **import**, not an edit.
3. **Book chapter — TID Ch 6** (state operations) (~1 hr) — safe patterns and recovery.
4. **Book chapter — TUR Ch 3** (the isolation half) (~30 min) — why state operations get easier the smaller each state file is. Captured as [[03-manage-state]]: the bulkhead metaphor, the environment-then-component tree, and the three costs of it (many folders means many applies, duplication until modules, and cross-folder references that force `terraform_remote_state`).

!!! tip "🔧 Why `moved` avoids a destroy — the order of operations"
    Every other source states the *effect*; [[tf-block-moved]] states the *mechanism*:

    > **Before creating a new plan** for the resource specified in the `to` field, Terraform **checks the state** for an existing object at the address specified in the `from` field. Terraform **renames existing objects** to the string specified in the `to` field and **then creates a plan**. […] **As a result, Terraform does not destroy the resource during the Terraform run.**

    The state rename happens **before** the plan is computed. So by the time Terraform diffs configuration against state, the new address already has an object bound to it and there is nothing to create. Without the block, the plan runs against a state where the new address is empty and the old one is unclaimed — one creation and one deletion, because an address *is* an identity.

    It also explains why the block is safe to leave in place: a `from` that matches nothing in state finds nothing to rename, and planning proceeds unchanged.

!!! warning "📌 Instance keys across the three refactoring blocks — `removed` is the odd one out"
    With all three block references captured, the comparison is complete and it is **not** symmetric:

    | Block | Instance keys in the address? |
    |---|---|
    | **`import`** — `to` | **Yes** — `aws_instance.example[0]`, `aws_instance.example["env"]` ([[tf-block-import]]) |
    | **`moved`** — `from` / `to` | **Yes, on either side**, and mixing an index with a key is exactly how a `count` → `for_each` migration is written ([[tf-modules-refactoring]]) |
    | **`removed`** — `from` | **No** — *"You cannot include instance keys… if the resource is configured to provision multiple instances"* ([[tf-state-remove]]) |

    **And the reason is not in any of the three pages.** The address reference says an incomplete address is legal, and that *"the meaning depends on the context, so you'll need to refer to the documentation for the **specific feature** you are using which parses resource addresses"* ([[tf-resource-addressing]]). There is one grammar and several parsers. Nothing forbids `removed` from taking an instance key; `removed` declines to accept one.

    A multi-instance resource can therefore be **adopted per instance** and **rearranged per instance**, but only **forgotten wholesale**. Worth knowing before planning a staged migration in either direction.

!!! warning "📌 Every `state` subcommand that writes leaves a backup you cannot turn off"
    From [[tf-cmd-state]], the hub for the family, and it is the rule none of the individual command pages repeat: *"backups for state modification **can not be disabled**. Due to the sensitivity of the state file, Terraform **forces** every state modification command to write a backup file. You'll have to remove these files manually."* The path is set with `-backup`; read-only subcommands (`list`, `show`) write nothing.

    **The guarantee is narrower than it sounds.** It covers `terraform state <subcommand>` only. A `removed` or `moved` block goes through `apply`, so nothing here protects that path — which is the path this topic otherwise recommends. The unconditional *keep backups* instruction on [[tf-cli-state]] is therefore aimed squarely at the config-driven route, where the CLI does nothing for you.

    **The boundary is the command family, not the act of writing state.** `terraform untaint` modifies state and takes the state lock, yet its `-backup` is grouped with `-state`/`-state-out` as a **legacy, local-backend-only** option ([[tf-cmd-untaint]]) — the flags [[tf-backend-local]] records the docs as disowning. So an `untaint` against a remote backend writes state with **no automatic backup at all**, exactly like the `removed`/`moved` path.

    Two follow-ons. **Backups accumulate**, each a full copy of a file the same page calls sensitive, and nothing prunes them. And on **remote state** the subcommands still write those backups *to disk locally* — so a `state mv` against an S3 backend leaves a complete copy of the state on whatever machine or CI runner ran it. That is one more hole in the argument [[gitlab-tf-state]] and [[tf-state-backends]] make about who can read state.

    On PowerShell, write `-backup path` or quote the pair; an unquoted `-backup=path` gets split and the backup lands somewhere else.

!!! warning "📌 An address with the index left off means *every* instance"
    From [[tf-resource-addressing]], and it is the sentence to keep from that page: *"Omitting an index when addressing a resource where `count > 1` means that the address **references all instances**."* The same holds one level up — `module.foo` is every resource in every instance of that module call, and `module.foo[0]` is the narrowing.

    An address identifies **zero or more** instances, so how big the set is depends on how much you left off. What that means in practice depends entirely on which command is parsing it:
