    | Parser | A bare `aws_instance.web` with `count = 4` |
    |---|---|
    | `terraform state list` | filters to **all four** ([[tf-cmd-state-list]]) |
    | `terraform state show` | **error** — it requires an address resolving to one instance ([[tf-cmd-state-show]]) |
    | `-target` | the whole set, and its dependency walk is per **resource** anyway, not per instance (this topic's `-target` callout below) |
    | `terraform state rm` | the whole set — which is the dangerous one |

    Two legacy rules from the same page, both of which changed what an unchanged address means. **Module indexes need v0.13+**, since before that a module could not have multiple instances. And **a bare resource spec has meant the root module only since v0.12** — earlier versions matched the same type and name in *any descendant module*, so a pre-0.12 `terraform taint aws_instance.web` could reach into a child module where today's cannot.

!!! note "📌 `moved` is captured hands-on in A8 — two things to bring back here"
    The `moved` block's own tutorial sits in **A8** ([[tut-move-config]]), but two of its findings belong to this topic's model of state. **An address is an identity**, so a plan line reading *"because X is not in configuration"* means an address in state has no matching block — indistinguishable, to Terraform, between a deletion and a rename. And **`moved` preserves identity, not configuration**: moving a resource into a differently-implemented module produced `0 to add, 3 to change, 0 to destroy`, because the destination's defaults differ from the source's. A zero-destroy plan is not a zero-impact plan.

    `moved` also works only **within one state file**. Splitting a state still needs [[tf-state-refactor]]'s machinery, which is the migration tax [[tut-organize-configuration]] names and then dodges by destroying everything.

!!! note "📌 `state mv` moves state and leaves the configuration behind"
    The cross-file form is shorter than [[tf-state-refactor]]'s two-file version — run from the source directory, only the destination needs naming:

    ```shell
    terraform state mv -state-out=../terraform.tfstate aws_instance.example_new aws_instance.example_new
    ```

    But *"the move command will update the resource in state, but not in your configuration file"*, so a successful move leaves the destination one plan away from undoing it:

    ```text
      # aws_instance.example_new will be destroyed
      # (because aws_instance.example_new is not in configuration)
    ```

    The fix is manual — paste the `resource` block into the destination configuration, then the next apply is a no-op. `moved` has no such step, but works only **within one state file**, which is why the cross-file case still needs `state mv` (or `removed` + `import`, which [[tf-state-refactor]] prefers for new migrations). Verified in [[tut-state-cli]].

!!! note "📌 The `.` plan symbol — the forget marker"
    A `removed` block with `lifecycle { destroy = false }` plans as a lone **period** where `+`/`-`/`~`/`-/+` would go, and no legend the plan prints explains it:

    ```text
     # aws_instance.example_new will no longer be managed by Terraform, but will not be destroyed
     # (destroy = false is set in the configuration)
     . resource "aws_instance" "example_new" {

    Plan: 0 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy.
    ```

    `0 to destroy` on a plan that rewrites state — same counter semantics as `apply -refresh-only`. The accompanying warning names the exit cost: *"You will need to import them into Terraform to manage them again."* Transcript from [[tut-state-cli]]; the contrasting `1 to destroy` for a **bare** `removed` block was measured in [[tf-block-removed]].

!!! danger "📌 Forget a resource without destroying it — `removed` alone is NOT enough"
    Terraform's config-driven way to drop a resource from state is the `removed` block (preferred over `terraform state rm`) — but you **must** write `lifecycle { destroy = false }`. A bare `removed` block **destroys the object**.

    ```hcl
    removed {
      from = aws_instance.legacy
      lifecycle { destroy = false }   # omit this and the instance is destroyed
    }
    ```

    Verified on **v1.15.6**: without the `lifecycle` block, `plan` reports `1 to destroy`; with `destroy = false` it reports `0 to destroy` and warns "will no longer be managed by Terraform, but will not be destroyed." The `removed` block was **forget-only in v1.7**, so older material (including TID Ch2 §2.9) describes the old behavior. (See [[tf-block-removed]].)

    Two more constraints from [[tf-state-remove]]. `from` **cannot take an instance key** — `aws_instance.example[1]` is rejected, so a `count`/`for_each` resource is forgotten in full or not at all. And you must delete every reference to the resource's attributes before applying; `terraform validate` enumerates them.

    **OpenTofu 1.12** adds a lifecycle alternative — `destroy = false` on the **resource** itself — doing the same "forget, don't destroy" without a `removed` block. **No longer OpenTofu-only: Terraform 1.16 adds the same `lifecycle` argument** (`destroy = false` to prevent a resource being destroyed), so this converges rather than staying a fork difference. Until 1.16 is stable, the `removed` block is still the Terraform answer. (See [[release-feature-map]], [[tf115-ot112-features]], [[ot-dynamic-prevent-destroy]].)

!!! tip "🔧 Hand-write or generate? The docs have a rule"
    > We recommend **manually writing the resource block when you know how to configure all or most of the resource's arguments**. Use **generated configuration when importing multiple resources or a single complex resource that you do not already have the configuration for**.

    So `-generate-config-out` is the fallback for resources you do not already understand, not the default route ([[tf-import-single]]). Worth knowing before reading [[tut-state-import]], which generates because it is teaching the flag.

    **Either way the target is identical, and both pages state it from opposite ends.** Hand-writing: the strict minimum block is just type and label, but *"you should include provider-specific resource arguments that have **non-default values**"*, because *"Terraform uses default values for arguments you do not include"* and a substituted default that does not match reality plans an update — or a replacement, if the argument forces one. Generating: prune the all-arguments draft down to *"only required arguments and arguments whose values differ from defaults."*

    **The configuration should name exactly the arguments whose values differ from the provider's defaults.** Too many is noise; too few is [[tut-state-import]]'s `env = null`, which turned an adoption into a destroy.

!!! warning "📌 You can adopt one instance of a `count`/`for_each` resource. You cannot forget one."
    `import`'s **`to` takes an instance key** ([[tf-import-single]]):

    ```hcl
    import {
      to = aws_instance.example[0]        # count
      id = "i-abcd1234"
    }

    import {
      to = aws_instance.example["env"]    # for_each
      id = "i-abcd1234"
    }
    ```

    The `removed` block's **`from` explicitly cannot** — *"You cannot include instance keys, such as `aws_instance.example[1]`, if the resource is configured to provision multiple instances"* ([[tf-state-remove]]). So a multi-instance resource is **populated per instance and forgotten wholesale**. A staged migration into Terraform is possible; a staged migration out is not.

    **Two more things `to` and the block accept.** A **`module.` prefix**, so the destination `resource` block can live in a child module while the `import` block stays in the root — `to = module.instances.aws_instance.example`. And a **`provider` argument** taking an alias, `provider = aws.europe`, which is how you import an object that the default provider configuration cannot see. That is the correct fix for [[tut-refresh]]'s scenario, where a wrong-region provider made a live instance refresh as `has been deleted`.

!!! note "📌 `import` blocks are idempotent, which is why you can leave them in"
    > Because the import block is **idempotent**, applying an import action and running another plan does not generate another import action as long as that resource remains in your state. Furthermore, attempting to import a resource into the same address more than once **has no impact**.

    Terraform also *"records that it imported the resources and that it did not create them."* Both [[tf-import-single]] and [[tf-import-bulk]] therefore recommend keeping the block *"as a record of the resource's origin for future module maintainers"* — free on every later plan, and the only thing in the configuration distinguishing an adopted resource from a created one. Same disposition as a `moved` block ([[tut-move-config]]).

!!! note "📌 Resource identity — `id` **or** `identity`, and the choice is not stylistic"
    Terraform **1.12** added an **`identity` attribute** to `import` blocks as an alternative to `id` (the two are mutually exclusive). The reason is in [[tf-import]]: Terraform identifies a resource *"according to either the ID assigned by the cloud provider **or a collection of specific attributes defined by the provider**."*

    The page's own example is the clearest argument for it. An AWS **`s3_bucket`** identity is three attributes — **`account_id`**, **`bucket`**, **`region`** — because a bucket name is not unique on its own; the same name in another account or region is a different object. An **`aws_instance`** ID already is globally unique, so `id` suffices there. Structured identity exists for objects whose identity is genuinely compound, not as a nicer syntax.

    The standing rule is unchanged and applies to both forms: **ask the provider.** *"Refer to your provider documentation for information about how to reference resource identities."* [[tut-state-import]]'s Docker SHA256 and [[tut-resource-drift]]'s five-field composite `sg-…_ingress_tcp_8080_8080_0.0.0.0/0` are the same lesson from the `id` side.

    **Settled** by [[tf-block-import]], the reference every other import page defers to. `id` takes *"a string **or an expression that evaluates to a string**"*, and the one constraint is that *"the ID must be **known during the plan operation**."* So `each.value`, a `var`, or a `local` are all legal, and another managed resource's attribute is not — it is `(known after apply)`. `identity` is an **object of key-value pairs**, not a string. (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 Bulk import — `list` blocks, `terraform query`, and no tutorial anywhere"
    The second of [[tf-import]]'s two workflows, documented in [[tf-import-bulk]] and with no hands-on in any HashiCorp tutorial. Individual import assumes *"you can easily access unique infrastructure resource IDs"*; this is what you use when you cannot. Three steps: write `list` blocks, generate configuration, apply.

    ```hcl
    # queries.tfquery.hcl
    list "aws_instance" "prod" {
      provider = aws
      limit    = 50

      config {
        region = "us-east-2"
        filter {
          name   = "tag:Name"
          values = ["prod-*", "staging-*"]
        }
      }
    }
    ```

    Two labels like a `resource` block — the type queried and a local name. **`provider` is required** and defaults to the `terraform` block's configuration, though a `provider` block declared inside the query file can point the query at another region or account without touching `main.tf`. **`config` is provider-specific**, `filter` included. **`include_resource` defaults to `false`**, so only identities come back — set it `true` to reference any *attribute* of a discovered resource, at a performance cost. **`limit` defaults to 100 per block.** Meta-arguments work too, `count` included. The query file also takes `variable` and `locals`, so one `.tfquery.hcl` is reusable across accounts.

    **`-generate-config-out` is a flag on two different commands and they do different jobs** — the single most confusable thing here:

    | Command | Input | Generates |
    |---|---|---|
    | `terraform plan -generate-config-out=…` | `import` blocks **you wrote** | the missing `resource` blocks ([[tut-state-import]]) |
    | `terraform query -generate-config-out=…` | `list` blocks | **both** `resource` and `import` blocks, identities included |

    ⚠️ **Two operational traps.** The `generated.tf` file is written **to the local workstation even when connected to HCP Terraform or Terraform Enterprise** — remote execution does not eliminate the local step. And Terraform **refuses to overwrite it**: rerunning with the file already present *"results in an error"*, so iterating on a query is `rm generated.tf` then re-run.

    Afterwards, `import` blocks may be deleted or kept *"as a record of the resource's origin for future module maintainers"* — the same disposition question `moved` blocks have, answered the same way, and the argument for keeping is provenance.

    **On HCP Terraform the payoff is cross-workspace:** *"HCP Terraform uses resource identities to determine when resources are managed by another workspace."* That is the guard against a bulk import adopting something another team already owns — [[tf-state-purpose]]'s one-to-one mapping broken in the least visible way possible, two states over one object with neither aware.

    **Version:** the docs page's *"v1.12 or newer"* is scoped to identity-based search. `list` and `terraform query` are **1.14** — verified while capturing: `internal/command/query.go` is absent at `v1.12.0` and present at `v1.13.0`, the 1.13 changelog names neither, and 1.14's NEW FEATURES section announces both. Code in 1.13, announced in 1.14, same pattern as `state_store` ([[release-feature-map]]). **B8**'s existing 1.14 date is correct and needs no revisit.

    **Four things the `list` block reference adds** ([[tf-block-list]]). **`list` blocks are legal only in `.tfquery.hcl` files** — a hard restriction, not a convention. **`count` works too**, mutually exclusive with `for_each`, with the usual split: `count` for near-identical queries (`count = length(var.subnet_ids)`), `for_each` for map- or set-driven ones (`for_each = toset(["Todd", "James", "Alice"])`). **`limit` is a hard stop, not a filter** — *"when the number of results reaches the specified limit, Terraform breaks the connection to the provider and stops reporting results"* — so you get the first N in whatever order the provider returned them, and raising `limit` is how you learn whether you were seeing everything. And **lists can reference other lists**: with `include_resource = true` on the first, a second reads `list.<type>.<label>.data[<index>].state.<attribute>`, so results arrive as a `data` list of `state` objects and queries chain — discover one set, size or filter a second from it.

    ⚠️ **Take syntax from the workflow pages, not the block references.** [[tf-block-import]] and [[tf-block-list]] carry six and seven defects respectively — examples that do not parse, `count` written inside a `config` block, an Azure resource declared with `provider = aws`, a `provider = concept`, Summary boxes contradicting their own prose. The argument tables and behavioural sentences are authoritative; the samples are unproofread.

    **What this reframes:** every State-collection tutorial does discovery by hand — `docker inspect --format="{{.ID}}"`, `aws ec2 create-security-group` then `echo $SG_ID`, `terraform output -raw instance_id`. That step is exactly what a `list` block replaces, and none of them mention it.

!!! tip "🔧 `import` `for_each` — one block adopts an estate"
    `import` blocks take **`for_each`** (TF/OpenTofu 1.7), accepting a map or a set of strings. The path had this as a one-line feature note; [[tf-block-import]] shows what it is actually for.

    ```hcl
    locals {
      buckets = {
        "staging" = "bucket1"
        "uat"     = "bucket2"
        "prod"    = "bucket3"
      }
    }

    import {
      for_each = local.buckets
      to       = aws_s3_bucket.this[each.key]
      id       = each.value
    }

    resource "aws_s3_bucket" "this" {
      for_each = local.buckets
    }
    ```

    `each.key` indexes the destination, `each.value` supplies the ID, and driving the destination resource's own `for_each` from the **same** local is what keeps the addresses aligned.

    **The reference's second example goes further and is the one worth remembering** — `for_each` over a list of objects, importing into *module* instances:

    ```hcl
    import {
      for_each = local.buckets   # list of { group, key, id }
      id       = each.value.id
      to       = module.group[each.value.group].aws_s3_bucket.this[each.value.key]
    }
    ```

    A module instance key **and** a resource instance key in one address, both computed from the loop. That is the shape adopting a real estate takes, and nothing in the tutorials comes near it.

    **Placement**, which only the reference states: *"we recommend either creating an `imports.tf` file for all import configurations or placing each import block beside the destination resource block."* The first suits a bulk adoption you delete afterwards; the second suits keeping the block as the provenance record, since the origin stays beside the thing it explains.


!!! danger "📌 `-generate-config-out` is still an experimental feature"
    Easy to miss, because nothing that *uses* the flag says so. From [[tf-import-generate]], on a page banner-versioned **v1.15.x (latest)**:

    > **Experimental:** Configuration generation is available in Terraform **v1.5 as an experimental feature**. Later minor versions may contain changes to the **formatting of generated configuration** and **behavior of the `terraform plan` command using the `-generate-config-out` flag**.

    Every run prints `Warning: Config generation is experimental` alongside the plan. The **`import` block itself is not experimental** — only the generation is, and the distinction matters because config-driven import is otherwise the recommended path.

    So: fine for a one-off adoption you review by hand, which is the only use the docs describe. **Do not build tooling on the output format**, and expect a minor release to change the command's behaviour.

    Three operational rules from the same page. A path that **already exists is an error**, same as on `terraform query` — so it is a property of the flag, not of either command. If the configuration contains **no other resources for that provider** you must add a `provider` block explicitly, *"otherwise Terraform displays an error if it can not determine which provider to use"* — and adding one costs a re-`init`, which bites precisely in the empty-repository adoption case. And generation **stops repeating itself**: *"Terraform knows it doesn't need to generate configuration for resources that already exist in your state."*

!!! warning "📌 Generated configuration can be invalid, and the failed run still writes the file"
    > For certain resources with **complex schemas**, Terraform may not be able to construct a **valid** configuration from these values.

    The documented case is mutually-exclusive arguments — generation works attribute by attribute from provider values and emits both:

    ```text
    │ Error: Conflicting configuration arguments
    │   with aws_instance.ubuntu,
    │   20:   ipv6_address_count                   = 0
    │ "ipv6_address_count": conflicts with ipv6_addresses
    ```

    ⚠️ **Two rules on that page collide, and neither section mentions the other.** *"Do not supply a path to an existing file, or Terraform throws an error"*, and — of the failing run — *"Terraform still generates configuration and writes it to `generated.tf`."* So a generation that errors has nonetheless produced the file, and re-running the identical command then fails for a **different** reason. Edit the generated file in place (delete one of the conflicting arguments, which is what the page intends) or remove it before regenerating. (See [[tf-import-generate]].)

    **The clean plan shape, for comparison:** `# aws_iot_thing.bar will be imported` / `# (config will be generated)`, the resource rendered with **no action symbol at all**, and `Plan: 1 to import, 0 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy`. A pure import proposes no infrastructure action, so there is nothing to mark — a fifth rendering after `+`/`-`/`~`/`-/+` and the `.` forget marker.

!!! danger "📌 A generated import config is a draft, and its defaults can force replacement"
    `-generate-config-out` emits **all possible arguments**, including ones set to defaults and ones with no value at all. That is not a tidiness problem. In [[tut-state-import]] the generated `env = null` — where the Docker provider's schema wants a *set of strings* — read as a change on an attribute that forces replacement, so the very first plan of an adoption said `1 to import, 1 to add, 0 to change, 1 to destroy` with `# Warning: this will destroy the imported resource`. Setting `env = []` and pruning to the arguments that genuinely differ from defaults turned the same plan into `1 to import, 0 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy`.

    The remaining in-place changes were safe for a reason specific to the resource: Terraform uses `attach`, `must_run`, `wait_timeout` and friends to *create* a container, but Docker does not store them, so they become state-only values that never touch the running object. **Nothing in the plan says which changes are safe** — the tutorial states it outright: *"Provider documentation may not indicate if a change is safe. You must understand the lifecycle of the underlying resource."* A `~ update in-place` covers both a harmless default and a database restart.

!!! note "📌 Not everything needs an `import` block — but ordering still bites"
    Resources identified by a single unique ID or tag can be adopted by **declaring them and applying**, no `import` block involved. [[tut-state-import]] does this for the Docker image. The trap is the reference: swapping the container's hardcoded image SHA for `docker_image.nginx.image_id` *before* that image resource is in state forces replacement, because the referenced value is unknown and unknown never compares equal. Apply the new resource first, then point at it, and the next plan is a clean no-op. Same rule as [[tut-dependencies]], in an adoption setting.

    Two commitments the tutorial names and the docs page does not. Importing means Terraform owns the **entire** lifecycle including destruction, so a later `terraform destroy` takes the adopted object with it. And adoption reads only what the provider reports — never the infrastructure's **health**, its **intent**, or out-of-band state such as a container's filesystem — so import never tells you whether the thing you just adopted is the thing you wanted.

!!! tip "🔧 The hands-on for the `terraform refresh` deprecation — stage the failure, don't just read about it"
    [[tut-refresh]] (HCTut ["Use refresh-only mode to sync Terraform state"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/state/refresh), ~10 min, AWS and **not** free-tier) reproduces the exact hazard [[tf-cmd-refresh]] quotes, without needing a broken credential. Create an instance in the `region` variable's default, then write one line of `terraform.tfvars`:

    ```hcl
    region = "us-west-2"
    ```

    The provider now looks in the wrong region, and `terraform plan -refresh-only` reports a **running** instance as `# aws_instance.server has been deleted`. Nothing is broken — the provider is answering honestly about a region that has no such object. `terraform refresh` would accept that answer and drop the resource with no prompt; the tutorial's instruction at this point is *"do not run the apply operation."* Cleanup starts with `rm terraform.tfvars`, because removing the misconfiguration is a step rather than an afterthought.

    **Two facts from this page that are on no reference page in this path.** `terraform refresh` is **not supported in workspaces using HCP Terraform as a remote backend** while `-refresh-only` is — so on a `cloud` block the choice is already made for you. And the deprecation is explicitly *not* a removal: *"Though Terraform will continue to support the `refresh` subcommand in future versions, it is deprecated."* That is why it keeps reappearing in material written years apart.

    **The output behaviour has two readings, and both are right.** Here it is a feature — *"if you have any other workspaces that use the `terraform_remote_state` data source to access the outputs of the current workspace, the `-refresh-only` mode allows you to anticipate the downstream effects."* In [[tut-resource-drift]] it is a hazard, because accepting drift rewrites outputs that [[tf-remote-state-data]]'s consumers read. Same mechanism: the plan step is what turns the hazard into a preview.

!!! danger "⚠️ One tutorial in the State collection still teaches `terraform refresh` straight"
    [[tut-state-cli]]'s last exercise deletes an EC2 instance out of band and reconciles with a bare `terraform refresh` — **no deprecation notice, no `-refresh-only` alternative, no mention of the risk**. Two pages later in the same collection, [[tut-resource-drift]] calls `-refresh-only` *"safer than the `refresh` subcommand, which automatically overwrites your state file without displaying the updates"*, and the command reference deprecates `refresh` outright ([[tf-cmd-refresh]]).

    The hazard is live in that exercise rather than theoretical: its whole point is that `refresh` **silently drops a resource from state**, and the transcript shows it doing so with no prompt. Run `plan -refresh-only`, then `apply -refresh-only`. Same outcome, with the review step back.

    It is the collection's **outlier**, not its position: [[tut-refresh]] six pages later argues the case against that command at length, and [[tut-resource-drift]] calls `-refresh-only` preferred. One page out of step with two.

    **And the same shape appears on the docs side, which makes it a pattern rather than one stray tutorial.** [[tf-cli-state-inspect]], the section index directly above the `refresh` reference, describes the command in neutral present tense — *"updates state data to match the real-world condition of the managed resources"* — with no deprecation notice and no mention of `-refresh-only`. That is the worse instance of the two: a tutorial can be old, but a reference section index is what a reader consults *to find out what is current*. **Read the reference before trusting the index that led you to it.**

    The one thing [[tf-cli-state-inspect]] does supply is the only justification any captured page gives for a standalone refresh existing at all: refreshing happens automatically during plans and applies, *"but not when interacting with state directly"* — the `state` subcommands do not refresh, so anything read through them is as stale as the last plan left it.

    Its follow-through is worth keeping though, including the note most people miss: *"Terraform automatically performs a refresh during the `plan`, `apply`, and `destroy` operations. All of these commands will reconcile state by default, and have the potential to modify your state file."* An ordinary `plan` can write state.

!!! note "📌 `-refresh-only` drift reconcile"
    To reconcile drift without changing config, use **`-refresh-only`** mode (`terraform plan/apply -refresh-only`, since 0.15.4) — it updates state to match reality and shows what drifted, without proposing config-driven changes.

    Four things [[tut-resource-drift]] makes concrete that the flag's description does not. The plan reports drift in the **past tense** — `# aws_instance.example has been changed` — where a normal plan reports intent in the future tense, which is the fastest way to tell the two outputs apart. The apply **prompts** ("Would you like to update the Terraform state to reflect these detected changes?"), which is exactly the review step `terraform refresh` cannot give you. Its counters read `0 added, 0 changed, 0 destroyed` **while the state file changes**, because they count infrastructure actions, not state writes. And **outputs drift too**: a computed output over a drifted attribute is rewritten by the same apply, so a downstream configuration reading this state through `terraform_remote_state` consumes drift it never asked for ([[tf-remote-state-data]]).

!!! note "📌 Drift has three answers, and adopting it needs the import *before* the apply"
    A plain `apply` **reverts** drift; changing the configuration and importing what was made by hand **adopts** it; `ignore_changes` **keeps** it and stops planning against it. [[tut-resource-drift]] walks the middle path and names the first, [[tut-resource-lifecycle]] is the third — same repo, same `drift_example` tag.

    The ordering in the adopt path is the part worth rehearsing. Import writes state and **never touches the object**, so after `terraform import` the tutorial's instance is still missing its SSH group; the reconciling apply is a separate step. But the import has to come first regardless: without it the apply would have created a *second* security group and orphaned the hand-made one. `0 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy` is the evidence that the adoption worked.

    Two ageing notes on that tutorial. It still drives `terraform import` from the CLI — its own tip points at config-driven import as the 1.5+ replacement — and its transcripts are from **AWS provider v3.26.0** (2021), so the `aws_security_group_rule` idiom it uses is no longer the provider's recommended shape and its composite import IDs should be re-checked against current provider docs.

    **`terraform refresh` is deprecated outright**, not merely in spirit — the CLI reference says so in its second sentence ([[tf-cmd-refresh]]). It is "effectively an alias for `terraform apply -refresh-only -auto-approve`", and the auto-approve **cannot be switched off**. The documented hazard: with misconfigured provider credentials, "Terraform may be misled into thinking that all of the managed objects have been deleted, causing it to remove all of the tracked objects without any confirmation prompt." The docs go further than "prefer the flag" — prefer *neither*, and rely on the automatic refresh a normal plan already performs. (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! warning "📌 `-target` selects a subgraph in one direction — and not the one you'd guess"
    Before the OpenTofu comparison below, the Terraform mechanics, from [[tut-resource-targeting]] (HCTut ["Target resources"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/state/resource-targeting), ~20 min, needs AWS and is **not** free-tier).

    **The rule, in the page's own sentence:** *"Resource targeting updates resources that the target depends on, but not resources that depend on it."* Targeting walks **upstream only**. Measured on the same one-line configuration change: an untargeted plan was `8 to add / 8 to destroy`; `-target="random_pet.bucket_name"` was `1/1`; `-target="module.s3_bucket"` was `4/4` — the module **plus** the pet it depends on. So a target is a subgraph selection closed under "depends on" in one direction, not a filter over the plan. Addresses accepted: a resource, an indexed instance, a whole collection, a module. `-target` is repeatable, and works on `destroy`.

    **Dependencies are computed per resource, not per instance.** `-target="aws_s3_object.objects[2]"` planned the replacement of **all four** `random_pet.object_names` instances, because `objects[2] → object_names[2]` is not an edge Terraform has — `objects → object_names` is. This is [[dependency-graph]]'s source finding (`nodeExpandPlannableResource` per resource) showing up as behaviour. Instance-level targeting therefore gives no upstream precision at all, which is the opposite of what someone reaching for it usually wants.

    **The two warnings differ.** Plan prints `Warning: Resource targeting is in effect`; apply prints `Warning: Applied changes may be incomplete` and tells you to run `terraform plan` to see what is still pending. Both close with the legitimate-use list — *"exceptional situations such as recovering from errors or mistakes, **or when Terraform specifically suggests to use it as part of an error message**"* — and that last clause is the one case where using the flag is following instructions rather than improvising.

    **It leaves state internally inconsistent, not corrupt.** After one targeted apply, the tutorial's `bucket_name` output (which read the targeted `random_pet`) and `bucket_arn` (which read the untargeted bucket) described different buckets. Every value correctly recorded; they simply describe different moments. Always follow a targeted run with an untargeted `apply` — in the exercise that cost `7 to add / 7 to destroy`, so the partial apply deferred and fragmented the work rather than reducing it.

!!! warning "📌 OpenTofu: a plan is allow-listed or deny-listed, never both"
    OpenTofu's `-exclude` (1.9) is the inverse of `-target`, and the two **cannot be combined**. Verified in source (`internal/command/arguments/extended.go`), not taken from the docs, and the rule widened between releases:

    - **1.9** guarded only the two direct flags: *"-target and -exclude flags cannot be used together. Please remove one of the flags"*.
    - **1.10** widened it to the two **families**, on the same commit that added the file variants (`556ba25638`, first in v1.10.0). Any of `-target`/`-target-file` with any of `-exclude`/`-exclude-file` now fails with *"The target and exclude planning options are mutually-exclusive. Each plan must use either only the target options or only the exclude options."*

    It fails at argument parsing, before any planning. The consequence for recovery work: you cannot express "everything except X, but only within module Y" in one run — pick the allow-list or the deny-list and run twice if you need both. Terraform has neither `-exclude` nor the file variants, so this is an OpenTofu-only escape hatch and an OpenTofu-only constraint. (See [[opentofu-release-feature-map]], [[ot-exclude-flag]].)

!!! note "📌 Import is also the recommended way *off* CloudFormation"

    HashiCorp’s validated pattern [“Migrate CloudFormation templates to Terraform configurations”](https://developer.hashicorp.com/validated-patterns/terraform/migrate-from-cloudformation) (~11 min) sets out three routes, and the one it recommends is this topic’s: *“leverage Terraform’s `terraform import` functionality”* to bring the resources a CloudFormation stack already created under Terraform management, without disrupting them. The second route is a **translation** of the template into HCL, replacing intrinsic functions such as `!Ref` with ordinary resource references. The third is the transitional one worth knowing exists: the **`aws_cloudformation_stack`** resource lets Terraform go on running an existing template you are not ready to convert.

    Its migration advice is the same shape as this topic’s state advice — go incrementally, start with the least critical resources, and run *“CloudFormation and Terraform in parallel during the transition period”* with the stack retained as the rollback. It is the only migrating-from-another-tool material in this path; **B1** compares the two tools but does not move anything between them. Cached: `cache/web/vp-migrate-from-cloudformation.txt`.

**Milestone:** You can adopt an unmanaged cloud resource via an `import` block and rename a resource with a `moved` block — both with an empty plan afterward.

---

### ⬜ I8 — Provider configuration in depth

**What it is:** Multiple provider instances via `alias`, per-resource `provider` selection, multi-region/multi-account setups, provider version constraints, and authentication patterns.

**Why you need it:** Multi-region and multi-account deployments require aliased providers; version drift between team members causes silent breakage.

!!! note "Cover here (deferred from Ch5 §5.4)"
    Provider-wide conveniences that do not port between clouds belong in this chapter, not first-contact §5.4: AWS's `default_tags` block (stamps a tag set on **every** resource the provider manages, so you don't repeat a `tags` block) and GCP's `user_project_override` / `billing_project` (quota-project routing, which AWS has no parallel to).

!!! info "OpenTofu — one provider instance per element with `for_each` (deferred from Ch5)"
    Alongside `alias`: OpenTofu (since 1.9) lets an **aliased** `provider` block take `for_each` to spin up one instance per map/set element — e.g. one AWS region per element — without repeating the block. The default (unaliased) configuration must still be exactly one instance. Terraform has no equivalent as of 1.15; deep OpenTofu treatment is E3.

    ⚠️ **Give the provider its own collection.** The obvious move is to drive both the provider and the resources from one `var.regions` map, and it is the one thing that breaks: dropping a region then removes the resource instance and the provider instance needed to destroy it **in the same plan**, and OpenTofu makes you re-add the element, destroy, and remove it again. The constraint is about destroy ordering, not a subset check — full explanation and the source error text in the **E3** callout.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Reference — [HCDocs "`provider` block reference"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/block/provider)** (~40 min) — alias syntax and passing providers to modules. Captured as [[tf-provider-block]]. A module that needs a caller to hand it multiple provider configs declares **`configuration_aliases`** inside its `required_providers` — the explicit contract for the `providers = { … }` argument on the module block. (See [[feature-history]].)

    Three things this page pins down that the alias syntax alone doesn't. Configuration inherits into child modules but **`source` and `version` do not**, so every child declares its own `required_providers`. Provider arguments may use expressions over **plan-time-known values only** — input variables yes, computed attributes like `google.web.public_ip` no. And the `version` argument **inside** a `provider` block is **deprecated**; constraints belong in `required_providers`.

2. **Reference — [HCDocs "`providers`"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/meta-arguments/providers)** (~15 min) — the caller's half of the `configuration_aliases` contract. Captured as [[tf-meta-providers]]. The map's semantics are the part worth memorizing: **keys are configuration names used inside the child, values are configuration names in the parent**, both written as unquoted references (bare local name for a default, `<PROVIDER>.<ALIAS>` for an alternate). So `providers = { aws = aws.usw2 }` **remaps what `aws` means inside the child** rather than adding a provider to it — the module keeps writing plain `aws_*` resources with no `provider` argument, and each caller decides which region they land in. That is how you retarget a module without editing it. The argument is optional **only while the child declares no `configuration_aliases`**; declaring one makes it mandatory, because non-default configurations are never inherited. It also applies to Stacks' `component` and `removed` blocks, which the meta-arguments index page omits.

    ⚠️ This page still says supplying `providers` "cancels the default behavior" so the child "only has access to the provider configurations you specify" — the un-narrowed wording that [terraform#35781](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35781) was filed against. The correction landed on *Providers Within Modules*, not here. Read it as per-provider.

3. **Reference — [HCDocs "Providers Within Modules"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/develop/providers)** (~35 min) — also cited from **I5**, which takes the module-authoring half; here it is the module-boundary half, and the page reference #2 defers all module-author guidance to. Captured as [[tf-modules-providers]]. **Read it for the *why*, which the tutorials never give:** a provider configuration is needed to destroy a resource as well as create it, and Terraform records in state which configuration last applied each resource, so *"a provider configuration must always stay present in the overall Terraform configuration for longer than all of the resources it manages."* A module holding both its resources and their provider would remove the two simultaneously and leave state pointing at a configuration that no longer exists — planning then errors until you reintroduce it. The `count`/`for_each` conflict everyone cites is the **later, mechanical** enforcement (v0.13), not the reason. **Memorize the inheritance asymmetry:** default configurations are inherited, **aliased configurations never are**, and **source/version requirements are never inherited either** — *"This is especially important for non-HashiCorp providers"*, because a child that omits `required_providers` loses the source address and Terraform falls back to assuming `hashicorp/<name>`. Shared modules should constrain **only a minimum** with `>=` so consumers can move forward. **And the limitation no wiring fixes:** the resource-to-provider association is *static*, so a `count`/`for_each` module call cannot vary providers per instance — multi-region fan-out is one `module` block per provider set, by design. (Escapes: OpenTofu's provider `for_each`, or Terraform's, gated behind Stacks — see the callout above and E2.)
4. **Interactive — deploy to two regions** (~1 hr) — use two aliased AWS providers in one config.
5. **Book chapter — TID Ch 2 §2.4 "Providers"** (~30 min) — provider registry, `required_providers`, provider configuration, and aliases; auth strategies and pinning. (Captured notes: [[02-hcl-components]]. Authoring your own provider is E1 → TID Ch 12.)

!!! warning "📌 Aliasing every provider block changes what your unqualified resources bind to"
    The `provider` block **without** an `alias` is the default configuration. If *every* block for a provider is aliased, Terraform invents an **implied empty default configuration**, and any resource that omits the `provider` meta-argument binds to *that* — not to any of your aliases. If the provider has required arguments, those resources fail with a "not properly configured" error. So adding `alias = "east"` to your one existing `provider "aws"` block silently reassigns every resource that never named a provider. (See [[tf-provider-block]].)

    **There is no way to suppress the implied default** — no flag, no argument. But it is **lazy**: the error fires only when a resource actually binds to it, so an empty default nothing uses is inert. Two mitigations. Keep **one unaliased `provider` block** so the default is real and configured (the documented fix). Or set **`provider =` on every** `resource`/`data`/`module`, which is airtight until someone adds a block that forgets. Note that `providers = {}` on a module block does **not** cut off inheritance of the default — passing an explicit `providers` map overrides inheritance only *for the providers you enumerate* ([terraform#35781](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35781), closed).

**Milestone:** You can deploy the same resource to two regions from one config using aliased providers, and pin all provider versions in a lock file.

---

### ⬜ Intermediate Checkpoint

You are ready to advance when you can:
- Author and consume versioned, validated modules with clean input/output surfaces.
- Run a team-safe setup: remote state, locking, pinned providers, multi-region via aliases.
- Import unmanaged resources, reconcile drift, and refactor with `moved`/`import` blocks to an empty plan.

**Certification target:** **Terraform Associate (004)** — validates B1–B9 and I1–I8 (plus intro HCP Terraform from A4). Attempt after this checkpoint using **Assoc** + **Krausen** practice exams.

---

## Advanced

**Goal:** Operate Terraform in production — tested, automated, governed, and secure — the level the Authoring & Operations Pro exam validates.
**Estimated time:** ~76 hrs

---

### ⬜ A1 — Provisioners, terraform_data & escape hatches

**What it is:** `provisioner` blocks (`local-exec`, `remote-exec`), `null_resource`/`terraform_data`, `connection` blocks, the **`local` provider** (`local_file` / `local_sensitive_file` — writing files from a config) and the **`external`** provider — and, crucially, when *not* to use any of them.

**Why you need it:** Sometimes you must run a script, write a rendered file to disk, or trigger a rebuild; you also need to recognize when a provisioner is a design smell to be replaced by a data source or provider feature. B7 renders templates with `templatefile()` but never writes one out — `local_file` is the piece that puts a rendered template on disk, and it carries the escape-hatch costs (it re-creates on any machine where the file is absent, adding diff noise).

**How to learn it:**

1. **Reference — [HCDocs "Provisioners"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/resources/provisioners/syntax) (incl. the "last resort" warning) + [terraform_data](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/resources/terraform-data)** (~40 min) — read HashiCorp's own case against provisioners. Note the knobs: **`when = destroy`** (run at teardown instead of create), **`on_failure = continue|fail`** (tolerate vs abort), and the `self` object + `connection` block for `remote-exec`. Notes: [[tf-terraform-data]]. ⬜ The provisioners page itself is **not captured yet** — capture it when starting this topic. It is also the **`connection` usage page**, so read it with item 2 in hand.
2. **Reference — the `connection` block** (~10 min) — how a `remote-exec` or `file` provisioner actually reaches the machine. It is documented in **two halves**, and the obvious URL is a redirect, so know where to look before you go hunting:
    - **Usage** is on the provisioners page from item 1. `/language/resources/provisioners/connection` **redirects** there and serves a byte-identical page (checked 2026-08-02); there is no standalone connection page. It carries the placement rule ("Add a `connection` block to either the `provisioner` block or to the `resource` block. All provisioners in the configuration can use connection settings defined in the `resource` block."), `self` for referring to the parent resource, **ephemeral values in connection blocks (TF 1.10+)**, and the bastion-host and proxy how-tos.
    - **Arguments** are only in the [`resource` block reference → `connection`](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/block/resource#connection) — roughly 25 of them, including the full bastion set (`bastion_host`, `bastion_port`, `bastion_user`, `bastion_private_key`, `bastion_certificate`) and proxy set (`proxy_scheme`, `proxy_port`, `proxy_user_name`, `proxy_user_password`), plus `type`, `user`, `password`, `host`, `port`, `timeout`, `private_key`, `certificate`, `agent`, `host_key`, `target_platform`, and WinRM's `https` / `insecure` / `use_ntlm` / `cacert`. Notes: [[tf-block-resource]].

    **Three positions, not two.** Alongside the `resource` and `provisioner` positions above, a `connection` block on a **`removed`** block sets defaults for every provisioner running during removal — the teardown counterpart to the `resource` position. Notes: [[tf-block-removed]]. Never put a credential in one of these literally; that is what the ephemeral path is for (→ **A6**).

3. **Interactive — replace a provisioner** (~45 min) — take a `local-exec` hack and re-express it with `terraform_data` triggers or a data source.
4. **Book chapter — [TID Ch 10](books/tid/chapters/10-advanced-topics.md) §10.3 "Provisioners"** (+ §10.4 External provider, §10.5 Local provider) **/ TUR Ch 8** (~45 min) — legitimate vs illegitimate uses. TUR Ch 8 is captured as [[08-production-grade]] and treats these under the exact name this topic uses — *"escape hatches"* (p305): provisioners, provisioners with `null_resource`, and the external data source. The artifact to lift is its **provisioners-versus-User-Data sidebar**: `remote-exec` needs SSH or WinRM open, **does not work with ASGs at all** because provisioners only run while Terraform does, and leaves no console-visible record or `/var/log/cloud-init*.log` trail — against which User Data's only real cost is a 16 KB cap. ⚠️ It still teaches `null_resource`; `terraform_data` replaces it, and Terraform 1.9 added a supported `moved` path between them. `terraform_data` and other state-only resources are Ch 6 §6.8.

    TID's argument is **portability**, HashiCorp's is *last resort*, and the notes add the third: **failure semantics**. A failed provisioner defaults to `on_failure = fail`, which taints the resource — a flaky script does not merely fail, it condemns a healthy server to destroy-and-recreate on the next plan. And `when = destroy` is not a guarantee twice over: deleting the resource block deletes the provisioner with it (the `prevent_destroy` trap), and `create_before_destroy = true` suppresses destroy-time provisioners entirely. ⚠️ The chapter's argument list is also incomplete — both `local-exec` and `remote-exec` accept `environment`, `interpreter`, `working_dir` and `quiet`, and `environment` is the difference between passing a secret and printing it. For the `external` provider, the contract the chapter omits is in the provider docs: exit zero, or write to **stderr** and exit non-zero (stdout is ignored on failure), and **Terraform Enterprise guarantees no language runtimes** beyond shell utilities, which rules the whole technique out on TFE.

!!! note "📌 The alternative to a provisioner is a *handoff*, and `terraform output -json` is the seam"
    HashiCorp's case against provisioners ends at "use a configuration management tool" without showing where the boundary sits. It sits at the **output**: Terraform builds the hosts and publishes their addresses as a root output; a config tool reads that output as its **inventory** and takes over. Nothing runs inside the apply.

    ```shell
    # Terraform publishes; the config tool consumes. Two runs, one seam.
    pyinfra @terraform/server_group.value.server_group_node_ips apt.packages iftop _sudo=true
    ```

    Ansible is the incumbent here (the Red Hat-certified **`cloud.terraform`** collection); [[pyinfra]] is the same idea in Python and is the smaller, clearer illustration of it. What the handoff buys over a `remote-exec`: a failed config step **isn't a tainted resource** that gets destroyed and recreated on the next apply; re-converging **doesn't need Terraform** at all (that's what the config tool's idempotence is for); and the plan stops pretending to describe work it can't diff. The cost is real and worth naming: **two tools, two runs, and an ordering dependency** that a human or CI has to enforce.

!!! warning "📌 Outputs vs. state — pick the narrower grant when wiring in a config tool"
    The two mechanisms are **not** equivalent in what they expose. pyinfra's `@terraform` connector reads **`terraform output -json`** — only what the root module deliberately published. Ansible's `terraform_provider` inventory plugin builds its inventory from the **state file** — every attribute of every resource, plaintext secrets included. Same "get the host list" job, very different blast radius: the outputs-only consumer can be granted `terraform output` without being handed the state snapshot.

    This is the **I6/A6 access-boundary rule** showing up in a new place: "who may read this state" is the real permission boundary, not "which outputs do I expose." (See [[pyinfra]] and [[tf-remote-state-data]].) And before publishing an output for a config tool to consume, recall that **`-json` bypasses `sensitive` redaction** ([[tut-outputs]]) — the consumer reads it in plaintext by design.

!!! note "`terraform_data` exists to launder a plain value into a resource address"
    `replace_triggered_by` accepts **only resource addresses** — "plain data values, such as local values and input variables, aren't valid" — because replacement is decided from the *planned operations* of the referenced resources. Since a `terraform_data` plans an action whenever its `input` changes, wrapping a variable in one turns it into something `replace_triggered_by` can legally reference:

    ```hcl
    resource "terraform_data" "replacement" { input = var.revision }

    resource "example_database" "test" {
      lifecycle { replace_triggered_by = [terraform_data.replacement] }
    }
    ```

    Distinguish the two arguments: changing **`input`** updates in place, changing **`triggers_replace`** forces replacement (and so re-runs any provisioner the resource hosts). (See [[tf-terraform-data]].)

!!! note "📌 `actions` block (Terraform 1.14)"
    Terraform **1.14** added the top-level **`actions` block** — provider-defined operations *outside* the normal CRUD lifecycle (e.g. invoke a Lambda, trigger a CloudFront invalidation). This is the modern, provider-native successor to abusing a provisioner or `terraform_data` for one-off side effects: the action is declared by the provider and invoked during apply (or via `-invoke`), with a count reported in the summary. Newer than both books; verify against the CHANGELOG. (See [[feature-history]].)

    **Firing them: `action_trigger`.** An `actions` block declares the operation; a **`action_trigger` block inside a resource's `lifecycle`** invokes it:

    ```hcl
    lifecycle {
      action_trigger {
        events  = [after_create]
        actions = [action.ansible_playbook.provision]
      }
    }
    ```

    `events` and `actions` are required; `condition` gates the run. **Only four events exist** — `before_create`, `after_create`, `before_update`, `after_update`. There is **no destroy event** (verified v1.15.6: `before_destroy` → `Error: No events specified`), so destroy-time work still needs a destroy-time provisioner. Beware a docs bug: the reference's spec snippet calls the argument `conditions`, which Terraform rejects as an unsupported argument — it is `condition`. (See [[tf-block-resource]].)

!!! note "📌 `encode_tfvars` / `decode_tfvars` (TF 1.8)"
    Last-resort data-shuttling: the built-in `terraform` provider ships **`provider::terraform::encode_tfvars` / `decode_tfvars` / `encode_expr`** (TF 1.8) to (de)serialize `.tfvars`/HCL expressions as strings. Handy when a provisioner must hand a generated `.tfvars` to another tool — but prefer data sources / remote state; HashiCorp flags these as last-resort. (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 The other direction — Terraform triggering Ansible, not Ansible reading Terraform"

    The pyinfra / `cloud.terraform` callout earlier in this topic wires Ansible **downstream** of Terraform by reading its outputs or state. HashiCorp’s validated pattern [“Integrate Terraform with Ansible Automation Platform”](https://developer.hashicorp.com/validated-patterns/terraform/terraform-integrate-ansible-automation-platform) (~20 min) runs the seam the other way, with an **AAP provider** whose `aap_inventory`, `aap_job_template` and `aap_job` resources let a Terraform apply hand straight over to an Ansible job template — provision the VM, then trigger the configuration run as part of the same graph, with no separate pipeline step holding the two together.

    Its tool-division table is the useful part regardless of which direction you wire: Terraform is declarative, stateful, built on immutable infrastructure and optimised for *“a small number of high-level control points”*; Ansible is procedural, stateless, mutable, and built to operate *“across large inventories”*. The hybrid it recommends is Packer for the golden image, Terraform to provision from it, AAP for post-provision configuration and periodic compliance runs — which is the same escape-hatch reasoning as this topic’s, one layer up: the question is never whether to shell out, it is **where the handover boundary sits and who owns each side of it**. Note the guide is explicit that it addresses the *enterprise* editions of both products. Cached: `cache/web/vp-terraform-integrate-ansible-automation-platform.txt`.

**Milestone:** You can use `terraform_data` with `triggers_replace` to force a controlled rebuild, and justify avoiding a provisioner in a given scenario.

---

### ⬜ A2 — Testing, validation & checks

**What it is:** The native `terraform test` framework (`.tftest.hcl`), variable `validation` rules, `precondition`/`postcondition` blocks, top-level `check` blocks for continuous assertions, and **Terratest** for Go-based integration/e2e testing of real deployed infrastructure.

**Why you need it:** Untested modules break silently; the Pro exam explicitly tests validation and checks, and testing is what makes modules trustworthy.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Interactive — HCTut ["Write Terraform tests"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/test)** (~1.5 hrs) — write unit + integration `.tftest.hcl` for a module. Also cited from **I5**, where it is the third hands-on step: the module you author there is the thing under test, and TUR Ch 8’s rule that every example gets a test is unexecutable without this framework.
2. **Reference — [HCDocs "Tests"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/tests), [Custom Conditions](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions), [Checks](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/checks)** (~45 min) — the difference between validation, pre/postconditions, and checks. For the expression side of writing conditions see [[tf-conditionals]] — `can()` (wrap an error-throwing expression as a bool), **`self`** in a `postcondition`, and `each`/`count` objects in expanded-block conditions.
3. **Reference — [Terratest docs](https://terratest.gruntwork.io/docs/) + [quick start](https://terratest.gruntwork.io/docs/getting-started/quick-start/)** (~1 hr) — the Go library for deploying real infra and asserting on it. Native `terraform test` proves your *code* is right (outputs, plan, variable behavior); Terratest proves the *infrastructure works* by querying each live resource through its own API — catching the case where a provider bug makes a real resource that native tests, which only see provider-reported state, would still pass. Learn native first; reach for Terratest when you need real external-behavior checks. (See [[terraform-testing]].)
4. **Reference — [[terratest-facts]]** (~40 min) — the same library mined from the local `repos\terratest` checkout: the version state, the four-variant naming convention that makes the API readable, the `terraform.Options` fields that matter, the hygiene rules that decide whether a suite is usable, and the v2 module split. Read it before writing a first test — three of its rules (`-count=1`, `-timeout`, `defer` the destroy) are the difference between a suite that works and one that lies or leaks money.
5. **Book chapter — [TID Ch 9](books/tid/chapters/09-testing-refactoring.md)** (~2.5 hrs) — the fullest treatment of both frameworks, and the chapter Ch7 and Ch8 defer to. Its scoping rule is the one to internalise: **test your code, not your providers** — passing a variable to a resource and asserting it came back out proves nothing the provider does not already prove, so test the *logic* (transformations, computed strings, regexes, `dynamic` blocks with zero/one/many) and whether the system actually works. Everything hard follows from launching real infrastructure: time, money, orphaned resources, and name collisions between concurrent runs (fix with a `random_string` suffix, and put it *inside* the module for resources that hold a name after deletion, like `aws_secretsmanager_secret` at a week). **Guaranteed teardown is the thing to get right** — `defer` the destroy, raise *both* timeouts (Go kills at 10 min; Actions defaults to 360), and keep an account-reset path for when teardown fails anyway. The highest-leverage practice is **examples as test fixtures**: one artifact serving as documentation, local dev loop, and integration-test configuration, which also means the examples cannot rot. ⚠️ Three staleness corrections in the notes — parallel test runs (TF 1.12) undercut its CI workaround, mocks are 1.7 on Terraform but **1.8 on OpenTofu**, and the `rebuy-de/aws-nuke` it installs is archived — prefer [`gruntwork-io/cloud-nuke`](https://github.com/gruntwork-io/cloud-nuke), which covers AWS **and** GCP in one binary and, unlike the book's tool, has a real non-destructive mode (`--dry-run`, `inspect-aws`/`inspect-gcp`) — the safeguard that matters most for a tool whose failure mode is deleting the wrong account.
6. **Book chapter — [TID Ch 10 §10.6](books/tid/chapters/10-advanced-topics.md#106-checks-and-conditions) "Checks and conditions"** (~30 min) — the validation half of this topic, and the chapter that separates it cleanly from testing: Ch9 tests modules during development, §10.6 asserts against infrastructure **already running**. The distinction to internalise is **blocking versus reporting** — `precondition`/`postcondition` stop the run, a `check` only warns, which is what makes a live health check safe to evaluate on every plan. Three details worth lifting: conditions evaluate **once per instance** under `count`/`for_each` with `count.index`/`each.key` in scope, but `self` is bound for **postconditions only** — the chapter introduces it under the shared precondition/postcondition heading, and source in both engines binds it for `addrs.ResourcePostcondition` alone, which follows from a precondition running before the object exists; **scoped data sources** (a `data` block inside a `check`, visible only there, whose own failure is also just a warning) need `depends_on` or the check runs on the very first plan and reports a failure that means nothing; and preconditions were historically the workaround for `validation` blocks that could only see their own variable — since **1.9 on both engines** that expression is legal in `validation` itself, which is where a developer looks first.
7. **Book chapter — TUR Ch 8** ("Testable Modules", ~40 min) — captured as [[08-production-grade]]. Two things this topic needs from it. The **`modules`/`examples`/`test` triple** as a structural rule, with examples written first and doubling as both the test fixture and the executable documentation. And the honest **limit of in-language checks**: to assert a deployed service answers HTTP you would need retries, which the `http` provider has none of, and network reach the runner may not have — which is the argument for Terratest rather than a postcondition.

    ⚠️ **Its condition material is out of date in one specific way, and the error is widespread.** Ch 8 states that a `validation` condition "can only reference the surrounding input variable", and builds its precondition example on that. **Terraform 1.9.0** made validation rules general expressions that may reference other variables and data sources ([hashicorp/terraform#34955](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/pull/34955)), and **OpenTofu 1.9.0** did the same ([opentofu#2216](https://github.com/opentofu/opentofu/pull/2216)) — verified in both local checkouts at those tags. So the chapter's free-tier check against `aws_ec2_instance_type` now belongs in a `validation` block by its own preference ordering. Preconditions still own assertions about resources and data sources, and `self` is still postcondition-only. Ch 8 also predates **`check` blocks** (Terraform 1.5.0), the warning-severity fourth mechanism with scoped data sources.

8. **Book chapter — TUR Ch 9** (~2 hrs) — testing strategy (unit/integration/e2e) and Terratest for deeper Go-based testing.

!!! note "📌 `terraform test` vs TUR"
    `terraform test` post-dates TUR's main testing chapter — use HCDocs for the native framework, TUR for the strategy.

!!! note "📌 Native `test` vs Terratest — when to use which"
    Balance the two: run **native `terraform test` on every commit** (fast, no Go, no real infra); reserve **Terratest for integration pipelines / pre-release** (slower, deploys real infra, needs Go 1.26+ and cloud creds).

    Terratest's own framing of the split is sharper than "unit vs integration": the `*-spec` tools (inspec, serverspec, awspec, Goss) verify **properties** of a resource, while Terratest asks whether the infrastructure **actually works** — make the HTTP request and check the response, write to the database and read it back. Gruntwork also runs its suites nightly specifically to catch regressions in *dependencies*, such as a new Terraform version breaking a module.

!!! note "📌 Terratest versions — v1.0.0 (2026-05-11) is stable, v2 is in beta"
    v1.0.0 released **2026-05-11** and v1.0.1 on **2026-06-27**; **v2.0.0-beta.2** landed 2026-08-10. From v1.0.0 the library follows semver, and the deprecation contract is explicit: symbols renamed in v1 stay as deprecated aliases and **only disappear in v2**. v1 is in maintenance — security fixes only, on the `v1` branch, until **12 months after v2.0.0 reaches GA** — and since v2 ships under new `/v2` module paths, a pinned v1 consumer is unaffected. Write new tests on v1 today; there is no urgency to chase the beta.

    **v2 is a multi-module split**, not a rewrite: sixteen independently versioned Go modules (`…/modules/terraform/v2`, `…/modules/core/v2`, and so on), so a Terraform-only test stops dragging in every cloud SDK. Small utilities (`random`, `retry`, `logger`, `shell`, `files`) consolidated into `modules/core`; `http-helper`, `test-structure` and `dns-helper` lost their hyphens; and six packages — `collections`, `environment`, `git`, `version-checker`, `slack`, `oci` — were **deleted outright**, so code importing them does not port by changing an import path. Details in [[terratest-facts]].

!!! note "📌 The `Foo` / `FooE` / `FooContext` / `FooContextE` convention"
    Every Terratest helper comes in up to four variants, and knowing the rule makes the whole API readable without godoc. The bare name calls `t.Fatal` on error, so a failure fails the test; the **`E`** suffix returns the `error` instead, which is what you want when you are asserting on a failure or retrying. The **`Context`** suffix, added in v1, takes a `context.Context` as the second argument for timeouts and cancellation.

    Prefer `terraform.ApplyContext(t, ctx, opts)` with **`ctx := t.Context()`**, which ties the context's lifetime to the test. This is not just a style preference: in v2 the non-`Context` forms are removed from the source, so writing `*Context` calls now *is* the migration.

!!! warning "📌 Three Terratest rules that decide whether a suite is honest"
    - **`defer terraform.DestroyContext(...)` immediately after apply**, so teardown runs even when an assertion fails. Gruntwork still runs [cloud-nuke](https://github.com/gruntwork-io/cloud-nuke) nightly on the test account, on the assumption that cleanup sometimes fails anyway.
    - **`-count=1`.** Go caches test results, and editing only `.tf` files leaves the Go code unchanged — so `go test` happily returns a stale pass for infrastructure you just broke.
    - **`-timeout 30m`.** Go's default test timeout is 10 minutes and it *kills the process*, which means the deferred destroy never runs and the resources stay up. Pair it with `-p 1` and Terratest's `logger.Log` (which writes to stdout immediately, unlike `t.Log`) so CI's log-inactivity watchdog does not kill a long apply either.

    The full invocation: `go test -count=1 -timeout 30m -p 1 ./...`

!!! note "📌 Functions in test doubles (Terraform 1.15)"
    Terraform **1.15** lets you call functions inside `mock_data` and `override_resource` blocks, so test doubles can generate realistic values (GUIDs, resource IDs) instead of hard-coded constants. (See [[tf115-ot112-features]].)

!!! note "📌 Cross-object variable validation (Terraform 1.9)"
    Terraform **1.9** widened variable `validation`: a condition can now reference *other* variables, data sources, and locals (previously only the variable itself), so you can enforce cross-field rules ("if `mode = ha` then `replicas >= 2`"). The referenced values must be known at plan time. (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 `terraform test -parallelism` (Terraform 1.12)"
    Terraform **1.12** added **`terraform test -parallelism=N`** plus per-run annotations to run independent test files/runs concurrently — meaningful when a suite provisions real infra per run. (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 Test framework improvements (Terraform 1.13)"
    Terraform **1.13** sharpened the test framework: define **external variables directly in `.tftest.hcl`**, reference **run outputs from file-level variables** (shorter setup), and parallel teardown. (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 `expect_failures` in `run` blocks"
    A `run` block can assert a config *should* fail: **`expect_failures = [...]`** lists the checkable objects (a variable `validation`, `precondition`, etc.) you expect to error — the test passes when they do. Essential for testing your validation logic, not just the happy path.

!!! note "📌 The `self` object in conditions"
    A **`postcondition`** validates a resource *after* it's created, so it needs to read that resource's own applied attributes. **`self`** is the resource object the enclosing `lifecycle` block sits in: `self.id`, `self.architecture`, etc. Terraform provides `self` precisely because the resource can't reference itself by its normal address (`aws_instance.app.id` inside `aws_instance.app` would be circular). Legal only where that self-reference makes sense — `precondition`/`postcondition`, and `connection`/`provisioner` blocks. A `precondition` runs *before* apply, so `self` there is limited to values already known at plan time. (See [[tf-conditionals]].)

**Milestone:** You can write a `.tftest.hcl` suite that provisions a module, asserts on its outputs, and tears down — plus a `precondition` that fails a bad plan early. Stretch: a Terratest case that deploys the module and verifies real behavior via the resource's own API, with resource names namespaced by `random.UniqueID()` so parallel runs cannot collide, and `terraform.ApplyAndIdempotentContext()` asserting that a second apply plans zero changes.

---

### ⬜ A3 — Terraform in CI/CD automation

**What it is:** Running Terraform non-interactively in pipelines — `-input=false`, `-auto-approve`, saved plans (`plan -out` → `apply plan`), remote state, and PR-based plan/apply gating.

**Why you need it:** Production Terraform runs in automation, not on laptops; the Pro exam tests automation-friendly workflows.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Reference — [HCDocs "Automate Terraform" tutorial](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/automation/automate-terraform)** (~40 min) — the non-interactive flag set and the plan-artifact pattern. Know the automation env vars: **`TF_IN_AUTOMATION`** (trims UI hints that assume a human), **`TF_INPUT=false`** (never prompt), **`TF_CLI_ARGS[_name]`** (inject flags), **`TF_DATA_DIR`**. (See [HCDocs env vars](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cli/config/environment-variables), and [[terraform-env-vars]] for the rest — the docs page lists 14, the binary reads more, and the extras are mostly the ones you need when something is wrong.)
2. **Interactive — build a pipeline** (~2 hrs) — a GitHub Actions (or GitLab CI) workflow that runs `fmt`/`validate`/`plan` on PR and `apply` on merge. Use **`terraform fmt -check`** rather than bare `fmt`: it exits `0` when everything is formatted and non-zero listing the offending files otherwise, which is the difference between a gate and a no-op. Pair it with a **`pre-commit`** hook so contributors hit the same check locally first and the pipeline stops being where formatting is discovered ([[code-styling]]; the toolchain is introduced in **B4**).
3. **Book chapter — TUR Ch 10** (~1.5 hrs) — production CI/CD patterns and approval gates.
4. **Book chapter — [TID Ch 7](books/tid/chapters/07-code-quality-ci.md)** (~2 hrs) — the *whole* local-to-CI arrangement rather than a pipeline in isolation, and the chapter this topic is really built on. Its definition is worth internalising first: **CI is integrating work into the mainline regularly**, and the test gate exists to make that safe — so a team cannot adopt CI before it has a quality system worth automating, which is why the chapter spends five sections on tools before reaching GitHub Actions. Two structural ideas do the work. The **makefile is the single interface**: `make test_tflint` is what a developer runs, what the pre-commit hook calls, and what the workflow's `run:` line invokes, so the three environments cannot drift — and the workflow ends up containing nothing project-specific. And a **`TF_ENGINE` / `TF_BINARY` variable pair** turns "Terraform or OpenTofu?" into a runtime argument, which is what makes a two-engine matrix free (`fail-fast: false`, `include:` for legs the cross-product can't express, `continue-on-error: ${{ matrix.experimental }}` for prereleases that report but don't block). Add **tenv** for per-project engine versions — its `min-required` / `latest-allowed` channels resolve the ends of your own `required_version`, so the matrix can be derived from the promise instead of maintained beside it. ⚠️ Every version the chapter prints has moved, including all four GitHub Actions ([[ci-quality-tooling-versions]]); read them as syntax, not values.
5. **Book chapter — [TID Ch 8](books/tid/chapters/08-cd-deployment.md)** (~2 hrs) — the delivery half, and the chapter that argues **CI is not CD**: CI keeps the mainline workable, CD gets changes into environments, and the practical thing that separates them is **continuous reconciliation**, which a CI system does badly. Four things to take. **State locking is not coordination** — it stops concurrent runs, not two people applying different versions of the code in sequence and each reverting the other; that, not fashion, is the argument for a single source of truth. **GitOps is four CNCF principles**, and "continuously reconciled" means *reconciliation keeps happening*, not instantly — a nightly reconcile qualifies. **Root-module structure is a versioning decision**: one shared root means every environment runs the same code with no staged rollout, so prefer a thin root per environment pinned to an *exact* module version, which makes each upgrade a pull request. And **the constraint asymmetry** — loose (`~> 1.1`) when you author a module, exact when you deploy one. ⚠️ Its `terragrunt run-all` is removed (now `run --all`), and its AWS OIDC `thumbprint_list` is dead weight for GitHub; both corrected in the notes.
6. **Interactive — read a real harness, then review it** (~45 min) — `btkrausen/terraform-testing` (local clone: `C:\opt\learn\terraform\repos\terraform-testing`) is the GitHub Actions harness that validates the lab configurations behind **KL**'s author's courses, and it is small enough to read end to end. Take the pattern from it: **OIDC instead of static keys**, the third-party action **pinned by commit SHA**, an explicit least-privilege `permissions:` block, `TF_IN_AUTOMATION` with `TF_CLI_ARGS: -no-color`, `setup-terraform` with `terraform_wrapper: false`, **`init -backend=false`** so validation never touches real state, a `fail-fast: false` matrix with per-leg `mktemp -d` isolation, and a **weekly cron** whose whole purpose is catching the day someone *else's* release breaks you. Then review it against the callout below — reading a working pipeline critically is the exercise. Full write-up in [[krausen-lab-ci-facts]].

!!! warning "📌 The two ways a plan gate stops being a gate"
    Both come from the same harness, in the same repository, failing in opposite directions — which is why they are worth learning as a pair. `terraform plan -detailed-exitcode` returns **0** for no changes, **2** for changes, **1** for an error, and that middle value is what makes it awkward to consume.

    **A gate that can never fail.** The workflow runs `terraform plan -input=false -detailed-exitcode || true` and then `echo $? > plan_exit_code.txt`. `$?` is the status of the **last command run**, which is `true` — so the file always holds `0` and the checking step always reports success. The `|| true` is there for a real reason, since a non-zero status fails an Actions `run:` step and `2` is non-zero, but it swallows the value before it is captured. Capture first (`set +e`, or the step's `continue-on-error`), then branch.

    **A gate that can never pass.** The repo's standalone script does capture correctly with `PLAN_EXIT_CODE=$?` — but opens with `set -e`, under which the `2` meaning "plan succeeded, changes present" aborts the script before that line, making the branch written to translate 2 into success unreachable.

    The generalisation is the one already made about `terraform fmt -check` in step 2: running a command is not the same as acting on its result. And a third variant from the same file — the failure-notification job is gated on `needs.test-labs.result` while the jobs are named `test-tfb-labs` and `test-hol-labs`, so it silently never runs. A skipped job and a passing job look alike in a run summary, so alerting paths need one deliberate failure to prove them.

!!! note "📌 Automated dependency updates (Dependabot / Renovate) → now **A10**"
    A pipeline gates *changes you make*. It does nothing about dependencies going stale, and exact pins turn into a frozen ratchet without something to move them. **[Dependabot](https://docs.github.com/en/code-security/dependabot/ecosystems-supported-by-dependabot/supported-ecosystems-and-repositories)** covers registry modules, publicly reachable Git repos, and private registries, plus OpenTofu `.tofu` files and `terragrunt.hcl`, and is GitHub-only. **Renovate** covers the same ground on GitHub, GitLab and Bitbucket alike, and now has its own topic — **A10** — because the multi-forge setup, the platform-by-platform feature gaps and the lock-file behaviour need more room than a callout. Either turns an upgrade into a PR carrying a version diff, which is what gives **module** upgrades the reviewable surface the lock file already gives providers (see I4).

    **Neither bot updates a SHA-pinned Git module source.** Renovate can't (its HCL parser drops the comments a digest annotation would need) and Dependabot skips non-semver refs. So a module SHA-pinned for supply-chain safety is one this note cannot keep current, and it needs a manual review cadence instead. The trade-off and the per-case choice are in **I4**; the Renovate-side detail is in **A10**.

!!! note "📌 Two machine contracts, not one — and what a parser has to know"
    **[TID Ch11 §11.1.1](books/tid/chapters/11-alternative-interfaces.md#1111-json-output-and-machine-readable-ui)** draws the line the docs leave implicit. Commands with a single answer — `show`, `validate`, `output` — return **one JSON object** carrying `format_version`. Long-running ones — `plan`, `apply` — speak the **machine-readable UI**: JSONL, one object per line, each with `@level`, `@message`, `@module`, `@timestamp` and **`type`**, where `type` decides which further fields exist. A CI step that pipes `plan -json` into a parser is consuming the second contract, so it must read line by line and switch on `type`.
