    Three things measured on 1.15.8 that a pipeline parser gets wrong otherwise: **`validate -json` exits 1 on an invalid configuration while still emitting a complete document** (so "non-zero" must not be read as "no output to parse"); **`change_summary` arrives twice on an apply that also plans**, distinguished by `operation: "plan"` then `"apply"`, so keeping the last value silently discards the planned counts a gate might want to compare against; and the counters now include **`action_invocation`** alongside add/change/remove/import.

!!! note "📌 Machine-readable plan/apply output"
    For machine-readable plan/apply output, `-json` replaces stdout entirely (you lose the human view). **OpenTofu 1.12**'s `-json-into=FILENAME` writes the JSON to a file while keeping the normal UI on stdout — so CI can parse the JSON *and* a human can read the log. OpenTofu-only. (See [[tf115-ot112-features]].)

!!! danger "📌 The saved plan is a secret, and it expires by itself"
    B3 teaches `plan -out=FILE` → `apply FILE`. Two disciplines belong to the pipeline that carries it.

    **Never commit a plan file.** [HCDocs](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cli/commands/plan) is explicit: "If your plan includes any sort of sensitive data, even if obscured in Terraform's terminal output, it will be saved in cleartext in the plan file." It also embeds a full prior-state snapshot and every input variable value. Move it between jobs as a **CI artifact** with short retention (`upload-artifact`/`download-artifact`, GitLab `artifacts:`), restrict who can download it, and never let it reach VCS. Same hazard class as state, so pair with **A6**.

    **Staleness is enforced for you.** The plan records the prior state's `lineage` and `serial`. `apply FILE` compares both against current state before doing anything and refuses on mismatch: *"Saved plan is stale — the state was changed by another operation after the plan was created"*, or *"Saved plan does not match the given state"* for a different lineage. Any apply landing in the gap invalidates the pending plan. Verified in `repos/terraform` at `internal/backend/local/backend_local.go:319-331`; the check dates to **v1.1.0**, so it holds everywhere you'll run. Consequence for pipeline design: a long approval window doesn't risk a wrong apply, it just guarantees a re-plan. Combine with state locking so two applies can't interleave. HCP Terraform handles this server-side with its own refusal reasons.

!!! danger "📌 `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp` in a pipeline fails the run when the collector is down"
    Tracing a pipeline's Terraform/OpenTofu runs is worth doing (E5), but the enable switch is not free. Both binaries **exit 1** with *"Could not initialize telemetry: …"* when the exporter cannot be constructed while that variable is set, so exporting it globally in a runner image means a collector outage breaks `plan`, not just observability. Set it per-step, and treat the collector as a dependency of the pipeline.

    The payoff is OpenTofu-only for now: it reads **`TRACEPARENT`** (1.10), so if the pipeline exports a W3C trace context before invoking `tofu`, the run's spans parent under the pipeline span and one trace covers checkout → plan → apply. Terraform ignores `TRACEPARENT` ([#35444](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35444)), so its spans always start a detached trace. Also mind the endpoint: the default protocol is `http/protobuf` (port **4318**), and pointing at **4317** without `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL=grpc` sends HTTP at a gRPC port. (See [[otel-tracing-facts]].)

!!! note "📌 Making a local plan and a CI plan agree"
    Byte-identical output is the wrong goal — rendering depends on terminal width and TTY detection, and CI has no TTY. Two plans taken at different moments also *should* differ if reality drifted. What you actually want is the same **actions**.

    Compare structurally with `terraform show -json tfplan` ([[tf-cmd-show]] — note that plan JSON carries **plan + configuration + prior state**, three documents, while state JSON carries only `values`; and a plan made with **`-refresh=false` may not be renderable as JSON at all** after a provider schema upgrade). Per the [JSON format spec](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/internals/json-format), `resource_changes[].change.actions` is a closed set — `["no-op"]`, `["create"]`, `["read"]`, `["update"]`, `["delete","create"]`, `["create","delete"]`, `["delete"]` — with `replace_paths` naming what forced a replacement. Project to `{address, actions}`, sort, hash, compare. Never dump that JSON into a build log: `before`/`after` hold real values, and `before_sensitive`/`after_sensitive` only *mark* sensitivity rather than redacting it.

    Then pin what causes illegitimate divergence. CLI version (`required_version` plus an exact version in the runner). Provider versions (committed lock file plus `init -lockfile=readonly`, B3). The lock file carrying the runner's platform hashes (`providers lock -platform=…`, B3). Module versions, which nothing locks (I4). Same backend and workspace (I6). Variable inputs, watching for a gitignored `*.auto.tfvars` or a stray `TF_VAR_*` in your shell. Cloud identity, since `AWS_PROFILE`/`AWS_REGION` differences mean CI is reading a different account altogether. And configuration that is nondeterministic by construction: `timestamp()`, `uuid()`, and `most_recent = true` data-source lookups will differ between any two runs no matter what you pin.

    When the goal is "what I reviewed is what runs", stop comparing: plan once in CI, review that artifact, apply that file. Chase local↔CI equality only to debug why CI sees something you don't, and the answer is nearly always state or credentials.

**Milestone:** You can build a pipeline that posts a plan on pull requests and applies a saved plan on merge, with remote state and locking — passing the plan as a restricted CI artifact rather than committing it, and explaining what makes `apply FILE` refuse a stale one.

---

### ⬜ A4 — HCP Terraform / Terraform Cloud

**What it is:** HashiCorp's managed platform — workspaces, VCS-driven and CLI-driven runs, remote execution, remote state, private module registry, and run tasks.

**Why you need it:** It's on both the Associate and Pro exams, and it's the most common way teams run Terraform at scale without building their own automation.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Interactive — HCTut ["HCP Terraform get started" track](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/cloud-get-started)** (~1.5 hrs) — connect a VCS repo, run a remote plan/apply, use remote state.
2. **Reference — [HCDocs "HCP Terraform"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs) [workspaces](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces) + [run workflow](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/run/remote-operations)** (~40 min) — workspace settings, variable sets, and the run lifecycle. Also know the platform extras: **cost estimation** (shows $ delta on a plan for supported providers), **run triggers** (one workspace's apply queues another's run), and run notifications. A **`.terraformignore`** file controls which files are excluded from the config upload on remote runs. To reach infra on a private network, HCP runs through self-hosted **agents** (the agent pool executes runs inside your network).
3. **Interactive — HCTut ["Share modules in the private registry"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/module-private-registry-share)** (~8 min, HCP-only) — publish a module from a VCS repo into the org's private registry, then consume it from a VCS-driven workspace. Notes: [[tut-module-private-registry-share]]. The address form is `<HOSTNAME>/<ORGANIZATION>/<MODULE_NAME>/<PROVIDER_NAME>` — four segments, `app.terraform.io` on SaaS — and versions come from **git release tags**, semver `x.y.z` with an optional `v`, non-matching tags ignored, at least one tag required to publish at all. Know the two publishing workflows and what actually separates them: tag-based imports a new version when you push a tag, branch-based makes you click **Publish New Version** and pick a commit SHA (versions still pin to a commit, so this is not the mutable-`ref` hazard from I4), cannot re-use a version number, and is **the only one that supports module testing** — a constraint that lives in the docs, not the tutorial. Registry scope differs by product: HCP shares within an org, **Terraform Enterprise within or across** orgs. ⚠️ The page misstates its own source-address format with a spurious `terraform/` segment and tells you to set the registry Module Name to the *repository* name; both contradict its own working example. Take the address from the registry page's Usage Instructions. Evidence: `cache/search/hcp-private-registry-publishing.md`.
4. **Interactive — HCTut ["Add public providers and modules to your private registry"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/private-registry-add)** (~5 min, HCP-only, no config) — curate public registry entries into the org registry. Notes: [[tut-private-registry-add]]. Short, but the thing to get right is what curation **isn't**: the private registry *"stores a pointer"* and lets you *"designate which public providers and modules are recommended"*, and after removal *"Users in the organization can still use the removed provider or module without changing their configurations."* So the source address never changes, uncurated modules stay usable, and nothing breaks on removal — it is a bookmark list with docs attached, and **enforcement lives in A5**, not here. Worth knowing beyond the page: curation is delegable via the **Manage Private Registry** permission rather than requiring org ownership, it is drivable through the Registry Providers/Modules **APIs**, Terraform Enterprise must reach **both** `registry.terraform.io` **and** the Algolia search host or the search box silently returns nothing, and **no-code provisioning can be enabled on a curated public module** (Standard/Premium) — which turns a community module into UI-provisionable self-service without you maintaining it.

5. **Reference — the open-source private registry: [Terrakube](https://docs.terrakube.io/)** (~45 min) — HCP is not the only way to run a private registry, and the comparison is what makes the HCP tutorials above legible as *product* rather than *protocol*. Terrakube's `registry` component implements the module and provider protocols directly (`ModuleWebServiceImpl`, `ProviderWebServiceImpl`, `WellKnownWebServiceImpl` for service discovery), so a `source` of `<host>/<org>/<name>/<provider>` works the same way it does against HCP. Verified against `repos/terrakube` at `de53c946`, 2026-08-16 — details in [[terrakube-facts]].

    **The publish loop is register-then-tag.** A Terrakube module is a *registration record pointing at a VCS repository*, not an upload: `name`, `provider`, `source`, an organization and a VCS connection or SSH credential. Versions are discovered from **Git tags** by a scheduled refresh job, so you publish a new version by pushing a semver tag — the same contract the public registry enforces. Writes are gated on the `manageModule` permission, which is **admin-only** under the predefined roles (see **A9**).

    **Two fields worth knowing because they contradict a rule taught as universal.** `folder` puts the module in a subdirectory and `tagPrefix` namespaces its tags, so **one repository can hold many independently versioned modules**. The `terraform-<PROVIDER>-<NAME>` naming rule and one-module-per-repository constraint from **I5**'s Standard Module Structure reference are *public-registry* rules, not Terraform rules, and a private registry can relax them.

6. **Book chapter — TID Ch 8 §8.7 "CD platform overview" (p283) / TUR Ch 10** (~1 hr) — where a managed platform fits versus self-hosted CI. This path previously cited a vague "TID Ch on HCP"; the book has no HCP chapter. Verified against the TOC 2026-08-15: **HCP Terraform is §8.7 inside the continuous-delivery chapter**, and it is a *comparison* section — HCP Terraform (p283) is one of six platforms alongside **Env0, Spacelift, Scalr, Digger and Terrateam**, which is the part worth reading, since it is the only source here that positions HCP against its competitors. ⚠️ **Its Terrateam row has since been overtaken:** Terrateam's engine was open-sourced under **MPL-2.0** and the product is folding into **Stategraph** (`stategraph/stategraph`, OCaml), which stores state as a graph in PostgreSQL rather than one JSON blob behind one lock, with enterprise features staying proprietary. Verified 2026-08-15; see the open-source landscape callout in **A9**. (The `cloud` block half is already captured in TID Ch 6 §6.4.5 — see the callout below. TID Ch 11 "Alternative interfaces" is *not* about HCP: it covers wrapping Terraform in a client library, JSON-instead-of-HCL, and CDKTF.)

!!! note "📌 Already captured — [[workspaces]]"
    The `cloud` block half of HCP workspaces is written up in [[workspaces]] and [TID Ch6 §6.4.5](books/tid/chapters/06-state-management.md): `tags` versus `name` selection, `project`, what an HCP workspace owns beyond state, and the asymmetry when you edit `tags` later (adds are pushed to HCP, removals are not). A4 adds the platform side — workspace settings, variable sets, and the run lifecycle.

!!! note "📌 HCP's run queue is not the same guarantee as a state lock"
    A state lock is held for the duration of **one command** and released when it ends, so two people can each produce a plan against the same state and the second apply silently invalidates the first person's reviewed plan. HCP Terraform "can also detect attempts to create a new plan when an existing plan is already awaiting approval, by queuing Terraform operations in a central location" — it holds the **whole plan-then-approve cycle**, not the command. Keep the two apart when comparing HCP against a self-hosted backend: S3 with `use_lockfile` gives you the lock, not the queue. (See [[tf-state-remote]], [[tf-state-locking]].)

!!! note "📌 HCP free tier cap"
    HCP free tier caps at **500 managed resources** (legacy free plan ended 2026-03-31).

!!! note "📌 The `cloud` block (TF 1.1)"
    Wire a config to HCP with the **`cloud` block** (TF 1.1) inside `terraform {}` — it sets the org/workspace(s) for remote runs and state, and is the modern replacement for the older `remote` backend. (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 `terraform login` / `logout`"
    Authenticate the CLI to HCP Terraform (or any private registry host) with **`terraform login`** — it runs an OAuth flow and stores a token in `~/.terraform.d/credentials.tfrc.json`; **`terraform logout`** revokes it. This is how CLI-driven runs and private-module pulls get credentials. ([HCDocs](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cli/commands/login))

!!! note "📌 Agents are the only layer that emits metrics"
    Self-hosted **HCP Terraform agents** ship first-class OpenTelemetry support, unlike either CLI: point them at a collector with **`-otlp-address`** / **`TFC_AGENT_OTLP_ADDRESS`** (a `host:port` gRPC address), optionally encrypting that link with `-otlp-cert-file` / `TFC_AGENT_OTLP_CERT_FILE`. Without the cert file the connection is unsecured. Use the collector's **`contrib`** distribution, not `core`; a DataDog agent can be targeted directly instead.

    The agent emits **traces and metrics**, where the CLIs emit traces only. Metrics are prefixed `tfc-agent.` with the unit last (`.milliseconds`, `.bytes`): `tfc-agent.core.*` for agent status and job fetch, `…core.runtime.*` for Go heap/GC/goroutines, `…core.profiler.*` for CPU and I/O while busy, `…core.terraform.*` for 30+ init/plan/apply measurements, `…core.policy.*` for OPA/Sentinel (**Premium only**). Spans and metrics carry `agent_id`, `agent_pool_id`, `organization_name`, `workspace_name`, `run_id`, `run_operation`, which is what lets you slice run duration by workspace. Span names are plain English and deliberately undocumented, so explore them in the backend rather than expecting a reference list. ([HCDocs [telemetry](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/agents/telemetry) · [tracing](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/agents/tracing) · [metrics](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/agents/metrics)]; see [[otel-tracing-facts]].)

!!! note "📌 Health assessments (managed drift detection)"
    **Health assessments** are HCP Terraform's managed **drift detection** + **continuous validation**: HCP periodically runs an out-of-band plan on a workspace and reports whether real infrastructure has drifted from state, and whether the config's `check` assertions still hold — without you triggering a run. Enable per-workspace (or org default); trigger an on-demand assessment from the workspace **Health** section. This is the managed counterpart to the CLI-side `-refresh-only` drift check in I7. Interactive: HCTut ["Use health assessments to detect infrastructure drift"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/cloud/drift-detection); the combined drift-plus-policy flow is ["Detect infrastructure drift and enforce policies"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/cloud/drift-and-policy). ([HCDocs](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces/health))

**Milestone:** You can run a VCS-driven workspace in HCP Terraform that plans on PR, applies on merge, and stores state remotely.

---

### ⬜ A5 — Policy as Code

**What it is:** Governance guardrails at three layers — **plan-time policy** (Sentinel, open-source OPA/Conftest) enforced against a plan in HCP Terraform or CI; **static analysis (SAST)** of the HCL source (Checkov, Trivy/tfsec, KICS); and **cost gating** (Infracost).

**Why you need it:** Organizations must prevent non-compliant infra (untagged, wrong region, oversized) before apply; the Pro exam covers policy/governance.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Video — [HashiCorp "Introduction to Sentinel, Policy as Code Framework"](https://youtu.be/Vy8s7AAvU6g)** (~40 min) — what policy-as-code enforces and where it runs in the flow.
2. **Interactive — HCTut [Sentinel](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/policy/sentinel-policy) / [OPA](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/cloud/validation-enforcement) policy lab** (~1.5 hrs) — write a policy that blocks a plan violating a tagging rule.
3. **Reference — [HCDocs policy enforcement / Sentinel](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/policy-enforcement) + [OPA Conftest docs](https://www.conftest.dev/)** (~40 min) — Sentinel for HCP, OPA/Conftest for provider-agnostic CI.
4. **Interactive — run a static scanner in CI** (~45 min) — add one of the SAST scanners below to your A3 pipeline and fail the build on a deliberately misconfigured resource.
5. **Book chapter — [TID Ch 7 §7.4–§7.5](books/tid/chapters/07-code-quality-ci.md)** (~45 min) — the **source-side** half of this topic, worked end to end: Checkov and Trivy run side by side (they are free and overlap imperfectly, so run both), then custom policy in either Rego-via-TFLint or **Checkov YAML**. Take the recommendation as given — the book tells you to skip its own Rego section unless your team already runs OPA elsewhere, because a Checkov YAML policy expresses the same rule with no new language. Its motivating example is worth having, since it is not a security rule at all: AWS's GP3 volumes were strictly faster *and* cheaper than GP2, so "still using GP2" is a policy violation no generic ruleset will ever flag. Two operational details generalise beyond the tools. **Suppression asymmetry** — `#checkov:skip=ID:reason` carries a mandatory justification while `#trivy:ignore:ID` takes none, so a Trivy exception needs an ordinary comment above it or the audit trail is gone; and `.trivyignore` is project-wide, which is almost never what you mean. **Policy distribution** — `checkov --external-checks-git <repo>` loads the rule set from one shared repository, so a policy change reaches every project on its next run, with no PR anywhere (worth pinning to a tag once the set matters). Plan-time policy — Sentinel/OPA against what Terraform *will do* — is the complementary layer this chapter defers to its Ch8.

!!! note "📌 Policy is where module governance becomes binding — A4 only makes it discoverable"
    A curated private registry does **not** restrict what practitioners can use: it *"stores a pointer"*, designates what is *"recommended"*, and after an entry is removed *"Users in the organization can still use the removed provider or module without changing their configurations"* ([[tut-private-registry-add]]). HashiCorp names the control explicitly in its module-design guide ([[tut-pattern-module-creation]]): *"With Sentinel, you can assign policy criteria to all Terraform plans before execution. This allows for enforcement such that **only modules from the private Terraform registry can be used**."*

    So "approved module list" is two mechanisms, not one — **A4** publishes and curates the set, **A5** makes it mandatory. Write that policy as a worked example alongside the untagged/oversized ones in the milestone. The complementary control on *versions* rather than sources is I4's exact-pin discipline, since [[tf-dependency-lock]] never locks modules.

!!! note "📌 Static analysis (SAST) layer"
    Sentinel/OPA evaluate the **plan** (what Terraform *will do*). A second, complementary layer is **static analysis (SAST)** that scans the *HCL source* for insecure defaults *before* a plan — public S3 buckets, `0.0.0.0/0` ingress, unencrypted volumes — against a built-in rule library. The common tools: **[Checkov](https://www.checkov.io/)** (Prisma/Bridgecrew, largest policy set, also does terraform_plan JSON), **[Trivy](https://trivy.dev/)** (Aqua — absorbed the now-archived **tfsec**; use Trivy for new work), and **[KICS](https://kics.io/)** (Checkmarx, multi-IaC). Run one on every PR next to `fmt`/`validate`; they need no cloud creds and no state, so they're the cheapest guardrail you can add.

!!! note "📌 tflint — the correctness linter next to the security scanners"
    The SAST tools above hunt insecure defaults. **[tflint](https://github.com/terraform-linters/tflint)** is the complementary correctness/style linter: deprecated syntax, invalid provider arguments, unpinned dependencies. Two of its [terraform ruleset](https://github.com/terraform-linters/tflint-ruleset-terraform/blob/main/docs/rules/README.md) rules enforce the module pinning that I4 recommends but Terraform itself cannot check, because there is no module lock file. [`terraform_module_version`](https://github.com/terraform-linters/tflint-ruleset-terraform/blob/main/docs/rules/terraform_module_version.md) requires registry modules to carry a version, and takes `exact = true` (default `false`) to reject every constraint operator except `=`. `terraform_module_pinned_source` catches Git and Mercurial sources with no pin at all. Both are cheap PR-time checks needing no creds and no state, so they belong in the same pipeline stage as Checkov/Trivy.

!!! note "📌 Cost governance (Infracost)"
    **Cost governance** — [Infracost](https://www.infracost.io/) estimates the **$ delta** of a plan (it reads `terraform plan -json` against cloud price APIs) and posts it as a PR comment, so reviewers see cost impact before merge. It's the open, CI-native counterpart to HCP Terraform's built-in cost estimation (A4). Pair it with a policy (`infracost` has its own policy/threshold gating) to block a PR that blows a budget.

!!! warning "📌 Multi-tenant note — policy *scoping* is per project, policy *authoring* is not"
    A5 assumes one team writing policy for one organization. On a shared platform the two halves separate, and only one of them delegates. A **policy set** scopes to specific projects, workspaces or tags with exclusions, so each tenant can carry its own mandatory policies on top of a global baseline. **Authoring** does not scope: creating, editing or deleting Sentinel policies needs the organization-level `Manage policies` permission, which additionally "implicitly gives permission to read runs on all workspaces". So there is no way to let a tenant write its own policies without also handing it visibility into every other tenant's runs.

    The consequence is a workable division rather than a blocker: the platform team owns the policy code, tenants own which sets apply to their project, and a tenant's policy request is a pull request against the platform team's repository. Two override paths need the same care, since `Manage policy overrides` grants org-wide workspace read (use `Delegate policy overrides`), and a policy set marked overridable beats even `Hard mandatory`. Full treatment in **A9**.

!!! note "📌 Sentinel and SCPs are not competing policy engines — they fire at different moments"

    The clearest statement of the split is in the AWS landing-zone guide cited from **A7** ([VP](https://developer.hashicorp.com/validated-patterns/terraform/build-aws-lz-with-terraform)), and it settles a question this topic otherwise leaves open: what policy as code does **not** cover. Sentinel evaluates **Terraform plans and applies**, resource by resource, **before** anything is provisioned — mandatory tags, IAM boundaries, per-environment rules. An AWS **service control policy** evaluates **every AWS API call**, at the account or OU level, **at execution time**, regardless of what the calling principal was granted — deny a region, deny turning off CloudTrail or GuardDuty, deny wildcard IAM.

    So Sentinel is fine-grained, customisable and **bypassable by anything that does not go through Terraform**, while an SCP is blunt, rigid and unbypassable. A control that must hold against a console user or a rogue script belongs in the SCP; a control about how infrastructure is *written* belongs in Sentinel. HashiCorp and AWS publish pre-written Sentinel policies as a starting set.

**Milestone:** You can write a policy that fails any plan creating an untagged or oversized resource, wire it into a run, and add a SAST scanner (Checkov/Trivy/KICS) plus an Infracost cost check to the PR pipeline.

---

### ⬜ A6 — Secrets & sensitive data

**What it is:** Handling secrets — the `sensitive` flag, sensitive values in state, **ephemeral values / ephemeral resources / ephemeral input variables & outputs / write-only arguments** (the modern keep-secrets-out-of-state mechanism), the Vault provider, and dynamic/short-lived provider credentials (OIDC into AWS/Azure/GCP).

**Why you need it:** State holds secrets in plaintext by default; long-lived cloud keys in pipelines are a top risk. The Pro exam tests sensitive-data best practices.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Reference — [HCDocs "Manage sensitive data"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/manage-sensitive-data) (umbrella) + ["Sensitive data in state"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/state/sensitive-data) + ["Dynamic Provider Credentials"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces/dynamic-provider-credentials)** (~40 min) — why `sensitive` isn't encryption and how OIDC removes static keys. (Umbrella captured in [[tf-manage-sensitive-data]] — the hide-vs-omit-vs-both decision framework, the version-requirements matrix (0.15 `sensitive` / 1.10 `ephemeral` / 1.11 write-only), write-only `_wo`/`_wo_version`, and the state-encryption backends.)
2. **Reference — [HCDocs "Ephemeral values"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/manage-sensitive-data/ephemeral)** (~40 min) — the current best-practice for secrets. `sensitive` only hides a value in output; it's still written to state in plaintext. **Ephemeral values** (TF 1.10) exist only during a single command run and are *never* written to state or plan. **Ephemeral resources** (a block type that opens/closes a short-lived external object, e.g. fetch a token) and **write-only arguments** (TF 1.11 — resource arguments like a DB password that Terraform can write but never read back, versioned via a companion `*_wo_version` attribute) together let you pass a secret straight into a resource with nothing persisted. OpenTofu reached parity in 1.11.
3. **Interactive — HCTut [dynamic credentials lab](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/cloud/dynamic-credentials)** (~1.5 hrs) — configure OIDC so a pipeline assumes a role with no stored secret.
4. **Book chapter — [TID Ch 8 §8.5](books/tid/chapters/08-cd-deployment.md) "Managing secrets" / TUR Ch 6 "Managing Secrets with Terraform"** (~1 hr) — Vault integration and secret-injection patterns. TID's contribution is the **hierarchy**: eliminate the secret with OIDC, else a secret manager, else orchestrator storage — in that order, with the last two carrying explicit costs. Three things it gets right that the reference pages underplay. **Registering an IdP is authentication, not authorization**, and without trust-policy *conditions* scoping to your repository and workflow, **any other customer of that IdP can assume your role** — the role ARN is not a secret and does not need to be. **A secret manager does not keep secrets out of state**: a value pulled by a data source is still written there, so where the platform supports it (ECS taking a Secrets Manager ARN directly) prefer machine-to-machine retrieval that never passes through Terraform. And the argument for eliminating secrets at all is Travis CI's 2021 disclosure — your orchestrator can always read what you store in it. ⚠️ Its `thumbprint_list` pattern is obsolete: the argument is now **Optional**, and for Auth0/GitHub/GitLab/Google AWS validates against its own trusted-CA library and ignores any configured value, so the `data "tls_certificate"` step can go.
5. **Reference — [Infisical, "A Guide to Terraform Secrets Management"](https://infisical.com/blog/terraform-secrets-management)** (~25 min) — the **end-to-end workflow** the HashiCorp reference pages leave out, captured as [[infisical-terraform-secrets]]. A **vendor post** (every example routes through the `infisical` provider), so read the product comparison as marketing — but the Terraform mechanics check out and three things here have no equivalent in the official docs: a `grep` that finds `random_password` stored **three times** in state (attribute, output, bcrypt hash) against an apply that printed `<sensitive>` throughout; the same secret fetched ephemerally leaving a **181-byte state with no trace**; and an **anti-pattern review table**. Also the vendor-neutral criteria for picking a secrets manager (official registry provider · ephemeral reads · dynamic secrets + rotation · hosting/license), the note that **cloud KMS is not a secrets manager** (no per-secret ACLs, audit, or rotation), and where **SOPS** fits (file encryption whose committed ciphertext still diffs — a complement, not a replacement).
6. **Reference — VP ["Integrate Terraform with Vault"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/validated-patterns/terraform/terraform-integrate-vault) (~14 min, marked beta) + ["HCP Terraform Vault-backed dynamic credentials for the AWS provider"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/validated-patterns/terraform/terraform-vault-backed-dynamic-credentials-aws)** (~30 min) — the Vault half of this topic, at the scale where it becomes a design question rather than a provider call. The first guide separates the four surfaces people conflate: authenticating Terraform *to* Vault, dynamic provider credentials for AWS/Azure/GCP, reading and writing secrets with the Vault provider, and the **Terraform secrets engine**, which runs the relationship the other way by having Vault mint credentials for Terraform runs. It recommends HCP Terraform’s native **OIDC** trust over any static Vault credential, and — the deployment detail that is easy to miss — **Terraform Cloud agents**, so *“Vault DNS is not exposed to the public internet”*. Its mapping advice is the part worth arguing about in your own org: organisation → Vault root namespace, line of business → namespace, environment → its own secrets-engine mount (`aws-dev`, `aws-uat`, `aws-prod`), with the honest caveat that no mapping is correct, only consistent. The second guide is the worked AWS build of that pattern, and it supplies the operational trap: **align the TTL of Vault’s STS credentials with the JWT lifetime HCP Terraform issues**, then keep runs short, or a long apply dies partway on expired credentials. Both are HCP-side, so they sit beside the direct-OIDC dynamic credentials in **A9** rather than replacing them. Cached under `cache/web/vp-terraform-integrate-vault.txt` and `cache/web/vp-terraform-vault-backed-dynamic-credentials-aws.txt`.

!!! tip "📌 A worked ephemeral + write-only example, end to end"
    References #1 and #2 explain the mechanism; [[tut-no-code-provisioning]]'s RDS module is the shortest complete example of it. An `ephemeral "random_password"` feeds **two** write-only arguments — `aws_db_instance.password_wo` sets the database password, `aws_ssm_parameter.value_wo` stores it — so the generated secret lands in neither state nor plan file, and *"the only way to retrieve that password is by querying the AWS SSM parameter"*. Contrast [[infisical-terraform-secrets]], where a plain `random_password` showed up in state **three times**.

    **The detail worth stealing** is the `_version` companions. Because the value is never stored, Terraform cannot diff it, so `password_wo_version` / `value_wo_version` are what signal a change. The module points **both** at one `local.db_password_version`, which is how the database and its SSM copy rotate in the same apply instead of drifting apart. Requires Terraform **1.10** (ephemeral resources) and **1.11** (write-only arguments).

    The framing that organizes this topic: Terraform touches secrets it **manages** (the DB passwords and API keys it sets on your infrastructure) and secrets it **uses** (the credentials that let it call your cloud and your secrets manager). The first kind is fixed *inside* the config — `ephemeral` blocks and write-only arguments. The second is fixed *outside* it: the credential should reach Terraform **from the environment it runs in, never from the code it runs**, which in CI means a short-lived OIDC token exchanged against a machine-identity binding (`repo:org/repo:ref:refs/heads/main`) rather than a stored client secret. The identity ID is public and safe to commit. The `TF_VAR_`-prefixed secret name is the tie-in — store `TF_VAR_db_admin_password` and the plan step reads `var.db_admin_password` with nothing on the command line. (See [[infisical-terraform-secrets]]; precedence in [[tf-input-variables]].)

!!! note "📌 Pass coordinates into modules, not values"
    A module input like `db_password = var.db_password` multiplies the problem: **every caller** must hold the raw value, and it lands in **each calling configuration's own state** once the module uses it — one secret copied into every stack. Pass the *location* instead (`workspace_id` / `env_slug` / `secret_path`) and let the module fetch its own value with a data source or, better, an `ephemeral` block. Access is then controlled in the secrets manager rather than by who holds the variable. (See [[infisical-terraform-secrets]].)

!!! note "📌 Dynamic secrets vs. rotation — shrinking the theft window"
    Both replace "one static value that's worth stealing for as long as it lives," and they are **not** the same mechanism. **Dynamic secrets:** the platform holds a privileged login to the backend and mints a *new user per lease* (creation statement in, revocation statement at expiry) — Terraform's apply stores only the **instructions**, and generating a lease is a separate out-of-band step, so a leaked credential is worthless within hours. **Rotation:** for systems that need a single standing login — keep **two** users, one live and one idle, set a new password on the idle one, and switch the mapped secret keys over, so consumers migrate with no downtime and nothing redeploys. Reach for rotation only when dynamic isn't possible. (See [[infisical-terraform-secrets]].)

!!! warning "📌 `sensitive` leaks through `terraform output -json` / `-raw`"
    `sensitive` redacts values in normal CLI logs and the HCP UI, but the redaction is **narrower than it looks**. It redacts on plan/apply/destroy and on `terraform output` (all). It does **not** redact when you query **by name** (`terraform output db_password` → plaintext), with `-json`, or with `-raw`, nor when a child module's output is used in the root — the flags feed automation, so they bypass redaction by design. Combined with the fact that `sensitive` values are stored in state/plan as **plain text** anyway, this is why `sensitive` alone is *hiding*, not *protecting*. Use `ephemeral` (+ write-only args) to keep a secret out of state entirely. (See [[tf-manage-sensitive-data]]; the full redaction matrix is in [[tut-outputs]].)

    **Verified on v1.15.8**, because the docs disagree with each other here. `terraform output` with no argument prints `password = <sensitive>`; `terraform output password` prints `"notasecurepassword"`. The **language** *Output Values* page claims a named query shows `<sensitive>` — that is **wrong**, and the CLI command reference has it right. Transcript in [[tf-cmd-output]]. Also settled: `terraform output` can never surface an ephemeral value, since an ephemeral output is rejected in a root module *and* a root output cannot derive from a child's ephemeral output.

!!! warning "📌 A saved plan file is as sensitive as state"
    State gets all the attention, but `terraform plan -out=FILE` produces a second plaintext artifact with the same problem. [HCDocs](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cli/commands/plan) puts it plainly: "If your plan includes any sort of sensitive data, even if obscured in Terraform's terminal output, it will be saved in cleartext in the plan file." It carries a full prior-state snapshot and every input variable value, so `sensitive` does nothing for it — the same *hiding not protecting* gap as the redaction warning above. `terraform show -json` on that file exposes the lot, with `before_sensitive`/`after_sensitive` merely *marking* which values are secret. Never commit one, never paste one into a build log. The pipeline handling is in **A3**; the durable fix is the same as for state, which is `ephemeral` and write-only arguments so the value never enters plan or state at all.

!!! danger "📌 `terraform_remote_state` is a state-exfiltration path"
    Granting a config the ability to read another workspace's **outputs** via `terraform_remote_state` necessarily grants it read access to that workspace's **entire state snapshot** — outputs and state live in one object, and anything that can fetch the outputs can fetch the object by direct network request. Secrets in that state are plaintext.

    Treat "who may read this state" as the real permission boundary, not "which outputs do I expose." On HCP Terraform use **`tfe_outputs`**, which authorizes outputs without full state access; elsewhere publish shared values to a store with its own ACLs. (See [[tf-remote-state-data]]; full treatment in **I6**.)

**Milestone:** You can run a pipeline that authenticates to a cloud via short-lived OIDC credentials (no static keys), set a resource secret via a write-only argument so it never lands in state, and explain why `sensitive` alone doesn't protect the state file. (See [[feature-coverage-matrix]].)

---

### ⬜ A7 — Multi-environment & multi-account patterns

**What it is:** Structuring dev/stage/prod and multiple accounts/regions — CLI workspaces vs directory-per-env vs HCP workspaces, and DRY strategies.

**Why you need it:** Environment isolation done wrong causes cross-env blast radius; the Pro exam and every real org need a scalable layout.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Book chapter — TUR Ch 3 (§ "Isolation via Workspaces" p94 / "Isolation via File Layout" p100) + Ch 7 "Working with Multiple Providers"** (~2 hrs) — file-layout vs workspace tradeoffs (the definitive treatment), then Ch 7 for the multi-account/multi-region half of this topic (§ "Working with Multiple AWS Accounts" p238). Ch 3 is captured as [[03-manage-state]], and it is the **third** source contradicting the `terraform.workspace` lookup-map pattern the warning below names: its two requirements for real isolation are a folder per environment **and a separate backend with different credentials and access controls**, with the second doing the actual work. Its footnote also explains why the anti-pattern survives — workspaces were once called "environments", so the feature's own former name taught it.
2. **Reference — [HCDocs "Workspaces" (CLI)](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/state/workspaces) vs [HCP workspaces](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces)** (~30 min) — understand why CLI workspaces are *not* environment isolation. In automation, **`TF_WORKSPACE`** selects the workspace non-interactively (instead of `terraform workspace select`). The CLI page is captured in [[tf-state-workspaces]], which pins down the piece the other sources leave vague: **which backends actually support named workspaces** (ten, enumerated — `http` and `oci` are not among them), so "remote backend" and "multiple states" are separate properties. The licensing angle is in [[workspaces-facts]]: CLI workspaces ship in OpenTofu too (`tofu workspace`), while the HCP workspace is a **proprietary** unit with its own config, variables, run history and RBAC — no single open-source product replaces it, but **Terragrunt** covers the isolation half and **Atlantis** or ordinary CI covers the platform half.
3. **Interactive — HCTut ["Refactor monolithic Terraform configuration"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/organize-configuration)** (~18 min) — walk one dev+prod configuration through monolith → separate files → separate state, with directories and workspaces as alternative endings. Notes: [[tut-organize-configuration]]. **Do this one for its middle step**, which is the best demonstration of blast radius anywhere in the tutorials: a shared `random_pet` feeds both bucket names, so bumping its `length` in `dev.tf` — a file containing nothing but development resources — destroys and recreates production too. Splitting `main.tf` into `dev.tf` and `prod.tf` changes readability and *nothing else*, because the coupling lives in the reference graph, not the filesystem. Take three more things from it: **`-target` is named and dismissed** as "risky and not a sustainable way to manage distinct environments"; **converting one state into per-directory states requires `state mv` work**, which the exercise dodges by destroying everything first and real projects cannot; and the local backend moves non-`default` workspace state to `terraform.tfstate.d/<name>/terraform.tfstate`. ⚠️ **The page teaches CLI workspaces as environment separation, which reference #2 above exists to contradict** — read its decision rule as being about configuration duplication, not isolation. Its resource counts are wrong twice over (says 5, describes 9, repo defines 15) and it tells you to comment out a `terraform` block the current repo does not contain — that repo pins no provider versions at all.
4. **Reference — VP ["Use Terraform to build an AWS landing zone"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/validated-patterns/terraform/build-aws-lz-with-terraform)** (~40 min) — the account-structure layer this topic otherwise stops short of, and the only landing-zone material in this path. Its organising split is **platform landing zone (PLZ) versus workload landing zone (WLZ)**: the PLZ carries AWS Organizations and the OU tree, federated IAM, hub-and-spoke networking, the security services and centralised logging, and *“is not intended for workloads, but rather for enabling and governing them”*; each WLZ is an application’s own set of accounts, autonomous to deploy but inheriting the PLZ’s guardrails. **The rule that makes it this topic’s material rather than A9’s** is one line of its design principles: deploy each zone in a dedicated AWS account *and* a dedicated Terraform workspace, so the account boundary and the state boundary are the same boundary — A7’s isolation argument at organisation scale. Read it after the file-layout material above, and pair it with the Sentinel-versus-SCP split in **A5**. Cached: `cache/web/vp-build-aws-lz-with-terraform.txt`.
5. **Reference — [HCDocs "S3 backend" § "Multi-account AWS Architecture"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend/s3)** (~25 min) — the one place HashiCorp publishes a multi-account layout as a worked recipe rather than as advice, captured in [[tf-backend-s3]]. Read it against the file-layout material above, because **it is also the strongest published case for the workspace pattern this topic otherwise argues against**. What makes it defensible is that the workspace name is not merely a state path: the provider does `assume_role = { role_arn = var.workspace_iam_roles[terraform.workspace] }`, so selecting a workspace selects the **AWS account** the apply runs in. The isolation is real, not nominal, which is exactly what TF2026's Lecture 09 demo below lacks. **Its structural rule is that the tool's own infrastructure lives outside what the tool manages** — *"ideally the infrastructure that is used by Terraform should exist outside of the infrastructure that Terraform manages"* — so one administrative account holds the human IAM users and **the single state bucket for every workspace**, while each environment account holds only a role that trusts the administrative account. **The split that makes it work:** the backend authenticates as the administrator's own user in the administrative account, and the provider assumes the environment role, so state operations and infrastructure operations run as different principals. In automation, put the runner on an EC2 instance in the administrative account with an instance profile, and use *"a separate EC2 instance for each target account so that its access can be limited only to the single account."* The page then closes the hole its own default leaves — *"all users can read and write states for all workspaces"* — with a per-object IAM policy, and the statement people omit is the third one: `s3:DeleteObject` on **`<key>.tflock`**, because a policy written before `use_lockfile` existed fails at lock acquisition rather than at write. The per-person half of this is **A9**.
6. **Interactive — restructure** (~1.5 hrs) — lay out one module consumed by isolated dev/prod stacks with separate state.

!!! warning "📌 TF2026's environment lecture teaches the pattern this topic argues against"
    Worth knowing before watching it, since the course is cited elsewhere in this path. Its Lecture 09 demo keys instance size off the workspace name — `lookup(var.instance_type, terraform.workspace)` over a `{ dev, staging, prod }` map — so production differs from development by one string inside one backend, one credential set and one directory. That is the shape reference #2 exists to contradict. The course's README does list folder-per-environment as an alternative, but the runnable demo is the workspace one. Its own bug is a useful tell: a two-argument `lookup` errors on a missing key and the map has no `default` entry, so the demo fails in the workspace a learner is in before creating any. Full read-through in [[tf2026-course-facts]].

!!! note "📌 Already captured — [[workspaces]]"
    The two meanings of "workspace" are already written up in [[workspaces]], from TID Ch6 §6.4.5 + §6.4.7 and the official docs: the CLI-vs-HCP comparison table, where each backend stores per-workspace state, `terraform.workspace` at plan time, and the documented limit that workspaces "are not appropriate for system decomposition or deployments requiring separate credentials and access controls." That second clause is the seam to **A9**, which picks up separate credentials and access controls as its own topic. What remains for A7 is the *decision* — TUR Ch3's file-layout-versus-workspace tradeoff — and the multi-account half from TUR Ch7.

!!! note "📌 DRY across environments → Terragrunt (E4)"
    A7 covers isolation with **native Terraform** primitives — the scope the Pro exam tests. The DRY tooling that removes the copy-paste between per-env directories is **Terragrunt** (third-party, Gruntwork), deep-dived in **E4 — Large-scale state & repo architecture** below. Reach for it once you have many states and teams, not for a single dev/prod split.

    Note that DRY is no longer how Terragrunt describes itself. Its own docs say the focus "has shifted to offering more tooling for *orchestrating* infrastructure," and warn that you "might miss the forest for the trees" by evaluating it on DRY alone. Evaluate it as a run orchestrator — discovery, a run queue, a cross-unit DAG — not as a config deduplicator. See [[terragrunt-facts]].

**Milestone:** You can design an env layout where prod and dev have fully isolated state and blast radius, sharing modules but not state.

---

### ⬜ A8 — Refactoring at scale

**What it is:** Safely evolving large codebases — `moved`/`import`/`removed` blocks, module version bumps across consumers, splitting monolith state, and state migration.

**Why you need it:** Big Terraform codebases must be refactored without downtime or resource re-creation; this is a senior/Pro-level skill.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Interactive — HCTut ["Use configuration to move resources"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/move-config)** (~14 min) — do this before the reading; it is the `moved` block end to end. Notes: [[tut-move-config]]. Extract a root resource into a local module *and* swap a hand-written module for a registry one, watch the un-`moved` plan report **4 to add, 4 to destroy** with the tell-tale reason line *"because X is not in configuration"*, then add `moved` blocks and re-plan. **The find worth carrying is that the fixed plan is `0 to add, 3 to change, 0 to destroy` — not empty.** `moved` preserves **identity, not configuration**, so swapping implementations is a move *and* a config change arriving in one apply, while extracting your own code into a module is a clean move (`has moved to`, no action symbol). A zero-destroy plan is not a zero-impact plan. Also: a module-level `moved { from = module.vpc, to = module.learn_vpc }` cascades to every address beneath it, but **requires `terraform init`** because the install key changes; and `moved` blocks are written in the **root**, naming addresses inside modules on both sides, with the target's `[0]` index keys taken from the destination module's internal addressing.
2. **Reference — [HCDocs "Refactor modules" (moved blocks)](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/modules/develop/refactoring)** (~40 min) — config-driven refactors that keep the plan empty. Captured as [[tf-modules-refactoring]]. Its author-side half — scoping, chaining, and the module-split shim — is **I5**'s Refactor modules entry; this is the whole page, read as an operator refactoring a live codebase. **The governing rule is the addressing one:** a bare address moves a resource *as a whole*, covering every instance, but an instance key on **either** side switches the block into instance mode — which is what makes `count` ↔ `for_each` ↔ neither migrations expressible at all, one `moved` block per index. Neither [[tut-count]] nor [[tut-for-each]] mentions this, so the migration they walk you through is a destroy-and-recreate without it. **Adding `count` to a resource that lacked it is the one free case** — Terraform auto-proposes moving the object to index `0` — but `for_each` needs a key you choose, and renaming is never free. **Two hard limits:** cross-*type* moves exist but are **provider-dependent**, and a managed resource can never become a `data` block (use `removed` with `lifecycle { destroy = false }` and a separate `data` block). **The section worth reading twice is splitting a published module** — the original becomes a *shim* that calls the new modules and declares the `moved` blocks, so existing consumers upgrade in place. The docs flag this as violating the closed-box rule and attach a precondition: *"all three of these modules are maintained by the same people and distributed together in a single module package."* Scoping keeps it safe — addresses resolve relative to the module they are written in, and *"a module may only make `moved` statements about its own objects and objects of its child modules"*, so you can reach down, never up or sideways. **Chain blocks when the same object moves twice** (`a→b`, `b→c`): each covers consumers arriving from a different starting version, which is why they accumulate like database migrations rather than as a courtesy changelog. Pair it with [HCDocs "Refactor Terraform state"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/state/refactor), captured in [[tf-state-refactor]] — the **cross-configuration** half that `moved` blocks can't do: the four refactor triggers, the three grouping properties (volatility, stateful-vs-stateless, team ownership), the three ways to keep references dynamic after the split, and the `removed` + `import` migration (**1.7+**) that HashiCorp now recommends over legacy `state mv -state/-state-out` (1.0+).
3. **Book chapter — TID Ch 9 §9.5 "Refactoring" + §9.6 "External refactoring" / TUR Ch 5 "Refactoring Can Be Tricky" (p186)** — TUR Ch 5 is captured as [[05-tips-and-tricks]], and its contribution is the *why*: renaming a variable, a resource identifier or a module — or adding `count`/`for_each` to something that lacked it — reads to Terraform as delete-and-create, which on an ALB or a security group is an outage. Its case for `moved` blocks over `terraform state mv` is social rather than technical: a CLI step must be remembered by every team consuming the module, a `moved` block travels with the code. (~1 hr) — splitting state and versioned module rollouts. The state-surgery side (`terraform state mv`, moved blocks) is Ch 6 §6.5 *Manipulating state*.
4. **Reference — VP ["Upgrade and refactor Terraform modules"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/validated-patterns/terraform/upgrade-and-refactor-terraform-modules)** (~23 min) — the **consumer** side of a module version bump, and the exact bill that comes due when a module author did *not* ship the `moved` blocks **I5** teaches. Worked on `terraform-aws-modules/s3-bucket` **2.15.0 → 3.0.0**, where the module split server-side encryption out into its own resource: the consumer’s plan then proposes to destroy and recreate, and the fix is an `import` block naming an address *inside* the module — `module.s3_bucket.aws_s3_bucket_server_side_encryption_configuration.this[0]` — with the id of the bucket the same module already made. **The rule to carry is the acceptance criterion:** *“Imports are the only acceptable change to see in a speculative plan when upgrading to a newer module version.”* Anything else is the module changing your infrastructure, not your infrastructure catching up to the module. It also states the preference reference #1’s tutorial leaves implicit — configuration-driven `import` blocks over the legacy `terraform import` command, because they are *“predictable, can be automated, and let you preview an import operation before modifying state”*. Same discipline as the provider guide in **A10**: one branch, one issue at a time, apply only on a plan with no errors, warnings or actions. Cached: `cache/web/vp-upgrade-refactor-modules.txt`.
5. **Interactive — split a monolith** (~1.5 hrs) — carve one big config into two states with `moved`/`removed` and `state mv`, verifying empty plans.

**Milestone:** You can split a monolithic configuration into two independently-stated configs with no resource re-creation.

---

### ⬜ A9 — Multitenancy, teams & RBAC

**What it is:** Serving several teams or tenants from one Terraform platform. The HCP Terraform access model supplies the control plane half: permission scopes at organization, project and workspace level, teams and team tokens, four fixed workspace roles alongside custom ones. The cloud half is making each tenant's runs carry only that tenant's credentials.

**Why you need it:** A7 isolates *environments*; this isolates *people*. State is where the boundary actually sits, because a workspace's state holds every secret its configuration touched, and the built-in read-only role can already read it in full.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Interactive — HCTut ["Manage permissions in HCP Terraform"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/cloud/cloud-permissions)** (4 min stated; **Essentials** edition, organization-owner required) — create a `Dev-Team`, deliberately leave its organization-level permissions blank, grant it the fixed **Write** set on one workspace, then invite a user by email. It is a UI walkthrough with no HCL, and its one teaching point is the sequencing: *"The owners team … has every available permission in the organization, so it is important to create restricted team access before adding new members."* You need a second email address you control to finish the invite step.
2. **Reference — HCDocs [permissions overview](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/users-teams-organizations/permissions) then the three role tables: [organization](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/users-teams-organizations/permissions/organization) · [project](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/users-teams-organizations/permissions/project) · [workspace](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/users-teams-organizations/permissions/workspace)** (~1 hr) — the role tables are ground truth for this topic. Read the overview first, because the effective-permissions rule it states governs how every table below it composes.
3. **Reference — HCDocs ["Teams overview"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/users-teams-organizations/teams)** (~20 min) — the owners team (capped at five members on free organizations, never deletable, never empty), per-team API tokens not tied to a user, and the Teams / Team Members / Team Tokens / Team Access APIs. Those APIs have provider equivalents in `tfe_team`, `tfe_team_members` and `tfe_team_access`, which is how the access model itself becomes reviewable code instead of console clicks.
4. **Book chapter — TUR Ch 10 § "A Workflow for Deploying Infrastructure Code" (pp. 390–411)** (~1 hr) — the pre-HCP form of the same problem. Covers the live-repo-versus-modules-repo split and one infrastructure team publishing versioned modules that every other team consumes "a bit like a service catalog". Its core argument is that infrastructure CI is a harder permission problem than application CI, because arbitrary infrastructure changes need arbitrary permissions, so the CI server ends up holding admin credentials on a box that is "accessible to all the developers at your company" and "used to execute arbitrary code". Five mitigations follow: lock the server down, keep it off the public internet (accepting that webhooks stop working and you must poll instead), require an approval from someone other than the requester who can see both the diff and the plan output, use temporary credentials such as IAM roles and OIDC rather than permanent keys (**A6**), and isolate the admin credentials on a separate locked-down worker whose API allows only specific commands, in specific repos, on specific branches.
5. **Reference — HCDocs [dynamic provider credentials](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces/dynamic-provider-credentials) + [the AWS configuration](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces/dynamic-provider-credentials/aws-configuration)** (~1 hr) — the cloud half of the boundary, and the page that makes this topic concrete rather than a permissions tour. HCP mints an OIDC workload identity token per run, the cloud verifies it against HCP's public signing key and returns temporary credentials, and the run environment is torn down with the credentials discarded. What matters for multitenancy is the **`sub` claim**, which is literally `organization:ORG_NAME:project:PROJECT_NAME:workspace:WORKSPACE_NAME:run_phase:RUN_PHASE`, so the tenant boundary is expressible in the AWS trust policy itself.
6. **Reference — HCDocs [single sign-on](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/users-teams-organizations/single-sign-on) + [API tokens](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/users-teams-organizations/api-tokens) + [audit trails API](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/api-docs/audit-trails)** (~45 min) — the three pages that decide how membership arrives, how automation authenticates, and how you reconstruct who did what. Read them together, because they interlock: SAML mapping makes the directory authoritative over team membership, team tokens deliberately strip user attribution from API actions, and the audit trail is the only place those two facts get reconciled after the event.
7. **Reference — the open-source stack: [Atlantis security](https://www.runatlantis.io/docs/security) + [repo and project permissions](https://www.runatlantis.io/docs/repo-and-project-permissions) + [Terrakube team management](https://docs.terrakube.io/user-guide/organizations/team-management)** (~45 min) — how the same boundary is drawn without a control plane. Read the Atlantis security page first and for one specific sentence, that malicious code runs during `plan`, because it decides the architecture of every self-hosted option.
8. **Interactive — build a two-tenant organization** (~3 hrs) — two projects with one team each, a project-owned variable set per tenant carrying that tenant's `TFC_AWS_*` dynamic-credential variables, and a custom auditor role granting *Read outputs only* rather than state read. Verify from the second account that tenant B can reach none of tenant A's state, variables or outputs, then verify the harder half: set `TFC_AWS_RUN_ROLE_ARN` as a workspace variable in tenant B pointing at tenant A's role, confirm the override takes effect (the platform allows it), and confirm the run still fails because A's trust policy rejects B's token. Re-create the whole arrangement with the `tfe` provider.

!!! warning "📌 No book covers this topic — the docs are the only source"
    Both books predate the current HCP permission model. **TUR** Ch 10 covers the team workflow and the CI-server permission problem, which is the pre-platform form of the question, and Ch 6 "Managing Secrets with Terraform" supplies the temporary-credentials argument Ch 10 leans on, but neither touches organizations, projects, teams or roles. **TID** has no access-control section at all: Ch 10 "Advanced Terraform topics" is naming schemes, network topology, provisioners, the external and local providers, and checks, and its only platform material is the six-way comparison in §8.7. So step 2's reference pages are not supplementary reading here, they are the primary text, and the role tables in them are the part to read rather than the prose.

!!! danger "📌 Every built-in workspace role reads the entire state file"
    In the workspace role table, **`Read state` is ✅ for Admin, Write, Plan and Read alike** — only `Read and write state` narrows to Admin and Write. So the least-privileged preset still hands a user every secret the configuration touched (**A6**), the docs granting it permission to "read complete state files from the workspace". The same holds one level up: project `Read state` is ✅ down to the project **Read** role.

    The fix is a **custom role**, which can grant `Read outputs only` and stop there. Custom permissions can assign anything in the workspace table *except* the admin-only rows. This is the single most important thing on this page: if a tenant boundary is supposed to be a secrecy boundary, none of the four presets implement it.

!!! note "📌 Who can `terraform state pull` whose secrets"
    The docs split state access four ways: **No access**, **Read outputs only**, **Read**, **Read and write**. Read them as three different consumers rather than as a ladder.

    `Read outputs only` is the *cross-tenant* level. It is what the docs require for the `/state-version-outputs` endpoint, for `terraform output`, and for `terraform_remote_state` against the workspace, and it exposes only values a configuration author explicitly published as root outputs (**B9**, **I6**). One tenant consuming another's VPC ID should hold exactly this and nothing more.

    `Read` is where secrecy ends. The docs describe the state file as often containing sensitive information and grant this level "read complete state files from the workspace" through the UI and API. `Read and write` is required for local-execution runs and, per the docs, "to use any of the Terraform CLI's state manipulation and maintenance commands against this workspace, including `terraform import`, `terraform taint`, and the various `terraform state` subcommands". So the CLI and the API do not present the same surface at the same level, and a tenant boundary drawn only from CLI behaviour will be wrong. Assume anyone at `Read` or above has already pulled everything.

    The practical consequence for a shared platform: a tenant's secret is exposed to every principal with `Read` on the workspace that touched it, which is a larger set than the tenant. That set only shrinks by keeping the secret out of state in the first place (**A6** ephemeral values and write-only arguments) or by not putting the workspaces in reach of the same teams.

!!! danger "📌 Workspace-to-workspace state access is a second, separate control — and it has a documented bypass"
    Team permissions answer "which *people* can read this state". They do not answer "which *workspaces* can read it during a run", which is a different setting called **Remote State Sharing** with three modes: share with all workspaces in the organization, share with all workspaces in **this project**, or share with a named list. The default is the safe one, since "new workspaces in HCP Terraform do not allow other workspaces to access their state", and the docs recommend least privilege here explicitly.

    Read the middle mode as the tenant-shaped one. Sharing within the project lets a tenant's own workspaces compose while keeping other tenants out, which is the same boundary the project draws everywhere else in this topic. Organization-wide sharing is the setting that quietly turns every tenant's outputs into a shared bus.

    Two things that break the mental model. **The control does not cover everything that reads outputs:** "Remote state access controls do not apply when using the `tfe_outputs` data source", so a tenant that switches from `terraform_remote_state` to `tfe_outputs` is governed by *token permissions alone*, which is the `Read outputs only` level above rather than this setting. And **local execution mode moves the check to a user token**, which must carry permission to read and write state versions for the workspace, plus read state outputs for any workspace it references. Auditing sharing settings while ignoring who holds tokens audits half the surface.

    There is also a visibility floor worth knowing before you trust a review: configuring sharing requires read access to the destination workspace, so a reviewer with scoped project or workspace permissions will not see the complete list of workspaces that can reach a given state.

!!! note "📌 Agent pools are the compute half of the boundary, and they scope like everything else"
    A tenant boundary that stops at identity still runs both tenants' code on the same execution surface. **Agent pools** are HCP's answer for private or on-premises infrastructure, and they scope the same way projects do: "Scoping an agent pool lets you specify which workspaces, projects, or Stacks can use it", after which the pool "does not appear in other workspace's, project's, or Stack's lists of available agent pools". A per-tenant pool sitting in the tenant's own network is what keeps tenant B's run from having route-level reach into tenant A's estate.

    Two operational catches. Creating and scoping pools needs the organization-level **Manage agent pools** permission, which the docs note "implicitly grants access to read all workspaces and projects" — another org-level grant that widens read access as a side effect, exactly like `Manage policy overrides`. And scoping is not revocation: "Scoping an agent pool does not remove it from workspaces, projects, or Stacks that are actively using it", so a tightened scope leaves existing users attached until you detach them in each workspace.

!!! note "📌 Policy sets scope to tenants; the private registry does not"
    Two governance surfaces, two different answers, and the asymmetry decides how far one organization can be stretched.

    **Policy sets scope.** A set applies "globally, to specific projects and workspaces, or to workspaces with specific tags" (tag scoping is **beta**), and any project, workspace or tag can be **excluded** from a set. So a tenant can carry its own mandatory policies while the platform team keeps a global baseline. Two traps sit next to it. The organization-level **Manage policy overrides** permission "also gives teams read access to all workspaces in the organization", which the docs themselves say to avoid by using **Delegate policy overrides** instead, so the naive way to let a tenant override its own soft failures hands it read access to every other tenant. And a policy set carrying **This policy set can be overridden in the event of mandatory failures** lets holders override even `Hard mandatory` policies, because that switch "takes precedence over the individual policy's enforcement level".

    **The private registry does not scope.** Private modules and providers are "only available to members of that organization", and that is the entire access model. Nothing in the organization permission table is a registry *read* scope: `Manage modules`, `Manage providers` and `Manage Stack component configurations` control publishing and deletion, and the two remaining entries govern attaching project tags and reserved keys to registry artifacts. So **every member of the organization can see every private module**, including its documentation, examples and source address. If tenants must not see each other's modules, they need separate organizations, not separate projects. What you *can* control is consumption rather than visibility, through Sentinel: the docs point at policies that "mandate that all non-root modules in Terraform configurations must be private or public modules from your own private registry", which is the enforcement half A5 already owns.

    (In **Terraform Enterprise** the direction is the opposite of tenant isolation: private modules "are also available to other organizations that are configured to share with that organization".)

!!! warning "📌 Permissions are additive across scopes, and the most permissive one wins"
    *"Each permission is additive, granting a user the highest level of permissions possible, regardless of which scope set that permission."* An organization-level **Manage all workspaces** silently overrides a carefully scoped workspace **Read**. The relationship is not symmetric: organization **View all workspaces** does *not* cut a team's workspace **Write** down to read-only. Treat organization scope as the floor a team can never fall below, and project or workspace scope as the ceiling. Granting broadly at the top and then "restricting" further down does nothing.

!!! note "📌 The boundary leaks through everything integrated with it"
    HCDocs is explicit that the model covers only what HCP Terraform manages, and names three leaks. A VCS-connected workspace inherits the repository's permissions, so anyone who can merge to its tracked branch can queue plans there *"regardless of whether they have explicit permission to queue plans or are even a member of your HCP Terraform organization"*, and with auto-apply enabled the merge applies them. An API-driven Slack bot lets anyone who can issue it commands act with the bot's permissions. A run task receives an access token good for the plan JSON and the callback, with a 10-minute lifetime. The governing sentence: *"An integrated system is able to delegate any level of access that it has been granted."*

!!! note "📌 Two more ways tenant data escapes the role you granted"
    **Sentinel mocks** are downloadable at workspace Write and above, and the docs describe that run data as *"detailed and may contain unredacted sensitive information"*. A mock download is a state read wearing a different name, which matters when you hand Write to a tenant on the assumption that Write means "can deploy".

    **Secret teams** are invisible to workspace and project admins unless those admins are also organization owners. An admin auditing who can reach a workspace is therefore reading a list that may be incomplete by design.

!!! note "📌 Projects are the tenant container; teams are the subject"
    A project is *"a folder containing one or more workspaces, Stacks, or both"*, and its four roles (**Admin / Maintain / Write / Read**) apply to every workspace and Stack inside it. Grant per project rather than per workspace, or the model stops scaling at the tenant's tenth workspace. Only project **Admin** can update or delete the project, manage other teams' access, move workspaces between projects, or manage run tasks. **Maintain** is the useful middle: full control of everything inside, including creating and deleting workspaces, with no authority over the project itself.

    ⚠️ **Two docs pages disagree about variable sets, and the gap matters here.** The project permission table marks both `Manage variable sets` and `Read variable sets` as Admin-only. The variables page instead says that creating, editing or applying a project-owned variable set needs any of project **Write**, **Maintain**, **Admin**, a `Manage variable sets` project permission, or organization `Manage all projects`. Since variable sets are how per-tenant credentials get delivered, assume the looser page describes the behaviour and verify in your own organization before treating "only Admin can change the credentials" as a control.

    Team management requires **Essentials, Standard or Premium**. In **HCP Europe** organizations the subject is an HCP group rather than a team, HCP roles (Admin / Contributor / Viewer) grant HCP Terraform permissions automatically, and project permissions cannot be assigned in HCP Terraform at all. The least-privilege advice there is to add narrow HCP Terraform permissions instead of a broader HCP role.

!!! warning "📌 RBAC governs who starts a run, not what the run may do"
    A tenant's plan executes with the workspace's provider credentials, and those credentials answer to the cloud's IAM, not to HCP's permission model. Two tenants whose workspaces share one AWS role are one tenant with extra paperwork. The access model only becomes a tenant boundary when the cloud identity is per-tenant too, which is the next callout. Blast-radius isolation of the *state itself* is **A7**, and the module-consumption half is **A4** plus **E6**.

!!! danger "📌 The tenant boundary is a trust-policy condition, and one wildcard erases it"
    Dynamic credentials put the boundary somewhere HCP permissions cannot reach it. The workload identity token's **`sub` claim** is `organization:ORG_NAME:project:PROJECT_NAME:workspace:WORKSPACE_NAME:run_phase:RUN_PHASE`, so an AWS role's trust policy can require, by `StringEquals`, that the assumer be exactly one workspace in one project of one organization. Give each tenant its own role, condition that role on the tenant's project segment, and no configuration change inside tenant B's workspace can reach tenant A's role — the ARN is not a secret, and it does not need to be, because the trust policy refuses the token.

    Everything hangs on how that condition is written. The docs' own convenience variant relaxes `StringEquals` to `StringLike` with `run_phase:*` in order to share one role across plan and apply. A wildcard that drifts one segment left, to the workspace or the project, silently widens the role to every workspace in the organization, and nothing in HCP will report it. HashiCorp states the floor directly: *"you should always check, at minimum, the audience and the name of the organization in order to prevent unauthorized access from other HCP Terraform organizations!"* Treat that as the floor, not the target — the organization is the *platform* boundary, and the project is the *tenant* boundary.

    Two refinements worth taking. Separate roles for the two phases (`TFC_AWS_PLAN_ROLE_ARN` and `TFC_AWS_APPLY_ROLE_ARN`, falling back to `TFC_AWS_RUN_ROLE_ARN`) give a read-only plan and a writing apply, which is the credential-side version of the workspace **Plan** role. And the variables selecting the role belong in a **project-owned variable set** applied to the whole project, so a new workspace joins the tenant's identity by construction rather than by someone remembering to set three variables.

!!! warning "📌 A variable set does not pin anything by default — a workspace variable beats it"
    The obvious next assumption is that putting the credential variables in a project-owned set stops a tenant from changing them. It does not. The docs state the rule flatly: **"Workspace-specific variables always overwrite variables from variable sets that have the same key"**, and they offer credential swapping as the motivating example, that you "may want to use a different set of provider credentials in a specific workspace". So workspace **Write**, which carries `Read and write variables`, is enough to repoint `TFC_AWS_RUN_ROLE_ARN` at another tenant's role. The UI flags it with an `OVERWRITTEN` badge, which is a notification, not a control.

    The control is marking the set **priority**: "When a variable set is priority, the values take precedence over any variables with the same key set at a more specific scope", and only the set's owner decides that. Do this for anything that carries identity.

    Even unpriorified, the attempt fails — but for a different reason than the one you configured. Repointing the ARN gets tenant B nothing, because the token HCP mints still carries B's own project and workspace in its `sub` claim, and A's trust policy rejects it. That is worth internalising rather than treating as luck: **the trust policy is the control that holds, and the variable-set arrangement is only hygiene.** A design that relies on variable placement for isolation is one workspace variable away from failing, and would fail today if the two tenants shared a role that trusted the project segment loosely.

!!! note "📌 Cross-account tenants: `assume_role` is the other half"
    Where tenants live in separate cloud accounts, the provider does the crossing (**I8**). The AWS provider's `assume_role` block takes `role_arn`, `external_id`, `session_name`, `source_identity`, `duration`, `tags`/`transitive_tag_keys`, and — the argument that matters most here — `policy` and `policy_arns`, which further restrict the assumed role's permissions for this session. Repeating the block chains roles in order, so a platform role can hop into a tenant account without either side holding the other's keys.

    `external_id` exists to stop the **confused deputy**, and its rule is about control rather than secrecy.
