A role ARN, as AWS puts it, "isn't a secret", so any tenant that learns another tenant's role ARN could ask the platform to assume it. The external ID defeats that only because the *platform* generates a distinct value per tenant and supplies it on every call: AWS is explicit that the value "must be unique among Example Corp's customers and controlled by Example Corp, not its customers". A platform that lets a tenant choose its own external ID has implemented the field and none of the protection.

    `session_name` and `source_identity` are what make CloudTrail readable per tenant, which is the difference between an audit trail and a list of identical `terraform` sessions.

!!! note "📌 Blast radius across tenants is a different property from blast radius across environments"
    A7's blast radius is a **graph and state** property. Dev and prod collide because one `random_pet` feeds both bucket names, or because both live in one state file, and the fix is separation of state and of the reference graph. Nothing about it involves people, and it does not care who ran the apply.

    A tenant's blast radius is an **identity** property. Tenant A damages tenant B when A's run holds credentials that can write B's resources, when A can read B's state, or when A can publish a module B is already consuming. Separate state does nothing about any of the three, which is why A7's file layout is necessary here and nowhere near sufficient.

    Read the four controls as answering four different questions. *Who can start a run against this workspace?* is HCP RBAC. *What can that run touch once started?* is the trust policy plus the assumed role. *What is that run allowed to declare?* is the policy set. *What can a reader learn afterwards?* is state access. A tenant boundary is only as strong as the weakest of the four, and they fail independently: a perfect role split still leaks everything if both tenants' teams hold workspace `Read`.

!!! info "📌 Without a control plane, the four controls do not disappear — they relocate"
    Neither CLI has a user model, so nothing above has a `tofu` counterpart the way `tofu workspace` counters `terraform workspace`. That does not make multitenancy an HCP-only subject; it makes the boundary someone else's job, and the useful skill is knowing whose.

    | Control | HCP Terraform | Open-source stack |
    |---|---|---|
    | Who may start a run | Teams, projects, workspace roles | VCS branch protection and CODEOWNERS, or the runner's own allowlist |
    | What the run may touch | Workspace dynamic credentials, OIDC `sub` claim | CI OIDC federation, one cloud role per repo or per directory |
    | What the run may declare | Policy sets scoped to a project | A policy step in the pipeline (Conftest/OPA, Checkov) that the tenant cannot edit |
    | What a reader learns afterwards | Workspace state access levels | Backend IAM (bucket, prefix, KMS key) plus, on OpenTofu, client-side state encryption |

    Two properties transfer badly. **Cross-workspace state reads** have no equivalent of Remote State Sharing — a tenant that can read the bucket can read every state under it, so the boundary must be an object prefix with an IAM condition, decided once and enforced by the cloud rather than the tool. And **the audit trail** becomes whatever the CI system and the cloud already log, which is usually better than HCP's 14 days, at the cost of correlating two systems by hand.

!!! danger "📌 With a shared runner, `plan` is arbitrary code execution — so apply approval is not a tenant boundary"
    This is the load-bearing fact for any self-hosted multi-tenant setup, and Atlantis's own security page states it plainly: *"If attackers submitting pull requests with malicious Terraform code is in your threat model then you must be aware that `terraform apply` approvals are not enough. It is possible to run malicious code in a `terraform plan` using the external data source or by specifying a malicious provider. This code could then exfiltrate your credentials."*

    A single runner holding every tenant's credentials therefore has no boundary at all, no matter how the approval rules are written, because tenant B's pull request runs code beside tenant A's secrets. HCP hides this by giving each workspace disposable, per-run credentials; a self-hosted stack has to buy it deliberately, with **one runner per trust domain** and credentials that arrive per-run from the CI's OIDC federation rather than sitting in the runner's environment. This is the same argument TUR Ch 10 makes for isolating admin credentials on a separate worker, sharpened by the fact that plan-time execution needs no approval at all.

!!! note "📌 Atlantis — permissions come from the VCS, and the interesting hook is the external command"
    [Atlantis](https://www.runatlantis.io/) (`runatlantis/atlantis`, **Apache-2.0**) has no user database. Identity is the VCS account, and authorization runs **twice**: once for the repository as a whole, then again per project after pre-workflow hooks have run, because affected projects are not known at the first check. The coarse control is `--gh-team-allowlist`, which maps VCS teams to commands, as in `--gh-team-allowlist="*:plan,*:policy_check,myteam:apply"`. Note the documented trap in that example: if you use policy checking you must allowlist `policy_check` explicitly, or manual `atlantis plan` comments silently skip policy evaluation while autoplans still run it.

    For anything tenant-shaped, the real mechanism is the **external command** configured server-side, which receives the command, repo, project and the user's VCS teams and returns a decision, so per-environment or per-tenant rules live in your own code. Two hard requirements complete the picture: `--repo-allowlist` is mandatory, and a webhook secret is needed regardless, since without one an attacker can pose as an allowlisted repository. Server-side repo config is also what stops tenants granting themselves privileges through a repo-level `atlantis.yaml`.

!!! note "📌 Terrakube — the closest workalike, and it now has HCP's permission shape"
    [Terrakube](https://docs.terrakube.io/) (`terrakube-io/terrakube`, **Apache-2.0**, Java/Spring) is the nearest open-source shape to HCP: organizations containing workspaces, a private registry, teams and team tokens, plus an `api` / `executor` / `registry` / `ui` split and Dex for identity.

    Identity is **Dex**-backed, and a team is named after a group in the upstream directory, so the team name literally is the IdP group (an Entra group such as `TERRAKUBE_ADMIN`, or `MyGithubOrg:TERRAKUBE_ADMIN` on GitHub). The source resolves this by **string equality against the token's `groups` claim** — no mapping table — so membership federates by construction, with none of HCP's `MemberOf` mapping and none of its fail-open behaviour. The flip side is that a mistyped team name grants nobody anything, silently.

    **Correction, 2026-08-15.** This callout previously said permissions are organization-wide booleans with no project tier, so a tenant had to be a whole organization. That described the pre-2.30 model. **RBAC v2** landed in **2.30.0** (2026-02-26) with HCP-style roles — `admin`, `write`, `plan`, `read`, and `custom` — and **project-level team permissions** landed in **2.32.0** (2026-05-17). Permissions now hang off three entities: `Team` (organization), `ProjectAccess` (project) and `Access` (workspace). **A tenant can be a project.** Two behaviours to know: under a predefined role the fine-grained booleans are *ignored*, and a null or blank role normalizes to `custom`, which is what keeps pre-2.30 rows behaving as before. The roles are not a ladder — `write` can plan and apply, but registry, VCS and template management are **`admin`-only**. Details in [[terrakube-facts]].

    Two things HCP does not match. **`manageState` is its own toggle at every tier**, where HCP grants full state reads to all four fixed workspace roles. And **federated credentials** (2.31.0) let a CI job authenticate *to* Terrakube with a workload-identity token — issuer, audience, and required claims, with list-valued claims matching any element, which is the shape of a GitHub Actions token — instead of holding a long-lived API token. Creating organizations, teams and federated credentials is superuser-only, where a superuser is a member of one deployment-wide group (`TERRAKUBE_ADMIN_GROUP`).

!!! note "📌 Terrakube's ephemeral executor is the strongest open answer to run isolation"
    A9's governing constraint is that a plan can exfiltrate credentials, so a single runner holding every tenant's credentials has no boundary. Terrakube's **ephemeral executor runs each job as its own Kubernetes Job**: configurable namespace, image, secrets and replicas, plus per-job CPU and memory requests and limits set through workspace variables. The lever that matters for tenancy is `EPHEMERAL_CONFIG_POD_ANNOTATIONS`, which annotates the generated pod template and so enables **Vault Agent Injector** or **GKE Workload Identity** — each run's pod fetches its own credentials from the identity system rather than reading a shared secret. With a namespace and service account per tenant, the run boundary becomes a pod boundary.

    Keep the limit in view: the tenant's Terraform still executes inside that pod, so a malicious plan can still reach whatever identity the pod holds. Ephemeral pods shrink the blast radius to one run's credentials; they do not make a plan safe under a shared identity.

!!! note "📌 Terrakube speaks the HCP API, which changes the migration question"
    `RemoteTfeController` implements the TFE v2 API at `/remote/tfe/v2/` — organizations, workspaces, workspace lock/unlock, `state-versions`, `configuration-versions`, `runs` with **apply** and **discard** actions, and `relationships/remote-state-consumers`. So the **`cloud {}` block and the `remote` backend work against a self-hosted Terrakube**, CLI-driven runs behave as they do against HCP, and cross-workspace state reads are a modelled relationship rather than a bucket IAM condition — which is the gap the pure-CI design leaves open. There is also an **HCP Terraform importer** that carries workspaces, variables, variable sets, tags and state versions, with a preview step for sensitive variables. (See [[terrakube-facts]].)

!!! note "📌 The rest of the field, and one rename worth knowing"
    **Digger** (`diggerhq/digger`, **MIT**) runs the plan and apply inside your existing CI rather than on its own workers, which sidesteps the shared-runner problem above by inheriting the CI's isolation and OIDC identity per job. **Stategraph** (`stategraph/stategraph`, **MPL-2.0**, OCaml) is the notable renaming: Terrateam's engine was open-sourced and the product is folding into Stategraph, whose pitch is storing state as a graph in PostgreSQL rather than one JSON blob behind one lock. Enterprise features stay proprietary. TID §8.7's six-way comparison predates that move, so read its Terrateam row with the rename in mind. **Env0, Spacelift and Scalr** remain commercial, with their own RBAC models and no open core to inspect.

!!! info "📌 OpenTofu — the one tenant control HCP does not have"
    OpenTofu's **client-side state encryption** (**E3**) is the piece with no HCP equivalent, and it changes who the state boundary defends against. State and plan files are encrypted at rest, in the backend and locally, and `terraform_remote_state` reads work through it. Give each tenant its own key provider, an `aws_kms` or `openbao` key its role can use and other tenants cannot, and a bucket-wide read or a mis-scoped IAM policy no longer yields plaintext. Set `enforced = true` to refuse unencrypted writes, and note that once enabled OpenTofu "will refuse to read plain text data", so migration goes through a `fallback` block.

    The documented limit is exactly the multi-tenant caveat to state: encryption *"does not and cannot protect the sensitive values in the state file from the person running the `tofu` command"*. It defends the tenant against the platform operator and against other tenants, never against the tenant's own operators. Learn the exit before the entrance, since the `unencrypted` method that a `fallback` uses is documented in **E3** and appears in no changelog.

!!! warning "📌 SAML team mapping makes the directory the source of truth, and it fails open"
    Manual invites do not scale past a handful of tenants, so team membership moves to the identity provider. HCP supports **Microsoft Entra ID, Okta and generic SAML**, and once SSO is on, "all non-owner members must sign in through SSO in order to access the organization". Note the exception in that sentence, because it is permanent: **owners retain a username-and-password bypass** by design, so that an IdP outage does not lock the organization out. HashiCorp recommends creating two owner-level break-glass accounts before enabling SSO. SSO also does not provision accounts, so a first-time user still creates or links an HCP Terraform account.

    Enable **team management** and a SAML attribute, `MemberOf` by default, drives membership. The behaviour to internalise is that it is **authoritative and re-evaluated every login**: Terraform "automatically adds them to the teams included in their assertion and automatically removes them from teams that are not included", and "this automatic mapping overrides any manually set team memberships". So a directory edit moves a person between tenants at their next login, and a manual fix in the HCP UI survives only until then.

    Two failure modes belong in a runbook. A team name in the assertion that does not **exactly** match an existing team is ignored, and no team is created, so an IdP-side typo silently produces a user with no access rather than an error. And if the configured attribute is missing from the `SAMLResponse` entirely, users are assigned to a default team named `sso` and are **not removed from any existing teams**, which is the fail-open case: a broken assertion leaves stale membership in place instead of revoking it.

!!! note "📌 Audit trails answer “which tenant did that”, and the API is deliberately awkward"
    Permissions decide what can happen; the audit trail is how you reconstruct what did. `GET /organization/audit-trail` streams changes to workspaces, runs and other entities, and three constraints shape any design built on it. Retention is **14 days**, so long-term tenant accountability means shipping events somewhere else on a schedule rather than querying HCP when an incident surfaces. It is **Standard and Premium only, and not available in Terraform Enterprise at all**, which is a genuine surprise given TE's usual positioning. And the endpoint "cannot be accessed with a user token or team token" — it requires an **organization token or an audit trail token**, so the exporter is necessarily a platform-team credential rather than something a tenant can run for itself.

!!! warning "📌 Team tokens trade attribution for durability, and the owners team's token is an org-wide key"
    A team token grants "access to the workspaces that the team has access to, without being tied to any specific user". That is exactly what you want in automation, since it survives the departure of whoever created it, and exactly what you lose in an audit, since every action arrives as the team rather than a person. The docs are direct about the worst case: a token generated for the **owners** team "will have all of the same permissions that an organization owner would", which in a multi-tenant organization is a single string that crosses every tenant boundary in this topic.

    Three controls worth knowing. Teams can hold several tokens as long as descriptions are unique, which is what makes rotation possible without downtime, while a description-less **legacy** token is limited to one at a time and regenerating it invalidates the previous one. **Allow member token management** decides whether ordinary team members, rather than owners and holders of `Manage teams`, can mint and revoke their team's tokens. And an organization can switch off user tokens entirely with **Allow members to access organization resources with their user tokens**, which pushes all automation onto team and organization tokens; be aware it breaks GitHub App VCS connections, which depend on a user token completing a personal OAuth flow.

!!! note "📌 The same OIDC boundary, three different trust primitives"
    The `sub` claim is identical across clouds, but what each platform lets you match on differs enough to change the design.

    **AWS** takes an IAM trust policy condition on `SITE_ADDRESS:sub` with `StringEquals` or `StringLike`, so a project-wide role is one wildcard away — convenient, and the wildcard hazard described above.

    **GCP** uses a workload identity pool and OIDC provider, mapping `assertion.sub` to `google.subject`, and restricting with an **attribute condition** such as `assertion.sub.startsWith("organization:my-org:project:my-project:workspace:my-workspace")`. Prefix matching means a per-project condition is natural, which fits this topic's grain best of the three. The run then authenticates as the pool principal directly, or impersonates a service account.

    **Azure** creates a federated identity credential on an Entra ID application, with **Other issuer** and a subject identifier of the same `organization:…:project:…:workspace:…:run_phase:…` string. The critical difference: that subject "is a direct string match", so no wildcards exist, and "two federated identity credentials need to be created for each workspace using dynamic credentials: one that matches `run_phase:plan` and one that matches `run_phase:apply`". Azure therefore cannot express "any workspace in this tenant's project" at all — safe by construction, and two credential objects per workspace to automate.

    Both the AWS and GCP pages carry the same floor: "you should always check, at minimum, the audience and the name of the organization". Kubernetes and Vault follow the same shape, and Vault-backed variants mint the cloud credential through Vault's secrets engines instead.

!!! danger "📌 Offboarding a tenant is not the reverse of onboarding one"
    Every control in this topic is written for standing one up. Removing one runs into a different set of rules, and the docs are blunt about the worst of them: **"Terraform does not automatically destroy managed infrastructure when you delete a workspace."** Delete the workspace and its state, and the tenant's cloud resources keep running with nobody tracking them, recoverable only by manual deletion or by importing them into another workspace. Destroy first, delete second, and note that a destroy plan needs the **Allow destroy plans** toggle enabled on the workspace before it can even be queued.

    The ordering is forced from below. Workspace deletion is guarded by default, since "workspace administrators can only delete unlocked workspaces that are not managing any infrastructure", and **organization owners can force delete** past that guard, or delegate force-delete to workspace admins with an organization setting. Project deletion is stricter still: "you can only delete projects that do not contain Stacks or workspaces". So a tenant is dismantled workspace by workspace, and only then does its project go.

    A complete offboarding touches things this topic introduced separately, and each one is a leak if skipped. Revoke the tenant's **team tokens**, because a token outlives the workspaces it was minted for. Delete the **team** itself, or a later directory assertion can repopulate it. Remove the tenant's project from any **variable set**, **policy set** and **agent pool** scoping, and remember that unscoping a pool "does not remove it from workspaces … actively using it". Drop the tenant's project from other workspaces' **remote state sharing** lists. And delete the **cloud-side role and its trust policy**, which no HCP action touches: an orphaned role whose trust policy still names a deleted project is dormant rather than dead, and it becomes live again the day someone recreates a project with the same name.

    Two edition-dependent points. **Ephemeral workspaces** (Standard and Premium) can auto-destroy on a schedule or after inactivity, and settings are inherited from the project, which makes them a reasonable default for tenant sandboxes; be aware auto-destroy runs "do not prompt you for apply approval". And **data retention policies are Terraform Enterprise only**, offering soft deletion of configuration and state versions with a garbage-collection grace period. HCP Terraform has no equivalent, so "how long does a departed tenant's state data persist" has a different answer on each product, and on TE a workspace admin can override the organization default for their own workspace.

!!! note "📌 The three neighbouring topics, read through the tenant boundary"
    Three capabilities are owned by other topics, and each behaves differently than its own topic implies once tenants exist. Each carries a matching multi-tenant note where it lives.

    **Self-service (E6)** is the one capability a tenant can operate for itself. No-code provisioning needs `Manage all projects`, `Manage all workspaces` or **project Admin**, so project admin is enough, and the new workspace inherits both the project's team access and its project-scoped variable sets. That is the credential path from this topic arriving automatically. Watch that **auto apply is the default**, and that no-code readiness is a registry property, so every tenant can provision every no-code module.

    **Policy (A5)** splits in half. Scoping delegates cleanly, since a policy set targets projects, workspaces or tags. Authoring does not, because `Manage policies` is organization-level and drags org-wide run visibility with it. Tenants therefore choose which sets apply, and ask the platform team for new policy code.

    **Stacks (E2)** are the safer primitive for tenant credentials. Their `store` block references a variable set by name or ID, and "variable precedence does not affect Stacks", so the workspace-variable override that this topic warns about cannot happen. The cost is that removing a referenced set breaks the Stack's deployments outright.

!!! note "📌 When one organization is the wrong container"
    Projects are the tenant boundary throughout this topic, but three things in it are organization-wide and cannot be subdivided, so they decide when tenants need separate organizations instead. The **private registry** has no per-team read scope, so every tenant sees every private module. **Cost estimation** is a single organization-wide toggle. And several organization-level permissions widen read access as a side effect, `Manage policy overrides` and `Manage agent pools` among them, so delegating any of those to a tenant leaks visibility across all of them.

    Use one organization with a project per tenant when tenants are internal teams under one platform team, where seeing each other's module catalogue is acceptable. Use an organization per tenant when tenants are external, mutually distrusting, or contractually separated, accepting that you then lose cross-tenant module sharing (a Terraform Enterprise deployment can share a registry *across* organizations, HCP cannot) and that you now administer N organizations.

    The related question is who administers the administrator. Expressing the access model as `tfe_team` and `tfe_team_access` code, as the milestone asks, means one workspace holds a token that can rewrite every tenant's permissions. Treat that platform workspace as the highest-privilege thing in the estate: its own state deserves the tightest state access in the organization, and it should not live inside any tenant's project.

!!! note "📌 Chargeback: HCP's cost estimate is org-wide and stuck on the legacy policy engine"
    Per-tenant spend attribution is the question a platform gets asked in its second month. HCP's built-in **cost estimation** is off by default and enabled with a single organization-wide toggle, "Enable cost estimation for all workspaces", so there is no per-project granularity in the feature itself. It runs as an extra phase between plan and apply, showing hourly, monthly and delta figures, and it covers **AWS, GCP and Azure** resources only, skipping anything usage-priced.

    The trap is on the enforcement side. Gating on cost uses Sentinel's `tfrun.cost_estimate.delta_monthly_cost`, and per the docs "cost estimation is only available in policy checks, HCP Terraform's **Legacy** policy execution mode", which "support Sentinel versions up to 0.40.x". Choosing cost gating therefore pins that policy set to a deprecated execution mode and an old Sentinel. This is the strongest argument in the topic for putting cost governance in **Infracost** in the pipeline (**A5**) and using the project as the chargeback unit, rather than expecting the platform to bill tenants for you.

!!! note "📌 The open-source boundary, concretely — and the one place it beats HCP"
    The relocation table above says where each control goes. This is what it is made of, because "use CI OIDC" is not an architecture until the claim is named.

    **Identity of a run.** GitHub's OIDC provider mints a JWT per job whose subject looks like `"sub": "repo:octo-org/octo-repo:environment:prod"`, and the cloud role's trust policy conditions on it exactly as the AWS example earlier in this topic conditions on HCP's `organization:…:project:…:workspace:…` string. Same lesson, same failure: pin the tenant-identifying segment, and treat a wildcard that drifts left as the whole boundary gone. The token is "only valid for a single job, and then automatically expires", which is the property that makes the shared-runner problem survivable, since a stolen credential dies with the job.

    ⚠️ **A change landed a month ago that matters here.** Repositories "created after July 15, 2026 use an immutable default subject format that includes owner and repository IDs", not names, and this is not available on GitHub Enterprise Server. That is a direct fix for the reuse hazard flagged in the offboarding callout above: a trust policy naming a *deleted* tenant cannot be reactivated by recreating something with the same name, because the ID never repeats. Any trust policy written against the old name-based format needs re-checking as repositories get recreated.

    **Scaling the trust policy.** Writing one condition per repository does not survive fifty tenants. Organization and enterprise admins can surface **repository custom properties as OIDC claims**, which "enables attribute-based access control (ABAC) policies in your cloud provider … driven by repository metadata rather than hard-coded allow lists". Tag each repository with its tenant and condition the role on that claim, and onboarding a tenant stops touching IAM at all. This is the open-source counterpart to HCP's project segment.

    **State.** One bucket, one prefix per tenant, an IAM condition restricting each tenant's role to its own prefix, versioning on, and a distinct KMS key per tenant so a mis-scoped bucket policy still yields ciphertext. On OpenTofu, add client-side encryption on top with a per-tenant key provider (**E3**), which is the layer HCP has no equivalent for.

    **Policy.** The tenant must not be able to edit its own gate, so the pipeline definition is the thing to protect: **CODEOWNERS** on the workflow path plus required review, or a reusable workflow owned by the platform repository that tenant repositories are required to call. This is the structural equivalent of `Manage policies` being organization-level in HCP, achieved with repository permissions instead.

    **Modules — and here open source is strictly stronger.** A9's registry finding is that HCP's private registry has *no per-team read scope*, so tenants who must not see each other's modules need separate organizations. With plain git sources, module visibility is repository visibility: a tenant reads the modules whose repositories it has access to, and nothing else. The capability HCP charges an organization split for is the VCS's default behaviour.

!!! note "📌 Choosing between them"
    The decision is not HCP versus open source but **where you want the boundary to live**. HCP puts it in a control plane you rent, with three permission scopes and per-run disposable credentials, and charges for the editions that make it work (teams from Essentials, audit trails from Standard). A self-hosted stack puts it in the cloud's IAM and the VCS's permissions, which you already operate and already audit, at the cost of building state isolation, policy scoping and run isolation yourself, and of getting the shared-runner question right.

    A rough rule from the constraints in this topic. Few tenants who trust each other, and a team that would rather not run infrastructure for its infrastructure: HCP with a project per tenant. Tenants that must not see each other's modules or state, or an organization with strong existing IAM and CI practice: self-hosted, one runner and one cloud role per tenant, state separated by prefix and key. Tenants that are external or contractually separated: separate HCP organizations, or separate self-hosted instances, because in both worlds the shared layer is the thing that eventually leaks.

    **Is a fully open-source multi-tenant platform achievable? Yes, and two of its controls are stronger than the paid equivalent** — per-tenant module visibility, which HCP cannot express below the organization, and client-side state encryption, which HCP does not have at all. Three things are genuinely harder and are where the effort goes. **Run isolation** must be built rather than assumed, because plan-time code execution means one runner per trust domain. **The permission surface is uneven**: Terrakube is now the exception rather than the example, since RBAC v2 and project-level access give it organization, project and workspace tiers with HCP-shaped roles — but Atlantis still has no user model beyond the VCS, so outside Terrakube fine-grained roles become repository topology and an external authorization command rather than a table you fill in. And **there is no single console** that answers "who can reach this tenant", so that answer is assembled from VCS permissions, IAM policies and CI configuration, which is exactly the correlation work HCP sells. Budget for building the boundary once and auditing it continuously, and the result is defensible; expect a product and you will ship a permission model that exists only in a diagram.

**Milestone:** You can run one organization serving two tenants where each tenant's team plans and applies only inside its own project, neither can read the other's state, variables or outputs, each workspace shares state no wider than its own project, an auditor role sees outputs but never state, each project's runs assume a different cloud role whose trust policy pins the project segment of the OIDC `sub` claim, each tenant carries its own policy set on top of a global baseline, and the whole arrangement is expressed as `tfe_team` / `tfe_team_access` configuration rather than console clicks. You can also draw the same boundary without HCP, naming which component enforces each of the four controls and why a shared runner breaks all of them at once. You can also say which of those controls fails first and what an attacker gets when it does, name where each tenant's membership actually comes from once SAML mapping is on, and explain why the audit trail cannot be delegated to the tenants it describes. Then remove one of the two tenants completely, in the right order, and show that nothing of it survives: no running infrastructure, no live token, no team a directory assertion could repopulate, no dangling scope entry, and no cloud role whose trust policy still names the project you deleted.

---

### ⬜ A10 — Automated dependency updates with Renovate

**What it is:** Running **Renovate** as the thing that moves your pins. It reads `*.tf` and `*.tofu`, works out which module, provider and engine-version constraints have newer releases, and opens one pull request or merge request per update carrying the version diff. Unlike Dependabot it is not tied to one forge: the same `renovate.json` drives GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket Cloud and Bitbucket Data Center.

**Why you need it:** A3 builds a pipeline that gates *changes you make*. Nothing in it notices that the AWS provider moved four minor versions since you wrote the constraint, and **I4**'s advice to pin exactly turns a stale pin into a ratchet with no release handle. Renovate is the handle, and it is the only realistic one once the code lives anywhere other than GitHub — which is exactly where Dependabot stops.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Reference — [Renovate's Terraform manager](https://docs.renovatebot.com/modules/manager/terraform/)** (~30 min) — start here rather than with the runner, because what Renovate can *see* decides everything downstream. It matches `**/*.tf` and `**/*.tofu` by default and extracts these `depType`s: `module`, `provider`, `required_provider`, `required_version`, `helm_release`, `tfe_workspace`, plus roughly twenty Kubernetes resource kinds and the `docker_*` ones. The asymmetry in sources is the fact to carry: **modules** resolve from `GitTags`, `GithubTags` or a Terraform registry, public or private, while **providers** resolve from a **registry only**. Each `depType` can be disabled individually, which is how you stop it rewriting Kubernetes manifests embedded in a config while still tracking providers. Note also that the bare `provider` type is the pre-0.13 form; on any modern config the live type is `required_provider`.
2. **Reference — [Running Renovate](https://docs.renovatebot.com/getting-started/running/)** (~30 min) — the seven distributions, and the choice that shapes the rest. The **Mend hosted app** needs no administration. The **npm package** runs anywhere Node does, even via `npx`, and processes repositories then exits. The **Docker images** come in two flavours: the default one carries only Node and installs tools at run time, while the `-full` tag ships most package managers preinstalled. Then the platform wrappers — a **GitHub Action** (`renovatebot/github-action`), the **GitLab Renovate Runner** project, and **Mend Renovate Self-Hosted CE/EE**, which is stateful and "does not exit after processing all repositories". **Forking Renovate** needs no `write` permission but is public-repo-only and cannot automerge.
3. **Interactive — dry-run it before it can write anything** (~30 min) — point `npx renovate` at one Terraform repo with a **read-only** token and `--dry-run`, and read the log to see which dependencies it found and which it could not resolve. On GitLab that is the `read_api` scope rather than `api`, so the credential itself makes the run harmless. Getting a clean extraction here is the whole point; a manager that silently found nothing looks identical to one with nothing to update.
4. **Interactive — GitLab, the platform this topic exists for** (~1 hr) — use the [Renovate Runner](https://gitlab.com/renovate-bot/renovate-runner) templates, which run a self-hosted instance on GitLab **scheduled pipelines**. Include `project: 'renovate-bot/renovate-runner'`, `file: '/templates/renovate.gitlab-ci.yml'`, then set `RENOVATE_TOKEN` (a PAT or Group Access Token with `read_user`, `api`, `write_repository`), `RENOVATE_GITHUB_COM_TOKEN` so changelog lookups against github.com are authenticated rather than rate-limited, and `RENOVATE_EXTRA_FLAGS` to control discovery (`--autodiscover=true --autodiscover-filter=group1/* --onboarding=false`). The project recommends running **hourly** (`3 * * * *`), configured under Build › Pipeline schedules. Nothing about this needs a hosted app or a marketplace listing, which is the practical answer to "can we have Renovate on GitLab".
5. **Reference — the platform pages you will actually deploy on: [GitLab](https://docs.renovatebot.com/modules/platform/gitlab/) · [Bitbucket Cloud](https://docs.renovatebot.com/modules/platform/bitbucket/) · [Bitbucket Data Center](https://docs.renovatebot.com/modules/platform/bitbucket-server/)** (~45 min) — read these for credentials and for the feature holes, which differ per forge (see the callout below). **GitLab** wants `api` for real runs and `read_api` for dry runs, plus `read_registry` for a private container registry, and the bot needs at least **Developer** to open MRs, **Maintainer** where merges are restricted to maintainers. **Bitbucket Cloud** wants an API token carrying the read and write scopes for repository, pull requests and issues plus `read:user` and `read:workspace`, with `username` set to the Atlassian account email — and, easily missed, a workspace **user group with Create repositories permission** containing the bot, which is what lets it validate that a PR reviewer is a workspace member. **Bitbucket Data Center** wants an HTTP access token, supports every non-EOL version, and needs `unicodeEmoji: false` behind MySQL or MariaDB.
6. **Reference — [Configuration options](https://docs.renovatebot.com/configuration-options/)** (~45 min) — read for the defaults rather than the option list, because the defaults are what a first deployment actually runs. `lockFileMaintenance` is **`false`**, so `.terraform.lock.hcl` refreshes do not happen until you ask for them. `rangeStrategy` is **`"auto"`**, which is the option that decides whether a `~> 5.0` constraint gets widened or left alone, and it is where TID Ch 8's constraint asymmetry becomes config. `automerge` is **`false`** and `automergeType` is `"pr"`. `schedule` is `["at any time"]`, `prConcurrentLimit` is **10**, `separateMinorPatch` is `false`, and `minimumReleaseAge` is `null` — that last one being the knob that stops you adopting a provider release on its first day, which is a supply-chain control as much as a stability one. Add [`:dependencyDashboard`](https://docs.renovatebot.com/key-concepts/dashboard/) to get one issue summarising every pending and rejected update.
7. **Book chapter — [TID Ch 7 §7.7.5 "Automated updates with Dependabot"](books/tid/chapters/07-code-quality-ci.md)** (~20 min) — the nearest book treatment, and worth reading for the framing rather than the tool: a pipeline gates the changes you make, and this chapter has just produced a great many pins (ruleset versions, hook `rev:`s, action tags, module and provider versions) that nothing in it will ever move. Its Renovate content is one sentence, which is why the rest of this topic is docs.
8. **Reference — VP ["Manage Terraform provider upgrades"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/validated-patterns/terraform/upgrade-terraform-provider)** (~26 min) — the manual workflow this topic automates, written by HashiCorp and worth reading precisely because **it names Renovate first**: *“To effectively monitor for new releases of a Terraform provider, we recommend using an automation tool like Renovate or GitHub’s Dependabot.”* Its fallback for a team not running a bot is **Watch → Releases** on the provider’s GitHub repository, which is the manual shape of what reference #1 does by parsing your `.tf` files. **What Renovate cannot do is the half worth learning here:** the bot opens the pull request, and this guide is what you do with it. Isolate the upgrade in its own branch and pull request, make the first plan show **only** the version change, refactor **one issue at a time**, re-plan after each, and *“only run `terraform apply` once there are no errors, warnings, or actions”* — a bar stricter than an empty diff, because a warning is a deprecation you are about to inherit. The `.terraform.lock.hcl` changes are reviewed and committed as part of the same pull request, which is the link back to reference #6’s `lockFileMaintenance`. Its module counterpart is **A8**. Cached: `cache/web/vp-upgrade-terraform-provider.txt`.
9. **Interactive — write the config for a real repo** (~2 hrs) — take a repository with providers, at least one registry module and one Git-sourced module, and build a `renovate.json` that: groups all provider updates into one PR, separates `required_version` onto its own schedule, turns on `lockFileMaintenance` weekly, sets `minimumReleaseAge` to a few days, and automerges nothing until the A3 pipeline has run on the branch. Then deliberately roll a constraint backwards and confirm the bot opens exactly the PR you expected, on the schedule you set.

!!! warning "📌 No book covers this topic — the docs are the only source"
    **TID** Ch 7 §7.7.5 covers **Dependabot** with a working `dependabot.yml`, and mentions Renovate only in passing as the escape hatch for GitLab. **TUR** predates both by several years and has nothing. So steps 1, 2, 5 and 6 are the primary text here, not supplementary reading, and the Renovate docs are versioned with the tool rather than with a book — check them rather than this page when a behaviour surprises you.

!!! danger "📌 Renovate defaults to the Terraform registry, including on an OpenTofu project"
    Straight from the manager docs: *"Renovate can not know if you want to use the Terraform or OpenTofu registry. By default, Renovate uses the Terraform registry (`registry.terraform.io`) for providers and modules without a registry definition."*

    On an OpenTofu codebase that silently resolves every unqualified provider against HashiCorp's registry, so version availability comes from the wrong side of the fork. The fix is a `packageRules` entry setting `registryUrls` to `registry.opentofu.org` for the Terraform datasources. Pair this with the lock-file finding in [[opentofu-migration-mechanics]]: the two registries do not ship byte-for-byte identical builds of the same provider version, so resolving against the wrong one is not a cosmetic mistake.

!!! note "📌 The lock file is maintained by Renovate itself, and it is off by default"
    Renovate does not shell out to update `.terraform.lock.hcl`. Per the manager docs it *"performs lock file maintenance itself, without calling the underlying package manager"*, computing the hashes directly, so no `terraform` or `tofu` binary is needed in the runner image for this. The Terragrunt manager reuses that same logic for its `.terraform.lock.hcl` artifacts.

    Two consequences. Because `lockFileMaintenance` defaults to `false`, a fresh install updates constraints in your `.tf` files while leaving the recorded provider checksums untouched, which is a partial upgrade rather than a broken one — but not what most people assume they configured. And because the hashes are computed rather than requested from a CLI, a malformed upstream provider release can leave Renovate unable to produce them at all: [discussion #30984](https://github.com/renovatebot/renovate/discussions/30984) is a lock file that silently failed to regenerate for `aws` 5.64.0 and fixed itself at 5.65.0, with no error in the logs either way.

!!! warning "📌 The feature set is not the same on every platform"
    The config is portable; the behaviour is not. Check these before promising a workflow to a team.

    **GitLab** has not implemented `automergeStrategy`, so every value behaves as `auto`. Multiple assignees are a Premium/Ultimate feature and otherwise collapse to the first assignee. If the project has push rules verifying the committer, `gitAuthor` must match the bot user's own name and email or every push is rejected.

    **Bitbucket Cloud** cannot add PR assignees at all, has no rebase merge strategy (Atlassian's BCLOUD-16610), and does not render collapsible markdown (BCLOUD-20231), which is what Renovate's PR bodies use for detail sections. **Bitbucket Data Center** additionally cannot add or remove labels, does not yet raise issues for configuration errors, and ignores `automergeStrategy` in favour of the repository's configured merge strategy.

    The **Dependency Dashboard** needs a platform supporting "issues with dynamic Markdown checkboxes", so treat it as a GitHub/GitLab feature and check the Renovate FAQ before designing a Bitbucket workflow around it.

!!! danger "📌 Neither Renovate nor Dependabot will move a SHA-pinned Git module source"
    This is the same limit recorded in **I4** and in [Ch 13](book/ch13-using-modules.md), and it is the one thing this topic cannot solve. Renovate's HCL parser strips comments, so there is nowhere to store the version-to-SHA annotation its Docker and Actions digest pinning depends on ([discussion #31006](https://github.com/renovatebot/renovate/discussions/31006)); a global `pinDigests: true` actively errors on Git-sourced Terraform modules ([issue #14790](https://github.com/renovatebot/renovate/issues/14790)), so set `"terraform": { "pinDigests": false }` if you enable it elsewhere. Dependabot skips non-semver refs for the same practical outcome.

    So a module SHA-pinned for supply-chain safety needs a **manual review cadence**, and adopting Renovate does not retire that calendar entry. The Terragrunt manager draws the boundary in writing: *"Terragrunt support is limited to Terraform registry sources and GitHub sources that include SemVer refs."*

!!! note "📌 Engine versions are three separate managers"
    `required_version` inside a `terraform {}` block is the Terraform manager's business, but the version files a toolchain switcher reads are not. Renovate ships a **`terraform-version`** manager for `.terraform-version` and a **`terragrunt-version`** manager for `.terragrunt-version`, both separate from the config managers. If A3's **tenv** setup pins the engine in one of those files, the pin is only tracked once you enable the matching manager — and a repository can easily end up with the constraint bumped and the version file frozen.

**Milestone:** You can stand up Renovate against a Terraform repository on a forge that is **not** GitHub, using a scheduled pipeline and a scoped token rather than a marketplace app, and have it open a grouped provider-update MR that runs through your A3 gates before anyone looks at it. You can state which of your pins it will never move and what your manual cadence for those is, configure it correctly for an OpenTofu project without falling back to `registry.terraform.io`, and say which features you lose by choosing Bitbucket.

---

### ⬜ Advanced Checkpoint

You are ready to advance when you can:
- Ship tested modules (`terraform test`, conditions, checks) through a PR-gated CI/CD pipeline.
- Run governed workflows in HCP Terraform with policy-as-code and OIDC (no static secrets).
- Structure multi-env/multi-account infra with isolated state and refactor at scale without re-creation.
- Serve two tenants from one platform where neither can read the other's state, and where each tenant's runs authenticate as its own cloud identity.
- Keep the pins moving: an update bot on whatever forge you use, opening PRs that run through the same gates, plus a stated manual cadence for the pins no bot can move.

**Certification target:** **Terraform Authoring & Operations Professional** — validates I5, A1–A8 (lifecycle, dynamic config, modules, providers, collaborative workflows, HCP). Lab-based; practice hands-on with **Pro** study path. Attempt after this checkpoint.

---

## Expert

**Goal:** Extend Terraform itself, architect it for large organizations, and master the tooling ecosystem.
**Estimated time:** ~70 hrs

---

### ⬜ E1 — Writing custom providers

**What it is:** Building a provider in Go with the Terraform Plugin Framework — schema, resources, data sources, CRUD, plan modifiers, and acceptance tests.

**Why you need it:** When no provider exists for an internal API, you write one; this is the deepest form of Terraform mastery.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Interactive — HCTut ["Implement a provider with the Plugin Framework"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/providers-plugin-framework/providers-plugin-framework-provider)** (~4 hrs) — build a working provider against a sample API end to end.
2. **Reference — [TPF docs](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/plugin/framework)** (~ongoing) — schema, resource lifecycle, plan modification, acceptance testing.
3. **Reference — [Provider-defined functions](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/plugin/framework/functions)** (~30 min) — the author side of the B7 note: expose your own `provider::<name>::<fn>()` functions from the provider (TF 1.8+), not just resources and data sources.
4. **Book chapter — TID Ch 12 "Terraform providers"** (~2 hrs) — the book's provider-authoring chapter and the mental model of the plugin protocol: §12.1 Design, §12.2 Developer environment, §12.3 Plugin Framework features, §12.4 Provider interface, §12.5 Data source, §12.6 Resources, §12.7 Functions, §12.8 Publishing. (Captured notes: [12-terraform-providers](books/tid/chapters/12-terraform-providers.md) — read the two listing corrections there before typing anything in, and the finished project is [`TerraformInDepth/terraform-provider-mastodon`](https://github.com/TerraformInDepth/terraform-provider-mastodon).)

!!! note "TUR does not cover writing providers"
    **TUR has no provider-authoring content** — verified against the book. Its Ch 7 is *"Working with Multiple Providers"* (using several providers: multiple regions/accounts/clouds), and its only "custom provider" mentions are about *downloading* one from a private registry. For authoring, use **TID Ch 12** plus the TPF docs above.

!!! note "📌 `dev_overrides` for local provider testing"
    To test a locally-built provider without publishing it, add a **`dev_overrides`** block (inside `provider_installation` in the [CLI config file](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cli/config/config-file), v0.14+) pointing at your compiled binary. It bypasses the registry, version and checksum checks. Keep a `direct {}` block beside it, or the override becomes the *only* installation method and every other provider stops resolving. Point `TF_CLI_CONFIG_FILE` at a dev-only config so you don't disturb your normal setup. Temporary dev use only. (See [[feature-history]].)

    **`init` is not skipped.** HashiCorp's docs say `terraform init` "will still attempt to select a suitable published version of your provider to install and record in the dependency lock file", while other commands "disregard the lock file's entry … and will use the given directory instead". So for a provider that has never been published, `init` **fails** and `apply` works anyway — which is why TID Ch12 §12.5.5 says to run `init` and ignore the error. Terraform also prints a warning on every command while an override is active.

    **OpenTofu reads `~/.tofurc`.** Verified in `repos/opentofu` at `v1.12.5` (`internal/command/cliconfig/config_unix.go` + `util.go`): `.terraformrc` is used only when `.tofurc` does not exist, so creating a `.tofurc` for anything else silently disables overrides you left in `.terraformrc`. Windows pair: `tofu.rc` / `terraform.rc`.

!!! note "📌 Provider SDKs: Plugin Framework vs SDKv2"
    Two provider SDKs exist: the modern **Plugin Framework** (use for new providers) and the **legacy SDKv2** (`terraform-plugin-sdk/v2`) that most existing providers still ship on. Know SDKv2 exists — you'll read its code and may maintain it — but author new work on the Framework. ([HCDocs which-SDK](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/plugin/framework-benefits))

!!! note "📌 `provider_meta` schema"
    A provider can also declare a **`provider_meta`** schema ([HCDocs internals](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/internals/provider-meta)) — module-specific metadata a module passes to the provider independently of provider config, mainly so a vendor shipping both a provider and official modules can collect per-module usage stats. Niche; you'll rarely author it. (See [[feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 The provider interface has grown since TID Ch12 — and two of the additions are Terraform-only"
    Verified against **terraform-plugin-framework v1.19.0** (2026-03-10) and `repos/opentofu` at `v1.12.5`, 2026-08-16. The framework's core interface is `Metadata`/`Schema`/`Configure`/`Resources`/`DataSources`; everything else is an opt-in extension interface, so none of this breaks an existing provider.

    | Extension | Needs | OpenTofu |
    |---|---|---|
    | `ProviderWithFunctions` | TF 1.8 | ✅ (OT **1.7** — shipped first) |
    | `ProviderWithEphemeralResources` | TF 1.10 | ✅ |
    | write-only attributes | TF 1.11 | ✅ |
    | managed resource identity | TF 1.12 | ✅ |
    | `ProviderWithListResources` | TF 1.14 | ❌ protobuf stubs only, no core support |
    | `ProviderWithActions` | TF 1.14 | ❌ protobuf stubs only, no core support |
    | `ProviderWithStateStores` | experimental | ❌ |

    One syntax divergence to keep out of portable code: OpenTofu also registers **alias-qualified** function calls, `provider::<name>::<alias>::<fn>`; Terraform builds exactly one name per function (`internal/lang/functions.go` at `v1.15.8`).

!!! note "📌 Publishing a provider — and the two registries do it differently"
    Beyond writing it, **publishing** a provider is its own step: tag semver releases, GPG-**sign** them, and register the signing key so the registry (public or a private HCP registry) will serve it. ([HCDocs publishing](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/registry/providers/publishing))

    - **Terraform Registry** — upload the ASCII-armored public key under *User Settings → Signing Keys*, then *Publish → Provider*. Keys must be **RSA or DSA; the default ECC type is rejected**, so ECDSA/Ed25519 will not work.
    - **OpenTofu Registry** — provider and signing-key submissions go through the **GitHub issue forms** on [`opentofu/registry`](https://github.com/opentofu/registry), and only those: the README rejects pull requests and hand-written issues because the validation automation parses the form fields.

    A release must carry per-platform zips, `_manifest.json`, `SHA256SUMS`, and a **binary (not armored)** `SHA256SUMS.sig` — which is what the scaffolding template's GoReleaser workflow produces.

**Milestone:** You can build and locally install a custom provider exposing one resource with full CRUD and passing acceptance tests. Stretch: expose a provider-defined function callable as `provider::<name>::<fn>()`.

---

### ⬜ E2 — Terraform Stacks

**What it is:** Terraform Stacks — the newer construct for defining components and deployments to provision the same infrastructure across many environments/regions from one definition.

**Why you need it:** Stacks are HashiCorp's answer to multi-deployment orchestration that previously required Terragrunt or custom tooling; increasingly relevant for platform teams.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Reference — [HCDocs "Stacks" overview](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/stacks) + configuration** (~1 hr) — components, deployments, and how Stacks differ from modules/workspaces. Note **deferred changes** — Stacks can plan partially when some values aren't known yet (e.g. a downstream component depends on an upstream one's not-yet-created output), then complete on a later run.
2. **Interactive — HCTut [Stacks: "Deploy a Stack with HCP Terraform"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/cloud/stacks-deploy)** (~2 hrs) — define a stack with multiple deployments and run it in HCP Terraform.
3. **Explainer — [HashiCorp "Terraform Stacks, explained"](https://www.hashicorp.com/en/blog/terraform-stacks-explained)** (~20 min) — see the deployment fan-out in practice; pair with a current Stacks demo on the [HashiCorp YouTube channel](https://www.youtube.com/@HashiCorp/search?query=terraform%20stacks).

!!! info "Deferred actions — the real fix for an unknown `count`/`for_each`"
    The plan-time-known limitation from **B7/I1** ([§4.8.4](books/tid/chapters/04-expressions-iterations.md#484-the-plan-time-known-limitation-important) — a `count`/`for_each` that depends on a not-yet-created attribute errors at plan) is exactly what **deferred actions** resolve. Instead of erroring, Terraform plans a *placeholder* for the unknown instances and defers their create/update to a **later apply round** — the productized version of "split the config so the dependency applies first," with no `-target` surgery.

    - **Stacks ship it in production:** a component whose inputs aren't known yet — the canonical *provision an EKS cluster, then deploy Kubernetes resources into it* — has its changes deferred, and every downstream component with it, preserving order ([Stacks EKS-deferred tutorial](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/cloud/stacks-eks-deferred)).
    - **Open-source CLI:** the same machinery is **experimental** (`plan -allow-deferral`, [PR #34651](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/pull/34651)), not GA as of 1.15.
    - **Terraform-exclusive** — OpenTofu has no deferred actions (see E3). This is a large part of *why* Stacks exist: they span create-then-configure boundaries a flat config can't.

!!! note "📌 Stacks is newer than both books"
    Stacks is newer than both books — rely on HCDocs and verify feature availability (HCP vs CLI) as it evolves.

!!! warning "📌 Two different things are called a stack"
    A Terraform **Stack** is a first-party construct with components and deployments, and its configuration files are `.tfstack.hcl` / `.tfdeploy.hcl`. A Terragrunt **stack** is a collection of units, optionally declared in a `terragrunt.stack.hcl` blueprint that generates unit directories at run time. Same word, different tools, different files, no interoperability. Terragrunt's stack blocks generate directories of `terragrunt.hcl` units and stay on the open-source CLI; Terraform Stacks currently need HCP Terraform to run. Details on the Terragrunt side in [[terragrunt-facts]]; it is covered in **E4**.

!!! note "📌 Multi-tenant note — Stacks close the variable-override hole, and open a different one"
    **A9** records that a workspace-specific variable "always overwrites" a variable of the same key from a variable set, so workspace **Write** is enough to repoint credentials unless the set is marked priority. Stacks do not work that way. A Stack references variable sets **directly by name or ID** through the `store` block, and "because Stacks reference variable sets directly by name or ID, variable precedence does not affect Stacks" — the Stack always uses that set's values regardless of any same-named variable elsewhere. For a multi-tenant platform this is the stronger primitive: the tenant's credential binding is part of the Stack configuration rather than a precedence outcome.

    The new failure mode is availability rather than confidentiality. Stacks read variable sets during deployment runs, so a value change lands in future deployments automatically, and "if a variable set is removed from a Stack's project or organization, the Stack fails to deploy if it references that variable set". Deleting a set during a tenant reorganisation therefore breaks deploys instead of silently falling back. Permissions follow the project: project **Admin** carries "Admin access for every Stack" in that project, and project roles apply to workspaces and Stacks alike.

**Milestone:** You can define a stack with reusable components and deploy it to three environments from a single stack configuration — and explain how **deferred changes** let a stack span a create-then-configure boundary (an unknown `count`/`for_each`) that a flat config can't.

---

### ⬜ E3 — OpenTofu deep dive

**What it is:** OpenTofu's divergence from Terraform — **state encryption**, provider/backend `for_each`, early variable evaluation, the `-exclude` flag, dynamic `prevent_destroy`, `destroy = false`, and `-json-into` — plus which gaps Terraform 1.15 closed, and migration between the two.

**Why you need it:** OpenTofu is a genuine fork with features Terraform's open-source CLI lacks; choosing between them and migrating is a real strategic/technical decision.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Reference — [OTDocs "Migrating from Terraform"](https://opentofu.org/docs/intro/migration/) + [state encryption](https://opentofu.org/docs/language/state/encryption/)** (~1 hr) — what's OpenTofu-only and how state stays compatible. The encryption half is captured as [[ot-state-encryption]] and read in full under **I6**; what belongs here is its consequence for the engine question, since an encrypted state is the one artifact that stops being portable back to Terraform. Pair it with [[opentofu-migration-mechanics]], which reads the *mechanism* out of the source: the guide gives the steps, and three behaviours that decide whether a migration is clean appear in none of them.

2. **Interactive — enable state encryption** (~1 hr) — turn on OpenTofu client-side state encryption and confirm state is unreadable at rest. **Learn the way back out before you turn it on:** OpenTofu registers an **`unencrypted` method** alongside `aesgcm`, and has since 1.7.0, which is what a `fallback` block uses to read a still-plaintext state during rollout and what you migrate *to* if you ever need to decrypt. It appears in **no changelog and no release blog** — found by reading `internal/encryption/default_registry.go` per tag — so it is easy to adopt encryption without knowing the exit exists. The registered set as of 1.12: key providers `pbkdf2`, `aws_kms`, `gcp_kms`, `azure_vault`, `openbao`, `external`; methods `aesgcm`, `external`, `unencrypted`. (See [[opentofu-release-feature-map]].)
3. **Reference — the OpenTofu-only divergence features** (~1.5 hrs) — read one doc page per feature; each has a captured note:
    - **Provider `for_each`** (OpenTofu 1.9) — [OTDocs "Provider Configuration"](https://opentofu.org/docs/language/providers/configuration/); one provider instance per region/account from a map/set. Aliased configs only. **Do not drive a resource's `for_each` and its provider's `for_each` from the same collection** — see the callout below. (Notes: [[ot-provider-for-each]].)
    - **Early variable evaluation** (OpenTofu 1.8) — [OTDocs "Backend Configuration" → Variables and Locals](https://opentofu.org/docs/language/settings/backends/configuration/); reference `var`/`local` in `backend`/`provider` blocks, resolved at `tofu init`. Kills backend partial-config boilerplate. (Notes: [[ot-early-eval-backend]].)
    - **`-exclude` flag** (OpenTofu 1.9) — [OTDocs "Command: plan" → Resource Targeting](https://opentofu.org/docs/cli/commands/plan/); inverse of `-target`. Terraform has no equivalent. **OpenTofu 1.10** added the file-driven variants **`-exclude-file`** and **`-target-file`** (read addresses from a file — cleaner for large blue/green or canary target lists in CI). Combining a target-side option with an exclude-side option is an **error**, not a merge; see the I7 callout for the exact rule and its version boundary. (Notes: [[ot-exclude-flag]].)
    - **Dynamic `prevent_destroy`** (OpenTofu 1.12) — [OTDocs lifecycle](https://opentofu.org/docs/language/meta-arguments/lifecycle/) + [1.12 release](https://opentofu.org/blog/opentofu-1-12-0/); bind it to a variable (Terraform requires a literal). See the I5 callout. (Notes: [[ot-dynamic-prevent-destroy]].)
    - **`destroy = false` lifecycle** (OpenTofu 1.12) — [1.12 release](https://opentofu.org/blog/opentofu-1-12-0/); remove an object from state without destroying the remote object, as a lifecycle arg rather than Terraform's separate `removed` block. See the I7 callout. (See [[tf115-ot112-features]].)
    - **`-json-into=FILENAME`** (OpenTofu 1.12) — [1.12 release](https://opentofu.org/blog/opentofu-1-12-0/); write the JSON stream to a file while keeping the human UI on stdout (vs `-json`, which replaces stdout). Handy for CI that wants both. See the A3 callout. (See [[tf115-ot112-features]].)
    - **`.tofu` file extension** (OpenTofu 1.8) — lets a file target OpenTofu specifically without disturbing a Terraform toolchain reading the same directory. ⚠️ **A `.tofu` file shadows its same-named `.tf` twin rather than adding to it**: the parser "will ignore the `.tf` file and only use the `.tofu` file", so `main.tofu` must be a complete replacement for `main.tf`, not a patch. Differently-named files load normally. Same rule for `.tf.json`/`.tofu.json` and for the test extensions `.tftest.hcl`/`.tofutest.hcl` (verified in `internal/configs/parser_config_dir.go`; see [[opentofu-migration-mechanics]]). ⚠️ **`.tofurc` is not part of this** — it is the renamed `.terraformrc` CLI config file and dates to the **1.6** fork baseline, verified present at `v1.6.0`. The two were previously listed together here as a 1.8 feature; only the `.tofu` config extension is new in 1.8. (See [[opentofu-release-feature-map]].)
    - **OpenTelemetry tracing** (OpenTofu 1.10, experimental) — [OTDocs "OpenTelemetry Tracing"](https://opentofu.org/docs/internals/tracing/); the divergence is **documentation and depth**, not the feature's existence. Terraform has the same `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp` hook, undocumented and covering only `init`-time work; OpenTofu traces the graph walk per resource instance and honours `TRACEPARENT`. See the E5 callout. (Notes: [[ot-otel-tracing]], [[otel-tracing-facts]].) Also **full cross-platform provider checksums** at `tofu init` (1.12) — [OTDocs "Dependency Lock File"](https://opentofu.org/docs/language/files/dependency-lock/), captured as [[ot-dependency-lock]]; see the B3 callout for the Terraform-side failure this removes. (See [[opentofu-feature-history]].)
    - **Ephemeral resources + `issensitive()` unknown-correctness** (OpenTofu 1.11) — OpenTofu 1.11 added **ephemeral resources/values** (matching Terraform 1.10) and made **`issensitive()` return an *unknown* result when its argument is unknown**. That's a **breaking change**: code that fed an `issensitive()` result into `count`/`for_each` (where unknowns are illegal — see B7/I1 §4.8.4) now **fails at plan** — key on a plan-known value instead, or don't branch on the sensitivity of an unknown. (See [[opentofu-feature-history]].)
4. **Book — TID (covers both), and [Ch 10 §10.7](books/tid/chapters/10-advanced-topics.md#107-opentofu-and-terraform-compatibility) in particular** (~1 hr) — the book's own compatibility report is the baseline claim to keep in mind: every example in it was tested on both engines and ran unchanged, so divergence is a list of additions, not a dialect. §10.7.1 is its `.tofu` treatment, and the practical framing it adds to the bullet above is that shadowing is what lets **one module exploit an OpenTofu-only feature while keeping a working Terraform fallback**. ⚠️ Two corrections found while capturing it: the book dates provider-defined functions to "Terraform and OpenTofu 1.8.0", but **OpenTofu shipped them in 1.7.0** ([opentofu#1439](https://github.com/opentofu/opentofu/pull/1439)); and the `winrm` **connection type is deprecated as of OpenTofu 1.12** ([opentofu#3899](https://github.com/opentofu/opentofu/issues/3899), warns on every use, expected to error in the unreleased 1.13) with **no matching move on Terraform** — a Windows-provisioning configuration will break on an OpenTofu upgrade and not on a Terraform one. Migration path is SSH, which modern Windows Server ships.

!!! warning "📌 Three quiet edges in the migration, all verifiable in a lab"
    **State provider addresses are translated in memory, not rewritten on disk** — and only for providers the configuration has *not* pinned in full form. `random = {}` in `required_providers` gets rewritten to `registry.opentofu.org/hashicorp/random`; `source = "registry.terraform.io/hashicorp/random"` is left alone deliberately, which is the lever for running OpenTofu against HashiCorp-built providers. There is also an undocumented off switch, `OPENTOFU_STATEFILE_PROVIDER_ADDRESS_TRANSLATION=0`, present since **1.6.0** and mentioned in no file in the repository.

    **Lock-file hashes do not carry over.** Since **1.10.0**, OpenTofu mirrors `registry.terraform.io/hashicorp/*` lock entries to its own registry — only that namespace, since no equivalence can be assumed elsewhere — preserving the version selection but dropping every hash, and it says why in a warning you see once: *"the OpenTofu project's provider releases are not byte-for-byte identical."* Same provider source, different builds. Treat the switch as a deliberate re-recording of provider checksums rather than continuity (**I4**, **A6**).

    **A `.tofu` file replaces its `.tf` twin.** See the `.tofu` bullet below — a partial override loses everything else that was in the original file.

!!! warning "📌 The fork goes deeper than the CLI — they don't share an HCL build"
    Easy to assume the two tools differ only in features, since both parse "the same HCL". Checked in the two `go.mod` files: **Terraform pins upstream `github.com/hashicorp/hcl/v2 v2.24.0`**, while **OpenTofu requires `v2.20.1` and then `replace`s it with its own fork**, `github.com/opentofu/hcl/v2`, at a pseudo-version built from a commit dated 2025-10-21. Different code, from bases four minor versions apart.

    The practical rule for anything you read or write about the language: a claim traceable to HCL's **specs** (`spec.md`, `hclsyntax/spec.md`) transfers to both tools, because the fork implements the same language. A claim resting on an **implementation detail, bug fix, or diagnostic message** does not, and needs checking per tool. Upstream changes after OpenTofu's base include mark propagation through conditional and `for` expressions — the machinery that decides whether `sensitive` survives them — and the rejection of invalid nested splats; the fork may or may not carry them. Enumerated, with the caveat that this is an upper bound on the divergence rather than a measurement, in [[hcl-library-facts]].

!!! danger "📌 Provider `for_each`: never share the collection with a resource"
    The rule this path used to state — "a resource's `for_each` must be a subset of its provider's" — describes the steady state but hides the actual failure and the fix. It is **not** a static validation. It is a **lifecycle ordering constraint**, and OpenTofu's own error text is the clearest statement of it:

    > To successfully remove an instance of a resource it must be possible to remove the corresponding element from the resource's `for_each` collection while retaining the corresponding element in the provider's `for_each` collection.

    So the failure mode is a **destroy**, not a create. Drive `aws_instance.this` and `provider "aws"` from the same `var.regions` map, drop a region from it, and the same plan removes the resource instance *and* the provider instance that has to destroy it. OpenTofu errors and tells you to **re-add the element**, destroy, then remove it again — a two-apply dance you can avoid entirely by giving the provider its own collection that is allowed to outlive the resources'. Source: `internal/tofu/node_resource_abstract_instance.go`. Adding `for_each` to a provider that already has single-instance resources in state has its own version of this and errors the same way.

!!! info "📌 OpenTofu ↔ Terraform — the gap runs both ways now"
    **Terraform 1.15** (2026-04-29) closed several long-standing OpenTofu-only gaps — dynamic module sources, variable/output deprecation, output type constraints (a side effect: a fully-typed module interface makes `module.<NAME>` a `list`/`map` under `count`/`for_each` rather than a `tuple`/`object`, so **OpenTofu module references stay structural** — verified, though auto-conversion means callers rarely notice either way, [[conditional-branch-evaluation]]) — and added the net-new **`convert()`** function ([opentofu #2630](https://github.com/opentofu/opentofu/issues/2630) is still open — though what OpenTofu lacks is the *function*, not the capability: a typed `variable` boundary does the same coercion, and the gap is only *inline* arbitrary object/tuple schemas), so "OpenTofu has features Terraform lacks" is narrower than it was. What remains OpenTofu-only: state encryption, provider `for_each`, early variable evaluation, `-exclude`, dynamic `prevent_destroy`, `-json-into`, `.tofu` files, `-show-sensitive`, the `pg` backend's multiple-states-per-database, and the `language` block. **`destroy = false` has dropped off that list** — Terraform **1.16** adds the same `lifecycle` argument. (See [[tf115-ot112-features]], [[version-facts]].)

!!! warning "📌 Don't let matching version numbers imply who led"
    Both projects number releases `1.x`, on different schedules, which makes "OpenTofu 1.10 vs Terraform 1.10" a trap. Sort by **date**, not by number.

    The case that bit this path: **S3-native state locking**. Terraform introduced it in **1.10 (2024-11-27)** and made it GA in **1.11 (2025-02-27)**; OpenTofu shipped it in **1.10 (June 2025)**, roughly seven months later. The I6 callout used to say the opposite.

    Genuinely near-simultaneous, and worth not mis-attributing either way: **provider-defined functions**, Terraform 1.8.0 on 2024-04-10 against OpenTofu 1.7.0 on 2024-04-30. Twenty days is parallel development, not one following the other. Where OpenTofu clearly led: state encryption, early evaluation, provider `for_each` and `-exclude`, OCI registries and short-circuit operators, `deprecated` variables and outputs (1.10, about ten months ahead of Terraform 1.15), and `enabled`. Full date-ordered breakdown in [[opentofu-release-feature-map]].

**Milestone:** You can migrate a project from Terraform to OpenTofu, enable state encryption **and get back out of it via the `unencrypted` method**, and explain the OpenTofu-only features — provider `for_each`, early variable evaluation in backend config, the `-exclude` flag, dynamic `prevent_destroy`, and `-json-into` — including *why* a resource's `for_each` and its provider's must not come from the same collection, and which gaps Terraform 1.15 and 1.16 have since closed.

---

### ⬜ E4 — Large-scale state & repo architecture

**What it is:** Organizing many states and many teams — Terragrunt as an orchestrator (units, stacks, the run queue, the DAG), monorepo vs multi-repo, dependency ordering across states, and remote-state composition.

**Why you need it:** At scale, flat repos and giant states collapse; deliberate architecture is what keeps plans fast and blast radius small.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Reference — [TG docs (Terragrunt)](https://docs.terragrunt.com)** (~1.5 hrs) — start with [Terminology](https://docs.terragrunt.com/getting-started/terminology) so the vocabulary is precise (unit, stack, component, discovery, run queue, runner pool), then DRY backends, `dependency` vs `dependencies`, and `run --all`. Terragrunt is the open-source route to strong dev/staging/prod isolation (directory-per-env, own backend/state each) — the open counterpart to proprietary HCP Terraform workspaces (see [[workspaces]]).
2. **Reference — [[terragrunt-facts]]** (~45 min) — the same docs mined from the local `repos\terragrunt` checkout at **v1.1.3**, with each claim version-gated: the full CLI and HCL surface, what the CLI redesign renamed, explicit vs implicit stacks, `--filter`, and the CAS. Read it instead of the docs' own narrative pages, two of which are stale (they still describe Terragrunt as pre-1.0 and `terragrunt.stack.hcl` as unshipped).
3. **Book chapter — TUR Ch 3 + Ch 5** (~2 hrs) — state isolation and repo-structure tradeoffs at scale.
4. **Reference — [HashiCorp "Terraform mono-repo vs. multi-repo: the great debate"](https://www.hashicorp.com/en/blog/terraform-mono-repo-vs-multi-repo-the-great-debate)** (~30 min) — monorepo-vs-multirepo decisions in the wild, plus [native monorepo support](https://www.hashicorp.com/en/blog/terraform-adds-native-monorepo-support-stack-component-configurations-and-more).
5. **Reference — [OpenTofu OCI registries](https://opentofu.org/docs/cli/oci_registries/)** (~40 min) — distributing **providers and modules via OCI registries** (`oci://` module sources; `oci_mirror` for provider plugins), reusing existing container registries (ECR, GAR, ACR, Docker Hub) instead of a dedicated Terraform registry. OpenTofu 1.10+, OpenTofu-first. (See [[feature-coverage-matrix]].) Terragrunt reaches the same registries from the other side: `oci://` module sources on a unit, added in Terragrunt **1.1.1** behind the `oci` experiment.
6. **Interactive — target a change, then run it** (~1 hr) — in a multi-unit layout, dry-run the blast radius with `terragrunt find --filter '<expr>'`, then reuse the identical filter on `terragrunt run --all`. Learning the filter language on `find` first is the habit that keeps `run --all` from being a whole-estate gamble. This layout is testable end to end: Terratest ships a full `terragrunt` helper module — `run --all`, `stack generate`, `stack run`, `stack output`, `dag graph`, `hcl validate` — so a stack can be generated, applied, asserted on and destroyed from a Go test (see [[terratest-facts]], and **A2** for the testing side).

!!! note "📌 Terragrunt today — 1.0 (2026-03-30) through 1.1.3 (2026-08-13)"
    **1.0** shipped 2026-03-30, the first release with a backwards-compatibility commitment, and the line is now at **1.1.3**. It orchestrates both Terraform and OpenTofu, and **defaults to OpenTofu when both are on `PATH`** (`terraform_binary` overrides).

    The CLI redesign is the part that breaks old scripts and old tutorials. `run-all` and `graph` are now `run --all` and `run --graph`. The per-command `*-all` forms — `plan-all`, `apply-all`, `destroy-all`, `output-all`, `validate-all`, `spin-up`, `tear-down` — were **removed outright**, not deprecated. Flags dropped their `terragrunt-` prefix and environment variables are now `TG_`-prefixed. Several renames are not mechanical: `--terragrunt-debug` became `--inputs-debug`, `--terragrunt-exclude-dir` became `--queue-exclude-dir`, and `--terragrunt-fail-on-state-bucket-creation` has no replacement because backend provisioning moved to its own explicit `terragrunt backend bootstrap` command.

    Post-1.0 the guarantee covers the CLI, HCL configuration, the run report, and `find` output. It explicitly does **not** cover experiments, `list`, `catalog`, raw stdout/stderr, or the Go library — which is how `catalog` kept changing shape across 1.0.x. Breaking work ships through two opt-in valves: `--experiment <name>` to turn on unfinished features, `--strict-control <name>` to adopt a pending breaking change early.

!!! note "📌 Three capabilities with no native Terraform equivalent"
    Worth knowing these exist before deciding Stacks (**E2**) covers the same ground.

    - **`--filter`** — a query language for selecting units by name, path glob, config attribute, dependency-graph traversal, or **Git diff** ("units affected by this change"), with negation, intersection and union. It supersedes the old `--queue-include-dir` / `--queue-exclude-dir` flag pile and works on `find`, `list`, `run`, `hcl fmt`, `hcl validate`, and the `stack` commands.
    - **Explicit stacks** — a `terragrunt.stack.hcl` blueprint whose `unit` and `stack` blocks *generate* units at runtime into a gitignored `.terragrunt-stack/` directory. A directory may be a unit or a stack, never both. The stated design goal is that a stack file is only shorthand for an equivalent directory tree, so the two layouts stay interchangeable.
    - **The Content Addressable Store (CAS)** — on by default since **1.1.0** (`--no-cas` to disable). Each source getter resolves through a cheap probe that yields a cache key without downloading (`git ls-remote` for Git, `HEAD`/`ETag` for HTTP, `HeadObject` for S3, a manifest digest for OCI), then hard-links deduplicated blobs into place.
