This, not just parallelism, is why Terragrunt stays fast on a large estate.

**Milestone:** You can design a multi-team layout with per-component state, cross-state dependencies, and DRY backend config that keeps each plan small — and you can state, without hedging, which parts of that design Terragrunt gives you and which parts (RBAC, run history, remote execution, policy enforcement, drift detection) still need HCP Terraform, Atlantis, or Gruntwork's commercial tier.

---

### ⬜ E5 — Debugging, performance & scaling

**What it is:** Diagnosing and speeding up Terraform — `TF_LOG` levels, the resource graph, `-parallelism`, provider plugin caching, `-refresh=false`, targeted plans, and large-state performance.

**Why you need it:** Slow plans and cryptic errors block whole teams; senior engineers must diagnose the graph and tune throughput.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Reference — [HCDocs "Debugging" (`TF_LOG`)](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/internals/debugging)** (~25 min) — log levels (`TRACE`…`ERROR`), sending logs to a file with **`TF_LOG_PATH`**, splitting core vs provider logs (`TF_LOG_CORE`/`TF_LOG_PROVIDER`), and where a panic writes **`crash.log`**. The debugging levers that no docs page lists are in [[terraform-env-vars]], read out of the source — including **`TF_DISABLE_PLUGIN_TLS`**, which turns off the auto-generated mutual TLS on the provider channel and is therefore the way to confirm that a local traffic interceptor is what's producing `Failed to load plugin schemas` on every provider. The source marks it "not intended to be set by end-users", so use it to diagnose and then fix the interceptor, rather than leaving it set.
2. **Reference — [HCDocs `terraform graph`](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cli/commands/graph)** (~20 min) — the DOT output, `-type=plan|apply|plan-destroy|plan-refresh-only` for the runtime graph (provider nodes, `(expand)` nodes), `-plan=tfplan` for a saved plan, and `-draw-cycles`. Notes: [[tf-cmd-graph]], [[dependency-graph]].
3. **Interactive — diagnose a cycle** (~30 min) — write two resources that reference each other, watch `plan` fail with `Error: Cycle: …`, then run `terraform graph -type=plan -draw-cycles | dot -Tsvg > cycle.svg` and find the red edges that close the loop. A ready-made specimen, if you would rather not build one: HCTut ["Troubleshoot Terraform"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/state/troubleshooting-workflow) ships a broken configuration whose two `aws_security_group` resources each name the other in an in-line `ingress` block. Captured in [[tut-troubleshooting-workflow]], along with the fix worth generalizing.
4. **Interactive — profile a slow plan** (~1.5 hrs) — enable the plugin cache, tune `-parallelism`, and measure the difference on a large config.
5. **Reference + interactive — [OTDocs "OpenTelemetry Tracing"](https://opentofu.org/docs/internals/tracing/)** (~1 hr) — run a local Jaeger, export `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp`, and read a real `plan` trace. This is the only way to answer "which resource is slow" rather than guessing from wall-clock time. Notes: [[ot-otel-tracing]], facts and the Terraform comparison in [[otel-tracing-facts]].
6. **Book chapter — TID Ch 5 §5.7 "Common pitfalls and errors" / TUR Ch 5 (the "Gotchas" half of *Tips and Tricks: Loops, If-Statements, Deployment, and Gotchas*)** (~1 hr) — common failure modes and their fixes. (TID has no dedicated troubleshooting chapter; §5.7 is the closest — captured in [[05-terraform-plan]].)

!!! note "📌 The four-layer troubleshooting model — work outward from yourself"
    HashiCorp's own ordering of Terraform error types, by distance from the user, and the most reusable thing in [[tut-troubleshooting-workflow]]:

    | Layer | What it is | What to do |
    |---|---|---|
    | **Language** | HCL, which core interprets. Syntax errors print line numbers and an explanation. | Read the line number; fix the configuration. |
    | **State** | The record of provisioned resources. *"If state is out of sync, Terraform may destroy or change your existing resources."* | After ruling out configuration errors, reconcile by *"refreshing, importing, or replacing"* — **I7**'s toolkit. |
    | **Core** | Interpreting configuration, managing state, building the graph, talking to plugins. *"Errors produced at this level may be a bug."* | File against `hashicorp/terraform`. |
    | **Provider** | Authentication, API calls, resource-to-service mapping. | File against the provider's own repository. |

    The ordering *is* the advice: your HCL, then your state, and only then the software. Note how much of this path the model routes through **I7** — the state row's three verbs are that topic's entire subject.

!!! tip "🔧 Filing a bug: how to tell whose bug it is"
    The precondition is a filter, not a formality — *"once you eliminate the possibility of language misconfiguration, version mismatching, or state discrepancies."* Then four steps ([[tut-troubleshooting-workflow]]):

    1. **`terraform version`** prints the CLI, the platform, and every provider from the lock file in one block. Check the lock file before filing; an old pinned provider is a common false alarm. (The "your version is out of date" line comes from the Checkpoint service — see the opt-out callout below.)
    2. **Split the logs.** `TF_LOG_CORE=TRACE` and `TF_LOG_PROVIDER=TRACE` (both since 0.15), plus `TF_LOG_PATH=logs.txt`, which **creates the file and appends** to it. TRACE is the level the development teams ask for.
    3. **Find the owner in the log.** *"Find the final error message and trace it back to the source. It should contain `provider-terraform-<PROVIDER-NAME>` if it is a provider issue."* That prefix is the whole test — a `[DEBUG] provider: plugin process exited: path=.terraform/providers/…/terraform-provider-aws_v5.56.1_x5` line is the provider's; a `[TRACE] vertex "root": starting visit (*terraform.nodeCloseModule)` line is core's.
    4. **File it** against `hashicorp/terraform` (which has a bug-report issue template) or the provider's repo, found via its registry listing. The [HashiCorp Discuss forum](https://discuss.hashicorp.com/) is the suggested stop first if you want community input.

    Unset one stream with an empty value — `export TF_LOG_CORE=` leaves provider logging on.

!!! tip "🔧 Diagnosing `Error: Cycle`"
    The error names the cycle's **members**, not the **edges** that close it. On a two-resource cycle that's the same thing; on a real config it isn't. `-draw-cycles` reddens the offending edges:

    ```dot
    "[root] terraform_data.a (expand)" -> "[root] terraform_data.b (expand)" [color = "red", penwidth = "2.0"]
    ```

    It requires an explicit `-type=`. Without one, `-draw-cycles` is **silently ignored** — no warning, exit 0 — and the default resources-only graph renders only *one* of the two edges, so the cycle is invisible. Verified on v1.15.6. Common causes: a `depends_on` pointing back up the chain, and `create_before_destroy` on only one resource of a mutually-referencing pair. (See [[tf-cmd-graph]].)

    **The structural fix, when the cycle is a mutual *association*:** split the relationship into its own resource. Two `aws_security_group` resources that each name the other in an in-line `ingress` block cannot be created — the provider needs both to exist first. Moving the rules out into standalone rule resources makes three nodes in a line out of two nodes in a loop: both groups are created with no interdependent rules, then the rules, then the attachment. Generalizes wherever a provider offers both an in-line and a standalone form of an association — security-group rules, IAM policy attachments, route-table associations. (See [[tut-troubleshooting-workflow]], [[dependency-graph]].)

    **Terraform 1.16 makes the error itself readable.** Today `Error: Cycle:` prints the members on **one long line**, which is why the `-draw-cycles` detour above exists at all. From 1.16 it renders **one node per line**, ordered by **reference** rather than graph-traversal order, and from a consistent starting node, so a loop spanning many nodes can be read without rendering an SVG. Same error, same fix, and `-draw-cycles` still wins when you need the *edges* rather than the members. This is in **no changelog** — it is commit `4c4adca78b` on the `v1.16` branch, found by reading the tree. 1.16 is at **rc1** as of this check, so treat it as imminent rather than available. (See [[release-feature-map]].)

!!! warning "The graph shows what Terraform knows, not what's true"
    `terraform graph` renders every edge Terraform built — and a **missing hidden dependency has no edge**, so it looks identical to a resource with no dependencies. Use the graph to *confirm* a suspected ordering bug, never to *discover* one. When a plan passes locally and races in CI, suspect a missing `depends_on` (I1) before you suspect `-parallelism`. (See [[dependency-graph]].)

!!! tip "Cleaning up the noisy `terraform graph` output"
    The raw DOT carries provider, root, and `(expand)` nodes on top of your resources, so a real config's graph is hard to read. Three easier-to-read options:

    - **SVG over PNG.** `terraform graph | dot -Tsvg > graph.svg` is zoomable and searchable.
    - **Online renderer.** Paste the DOT into [edotor.net](https://edotor.net); no Graphviz install needed.
    - **Prune with InfraMap.** [`cycloidio/inframap`](https://github.com/cycloidio/inframap) (v0.8.1, actively maintained as of mid-2026) reads state or HCL and emits only the resources that matter, dropping provider and meta nodes. It auto-detects the input: `inframap generate main.tf | dot -Tsvg > graph.svg`, or a state file with `inframap generate terraform.tfstate`. Force it with `--hcl`/`--tfstate` if detection guesses wrong.

    Interactive browser viewers (Rover, Blast Radius) exist, but the well-known ones are unmaintained (Rover's last release was 2022), so check that a tool is current before relying on it. (See [[tf-cmd-graph]].)

!!! note "📌 Global provider plugin cache"
    A shared **global provider plugin cache** (`TF_PLUGIN_CACHE_DIR` / `plugin_cache_dir`) avoids re-downloading providers per project. It historically wasn't safe for concurrent `init`s; **OpenTofu 1.10** added filesystem **cache locking** (flock/LockFileEx) so parallel CI jobs can share one cache without corruption. (See [[opentofu-feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 Provider mirrors for air-gapped setups"
    For air-gapped or locked-down environments, the CLI config's **`provider_installation`** block configures **`filesystem_mirror`** (serve providers from a local directory) and **`network_mirror`** (serve from an internal HTTPS mirror) instead of the public registry. Populate a mirror with **`terraform providers mirror DIR`**. ([HCDocs CLI config](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cli/config/config-file#provider-installation))

!!! note "📌 Checkpoint telemetry opt-out"
    The CLI phones home to HashiCorp's **Checkpoint** service for version + security-bulletin checks (this drives the "newer version available" line in `terraform version`). Only anonymous data is sent. Disable it in airgapped/privacy-sensitive setups: **`CHECKPOINT_DISABLE=1`** (env, all HashiCorp tools) or CLI-config **`disable_checkpoint = true`** (all calls) / **`disable_checkpoint_signature = true`** (keep bulletin checks, drop the anonymous signature). (See [[tf-cli-commands]].)

!!! warning "📌 `experiments` — not for production"
    The `terraform {}` block's **`experiments = [...]`** argument opts a module into pre-release language features for feedback (e.g. optional object attrs before 1.3 GA'd them). Advanced and **not for production** — it warns on every plan/apply and can break in minor/patch releases. ([HCDocs terraform block](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/block/terraform))

!!! info "📌 OpenTelemetry tracing — OpenTofu documents it, Terraform hides it"
    Both binaries enable an experimental OTLP trace exporter on **`OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp`**, and both delegate every other knob to the standard `OTEL_*` variables. This path previously said "Terraform has no built-in OTel equivalent" — **wrong**: the hook exists in `telemetry.go`, it is simply undocumented (a grep of the Terraform repo's `website/` for `opentelemetry` returns nothing). What differs is what you get back.

    **OpenTofu 1.10+** traces the graph walk: `Validation phase` / `Plan phase` / `Apply phase`, then one span per graph node (`Plan resource instance changes`, `Apply resource instance changes`, `Configure provider`), tagged with `opentofu.resource_instance.address`, `opentofu.resource.type`, and `opentofu.provider.source`. That is what makes "which resource dominates the apply" answerable.

    **Terraform 1.15** has ~10 span sites, all in `main`, `checkpoint.go`, and `internal/command` — module and provider installation, backend init. **The graph walk is not traced at all**, so a trace of `terraform apply` tells you nothing about which resource was slow. The instrumented plan/apply code lives in the **Stacks** runtime (`internal/stacks/stackruntime`, `internal/rpcapi`), which HCP Terraform drives over RPC rather than the CLI.

    Three more differences that decide whether this is usable in CI: OpenTofu reads **`TRACEPARENT`** (1.10) so a run nests inside the pipeline's trace, and Terraform does not ([hashicorp/terraform#35444](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35444), open since 2024-07-10); OpenTofu batches span export while Terraform uses a `SimpleSpanProcessor` that exports synchronously per span; and Terraform names its root span `terraform <full command line>`, so a `-var="password=…"` lands in the tracing backend, where OpenTofu's root span is just `tofu`. Sampling is fixed at 100% in both. Details and the version gating: [[ot-otel-tracing]], [[otel-tracing-facts]].

!!! note "📌 High-cardinality perf + `rpcapi` (Terraform 1.13)"
    Terraform **1.13** made evaluation of **high-cardinality `count`/`for_each`** (hundreds+ of instances) much faster — relevant when a big config's graph is the bottleneck. For building tooling *around* Terraform, `terraform rpcapi` (1.13, GA) exposes core operations over a plugin RPC interface. (See [[feature-history]].)

    **Read it against [TID Ch11 §11.1](books/tid/chapters/11-alternative-interfaces.md#111-wrapping-terraform), which says the CLI is the only way in.** Source settles it — `commands.go` registers `rpcapi` under a comment saying its *"whole point … is to bypass the CLI layer so wrapping automation can get as-direct-as-possible access to Terraform Core functionality"*, bypassing the `command` package entirely. Verified out of experimental as of **v1.13.0** by `git tag --contains`. Two limits worth knowing before betting on it: it lives in `HiddenCommands`, so it is plumbing rather than an advertised interface, and it came out of the Stacks work rather than as a general integration API. For Go specifically there is also **`hashicorp/terraform-exec`**, HashiCorp's own CLI wrapper returning `terraform-json` types, which is what a Go integration should start from instead of hand-rolling one.

    ⚠️ **OpenTofu has neither** — no `rpcapi` package, no registration, zero occurrences in the 1.12.5 tree. So anything that must run on both engines still wraps the CLI, and that, rather than the absence of an API, is the reason the wrapper pattern is worth learning.

**Milestone:** You can diagnose a failing/slow apply from `TF_LOG` output and cut plan time on a large config via caching and parallelism tuning.

---

### ⬜ E6 — Platform engineering & self-service

**What it is:** Terraform as an internal platform — golden/opinionated modules, self-service via HCP no-code modules or an IDP (e.g. Backstage), run tasks, and org-wide drift detection.

**Why you need it:** The end state of Terraform maturity is letting product teams provision safely without writing raw HCL; this is the platform-engineering frontier.

**How to learn it:**

1. **Reference — [HCDocs "No-Code modules"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/no-code-provisioning/module-design) + [Run Tasks](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/workspaces/settings/run-tasks) + [private registry](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/cloud-docs/registry)** (~1 hr) — the self-service building blocks.
2. **Video — [HashiCorp "Terraform for platform engineers" (Armon Dadgar)](https://www.hashicorp.com/en/resources/terraform-for-platform-engineers)** (~45 min) — how mature orgs expose Terraform to non-experts via golden modules.
3. **Interactive — HCTut ["Create and use no-code modules"](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/tutorials/modules/no-code-provisioning)** (~14 min stated, budget ~1.5 hrs with AWS; **HCP Standard edition**) — put a golden module in the private registry and let users provision it with no HCL, straight from the HCP UI. Notes: [[tut-no-code-provisioning]]. **The design rule that defines the model:** a no-code workspace's entire change surface is *(variable values) × (module version)* — *"You cannot directly apply configuration changes to existing no-code workspaces"* — so anything you did not expose as a variable is fixed until the author ships a release. That makes minimizing inputs ([[tut-pattern-module-creation]]) a hard requirement rather than a style preference. **It also inverts the no-provider-blocks-in-modules rule and explains why the rule existed:** provider blocks are forbidden because they conflict with `count`/`for_each`/`depends_on`, and *"Since users will not reference no-code modules in written configuration, there is no risk of this conflict."* The cost is that HCP spawns a workspace per provision, so provider credentials must arrive automatically — a project-scoped **variable set** here, dynamic credentials in practice. Governance sits in **Configure Settings → Edit versions and variable options**: you pin which module version no-code deploys (pushing a tag publishes a version but does **not** roll it out), and you can turn a free-text variable into a **dropdown of allowed values**. ⚠️ Its own 1.0.1 upgrade adds a variable with **no default**, forcing every consuming workspace to answer a prompt — the breaking way to make an additive change, and a live counterexample to [[tut-module-object-attributes]]. Two things not to copy from its module: `publicly_accessible = true` on the RDS instance, and an ingress CIDR of `192.80.0.0/16` described as an internal VPN when it is public address space. Also cited from **I5**, which takes the author half — this is the one page documenting where the no-`provider`-block rule and the standard-structure rule change shape for a module nobody calls from HCL.

!!! info "📌 OpenTofu — MCP server (1.10)"
    OpenTofu **1.10** ships an **MCP server** — exposes OpenTofu operations to AI agents / LLM tooling via the Model Context Protocol, an emerging building block for AI-assisted self-service IaC. OpenTofu-only. (See [[opentofu-feature-history]].)

!!! note "📌 Multi-tenant note — no-code provisioning is the one thing here a tenant can run for itself"
    Most platform capabilities need an organization-level grant, which is why **A9** keeps pushing them back to the platform team. No-code provisioning is the exception, and it fits the project boundary cleanly. Provisioning requires `Manage all projects`, `Manage all workspaces`, or **project Admin**, and with custom project permissions a team needs only the ability to create workspaces, write variables and apply runs. So a tenant with admin over its own project self-serves without any org-wide permission.

    Two mechanics make it work inside a tenant boundary. The provisioning flow asks which **project** the new workspace goes in, and "teams with access to the specified project can view the workspace automatically", so the tenant's existing team access carries over. And "if the specified project contains any project-scoped variable sets, HCP Terraform automatically applies those sets to the workspace", which is exactly how a per-tenant dynamic-credential set reaches a workspace nobody configured by hand.

    Two cautions. **Auto apply is the default** for a no-code workspace, so self-service provisioning applies without review unless you change it. And no-code readiness is a property of a module in the **organization's** private registry, which has no per-team read scope, so every tenant can provision every no-code module you publish. Restricting who may provision *what* is a policy question (**A5**), not a registry one.

**Milestone:** You can publish a governed golden module to a private registry and let another team provision it self-service with policy enforcement.

---

### ⬜ Expert Checkpoint

You have mastered Terraform when you can:
- Build and ship a custom provider with the Plugin Framework.
- Architect large-scale, multi-team state with Stacks/Terragrunt and fast, small plans.
- Choose and migrate between Terraform and OpenTofu on real technical merits, and stand up a self-service platform with governance.

!!! note "Research log"
    Newest entries first, though the run is not strictly sorted — some findings were written up after later ones. **Last updated: 2026-08-21 (*Manually Update State* opened up — [[tf-cli-state]], [[tf-resource-addressing]] and [[tf-cmd-state]]: the write-side section index, the address grammar behind the instance-key asymmetry, and the forced-backup rule that covers the CLI path but not the `removed`/`moved` one; plus [[tf-cli-state-inspect]] and [[tf-cli-state-taint]], which together separate a stale *index* from stale *naming* — and the latter supplies the mechanism behind `-replace` superseding `taint`; then [[tf-cmd-untaint]], which narrows the forced-backup rule to the `state` command family and corrects the glossary's claim that `untaint` was deprecated too).**

> **2026-08-21** — **[[tf-cmd-untaint]] captured, and it settles why one half of a deprecated pair is not deprecated.** `taint` was a way to *schedule* a replacement, and `-replace` does that directly. `untaint` answers a different question — **Terraform thinks this object is damaged and I disagree** — which no flag expresses, so the command stays. The page also states the marker itself better than anything else captured: tainted means an object *“might be damaged and so a future Terraform plan **ought to** replace it”*, hedged in both halves because it records a suspicion rather than a finding. Its auto-tainting trigger is narrower than the overview's two: *“an error… during a **multi-step 'create' action**”*, which arguably subsumes the failed-provisioner case, since a provisioner is a step of the create. Read together the rule is **the marker is set when a create was interrupted partway**. **A practical consequence worth knowing before you untaint anything:** changing your mind back needs no re-taint — *“you can create and apply a plan to replace it without first re-tainting the object”*, `apply -replace="aws_instance.example[0]"`. ⚠️ **And a correction to the scope of the forced-backup rule folded in above.** `untaint` modifies state and takes the state lock, but it is not a `terraform state` subcommand, and its `-backup` is grouped with `-state`/`-state-out` as **legacy, local-backend-only** — the flags [[tf-backend-local]] records the docs as having disowned. So `untaint` against a remote backend writes state with **no automatic backup**. The forced backup is a property of the `state` command family, not of state modification, which is now stated that way in the callout. Two glossary rows corrected while wiring this in: the old single `taint`/`untaint` row called **both** commands deprecated. They are now three rows — the deprecated command, the marker Terraform sets itself, and the command that is not deprecated. Also recorded: the address argument is the **strict** parser (one instance, like `state show`), `-allow-missing` makes the command idempotent by forgiving only a missing resource and nothing else, and `-ignore-remote-version` dates to **0.14.1** with the remote-workspace compatibility checks it opts out of ([[feature-history]]). Nav at **rung 1** under *Forcing Re-creation (Tainting)*. **I7 stays ⬜.**
>
> **2026-08-21** — **[[tf-cli-state-taint]] captured, and it **corrects** a claim this path made two entries ago.** The *Re-create resources* link and the sidebar group *Forcing Re-creation (Tainting)* both point at a tainting-named URL with `-replace` in neither label — from which the [[tf-cli-state]] entry inferred that a reader following the sidebar meets the deprecated pair first. **The destination says otherwise.** It opens on `-replace` as the manual workflow and names `taint` only in its last paragraph, to deprecate it. So the staleness there is **naming, not guidance**, and the genuine index-lags-reference case in this collection is [[tf-cli-state-inspect]] on `refresh`. Both notes and the I7 callout now draw that line. **What the page adds is a reason for the deprecation that no other captured source states as mechanism:** `-replace` is preferred because it *“avoids the need to create an interim state snapshot with a tainted object.”* The path, TID Ch5 and [[tut-state-cli]] all say `taint` mutated state out-of-band with no plan to review; this is HashiCorp saying the same thing structurally — `taint` had to **write** state to record the intent, `-replace` carries the intent through the plan, so state is written once at apply like any other change. **And tainting is a state field, not only a command.** Terraform sets it itself when it can infer damage, in two named cases: an object *“partially created in the remote system”*, and a **failed provisioner step** — which is the other half of the `on_failure = fail` sentence already in **A1**. The status is what makes `untaint` still useful after the command that wrote it by hand was deprecated: it is the override for Terraform's own inference, and after it the object is *“ready for use by any downstream resource declarations”*, which is the reminder that a tainted object poisons its dependents too. **One sentence worth keeping for the resource model itself:** Terraform cannot detect a crashed process inside a running VM *“because Terraform only directly manages the machine as a whole”* — the cleanest statement in the captured docs of where the resource abstraction stops, and the same boundary [[tut-state-import]] hits from the adoption side and [[pyinfra]] from the configuration-management side. Three typos recorded (*“does not have no way”*, *“when you meed to replace”*, *“in a tainted states”*). Nav at **rung 1** under *Forcing Re-creation (Tainting)*. **I7 stays ⬜.**
>
> **2026-08-21** — **[[tf-cli-state-inspect]] captured — four sentences, and the interesting part is what it omits.** All three commands it names were already captured ([[tf-cmd-state-list]], [[tf-cmd-state-show]], [[tf-cmd-refresh]]), so this is not a gap-filling capture. ⚠️ **It describes `terraform refresh` in neutral present tense with no deprecation notice and no mention of `-refresh-only`**, one click above the reference that opens with *“This command is deprecated”* and explains that the command is `apply -refresh-only -auto-approve` with `-auto-approve` unremovable. That turns an observation into a pattern: the path already flagged [[tut-state-cli]]'s final exercise for teaching bare `refresh`, and [[tf-cli-state]] for routing *Re-create resources* to `taint`/`untaint` without naming `-replace`. **Three index pages, all lagging the references they point at**, so the I7 callout now says to read the reference before trusting the index that led you there. **The page's one genuine contribution** is the only justification anything captured gives for a standalone refresh: refreshing happens automatically during plans and applies, *“but not when interacting with state directly”* — nothing in the `state` family refreshes, so state read through those subcommands is as stale as the last plan left it. Two smaller things kept. The group is defined as *“reading **and updating** state without taking any other actions”*, so its cut is **no infrastructure side effects**, not *read-only* — which is why `state list` and `state show` are filed here **and** under *Inspecting Infrastructure* ([[tf-cli-inspect]]), where the criterion genuinely is read-only. And `state list`'s filter is described as taking a **“partial resource address”**, the only place this section names the incomplete-address idea that [[tf-resource-addressing]] documents. Nav at **rung 1**, first in *Inspecting State* above the `refresh` note already there. **I7 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-21** — **[[tf-cmd-state]] captured — the `terraform state` hub, whose value is two rules that apply to the whole family and appear on none of the individual command pages.** **The first is forced backups.** *“All `terraform state` subcommands that modify the state write backup files”*, the path is set with `-backup`, read-only subcommands write none, and *“backups for state modification **can not be disabled**… You'll have to remove these files manually.”* Folded into **I7** as a callout, with the boundary spelled out: the guarantee covers `terraform state <subcommand>` and **not** the `removed`/`moved` path, which goes through `apply` — so the config-driven route this topic recommends is exactly the route with no automatic backup. It also sharpens a flaw already recorded on [[tf-state-refactor]]: that page's manual `terraform state pull > terraform.tfstate.backup` not only reuses the local backend's own backup filename, it now also collides with the backup the following `state mv` writes by itself. **The second is that remote state changes nothing except latency and locality** — the subcommands *“work with remote state just as if it was local state”*, each read and write costing *“a full network roundtrip”*, and *“backups are still written to disk”*. So a `state mv` against a remote backend leaves a complete copy of the state file on the machine that ran it, which is a hole in the access-control argument [[gitlab-tf-state]] and [[tf-state-backends]] make. ⚠️ **And the hub is incomplete:** it lists six subcommands — `list`, `mv`, `pull`, `replace-provider`, `rm`, `show` — and **omits `state push`**, verified to exist with its own reference page (*“uploads a local state file to remote state or a local state”*) and a sidebar slot beside `state pull`. The one subcommand that overwrites a state file wholesale is missing from the index of state subcommands; use the sidebar for the full set. Also recorded: the family's output is *designed* for `grep`/`awk` piping, which is the reason [[tf-cmd-state-list]]'s sort order is specified at all. Nav at **rung 1**, third in *Manually Update State*. **I7 stays ⬜** — four of the family's six pages are still uncaptured.

> **2026-08-21** — **[[tf-resource-addressing]] captured, and it supplies the missing *reason* behind an asymmetry this path had already recorded as a fact.** The page is the grammar — `[module path][resource spec]`, `[N]` for `count`, `["KEY"]` for `for_each` — but the load-bearing sentence is a caveat: an **incomplete address** is legal, and *“the meaning depends on the context, so you'll need to refer to the documentation for the **specific feature** you are using which parses resource addresses.”* **One grammar, several parsers.** That is why `import`'s `to` takes an instance key, `moved` takes them on both sides, and `removed`'s `from` refuses them — nothing in the notation forbids it, each feature decides what completeness it demands. The **I7** instance-key table now says so instead of leaving the asymmetry unexplained. **The rule worth memorising is about omission, not syntax:** *“Omitting an index when addressing a resource where `count > 1` means that the address references all instances.”* Same one level up — `module.foo` is every resource in every instance of that module call. Added as a callout with the four parsers side by side, because they disagree: `state list` filters to all instances, `state show` errors unless the address resolves to exactly one ([[tf-cmd-state-list]], [[tf-cmd-state-show]]), `-target` takes the set and walks dependencies per resource regardless, and `state rm` takes the set — which is the one that costs something. **Two legacy rules recorded for reading old configurations and old advice:** module indexes require **v0.13+**, and a bare resource spec has meant *root module only* since **v0.12**, where earlier versions matched the same type and name in **any descendant module** — so a pre-0.12 `terraform taint aws_instance.web` reached into child modules and today's identical command does not. **One imprecision flagged rather than repeated:** the page calls `for_each` keys *“alphanumerical”*, but they are arbitrary strings, and HashiCorp's own `for_each` page keys a set on `subnet-012345` ([[tf-meta-for-each]]). The quoting in the grammar is what does the work, which is why [[tf-cmd-state-show]] needs a whole section on shell escaping. Nav at **rung 1**, second in *Manually Update State* as the sidebar has it. **I7 stays ⬜.**

Three things it supplies. **The risk is stated in consequences rather than in cautions** — modifying state outside `plan`/`apply` *“can cause Terraform to lose track of managed resources, leading to increased costs, reduced productivity, or compromised security”* — and **the backup instruction is unconditional**, with no “if remote” or “if a team” qualifier. > **2026-08-20** — **[[tf-block-moved]] captured. Thin page — two arguments and one paragraph — but that paragraph states a mechanism every other source only states as an outcome.** [[tf-modules-refactoring]] already carried the deep treatment (instance-key moves, `count` ↔ `for_each` migrations, what `moved` cannot do) and [[tut-move-config]] the hands-on, so almost nothing here is new. The exception: *"**Before creating a new plan** […] Terraform **checks the state** for an existing object at the address specified in the `from` field. Terraform **renames existing objects** […] and **then creates a plan**. As a result, Terraform does not destroy the resource during the Terraform run."* **The state rename happens before the plan is computed.** That is *why* `moved` turns a destroy-and-recreate into a no-op: by diff time the new address already has an object bound to it, where without the block the plan sees an empty new address and an unclaimed old one — one creation and one deletion, because an address is an identity. It also explains why the block is safe to leave in place, since a `from` matching nothing simply finds nothing to rename. Folded into **I7** as a callout. **The capture's real payoff is a completed comparison.** With all three refactoring block references now in hand, the instance-key question resolves three ways and is **not symmetric**: `import`'s `to` takes instance keys, `moved` takes them on **either side** (mixing an index with a key is precisely how a `count` → `for_each` migration is written), and `removed`'s `from` **cannot** take them at all. So a multi-instance resource can be **adopted per instance** and **rearranged per instance**, but only **forgotten wholesale** — the sharper form of the two-way asymmetry recorded when [[tf-import-single]] landed, and now with the third data point that identifies `removed` as the outlier rather than `import` as the exception. **One defect, and it makes three in a row:** the configuration model types `from`/`to` as **reference** while the Specification table below types both as **string**; the unquoted example settles it in the model's favour. That follows `identity` (map versus String) in [[tf-block-import]] and `include_resource` (Default: None versus false) in [[tf-block-list]] — **three consecutive block references contradicting themselves between their own sections**, always with the prose and the configuration model correct and the summary tables wrong. **I7 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tf-block-list]] captured, and its most useful sentence is about a word everyone thinks they already understand.** `limit` on a `list` block is **not a filter over a complete result set**: *"When the number of results reaches the specified limit, Terraform breaks the connection to the provider and stops reporting results."* So a query returns the first N in whatever order the provider produced them, not the N most relevant — and for an adoption sweep that is the difference between "there are 100 of these" and "there are at least 100 of these". Raising `limit` is how you find out which. Three more additions to I7's bulk-import callout. **`list` blocks are legal only in `.tfquery.hcl` files**, stated as a restriction rather than a convention. **`count` is supported**, mutually exclusive with `for_each`, with the standard split — `count = length(var.subnet_ids)` for near-identical queries, `for_each = toset([…])` for map- or set-driven ones — where [[tf-import-bulk]] had mentioned meta-arguments only in passing. And **lists can reference other lists**: with `include_resource = true` on the first, a second reads `list.<type>.<label>.data[<index>].state.<attribute>`, which both reveals the result shape (a `data` list of `state` objects) and means queries **chain** — discover one set, then size or filter a second from what it found. **A referencing-syntax contradiction resolved by majority:** the Specification says to use `list.<label>.<attribute>`, but the page's own example and [[tf-import-bulk]] both use `list.<type>.<label>.…`, so the prose sentence is the outlier. **And a pattern now worth stating as a rule for this project.** This page has **seven** defects — `provider = concept`, `count` written inside a `config` block, an `azurerm_resource_group` declared with `provider = aws`, a "Complete configuration" block with `for_each [` missing its `=`, an `include_resource` Summary reading *"Default: None"* against its own prose, and an "attriubtes" typo — on top of [[tf-block-import]]'s six the capture before. **Two consecutive block-reference pages whose prose is authoritative and whose samples do not survive inspection.** The I7 callout now says so explicitly: read these for the argument tables and the behavioural sentences, take working syntax from the workflow pages or from a real plan. **I7 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tf-block-import]] captured, closing the import tree and answering the question three previous captures had to leave open.** Each of [[tf-import]], [[tf-import-single]] and [[tf-import-generate]] defers to this page, and each of my notes carried a ⚠️ saying that what `id` accepts as an *expression* was unverified and must not be inferred from tutorials that only ever use literals. The reference answers it in one sentence: *"You must specify a **string or an expression that evaluates to a string**"*, and *"the ID must be **known during the plan operation**."* So `each.value`, a `var` or a `local` are all legal, and another managed resource's attribute is not, because it is `(known after apply)`. **That is the plan-time-known rule met in a third place** — the path already tracks it as the constraint on `count`/`for_each` arguments (TID §4.8.4, from the plan side in [[05-terraform-plan]] §5.7) and hit it again via OpenTofu's `issensitive()` change. It is not a rule about loops; it is a rule about anything Terraform must resolve before it can build a plan, and importing qualifies because it has to ask the provider about the object while planning. The ⚠️ in **I7** is replaced with the answer. **`identity` is an object of key-value pairs**, not a string, and is stated mutually exclusive with `id` on both arguments. **`for_each` gets promoted from a one-line feature note to a worked callout.** The reference's first example adopts three buckets from a `locals` map with `to = aws_s3_bucket.this[each.key]` and `id = each.value`, the destination resource's own `for_each` driven by the same local so the addresses line up. Its second example is the one worth remembering: `for_each` over a **list of objects** importing into **module instances** — `to = module.group[each.value.group].aws_s3_bucket.this[each.value.key]`, a module instance key and a resource instance key in one address, both computed from the loop. That is what adopting a real estate looks like and nothing in the tutorials approaches it. Also captured: a **placement recommendation** no other page gives — an `imports.tf` holding every block, *or* each block beside its destination resource — which maps neatly onto the two dispositions [[tf-import-single]] describes, delete-after-adoption versus keep-as-provenance. **Six defects on the page recorded rather than reproduced:** the single-resource example has an extra closing brace and does not parse; `identity`'s Summary box says *"Data type: String"* against both the configuration model (map) and its own prose (object); the "Complete configuration" block declares `for_each` **twice**, as map and as list, which is invalid HCL as printed; that block is introduced as *"The following **module** block"*; `provider` is shown quoted in the Specification and unquoted in the example, and only the latter is a reference; and the alias example sets `region = "us-westl-1"`. The prose is authoritative, the samples are unproofread. **The import reference tree is now complete** — hub, two workflows, generation, and the block reference. **I7 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tf-import-generate]] captured, and it corrects something three other captures had quietly assumed: `-generate-config-out` is still flagged experimental.** The page carries an **Experimental** banner — *"Configuration generation is available in Terraform v1.5 as an experimental feature. Later minor versions may contain changes to the formatting of generated configuration and behavior of the `terraform plan` command"* — and every run prints `Warning: Config generation is experimental` beside the plan. The page is banner-versioned **v1.15.x (latest)**, so this is current rather than stale 1.5-era text: three years and ten minor releases after it shipped. **Nothing that uses the flag mentions it.** [[tut-state-import]] demonstrates it without a word on its status, [[tf-import-single]] recommends it for complex resources unqualified, and the **TUR Ch5** reading note called generation one of three advantages of the declarative form — that note now carries the caveat. The distinction that matters: the **`import` block itself is not experimental**, only the generation, so config-driven import stays the recommended path. **Three operational rules folded into I7.** An **existing output path is an error** — the same rule [[tf-import-bulk]] records for `terraform query`, so it belongs to the flag rather than to either command. **A `provider` block is mandatory when the configuration has no other resources for that provider**, *"otherwise Terraform displays an error if it can not determine which provider to use"*, and adding one costs a re-`init` — which bites exactly in the empty-repository adoption case that generation exists to serve. And generation is **self-limiting**: *"Terraform knows it doesn't need to generate configuration for resources that already exist in your state."* **The limitation section documents invalid output, not merely verbose output:** for *"resources with complex schemas"* Terraform may emit mutually-exclusive arguments — the worked case is `ipv6_address_count` conflicting with `ipv6_addresses` on `aws_instance`. ⚠️ **And two of the page's own rules collide without either section noticing:** the failing run *"still generates configuration and writes it to `generated.tf`"*, while an existing path *"throws an error"* — so a generation that errors leaves behind the very file that makes re-running the identical command fail for a different reason. Recorded as a callout, since the loop is edit-in-place or delete-then-regenerate. **One tension left open rather than resolved:** [[tut-state-import]] says the generated draft contains *"all possible arguments… including those set to default values and those without values"*, while this page's own example `generated.tf` is a **single line** and its prose is softer — *"Terraform's best guess at the appropriate value for each resource argument."* Schema size plausibly explains it, but the two claims are not the same and nothing captured reconciles them; the note says to plan for the tutorial's version, since the verbose draft is the case that needs work. Also recorded: the clean generate-plan renders the resource with **no action symbol at all** and `1 to import, 0 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy` — a pure import proposes no infrastructure action. **I7 stays ⬜**; no written chapter used the flag, so nothing flips to 🔄.

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tf-import-single]] captured, and its sharpest finding is an asymmetry no single page states: you can adopt one instance of a `count`/`for_each` resource, but you cannot forget one.** `import`'s **`to` takes an instance key** — `aws_instance.example[0]`, `aws_instance.example["env"]` — with worked examples for both meta-arguments. The `removed` block's **`from` explicitly cannot**: *"You cannot include instance keys… if the resource is configured to provision multiple instances"* ([[tf-state-remove]], captured 2026-07-30). Neither page mentions the other, so the consequence only appears when both are in view — a multi-instance resource is **populated per instance and forgotten wholesale**, which means a staged migration *into* Terraform is possible and a staged migration *out* is not. Folded into **I7** as a ⚠️ callout. **Two more things the block accepts, both absent from the path:** a **`module.` prefix** on `to`, so the destination `resource` block lives in a child module while the `import` block stays in the root, and a **`provider` argument** taking an alias (`provider = aws.europe`) — which is the correct fix for [[tut-refresh]]'s scenario, where a wrong-region provider configuration made a live instance refresh as `has been deleted`. **A decision rule the path lacked:** *"manually writing the resource block when you know how to configure all or most of the resource's arguments"*, and generated configuration *"when importing multiple resources or a single complex resource that you do not already have the configuration for."* So `-generate-config-out` is the fallback for resources you do not understand, not the default — which reframes [[tut-state-import]], which generates because it is teaching the flag. **And the two pages state the same target from opposite ends**, which is worth having as one sentence: hand-writing starts from an empty block and adds *"arguments that have non-default values"*, generating starts from an all-arguments draft and prunes to *"required arguments and arguments whose values differ from defaults"* — so **the configuration should name exactly the arguments differing from provider defaults**, no more and no fewer. Too few is [[tut-state-import]]'s `env = null`, which turned an adoption into a destroy. **Idempotency now has a mechanism behind the advice:** *"applying an import action and running another plan does not generate another import action as long as that resource remains in your state"*, and importing to the same address twice *"has no impact"* — which is why both import pages recommend keeping the block as *"a record of the resource's origin for future module maintainers"*, and it is the only thing in a configuration distinguishing an adopted resource from a created one. **The standing ⚠️ is narrowed rather than cleared:** the shape of **`to`** is settled (type and label, optional instance key, optional `module.` prefix), but what **`id`** accepts as an *expression* still is not — this page only ever shows a literal and defers to the **import block reference**, the last uncaptured child. One broken sample recorded: the `count` example opens its block as `resource "aws_instance" "example {`, missing the label's closing quote. **I7 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tf-import-bulk]] captured, and it turns list resources from a curiosity this path had noted into the front half of an adoption pipeline.** The previous capture flagged bulk import as a documented workflow with no hands-on; this is the page, and the mechanics were entirely absent from the path. **The `list` block** takes two labels like a `resource` block, and four arguments worth memorising: **`provider` is required** (defaults to the `terraform` block's configuration, but an in-query-file `provider` block can point at another region or account without touching `main.tf`), **`config` is provider-specific** including `filter`, **`include_resource` defaults to `false`** so only identities come back and must be flipped to reference any *attribute* — at a stated performance cost — and **`limit` defaults to 100 per block**. Query files also take `variable` and `locals`, and `list` accepts meta-arguments including `count`, so a `.tfquery.hcl` is a small reusable program rather than a filter expression. **The most confusable finding: `-generate-config-out` hangs off two different commands and does different work on each.** On `plan` it consumes `import` blocks you wrote and generates the missing `resource` blocks; on `query` it consumes `list` blocks and generates **both**, identities included. Individual import assumes you already know the IDs; bulk import is for when you do not. **Two operational traps recorded:** `generated.tf` is written **to the local workstation even when connected to HCP Terraform or Terraform Enterprise**, so remote execution does not remove the local step, and Terraform **refuses to overwrite it** — rerunning with the file present *"results in an error"*, making the iterate-on-a-query loop `rm generated.tf` then re-run. **The HCP capability is the one with real operational weight:** *"HCP Terraform uses resource identities to determine when resources are managed by another workspace"*, which guards against a bulk import adopting something another team's workspace already owns — [[tf-state-purpose]]'s one-to-one mapping broken in the least visible way available, two states over one object with neither aware. **A version claim settled rather than left open.** The page's only version statement is *"v1.12 or newer"*, scoped to identity-based search, and it never gives a minimum for the feature it documents — which sat awkwardly against this path's **1.14** for list resources and `terraform query`. Checked against the local checkout instead of guessing: `internal/command/query.go` is **absent at `v1.12.0`, present at `v1.13.0`**, `tfquery` already matches in the config parser at that tag, the **1.13 changelog names neither**, and **1.14's NEW FEATURES section announces both** verbatim. So the code shipped in 1.13 and was announced in 1.14 — the same in-the-tree-before-the-changelog pattern as `state_store` ([[release-feature-map]]) — and **B8's existing date is correct, so no 🔄**. Finally, the reframe: every State-collection tutorial performs discovery by hand (`docker inspect`, `aws ec2 create-security-group` plus `echo $SG_ID`, `terraform output -raw instance_id`), and that step is precisely what a `list` block replaces. **I7 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tf-import]] captured — I7 reference #2 had been a bare URL, and the page behind it has been rebuilt since anything in this path last looked at it.** It is now a **hub**, not the block reference: three short sections routing to child pages, none of which are captured. Two things folded into I7. **Resource identity gets a reason, where the path had only a version row.** The existing callout said `identity` (1.12) exists "for providers that identify a resource by a structured identity object" and stopped. The page supplies the argument: Terraform identifies a resource *"according to either the ID assigned by the cloud provider or a collection of specific attributes defined by the provider"*, and its example is decisive — an AWS **`s3_bucket`** identity is **`account_id` + `bucket` + `region`**, because a bucket name is not unique on its own, while an **`aws_instance`** ID already is globally unique and `id` suffices. Structured identity is for objects whose identity is genuinely compound, not a nicer syntax. **And bulk import is a documented workflow with no hands-on anywhere in this path.** The page presents two: individual import, which *"works best when you can easily access unique infrastructure resource IDs"*, and — when you cannot — *"define queries as HCL, add the results to your Terraform configuration, and use the `terraform apply` command to import the discovered resources."* That is the language side of **list resources / `terraform query`** (1.14), which this path had tracked only as *discovery*; the join is that discovery and adoption are meant to be one workflow, replacing the manual `aws ec2 describe-*` step every tutorial performs by hand. It also explains a pattern noticed three times this session: the docs moved twice — CLI command → `import` block (1.5) → query-driven bulk — and the State-collection tutorials were patched with a pointer at the first move and not at all at the second. **One question deliberately left open rather than guessed.** The page never says what `id`/`identity` accept as *expressions* — literal only, or variables and `each.key` too. That is the uncaptured **import block reference** child, and the I7 callout now carries a ⚠️ saying so, because every tutorial uses a literal and inferring the rule from them would be exactly the kind of confident wrong answer this path tries not to produce. Also uncaptured and now named: *Import resources in bulk* and *Import a single resource*. Filed under a new **Import resources** nav group mirroring the docs sidebar. **I7 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tut-console]] captured — the last page of the State collection, and the one whose subject is not state at all.** Reading state is the console's *input*, not its topic; the page is about **authoring**, which is what separates it from [[tut-variables]]'s use of the same REPL for lookup. Two facts land in **B7**, whose step 1 had been a bare "open the REPL and experiment". **The console takes the state lock** — *"you cannot plan or apply changes while the console is running"* — so a forgotten session in another terminal is a plausible and easily-missed cause of a lock error before anyone reaches for [[tf-cmd-force-unlock]]. Combined with the gotcha the path already carried (configuration loads at startup only), the console is a snapshot in **both** directions: blind to your new configuration, and exclusive against everything that would write state. It also reads **stdin**, so `echo 'expr' | terraform console` evaluates one expression non-interactively and releases the lock immediately — the scriptable form, absent from the path until now. **The JSON pattern is the transferable part** and now has its own callout: `jsondecode(file("policy.json"))` in the console turns a vendor's JSON policy into HCL, which you paste into a `jsonencode({ … })` and where `"<BUCKET_ARN>/*"` becomes `"${aws_s3_bucket.data.arn}/*"` — a real dependency edge a hand-edited string could never be. The payoff is visible in the diff, because Terraform special-cases `jsonencode` in the plan renderer and expands the structure line by line rather than printing one opaque blob. **With the anti-pattern stated alongside it:** having used `jsonencode` to *verify* the shape, the tutorial defines its output as a **plain map** and uses `terraform output -json`, since encoding inside the output would produce JSON nested inside a JSON string. Machine-readable form belongs on the command, not in the configuration. **A second B7 callout** records that the console prints **cty types** with values (`tolist([])` versus `toset([…])`, `tomap(null) /* of string */`) where `terraform show` does not — which makes it the cheapest available type checker, and would have answered [[tut-troubleshooting-workflow]]'s splat-into-`for_each` exercise in one line. Two glossary rows added: `terraform console` itself, and the **`<=` plan symbol** (*read (data resources)*, a data source deferred to apply because its arguments are not yet known) — the fourth distinct action marker this session after `+`/`-`/`~`/`-/+` and the `.` forget marker. **One real error in the page recorded rather than repeated:** its summary sentence says the configuration *"uses the `jsondecode()` function to convert the policy back into JSON"* when it uses **`jsonencode()`**, the exact opposite, in the code block directly above. Also: it calls the resource `aws_bucket_policy` (it is `aws_s3_bucket_policy`), and its HCP tab pins `~> 5.14.0` while the `init` transcript beneath installs **v4.10.0**. **The State collection is now 11 of 11.** Statuses unchanged — **B7** stays ✅, **I7** and **E5** stay ⬜.

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tut-refresh]] captured, and it settles the contradiction flagged two captures ago: the collection has a page that gets `refresh` right, and [[tut-state-cli]] is the outlier.** The coverage check came out **covered-but-unexercised** — **I7** already carried `-refresh-only` and [[tf-cmd-refresh]] already quoted the misconfigured-credentials hazard, but nothing in the path *demonstrated* it, and a warning nobody has seen fire is a warning people talk themselves out of. This tutorial fires it deliberately and cheaply: create an instance in the default region, write **one line** of `terraform.tfvars` moving the provider to `us-west-2`, and `plan -refresh-only` reports a **running** instance as `# aws_instance.server has been deleted`. Nothing is broken — the provider is answering honestly about a region with no such object — and `terraform refresh` would accept that answer and drop the resource unprompted. The tutorial then does something no other page in the collection does: it tells you **not to apply**. That refusal is the lesson, and cleanup begins with `rm terraform.tfvars`, treating removal of the misconfiguration as a step rather than an afterthought. **Two facts here appear on no reference page this path cites.** `terraform refresh` is **not supported in workspaces using HCP Terraform as a remote backend** while `-refresh-only` is, so on a `cloud` block the decision is already made. And the deprecation is explicitly *not* a removal — *"Though Terraform will continue to support the `refresh` subcommand in future versions, it is deprecated"* — which is the best available explanation for why the command keeps resurfacing in material written years apart, this collection included. **A framing correction folded in too:** the 2026-08-20 drift capture recorded refresh-only's output rewriting as a *hazard* for [[tf-remote-state-data]] consumers; this page presents the same behaviour as a *feature*, letting you *"anticipate the downstream effects"* before accepting. Both readings are correct and the plan step is what converts one into the other, so the I7 callout now states it that way rather than picking a side. The earlier ⚠️ on [[tut-state-cli]] now names its counter-examples instead of leaving the disagreement open. One transcript defect recorded: the HCP tab says Terraform will create the **`learn-terraform-locals`** workspace, while the `cloud` block above it names `learn-terraform-refresh` — text lifted from the locals tutorial. Also noted, unremarked by the page itself: its provider block is the collection's tidiest `default_tags` example, and its stated floor of **v1.1+** is higher than `-refresh-only` needs, since the flag shipped in **0.15.4**. **I7 stays ⬜.** The State collection is now **10 of 11** captured — only **Console** remains, and *Move resources* is [[tut-move-config]] under Modules.

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tut-resource-targeting]] captured, and `-target` turns out to have been a verdict without mechanics.** The coverage check was the **name-dropped** case in its purest form: [[core-workflow]] carried the clause *"`-target` is an antipattern for exceptional recovery only"*, **E4** referenced the flag only to contrast OpenTofu's `-exclude`, and nothing anywhere said what the option actually does. Three mechanics now sit in **I7**, directly above the `-exclude` callout so the pair reads together. **The direction rule** is the headline and the page states it in one sentence: *"Resource targeting updates resources that the target depends on, but not resources that depend on it."* Upstream only. Measured against one configuration change: untargeted `8 to add / 8 to destroy`, `-target` on the `random_pet` `1/1`, `-target` on the **module** `4/4` — the module plus the pet it depends on, which is the rule making a target a **subgraph selection** rather than a filter. **Dependencies are computed per resource, not per instance** — `-target="aws_s3_object.objects[2]"` planned the replacement of *all four* `random_pet.object_names` instances, because `objects[2] → object_names[2]` is not an edge Terraform has. That is the 2026-07-20 source verification in [Topics → The dependency graph](topics/dependency-graph.md) (`nodeExpandPlannableResource` created per managed resource, expansion decided before instances get nodes) appearing as observable behaviour, and it means instance-level targeting buys **no upstream precision at all** — the opposite of the blast-radius reduction someone reaching for it wants. **And the two warnings are not the same warning:** plan prints `Warning: Resource targeting is in effect`, apply prints `Warning: Applied changes may be incomplete` and names the follow-up (`terraform plan`). Both close with the legitimate-use list, whose final clause is worth knowing — *"or when Terraform specifically suggests to use it as part of an error message"* — the one context where the flag is an instruction rather than an improvisation. Also recorded: targeting leaves state **internally inconsistent rather than corrupt** (the tutorial's `bucket_name` and `bucket_arn` outputs ended up describing different buckets, every value correctly recorded), and the mandatory untargeted follow-up apply cost `7 to add / 7 to destroy` — the partial apply deferred and fragmented the work rather than reducing it. One glossary row added. Three transcript defects recorded rather than reproduced: the prose says *"all five of the `random_pet.object_name` resources"* where there are four, the destroy transcripts use the deprecated `aws_s3_bucket_object` name while the configuration uses `aws_s3_object`, and the randomised bucket names drift inconsistently between steps. **I7 stays ⬜.** With this the State collection is nine-elevenths captured; **Refresh state** and **Console** remain, and *Move resources* is [[tut-move-config]] under Modules.

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tut-state-cli]] captured, and it is the one page that puts all of I7 in a single narrative — but it also contradicts its own collection.** The coverage check was mixed rather than clean: `removed`, `state mv` and `-replace` all had references behind them ([[tf-state-remove]], [[tf-state-refactor]], the B3 `-replace` callout), so this is not a gap-filling capture. What it supplies is **transcripts the reference pages describe but never show**, plus a sequence. Three additions to I7. **The `.` plan symbol** — a `removed` block with `destroy = false` plans as a lone period where `+`/`-`/`~`/`-/+` would go, above *"will no longer be managed by Terraform, but will not be destroyed"*, and the run reports `0 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy` while rewriting state. No legend the plan prints explains that character. It is the exact counterpart to [[tf-block-removed]]'s measured `1 to destroy` for a **bare** block. **`state mv` moves state and leaves the configuration behind** — the cross-file form here is shorter than [[tf-state-refactor]]'s (run from the source directory, only `-state-out` needed), but a successful move leaves the destination one plan away from `# … will be destroyed # (because … is not in configuration)`, fixed only by pasting the `resource` block in by hand. That is [[tut-move-config]]'s address-is-identity rule again, and the reason `moved` does not help: it works only within one state file. **And a ⚠️ on the collection contradicting itself** — this tutorial's final exercise reconciles an out-of-band delete with a bare `terraform refresh`, **no deprecation notice and no `-refresh-only` alternative**, two pages away from [[tut-resource-drift]] calling that command unsafe and [[tf-cmd-refresh]] deprecating it outright. The hazard is live in context, since the exercise's whole point is that `refresh` silently drops a resource from state. Its own closing note is worth keeping regardless: refresh runs automatically inside `plan`, `apply` and `destroy`, all of which *"have the potential to modify your state file"* — an ordinary plan can write state. Two glossary rows added (the `.` symbol, `taint`/`untaint` and why `-replace` supersedes it). [Topics → State](topics/state.md) gains the `mv`-versus-`moved` distinction. **Method note recorded on the course index:** the State collection sidebar was resolved at **rung 3** with `fetch_nav.py`, which confirmed the numbering used for all four captures this session and revealed three uncaptured pages — **Target resources**, **Refresh state** (which presumably treats `-refresh-only` properly) and **Console** — plus the fact that *Move resources* is [[tut-move-config]] filed in two collections. **I7 stays ⬜**; four tutorials captured, no chapter written.

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tut-troubleshooting-workflow]] captured, and it lands in **E5** rather than I7 despite sitting in the State collection.** Only its second half is about state, and the coverage check on that half came out **absent**: E5 cited the `TF_LOG` docs page and [[terraform-env-vars]] for the *variables*, but the path had **no procedure for deciding whether a failure is yours, core's, or a provider's**, and nothing at all on filing. Two callouts now close that. The first is HashiCorp's **four-layer model** — language, state, core, provider, ordered by distance from the user — whose state row (*"refreshing, importing, or replacing"*) is **I7**'s entire subject, so the model quietly explains why so much of this path routes through that topic. The second is the four-step filing procedure, whose useful part is step 3: **the log line prefix is the whole ownership test**, `provider-terraform-<name>` means the provider's repository and everything else means `hashicorp/terraform`, which is what `TF_LOG_CORE`/`TF_LOG_PROVIDER` exist to make mechanical. Also recorded: `TF_LOG_PATH` **appends** rather than truncating, and an empty value (`export TF_LOG_CORE=`) unsets one stream while leaving the other. **E5 step 3 stops asking the reader to build a cycle** — this tutorial ships one, two `aws_security_group` resources each naming the other in an in-line `ingress` block — and the cycle tip gains the fix worth generalizing: **split the association into its own resource**, turning two nodes in a loop into three in a line, which works wherever a provider offers both an in-line and a standalone form (security-group rules, IAM policy attachments, route-table associations). The same pattern is now in [Topics → The dependency graph](topics/dependency-graph.md) beside the existing route-through-a-variable trick. **B4 gains a smaller but load-bearing correction:** `terraform fmt` is a **parser**, not only a formatter — it cannot rewrite what it cannot read, so on a broken configuration it reports what it reformatted and then prints ordinary HCL diagnostics with line numbers, which makes it a cheaper first gate than the style callout implied. The page's own boundary against `validate` is quoted, along with the reason `validate` needs `init` and `fmt` does not. **Three defects in the tutorial recorded rather than reproduced:** its prose says line 46 where its transcripts say 52, its `allow_8080` rule opens port **80** inside a group named "Allow 8080" while `user_data` serves on 8080, and the corrected instance block has an unbalanced brace. Its transcripts are **v1.8.3 / aws v5.56.1, dated 2024-07-02**, so its central `aws_security_group_rule` fix is the pre-`aws_vpc_security_group_ingress_rule` idiom — the graph argument survives, the resource name does not — and the `Error: Cycle` single-line rendering it shows is what **1.16** replaces with one node per line. Two glossary rows added (four-layer model, `TF_LOG_CORE`/`TF_LOG_PROVIDER`). **E5** and **B4** statuses unchanged: B4 stays ✅, E5 stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tut-resource-drift]] captured, completing I7 step 1 — and it corrects the path's thinnest callout.** The `-refresh-only` entry said what the flag does and stopped there. Four things the tutorial's transcripts show that the description does not: the plan speaks in the **past tense** (`# aws_instance.example has been changed`) where a normal plan states intent, which is how you tell the two outputs apart at a glance; the apply **prompts** before writing, which is precisely the review step [[tf-cmd-refresh]]'s deprecated command cannot give you; the counters read `0 added, 0 changed, 0 destroyed` **while the state file changes**, because they count infrastructure actions rather than state writes; and **outputs drift too**, so a computed output over a drifted attribute is rewritten by the same apply and anything reading this state via `terraform_remote_state` inherits drift it never asked for. A second callout now states the three answers to drift as a set — revert with a plain `apply`, adopt via configuration plus import, or keep it with `ignore_changes` ([[tut-resource-lifecycle]], same repo and same `drift_example` tag) — with the ordering rule that makes the adopt path work: import writes state and never touches the object, so the reconciling apply is a separate step, but skipping the import first would have created a **second** security group and orphaned the hand-made one. Two ageing flags recorded rather than repeated as current: the tutorial still drives `terraform import` from the CLI, with its own tip pointing at the 1.5+ block, and its transcripts run **AWS provider v3.26.0** (2021), whose `aws_security_group_rule` idiom the provider no longer recommends — its `aws_security_group` page now prefers `aws_vpc_security_group_ingress_rule` with one CIDR per rule and warns that mixing the two styles causes "rule conflicts, perpetual differences". Unlike the import tutorial this one **costs money**: the drift is manufactured with three `aws ec2` calls against a live EC2 instance, so there is no emulator version. One glossary row added (`-refresh-only` planning mode). [Topics → State](topics/state.md) gains the three-answers framing. I7 stays ⬜ — both tutorials are now captured, but no chapter is written.

> **2026-08-20** — **[[tut-state-import]] captured — the last of **I7**'s cited-but-unwritten tutorials, and it changes what the topic warns about.** I7 already recommended the tutorial by URL and already knew the flag (`-generate-config-out`), so the coverage check came out **name-dropped, not covered**: the path told you the flag existed and nothing about what it produces. What it produces is a draft containing *every* argument the schema has, defaults and nulls included — and in the tutorial's own run that draft's `env = null` (against a schema wanting a set of strings) made the **first plan of an adoption destructive**, `1 to import, 1 to add, 0 to change, 1 to destroy`, with Terraform printing `# Warning: this will destroy the imported resource`. Pruning to `env = []` plus the genuinely-non-default arguments produced the target shape, `1 to import, 0 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy`. Folded into I7 as two callouts. The first covers the generated-draft trap and the harder half of it — **the plan does not tell you which changes are safe**, in the page's words *"Provider documentation may not indicate if a change is safe"* — since `~ update in-place` reads identically for a state-only default and a restart. The second covers adoption **without** an `import` block (a resource identified by a unique tag can just be declared and applied) and the ordering rule that makes it fail: referencing `docker_image.nginx.image_id` before that resource is in state forces replacement, because unknown never compares equal — [[tut-dependencies]]' rule, met again in an adoption. Also recorded: import reports the provider's view of **current state only**, never health or intent, and adoption commits Terraform to the full lifecycle including destroy. Two glossary rows added (configuration-driven import, `-generate-config-out`), and [Topics → State](topics/state.md) gains the way *in* alongside its existing exits — [[tf-state-purpose]]'s one-to-one mapping rule is broken by hand on entry via import and on exit via `removed`, and the two are not symmetric. I7 stays ⬜; the drift half of its step 1 is still uncaptured.

> **2026-08-19** — **I6 refocused on the three backends it should have been about: `local`, `s3`, `gcs`.** The previous pass had widened it to the forges and to HCP, and the effect was that the topic's *hands-on* was a tour of managed platforms while the open-source backends were things to read about. The spine is now four labs, all run end to end before being written down (`labs/chapter15/`), and the managed tutorials have moved to one optional entry near the bottom. **Two reference notes finally exist**: [[tf-backend-s3]], whose page had been sitting in cache since the 2026-08-17 review, and [[tf-backend-gcs]], which nothing had covered. **Reading them side by side is what the topic now teaches**, because the differences are few and each one matters: `gcs` has **no `key`** and addresses state as `<prefix>/<workspace>.tfstate`, so the workspace name is always in the path, where `s3` prefixes only non-default workspaces with the literal **`env:`**; locking is **opt-in on `s3`** (`use_lockfile`, defaulting to `false`, so a valid backend block can be silently unlocked) and **on by default on `gcs`**; and encryption diverges at migration time, since a customer-supplied GCS key cannot be migrated automatically while a KMS one can, but only takes effect on the first write afterwards. **Underneath, both locks are one conditional object write** — `412 PreconditionFailed` on S3, `412 ifGenerationMatch … conditionNotMet` on GCS — which is the generalisation the module is now built to deliver, and both were verified rather than asserted. **The forge material survived by changing what it demonstrates.** Instead of GitLab-managed state, the lab now runs a **GitLab CI pipeline against the `s3` backend**, ending with `app/terraform.tfstate` and `ci/terraform.tfstate` in one bucket: forge holds the code, object store holds the state. GitLab-managed state, the `http` protocol, HCP and Terrakube are all still here, demoted into one "backends that are not object stores" entry. **Emulator note:** the `gcs` lab needs a GCP emulator (floci-gcp, MIT) behind a compose profile — the AWS one has no Google surface, which was checked in its source rather than assumed. **Two traps recorded from failed runs:** `gitlab/gitlab-runner:latest` is a pre-release whose helper image does not exist, so every job fails in `prepare_executor`; and a runner needs an explicit `--clone-url`, because the URL GitLab advertises to a browser is unreachable from a job container. **I6 stays ⬜** — the labs and references are ready, the chapter is not written.

> **2026-08-18** — **I6 was drifting into an HCP Terraform topic, and the fix was structural rather than another callout.** Three of its four hands-on entries were HCP tutorials, its only open-source exercise was a video to watch, and the question a team actually asks — *where does state live when our code is on GitLab, GitHub or Bitbucket?* — was not asked anywhere in the path. **The backend catalogue settles the premise**: `local`, `remote`, `azurerm`, `consul`, `cos`, `gcs`, `http`, `kubernetes`, `oci`, `oss`, `pg`, `s3` (read from the v1.15.x sidebar, rung 1). No GitHub entry, no Bitbucket entry, no GitLab entry either — **GitLab works because it implements the generic `http` protocol**, which is now captured as [[tf-backend-http]] and is the highest-leverage short page in the catalogue: GET/POST/DELETE, `LOCK`/`UNLOCK` with **423 Locked** or **409 Conflict** on a taken lock, and `lock_address` **defaulting to disabled** so a minimal `http` backend is silently unlockless. **[[gitlab-tf-state]] is the substantial new note.** State at `/api/v4/projects/<ID>/terraform/state/<NAME>`, lock at that path plus `/lock` with `POST`/`DELETE` overriding the protocol's verbs, and an **empty** `backend "http" {}` in the configuration. Its access-control story is the honest one: project roles replace IAM — Maintainer to write, Developer to read — but *“any user with the Developer role or higher can download and view Terraform state files”*, and the only in-product mitigation is a custom role excluding **`admin_terraform_state`**, which is **Ultimate-only**. OpenTofu users can encrypt state through GitLab's own CI/CD component; Terraform users on GitLab cannot. It also carries a failure mode no cloud backend has: state is encrypted with a key derived from **`db_key_base`**, so *“the instance must be available to decrypt a state file”* — never keep the state for GitLab's own infrastructure inside it. And it argues the `-backend-config` warning from a **functional** consequence rather than a disclosure one, since the cached values are carried to the apply and *“can lead to problems like being unable to lock the state files in CI jobs.”* **GitHub and Bitbucket got the other half — identity, not storage.** [[gha-oidc]] (fetched for **A9** in August and never written up) and [[bitbucket-pipelines-oidc]], new. The pair contrast usefully: GitHub's `sub` is a readable path with `environment`, `ref` and `job_workflow_ref` beside it, and repositories created after **2026-07-15** use an immutable ID-based subject that breaks a copied trust policy; Bitbucket identifies everything by **UUID**, defaults `oidc` to **true**, and treats step `max-time` as an access control — with a **5-minute buffer** silently added. **Topic changes:** *What it is* now leads with the open-source backends and names the forge question; the hands-on order is emulator-first (`s3` + `use_lockfile`, no cloud account), then GitLab, then the three HCP tutorials demoted to the managed-platform comparison they are; two reference entries added; a forge-comparison table callout added; and the milestone now asks for a defensible answer per forge rather than just a migration. **Three new source courses** — GitLab Docs, GitHub Docs, Atlassian Bitbucket Docs — with the nav rung recorded on each index, including two traps: `fetch_nav.py` resolves GitLab at rung 3 to the **version selector**, and Bitbucket at rung 1 to the **article's own heading list**, so both were mirrored from the sidebar rendered in the page instead. **I6 stays ⬜** — this changed what the chapter must cover, not whether it is written.

> **2026-08-18** — **Terrakube belonged in I6 as well as A9, and the docs page that proves it was uncaptured.** The path already knew Terrakube implements the TFE v2 API — [[terrakube-facts]] lists the endpoints, `state-versions` and workspace lock/unlock among them — but that fact lived only in **A9**, framed as *platform* comparison, so I6 never learned that an Apache-2.0 self-hosted option exists for the `cloud {}` block.
