[[terrakube-migrating]] captured, and the operative detail is one argument: **`hostname`**, mandatory in both `cloud` and `backend "remote"` because both otherwise default to `app.terraform.io` — *“It's crucial to include the `hostname` parameter”*, said twice on the page. **The two migration paths differ in a way worth knowing before choosing one.** From local state it is `terraform state pull > tf.state` then `login`, `init`, **`state push`** — so the lineage and serial guards in [[tf-state-backends]] are what stand between you and an overwrite, not the `init` copy prompt that [[tut-cloud-migrate]] answers. From HCP it is a plain `init -migrate-state`, since both ends speak the same API. **And Terrakube stores nothing itself**: state lands in *“azure, aws, gcp or minio”*, which is what makes a fully local deployment possible and also means its access-control story is your bucket's, not its own. The **Workspaces Importer** carries more than the CLI path does — name, description, Terraform version, execution mode, variables, tags and current state — with a sensitive-variable preview that only the source note records. I6 gains a reference entry and a fourth row in its forge table; the platform comparison stays in **A9** rather than being duplicated. ⚠️ The page is dated ≈**November 2025** and every example `hostname` is the authors' disposable Gitpod URL, so it predates the 2.30/2.32 RBAC work in [[terrakube-facts]]. New source course: **Terrakube Docs**, nav mirrored at **rung 3** from the rendered GitBook sidebar. **I6 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-18** — **[[tut-cloud-migrate]] captured; it was the one reference in **I6** cited by URL with no note behind it.** The tutorial had been fetched during the 2026-08-17 I6 review and promoted to the top of that topic’s hands-on list, but never written up, so the path pointed at a page rather than at anything readable offline. Three details the topic entry did not carry: an existing HCP workspace *“must not have any existing states”* while the workspace itself is created on demand and only the **organization** is a precondition; answering **no** at the `init` migration prompt *“ignore[s] the existing state and just activate[s] the configured HCP Terraform workspace with its existing state, if any”*, which makes it the right answer when the remote side is already authoritative rather than a way out of the prompt; and `terraform login` writes the token *“in plain text”* to `~/.terraform.d/credentials.tfrc.json`. **One conflict with the docs, recorded in the note rather than smoothed over:** the tutorial ends by telling you to `rm terraform.tfstate`, while [[tf-backend-configure]] says to back the file up *before* migrating; the note keeps both and sides with the docs. Its transcripts are also internally inconsistent — random **v3.3.2** on the first `init` and **v3.0.1** on the migration `init`, and the pet id changes between steps — so they are illustrations, not a reproducible run. Cross-linked from the [State](topics/state.md) and [Workspaces](topics/workspaces.md) topic pages; the latter now sends the `cloud`-block reader here for the hands-on version. Nav mirrored from the **State** tutorial collection, where it sits second, between *Import Terraform configuration* and *Manage resources in Terraform state*. **I6 stays ⬜** — a note is not a chapter.

> **2026-08-18** — **[[tut-cloud-state-api]] captured, the second of the two **I6** tutorials that had been fetched but never written up.** It is the only thing in the path that edits a state file on purpose, and the only coverage of the **HCP state-versions API**. Its premise is the argument for remote state that has nothing to do with collaboration: state *“can get corrupted due to partial apply operations or incorrectly running `terraform import` or `terraform taint`”*, and a store that keeps versions is what makes that recoverable. **Three mechanics worth having written down.** The workspace lock is a **precondition of the API upload** rather than hygiene, and it is identity-scoped to the user whose token you are using, which is a stricter thing than the per-command state lock in [[tf-state-locking]]. The document is not served inline — `/workspaces/$ID/current-state-version` returns a **`hosted-state-download-url`** pointing at an `archivist.terraform.io` object. And the POST body sends `serial`, `lineage` and an `md5` as attributes **beside** the base64-encoded state, which is how the server applies the same lineage/serial guards [[tf-state-backends]] documents for `terraform state push`. **The manual `serial` bump is the bridge to work already written:** [[06-state-management]] §6.5.4 gives it as the safe restore habit, and [[ch09-state-fundamentals]] measured what the counter actually tracks, so the tutorial's tidy `0 → 1` should not be read as what a real run looks like. **One error recorded rather than repeated:** the tutorial calls `payload.json` *“an encrypted version of your state”*; it is base64, and `base64 -d` reads every secret back. Version-mixed transcripts too — `required_version = "~> 1.10"` and state written by **1.10.3**, but the apply output prints **v0.15.1** and the API response is dated 2021. Filed eighth in the **State** collection, between *Manage resource lifecycle* and *Refresh state*; cross-linked from the [State](topics/state.md) topic page, whose `serial`/`lineage` bullet now points here for the case where the pair stops being trivia. **I6 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-17** — **I6 reviewed ahead of writing its chapter, and the gap was not depth but shape.** The topic had eight callouts and only **four** entries in *How to learn it* — one tutorial, two reference bundles, one book chapter — so almost everything it knew sat in asides rather than in things to read or run. Six references added, and the list reordered into the path’s own sequence of video, hands-on, depth reading, reference docs. **The most surprising omission is the [S3 backend page](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/backend/s3) itself**, which was cited nowhere in the entire path despite being the backend every callout in this topic assumes and the one the verified lab already exercises. It carries four things the generic backend pages do not: `dynamodb_table` and `dynamodb_endpoint` are **deprecated** and *“will be removed in a future minor version”* while both may be configured alongside `use_lockfile` during migration; a non-default workspace stores state at `<workspace_key_prefix>/<workspace_name>/<key>` with the default prefix being the literal **`env:`**; the IAM permission set is specific enough to be worth copying, needing `s3:DeleteObject` on the **`.tflock` object** but explicitly *not* on the state object *“as Terraform does not delete it”*; and encryption has three separate shapes (`encrypt`, `kms_key_id`, `sse_customer_key`), the last of which the page warns *“will cause it to be persisted to disk in `terraform.tfstate`”* if you set it in a `.tf` file. **TID Ch 6 §6.4 was the other structural gap** — the chapter is cited from **B9** for §6.1–§6.3 and from **I7** for §6.5, and §6.4 *“Storing state”* is squarely this topic (possible backends, the `backend` block, alternative authentication, the `cloud` block, migrating and upgrading backends) yet belonged to no topic at all. TUR Ch 3 had been carrying the book half alone. **Two uncaptured tutorials from the State collection.** *“Migrate state to HCP Terraform”* now leads the hands-on, because it is the only remote-state exercise in the topic that needs **no AWS account** — its example provisions a `random_pet` — and it states a constraint the longer S3 tutorial does not: *“Always use the same version of the Terraform CLI you used to create the resources. Using a newer version of Terraform may update the state file and cause state file corruption.”* *“Version remote state with the HCP Terraform API”* adds the disaster-recovery half nothing else here covered: download the remote state, modify it, upload it as a **new state version through the API**, on the premise that state *“can get corrupted due to partial apply operations or incorrectly running `terraform import` or `terraform taint`.”* **OpenTofu’s [State and Plan Encryption](https://opentofu.org/docs/language/state/encryption/) gets its own entry** rather than the passing mention it had inside the TUR caveat. It is the direct answer to this topic’s own four-properties hardening callout and Terraform has no equivalent: an `encryption {}` block pairing a `key_provider` with a `method`, applied to `state`, `plan` **and `remote_state_data_sources`**, or supplied wholesale through `TF_ENCRYPTION`. Its threat model is worth reading before adopting — encryption is at rest only and *“does not protect against data loss … and it also does not protect against replay attack”*, nor against *“the person running the tofu command”* — as is its deliberately non-seamless migration, where OpenTofu *“will refuse to read plain text data”* by design, and its narrow **+1 minor version** support promise for key providers and methods. **And the topic gains its only video**, TF2026 Lecture 07, which the course coverage map already mapped to I6 without the path ever citing it; it ships with a known defect, since its backend block locks with `dynamodb_table`. **Checked and deliberately not added:** the Krausen 004 lab repo, whose own inventory records **zero** occurrences of `backend`, workspaces, `import` or `lifecycle` — it stops at the Beginner half, so there is no lab resource to cite here rather than a missing citation. `[[ot-early-eval-backend]]`, which existed and was reachable only from **E3**, is now wired into the TUR entry that already mentioned the behaviour. Fetched to `cache/web/tf-backend-s3.txt`, `tut-cloud-migrate.txt`, `tut-cloud-state-api.txt` and `ot-state-encryption.txt`. **I6 stays ⬜** — the chapter is next.

> **2026-08-17** — **Validated Patterns audited end to end, and the first finding is that a third of the collection does not load.** HashiCorp publishes a *Validated Patterns* library — field guides written by its own field engineers, hosted at `developer.hashicorp.com/validated-patterns/`, **not linked from the tutorial library** and cited nowhere in this path until now. The Terraform section advertises **12 tutorials**. **Five of the twelve return HTTP 404**, verified from two independent clients (the headless browser and a plain fetch) and confirmed by navigating to one of them: *Migrate Terraform Enterprise organizations to projects*, *Integrate Terraform with Cortex Cloud by Palo Alto Networks*, *Vulnerability and patch management of infrastructure images with HCP*, *Automate deployments with HCP Terraform and ServiceNow Catalog*, and *Use Terraform to build an Azure landing zone*. **Rechecked the same day, and the sitemaps settle what the 404s mean.** `server-sitemap.xml` — the dynamic one, 1.2 MB — advertises **all 12** of these URLs, while only 7 are actually served, and the static `sitemap-0.xml` lists a different, smaller subset again. A third access path was tried on the recheck: clicking through from the index as an in-app client-side route, rather than a fresh page load, which renders *“We couldn't find the page you're looking for”* — so direct fetch, hard navigation and in-app routing all agree. They are not deliberately retired: the index still says *“12 tutorials”* and still carries 12 links, the content index still advertises all 12, and search engines still hold their text. **A page that is missing from the build but present in the sitemap is a publish regression, not a removal** — which is the difference between waiting for them and replacing them. Recorded so a later pass re-checks them instead of re-discovering them; the Azure landing zone is the one whose absence actually costs this path something, since its AWS twin turned out to be the only landing-zone material anywhere in it. **The seven reachable guides were read verbatim and all seven placed** — none was below the threshold, which is unusual and is explained by what these documents are: they are not tutorials but the process layer around Terraform (who does what, in which order, and what a plan has to look like before you apply), which this path was thin on everywhere. **A10 gains the manual workflow it automates:** *Manage Terraform provider upgrades* names Renovate and Dependabot in its own recommendation, so HashiCorp's guidance and this topic agree, and what it adds is what to do once the bot's pull request exists — one branch per upgrade, first plan shows only the version change, one issue at a time, and apply only when a plan has *"no errors, warnings, or actions"*, a bar stricter than an empty diff because a warning is a deprecation you are about to inherit. **A8 gains the consumer half of I5's module split:** *Upgrade and refactor Terraform modules* works `terraform-aws-modules/s3-bucket` 2.15.0 → 3.0.0, where the module split encryption into its own resource, and the consumer pays with an `import` block naming an address **inside** the module (`module.s3_bucket.aws_s3_bucket_server_side_encryption_configuration.this[0]`). Its acceptance criterion is the keeper — *"Imports are the only acceptable change to see in a speculative plan when upgrading to a newer module version"* — and it is exactly the bill that the `moved` blocks in I5's shim pattern exist to spare consumers, so the two topics now describe the same event from both sides. **A7 gains its only landing-zone material** (platform landing zone versus workload landing zone, and the design principle that puts each zone in its own AWS account *and* its own Terraform workspace, making the account boundary and the state boundary the same boundary), and **A5 gains the Sentinel-versus-SCP split** that the same guide states cleanly: Sentinel evaluates plans and applies, resource-level, pre-deployment, and is bypassed by anything that does not go through Terraform; an SCP evaluates every AWS API call at account level, at execution time, and cannot be bypassed at all — so a control that must hold against a console user was never a Sentinel control. **A6 gains the two Vault guides**, which between them separate four surfaces this path had been treating as one (authenticating Terraform to Vault, dynamic provider credentials, reading/writing secrets, and the Terraform secrets engine running the relationship in reverse), recommend Terraform Cloud agents so *"Vault DNS is not exposed to the public internet"*, and supply the operational trap worth writing down: **align the TTL of Vault's STS credentials with HCP Terraform's JWT lifetime**, or a long apply dies partway on expired credentials. **A1 gains the reverse of its own Ansible callout:** the AAP pattern's `aap_inventory` / `aap_job_template` / `aap_job` resources let Terraform *trigger* Ansible rather than Ansible read Terraform, which reframes this topic's question from whether to shell out to where the handover boundary sits. **I7 gains the migration route off CloudFormation**, whose recommended path is this topic's own `import`, with `aws_cloudformation_stack` as the transitional escape hatch and parallel stacks as the rollback — the only migrating-from-another-tool material in the path, where **B1** compares the two tools without moving anything between them. Resources table gains **VP** with the 404 caveat attached. All seven captures are in `cache/web/vp-*.txt`. No topic status changes and no chapter is affected, so nothing flips to 🔄.

> **2026-08-17** — **I5 audited for ordering and for missing hands-on work. Three of its cross-references pointed at the wrong entry, and the ordinals were the reason.** Reference #2, #3 and #5 each cited *"reference #1"* — for the complexity test, the 80%-of-use-cases rule and the two-deep nesting rule respectively — and all three of those ideas come from [[tut-pattern-module-creation]], which sat ten entries down the list. Reference #1 was TUR Ch 4 + Ch 8. **The rot has a mechanical cause worth naming rather than just fixing:** this list has now been renumbered twice by insertions (composition in, refactoring in), and both times the entries moved while the inline `#N` citations did not. Every cross-reference in and into I5 now names the page — *"the recommended-pattern guide above"*, *"I5's Standard Module Structure reference"* — so the next insertion cannot silently misattribute a rule again. Inbound citations in **I8**, **A4** and **A8** were rewritten the same way. **The list was also reordered to match the path's own stated pedagogy** (hands-on → depth reading → reference docs), which it had inverted: six reference pages sat ahead of both tutorials. New shape — build a module, refactor its interface into objects, test it, then read the scoping guide and TUR, then the six **Develop modules** pages in the docs' sidebar order, then the no-code exception, then your own module. **Two tutorials added, neither of them new to the site, both cross-cited rather than duplicated.** *"Write Terraform tests"* is **A2**'s, and belongs here as the third hands-on step because TUR Ch 8's rule that every example gets a test is unexecutable without it, and because a test is the second caller a module ever has. *"Create and use no-code modules"* is **E6**'s, and earns an author-side entry because it is the only page that documents where I5's own rules stop: a no-code module **declares its own `provider` blocks**, and the page states the reason the prohibition existed — *"Since users will not reference no-code modules in written configuration, there is no risk of this conflict"* — so the ban was never about providers but about `count`/`for_each`/`depends_on` on a `module` block. It also *tightens* the standard structure (*"define all resources in the root repository of the directory"*), and it is the only page in the collection kept current, using ephemeral resources (1.10) and write-only arguments (1.11) where eight of the other nine still pin 2022 providers. **Checked for genuinely uncaptured tutorials and found none in this collection:** the live *Reuse Configuration with Modules* collection still lists exactly **10** tutorials and all ten are captured and placed (I4 ×2, I5 ×4, A4 ×2, A7 ×1, A8 ×1), so I5's hands-on surface is now exhausted. **One uncovered surface found off to the side, recorded rather than folded in:** HashiCorp publishes **12 "Validated Patterns" for Terraform** under `developer.hashicorp.com/validated-patterns/terraform`, and **this path cites none of them**. The module-relevant one, *"Upgrade and refactor Terraform modules"* (23 min), is consumer-side — monitor for a new version, bump it, keep the speculative plan free of actions, `import` where the module split resources — so it belongs to **A8**/**I4**, not here; the rest map to A6, A7, A9 and E4. Fetched to `cache/web/vp-terraform.txt` and `cache/web/vp-upgrade-refactor-modules.txt` for a later pass. **I5 stays ⬜** — no chapter exists yet, so nothing flips to 🔄.

> **2026-08-17** — **I5 gains "Refactor modules" as reference #7, closing the `modules/develop` directory.** The page was already captured ([[tf-modules-refactoring]]) and cited from **A8**, but only there — and A8 reads it as an operator refactoring a live codebase. I5 cited four of the section's five child pages (develop, structure, publish, composition, providers) and skipped the fifth, even though **its own reference index names it**: [[tf-modules-develop]] describes refactoring blocks as recording *"how resource names and module structure have changed from previous module versions"*, which is an authoring concern by construction. **The author-side slice now written into I5:** scoping is what makes refactoring an author's privilege, since *"a module may only make `moved` statements about its own objects and objects of its child modules"* and addresses resolve relative to the module the block sits in, so internals can be re-shaped across a version bump and consumers get the migration in their next plan; chaining (`a→b`, `b→c`) makes the blocks the **upgrade path** rather than a changelog, each covering consumers arriving from a different starting version, and removal is safe only for a *private* module once every consumer has applied; and the **module-split shim** is the section an author reads twice, because the original module is kept as a shim carrying the `moved` blocks so existing users upgrade in place. HashiCorp flags the shim as violating the closed-box rule and bounds it — *"all three of these modules are maintained by the same people and distributed together in a single module package"* — so it licenses your own package and never someone else's. **The pairing that motivates the placement:** `moved` re-shapes structure and `deprecated` (TF 1.15, already an I5 callout) retires interface surface, and together they are the whole toolkit for evolving a published module without breaking consumers overnight — which is I5's subject, not A8's. A8 reference #2 and the I5 deprecation callout now cross-reference each other so the split is explicit rather than a silent duplication. Renumbering: old I5 references 7–10 shift down one place to 8–11, and reference #3's README contradiction now names the recommended-pattern guide rather than a number, since two-digit reference markers trip the bare-issue-ref check. No topic status changes — **I5 stays ⬜**, **A8 stays ⬜**, and no written chapter is affected, so nothing flips to 🔄.

> **2026-08-16** — **TID Ch 12 "Terraform providers" captured ([12-terraform-providers](books/tid/chapters/12-terraform-providers.md)) — the last chapter, so TID is now read end to end: 12/12.** It is the only chapter that leaves HCL entirely, building a Mastodon provider in Go with the Terraform Plugin Framework: design the user-facing HCL first (§12.1), `dev_overrides` for the local loop (§12.2), schemas/diagnostics/`tflog` (§12.3), the provider entry point (§12.4), a data source (§12.5), a resource with full CRUD (§12.6), a provider-defined function (§12.7), and signing/publishing (§12.8). Feeds **E1**, which had four references and no book note behind them. **Two printed listings do not run, and both are correct in the author's published repository — so they are print-only defects.** Listing 12.3 puts `Default: stringdefault.StaticString("mastodon.social")` on a **provider** schema attribute under the comment "schemas can have default values"; `provider/schema.StringAttribute` at framework **v1.19.0** has exactly `CustomType`, `Required`, `Optional`, `Sensitive`, `Description`, `MarkdownDescription`, `DeprecationMessage`, `Validators` — **no `Default`, no `PlanModifiers`**, because a provider block has no plan or state to hold one in. The real Listing 12.7 four pages later is clean, and [`terraform-provider-mastodon`](https://github.com/TerraformInDepth/terraform-provider-mastodon)'s `provider.go` sets no defaults anywhere. The corollary is the useful part: a provider option's "default" is the `os.Getenv` fallback in `Configure`. Listing 12.12 then defines the data source's five output attributes with `Optional: false, Required: false` and no `Computed`, which the framework rejects at runtime — *"Attribute missing Required, Optional, or Computed definition. This is always a problem with the provider and should be reported to the provider developer."* The published `account_data_source.go` sets `Computed: true` on all five. **Two smaller slips and a wrong make target:** the prose calls the post's text parameter `status` while every listing calls it `content`, the file to copy is printed as `port_resource.go`, and §12.5.6 says `make acctest` where the scaffolding template's `GNUmakefile` has `testacc` (and no `acctest`) — §12.3.3 gets the same target right. **The chapter's biggest gap is not an error but time: the provider interface has grown four methods since it was written.** Verified on pkg.go.dev against **v1.19.0** (2026-03-10): `Functions` is not part of `provider.Provider` at all but of `ProviderWithFunctions`, and the same opt-in pattern has since added `ProviderWithEphemeralResources` (TF 1.10), `ProviderWithListResources` and `ProviderWithActions` (both TF **1.14.0**, 19 Nov 2025, per the local checkout's changelog), and an experimental `ProviderWithStateStores`; the resource side gained write-only attributes (1.11) and managed resource identity (1.12). **Three OpenTofu divergences, all settled against `repos/opentofu` at `v1.12.5` rather than the web.** (1) The chapter says to edit `~/.terraformrc` for `dev_overrides`; OpenTofu reads **`.tofurc`** and falls back to `.terraformrc` only when `.tofurc` does not exist (`config_unix.go` + `util.go`), so creating a `.tofurc` for anything else silently disables overrides left in the other file. (2) List resources and actions exist **only as generated protobuf and mocks** in the OpenTofu tree — no core support, no `tofu query`, no `action` block — so a provider exposing them degrades to its ordinary resources there. (3) Provider functions are OpenTofu **1.7.0** ([opentofu#1439](https://github.com/opentofu/opentofu/pull/1439), which its 1.7.0 changelog credits) against Terraform's 1.8, confirming the correction Ch10 already recorded, and a second PR in the same release ([opentofu#1491](https://github.com/opentofu/opentofu/pull/1491)) added `internal/addrs/provider_function.go` with a `ProviderAlias` field, so OpenTofu also registers **alias-qualified** calls `provider::<name>::<alias>::<fn>` — Terraform has no equivalent, since `internal/lang/functions.go` at `v1.15.8` builds exactly one name per function and anything else hits its "Unknown provider function" diagnostic. **Two claims corrected in the path itself.** E1's `dev_overrides` callout said "`terraform init` is skipped entirely"; HashiCorp's CLI-config docs say init *"will still attempt to select a suitable published version of your provider to install and record in the dependency lock file"* while other commands disregard the lock entry — which is why the chapter tells you to run `init` and ignore the failure, and why `direct {}` is mandatory rather than decorative. And the registry story is now split per engine: Terraform takes signing keys through *User Settings → Signing Keys* on registry.terraform.io and accepts **RSA or DSA but not ECC**, while OpenTofu accepts providers and keys **only** through the GitHub issue forms on [`opentofu/registry`](https://github.com/opentofu/registry), whose README rejects pull requests outright because the validation automation parses the form fields. **Two things worth stealing from the chapter regardless of whether you ever write a provider.** First, *normalise every value the server rewrites, in all three of create/read/update* — Mastodon wraps post content in HTML, and missing the `bluemonday` pass in even one of the three functions produces permanent state drift; this is the provider-side origin of the "always detected changes" pathology Ch5 describes from the user's end. Second, *collect diagnostics instead of returning early*, and never `log.Fatal` in a plugin: the process dies without returning errors and can corrupt state. Also captured: the scaffolding template's `go:generate` directives now live in **`tools/tools.go`** (one of them runs `copywrite` against the `.copywrite.hcl` the chapter tells you to delete) and its headers read `Copyright IBM Corp. 2021, 2025`; `resource.Test` runs the real binary and is gated on `TF_ACC=1`, while `resource.UnitTest` is what functions use; and terraform-plugin-testing **v1.16.0** offers a typed `statecheck`/`knownvalue` assertion API beside the chapter's string-based `TestCheckResourceAttr`. Topic backlog gains **provider-development** (single-source — TUR has no provider-authoring content at all, so the second source has to be the Plugin Framework docs). Glossary gains eight rows, resources gains a provider-development section. **TID reading log: 12/12 done.**

> **2026-08-16** — **Verification pass on Ch 11 found its opening premise expired, which is a bigger correction than any of its listing bugs.** §11.1 states there is no Terraform SDK and that teams "need to wrap the CLI to do so". Two things say otherwise. **`hashicorp/terraform-exec`** is an official Go module *"for constructing and running Terraform CLI commands"* with structured returns from `terraform-json`, and its README positions itself exactly where the chapter says nothing exists: importing it *"is preferable to importing `github.com/hashicorp/terraform/command`, because the latter is not intended for use outside Terraform Core"*. It is still a CLI wrapper, so the architectural point survives — but a Go team should not hand-roll §11.1. **And `terraform rpcapi` is a real non-CLI surface**, registered in `commands.go` under a comment that answers the chapter directly: its *"whole point … is to bypass the CLI layer so wrapping automation can get as-direct-as-possible access to Terraform Core functionality"*, bypassing the `command` package entirely. Version-gated with `git tag --contains`: out of experimental as of **v1.13.0**. Two caveats recorded rather than glossed — it sits in `HiddenCommands` (plumbing, not advertised) and came out of the Stacks work, so it is an integrator's channel and not a stability promise. **The divergence is the practical part: OpenTofu has no `rpcapi` at all** — zero occurrences in the 1.12.5 tree, no package, no registration — so a wrapper is still the only door there, and CLI-wrapping remains the right choice for anything that must serve both engines. Both added to **E5**'s existing `rpcapi` row and to the glossary. Three smaller items from the same pass: **`check` blocks work fine in JSON** (`assert` takes the list form) even though the chapter's block list omits them *and* its Listing 11.33 drops the checks its own prose says it includes; `show` genuinely cannot read stdin (`show -json -` → "Failed to read the given file as a state or plan file"), so only the *extension* half of that claim was wrong; and the `version` event carries a **`ui` version field** of its own, `1.3` on 1.15.8 against the book's `1.2`, which is the field a parser should watch for format changes.

> **2026-08-16** — **TID Ch 11 "Alternative interfaces" captured ([11-alternative-interfaces](books/tid/chapters/11-alternative-interfaces.md)) — Terraform without HCL, and the chapter with the shortest shelf life in the book.** Its subject is the three escape routes: wrap the CLI from another language (§11.1, three quarters of the chapter, built as a real Python library), write configuration as JSON so a program can generate it (§11.2), and write it in a general-purpose language and synthesise (§11.3). Feeds **A3** (the machine-readable UI is what a pipeline parses), **B4** (`.tf.json`), **E5** (integrating the engine into your own tooling) and **E1** (its closing pointer is Ch12). **The headline is that §11.3's subject is dead: CDKTF was sunset and archived on 10 December 2025**, per both HashiCorp's docs ("deprecated as of December 10, 2025 … no longer supports or maintains") and the repository README ("no further updates, fixes, or improvements (including compatibility updates)"). The chapter's own §11.3.1 argued against adopting it, so the recommendation aged better than the subject — and its argument generalises: *"most of learning Terraform is not about the language but about the concepts around IaC"*, which is why switching to TypeScript buys less than teams expect. **The chapter also does not contain two things its introduction promises** — it says it covers "the CDKTF and Pulumi projects" and "we end the chapter with Terragrunt", and neither Pulumi nor Terragrunt appears anywhere in it; Terragrunt is uncovered by the book entirely, which is worth knowing before citing TID for **E4**. **Six defects found by running the listings**, three of which are in the published `tofupy` library rather than just in print. The worst is that **`_run_stream` does not stream**: it calls `subprocess.run`, which blocks until the child exits, so the event handlers whose stated purpose is live progress all fire after the command is over — measured with a one-line-per-second stand-in, `subprocess.run` returned at t+3.07s and all three events yielded at t+3.07s. Real streaming needs `Popen`. Second, **`ChangeContainer.__post_init__` assigns `address`, `previous_address` and `module_address` all from `data["address"]`** (verified in the shipped `schema.py`), so `previous_address` — the field that tells you a resource was `moved` — is silently wrong for every change, in the class most likely to back a policy gate. Third, **Listing 11.18 declares `sensitive_values` and never assigns it**, so typed-in code raises `AttributeError` on the one field that says which attributes are secret; the library has the line. Then three print-level slips: Listing 11.10's sample `validate` output does not match either engine (both say `Missing required provider`, and the book's own tests four listings later assert exactly that), Listing 11.38 pluralises the top-level key and fails with `No argument or block type is named "resources"`, and Listing 11.35 transposes a paren. **Two of the chapter's factual claims measured, one right and one wrong.** Right: `show -json` really is lossy against `state pull` — no `serial`, no `lineage`, and also no **`check_results`**, which the chapter does not mention and which matters because its own test module contains a `check` block. Wrong: `show` does **not** require a `.tfstate` extension; a state file copied to a name with no extension is read fine by both engines. Two smaller facts worth keeping: an apply that also plans emits **two `change_summary` events** (`operation: "plan"` then `"apply"`), so a last-write-wins parser discards the planned counts, and **`resource.terraform_data.main.output` is valid** — the explicit managed-resource prefix, counterpart to `data.`, applied and resolved on 1.15.8. Topic backlog gains **wrapping-and-machine-readable-ui** and **json-configuration**; A3 and B4 gain callouts. TID reading log: **11/12 done**, Ch12 (providers) remains.

> **2026-08-16** — **Renovate promoted out of a callout into its own Advanced topic, A10, because the question it answers is "what runs on GitLab and Bitbucket" and A3's aside could not carry that.** Everything is from the Renovate docs, fetched rather than remembered, since no book here covers the tool: TID Ch 7 §7.7.5 is Dependabot with one passing sentence on Renovate, and TUR predates both. **The extraction surface came out wider than the old callout claimed.** The Terraform manager matches `**/*.tf` and `**/*.tofu` and emits `module`, `provider`, `required_provider`, `required_version`, `helm_release` and `tfe_workspace` alongside about twenty Kubernetes kinds and the `docker_*` ones, each disableable by `depType` — and the sources are asymmetric, with **modules** resolvable from `GitTags`/`GithubTags`/registry but **providers from a registry only**. **Three findings are the reason the topic exists.** The OpenTofu default is stated in the docs as an admitted limitation, *"Renovate can not know if you want to use the Terraform or OpenTofu registry"*, so an unqualified provider on an OpenTofu project resolves against `registry.terraform.io` — which lands directly on [[opentofu-migration-mechanics]]'s finding that the two registries do not ship byte-for-byte identical builds, making it a supply-chain mistake rather than a cosmetic one. **The lock file is computed, not delegated**: Renovate *"performs lock file maintenance itself, without calling the underlying package manager"*, so no engine binary is needed in the runner image, but `lockFileMaintenance` defaults to **`false`**, meaning a default install bumps constraints and leaves the recorded checksums behind. And the failure mode of computing hashes yourself is silent: [discussion #30984](https://github.com/renovatebot/renovate/discussions/30984) is `aws` 5.64.0 producing no regenerated lock and no error, self-healing at 5.65.0. **The platform gaps are per-forge and worth checking before promising a workflow:** GitLab has not implemented `automergeStrategy` at all and needs `gitAuthor` to match the bot where push rules verify committers; Bitbucket Cloud has no assignees, no rebase merge strategy and no collapsible markdown, which is what Renovate's PR bodies use; Bitbucket Data Center adds no labels and no config-error issues. The **Dependency Dashboard** needs "issues with dynamic Markdown checkboxes", so it is not a Bitbucket feature. **The GitLab setup needs no marketplace app** — the [Renovate Runner](https://gitlab.com/renovate-bot/renovate-runner) templates run it from a scheduled pipeline (`3 * * * *` recommended) with `RENOVATE_TOKEN`, `RENOVATE_GITHUB_COM_TOKEN` and `RENOVATE_EXTRA_FLAGS`, which is the concrete answer to the question that started this. **One thing carried over unchanged:** neither bot moves a SHA-pinned Git module source, so **I4**'s manual cadence survives automation; the Terragrunt manager says so outright, limiting itself to registry sources and GitHub sources with SemVer refs. Also recorded: `.terraform-version` and `.terragrunt-version` are **separate managers**, so a tenv-pinned engine stays frozen unless they are switched on. Advanced re-estimated at ~76 hrs. No chapter flips to 🔄 — A3 is still ⬜, so nothing written has gone stale.

> **2026-08-16** — **TID Ch 10 "Advanced Terraform topics" captured ([10-advanced-topics](books/tid/chapters/10-advanced-topics.md)), which is the chapter A1 has been citing without a note behind it.** It is the book's grab-bag — naming schemes, a dynamically subnetted network module, provisioners, the `external` and `local` providers, checks and conditions, `.tofu` files, and when Terraform is the wrong tool — so it feeds **A1** (which it largely owns), **A2**'s condition half, **E3**, **I3**/**I4** and **B7**. **Two of its version claims did not survive checking, both against the local checkouts rather than the web.** Its §10.5.1 NOTE dates provider-defined functions to "Terraform and OpenTofu 1.8.0"; OpenTofu shipped them in **1.7.0** ([opentofu#1439](https://github.com/opentofu/opentofu/pull/1439)), so the engines were not level and OpenTofu was ahead. And the `winrm` connection type its §10.3.1 documents without qualification is **deprecated as of OpenTofu 1.12** ([opentofu#3899](https://github.com/opentofu/opentofu/issues/3899)) — it warns on every use, the stated reason being that the Go WinRM libraries are unmaintained, and the changelog says it is expected to error in **1.13**, which `git tag --contains` confirms is still unreleased. Terraform has made no matching move, so this is a divergence that breaks Windows provisioning on an OpenTofu upgrade only. **Three gaps between the chapter and the docs are folded into A1 rather than left in the note.** The chapter's provisioner argument list omits `environment`, `interpreter`, `working_dir` and `quiet` on both `local-exec` and `remote-exec`, and `environment` is the difference between passing a secret and printing it. The `external` data source's failure contract — exit zero, or write to **stderr** and exit non-zero with stdout ignored — is in the provider docs and not in the chapter, as is the fact that **Terraform Enterprise guarantees no language runtimes** beyond shell utilities, which rules the whole technique out there. And `direxists` **errors** on a file path rather than returning `false`, and does not expand `~`. **The framing worth keeping from §10.6 is blocking versus reporting**: `precondition`/`postcondition` stop the run, `check` only warns, and that is what makes a scoped data source doing a live HTTP health check safe on every plan — provided it carries `depends_on`, or it runs on the first plan before the infrastructure exists and reports a failure that means nothing. A2 gains that as its own item, and the four-mechanism ordering table (variable `validation` → `precondition` → `postcondition` → `check`) is in the chapter note. **One existing claim reinforced from a second direction:** the destroy-time provisioner in §10.3.4 has two ways to silently not run — deleting the resource block, which is the `prevent_destroy` trap, and `create_before_destroy = true`, which suppresses it outright ([[tf-meta-lifecycle]]) and can arrive by propagation from a dependent resource. **A verification pass over the finished note then found three more errors, all behavioural rather than version-shaped, which is the harder class to spot.** The largest: **`self` is bound for postconditions only**, settled in `internal/terraform/eval_conditions.go`, which sets a self reference for `addrs.ResourcePostcondition` and nothing else under the comment *"Only resource postconditions can refer to self"* — OpenTofu's `internal/tofu/eval_conditions.go` carries the identical line, so it is not a divergence, and it follows from a precondition running before the object exists. The chapter presents `self` under the joint precondition/postcondition heading; every example it prints is nonetheless correct. Second: an omitted `query` on the `external` data source sends an empty JSON **object**, not the "empty JSON array" the chapter states — the provider marshals a `map[string]string`, so `{}` is the only possible output. Third: `content_base64sha256` on `local_file` is *"Base64 encoded SHA256 checksum of file content"*, the `base64sha256()` output that AWS's `source_code_hash` wants, not the chapter's "hash of the base64 content", which would be a different value. **A fourth pass found two more defects in the same network module, so §10.2 is now marked as a design sketch rather than runnable code.** Its `count` is `local.subnet_count` — `pow(2, subnet_bits)`, the number of networks the CIDR was divided into — where it should be `var.availability_zones`. Those agree at 1, 2 and 4 and diverge at **3**, which is the value the chapter itself calls the standard for high availability: evaluated on 1.15.8, `availability_zones = 3` builds **four** locations, a fourth NAT gateway included, and then reports the CIDR the fourth is using in `spare_subnet_cidr_blocks`, so one range is both allocated and advertised as free. Second: **`aws_internet_gateway` appears nowhere in the chapter.** The submodules build only a private route table and the §10.2.3 NOTE defers the gateway to "the high-level module that we'll be creating in the next section", which creates a VPC, a data source, two module calls and outputs — and no gateway, and no route-table association for the public subnet. So the public subnet inherits the VPC's local-only main route table, the NAT gateway in it cannot egress, and the private subnets behind that NAT have no internet either. That also settles the annotation on `map_public_ip_on_launch` reading "This is the field that makes this a public subnet": AWS's VPC guide says *"The subnet type is determined by how you configure routing"* and a public subnet is one with *"a direct route to an internet gateway"* — auto-assigned public IPs are a separate setting, so a subnet full of them and no gateway route is not public, just expensive. Two smaller items from the same pass: the `file` provisioner's **trailing slash on `source`** decides whether the directory itself or its contents land in the destination (`internal/communicator/ssh/communicator.go`, `UploadDir`), which the chapter's example gets right without saying why; and its "about a week" for `aws_secretsmanager_secret` name reuse is the floor — `recovery_window_in_days` is 7–30, **default 30**, the same defect [Ch9](books/tid/chapters/09-testing-refactoring.md) already records. **A third pass ran the chapter's centrepiece listing instead of reading it, and it does not work.** Listing 10.8's two topology toggles are **inverted** — `two_tier_subnets` is created when `enable_isolated_subnet` is *true* while the `local` a page later selects `three_tier_subnets` on the same condition — so the module either errors or lies. Reproduced with stub submodules on **Terraform 1.15.8** and **OpenTofu 1.12.5**: `true` gives `This object does not have an attribute named "isolated_subnet_id"`, and `false` gives **no error at all** and every output as `[]`, with subnets created and nothing returned. Swapping the two `count` expressions fixes both branches, so the pattern is sound and only the direction is wrong. The generalisable half is worth more than the bug: `module.x` with `count = 0` is an **empty tuple**, so a ternary between a populated module list and an empty one takes its element type from the populated branch — selecting the empty branch therefore yields a well-typed `[]` rather than a type error, which is exactly why the silent failure is silent, and why an integration test asserting non-empty outputs catches what `terraform validate` cannot. Also measured while checking a smaller claim: a provisioner on `terraform_data` **can** use `self` (`self.id`, `self.output` both resolve); what it lacks is an address, so the chapter's "no self values we can use here" is loose rather than wrong. **And one of its examples is miswired:** Listing 10.40 passes `data.cloudinit_config.node.rendered` to `aws_instance.user_data`, but `cloudinit_config` defaults `gzip` and `base64_encode` to `true` while the AWS provider says *"Do not pass gzip-compressed data via this argument; see `user_data_base64` instead"* — so the listing needs either the other argument or both defaults turned off. **One book-adjacent claim corrected in the note itself:** §10.7.1's `.tofu` treatment is accurate, but `.tofurc` is not part of that feature — it is the renamed `.terraformrc` and dates to the **1.6** fork baseline, as E3 already records. Topic backlog gains **provisioners-and-escape-hatches**, **checks-and-conditions** and **naming-and-domains**; **opentofu-divergence** is now promotable, since TID's OpenTofu sections are captured. Topic pages [Modules](topics/modules.md) and [Meta-arguments and `lifecycle`](topics/meta-arguments-lifecycle.md) extended in place — the first with §10.2's swappable-interface lesson, the second with the condition rules. TID reading log: **10/12 done**, Ch11 and Ch12 remain.

> **2026-08-15** — **The Terraform checkout has already given up its graph internals, its plan mechanics and its config schemas, so this pass took the surface nobody audits: environment variables.** Swept `os.Getenv`/`os.LookupEnv` across `internal/` and the root package, dropped the acceptance-test harness variables, and checked each survivor against the CLI environment-variables page. That page documents **14**; the binary reads at least **eight more** that are on no page at all, plus two (`TF_LOG_CORE`, `TF_LOG_PROVIDER`) documented on the debugging page instead and `TF_TOKEN_<host>` on the credentials page. Captured as [[terraform-env-vars]], with the sweep command recorded so it can be re-run on a later release. **The one with immediate practical value is `TF_DISABLE_PLUGIN_TLS`.** Terraform secures each provider's local gRPC channel with auto-generated **mutual TLS**, and any non-empty value turns that off — which makes it the way to confirm that a local traffic interceptor, not the provider, is behind `Failed to load plugin schemas` on every provider. The source is blunt that it exists for the plugin SDK test framework and **"is not intended to be set by end-users"**, so it belongs in a diagnosis, not in a shell profile; with it set the plugin channel is unauthenticated on loopback. Folded into **E5** alongside `TF_LOG`. **`TF_ENABLE_PLUGGABLE_STATE_STORAGE` closes a loop from the 2026-08-13 schema sweep**, which found a `state_store` block gated behind `AllowExperimentalFeatures` since 1.13 and announced nowhere: this is how you switch it on, introduced in `f494ff5540` and first tagged **v1.14.0**, and initialising a backend under it prints warnings that describe the feature's maturity more candidly than any published page. Recorded on [[release-feature-map]] beside the original finding. **Two more worth knowing.** `TF_WARN_OUTPUT_ERRORS` lives in a file whose only contents are a comment reading "feature flags for the next release" and downgrades **output interpolation errors to warnings**, logging at `[ERROR]` and recording the output as unknown — a pre-0.12-era compatibility shim, and deliberately *not* applied to the newer sensitive-output checks, which the source excludes by name. And **`TF_SKIP_CREATE_DEFAULT_WORKSPACE`**, new in **v1.15.0** with a matching `-create-default-workspace` flag on `init`, has inverted precedence on purpose: the variable beats an explicit `-create-default-workspace=true`, because a `true` on the command line "is indistinguishable from the default value being used". That is the first sign of Terraform treating the `default` workspace as optional, which is **A7**'s territory. Also recorded as method: variables read by **prefix scan** (`TF_VAR_`, `TF_CLI_ARGS_<name>`, `TF_TOKEN_<host>`) do not show up in the sweep and must be searched for as prefixes, and **`TF_IGNORE` is not read anywhere in the repository** — it belongs to the config-upload library, not the CLI. **A3** and **E5** both gain the pointer. No status changes.

> **2026-08-15** — **OpenTofu is the best-covered repo here, so the useful angle was the one thing the changelog-derived pages could not reach: the migration mechanism.** The feature maps and the schema sweep already answer *what OpenTofu added*; nothing answered *what it does to your project when you switch*. Captured as [[opentofu-migration-mechanics]], with three findings that appear in none of the migration documentation. **State provider addresses are translated in memory rather than rewritten on disk** — `internal/tofumigrate` deep-copies the state on every load, at `tofu init`, at every local plan/apply, and at `state show` — and, crucially, **the translation is skipped for any provider the configuration pins in full form**. `random = {}` becomes `registry.opentofu.org/hashicorp/random`; `source = "registry.terraform.io/hashicorp/random"` stays put, by design. That is the supported lever for running the OpenTofu CLI against HashiCorp-built providers, and it is stated only in a package comment. There is also an **off switch documented nowhere**: `OPENTOFU_STATEFILE_PROVIDER_ADDRESS_TRANSLATION=0`, present since **v1.6.0** (commit `9c789368dc`, [opentofu#773](https://github.com/opentofu/opentofu/pull/773)) and absent from every `.md` in the repository. **The lock-file half is the one with a real consequence.** Since **v1.10.0**, `UpgradeFromPredecessorProject` mirrors `registry.terraform.io/hashicorp/*` entries into OpenTofu-registry ones — only that namespace, because the source says no equivalence can be assumed elsewhere — preserving version selections and **discarding every hash**, since the lock "can be used to select the same version number but not to guarantee that the provider package matches what was previously installed". The warning the user sees states the underlying fact plainly: *"the OpenTofu project's provider releases are not byte-for-byte identical."* Same provider source, different builds — which is the answer to the question people actually ask about the fork's providers, and it means the switch is a **re-recording** of provider checksums rather than continuity, worth a deliberate review for anyone treating the lock as a supply-chain control (**I4**, **A6**). A migration performed on 1.6–1.9 had no lock mirroring at all. **One existing claim sharpened:** two pages described the 1.8 `.tofu` extension as "parsed alongside `.tf`". True of the directory, misleading for a pair — the parser "will ignore the `.tf` file and only use the `.tofu` file", so `main.tofu` must be a **complete replacement** for `main.tf`, not a patch. Corrected in E3 and [[opentofu-feature-history]]. The same shadowing covers `.tf.json`/`.tofu.json` and the test extensions, which surfaced something the notes had never recorded: OpenTofu's test framework accepts **`.tofutest.hcl`** and `.tofutest.json`. **E3 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-15** — **Reading `hashicorp/hcl` overturned an assumption the notes had already reasoned from: the two tools do not build against the same HCL.** Checked in the two `go.mod` files rather than assumed — **Terraform pins upstream `github.com/hashicorp/hcl/v2 v2.24.0`**, while **OpenTofu requires `v2.20.1` and `replace`s it with its own fork**, `github.com/opentofu/hcl/v2 v2.20.2-0.20251021132045-587d123c2828`, a pseudo-version from a commit dated 2025-10-21. Different code, from bases four minor versions apart. [[conditional-branch-evaluation]] had written "it holds for OpenTofu identically — both consume the same `hcl/v2`", which is a false premise; **the conclusion survives** because it rests on `hclsyntax/spec.md`, which both implement, but the reasoning did not, and it is corrected in place with the general rule: **spec-level claims transfer between the tools, implementation-level ones need checking per tool.** Captured as [[hcl-library-facts]], with the upstream delta enumerated from the CHANGELOG and gated with `git tag --contains` — mark propagation through conditional and `for` expressions (**v2.23.0**, the machinery deciding whether `sensitive` survives them) and rejection of invalid nested splats (**v2.24.0**) are the two that would be visible in configuration terms. Recorded with an explicit limit: OpenTofu's fork carries ~19 months of its own commits and may have back-ported any of them, so the table is **an upper bound on divergence, not a measurement** — the fork is not cloned locally. **Spec facts captured for the book, quoted rather than paraphrased:** identifiers are `UAX #31` plus a dash that the spec itself calls unidiomatic; numeric literals are decimal only, with no hex or octal syntax in the language; **escape sequences are defined only for *quoted* templates**, which independently confirms Ch 7's existing claim that a backslash in a heredoc is literal; `<<-` strips "the minimum number of leading spaces", not all indentation, and only from lines starting with a literal string; and strip markers work at **syntax level**, so `${"hello" ~}${" world"}` still produces `"hello world"`. **One gap found in a written chapter, additive rather than contradictory, so nothing flips to 🔄:** Ch 7 teaches `[*]` correctly but never covers the **attribute-only `.*.` form**, whose trailing index applies to the result list where `[*]`'s applies inside each element, nor the splat rules that have no `for` equivalent — a non-collection auto-wraps (`any_number.*` is `[any_number]`) and a **null scalar splats to an empty tuple**, which is the idiomatic zero-or-one conversion. Logged for the next revision of that chapter. **Also worth knowing for future lookups:** `dynamic` blocks and `try`/`can` live in `ext/`, so they are HCL *extensions* rather than language features, which is why the specs never mention them — and HCL defines no functions of its own, the host application supplies the table, which is how Terraform's and OpenTofu's function lists can differ without either breaking the spec. **B4** gains the specs as a reference for when the docs paraphrase; **E3** gains the fork finding. No status changes.

> **2026-08-15** — **KL is cited in nine places, so this pass was a citation audit, and two citations pointed at content the repository does not contain.** Both were checked by grepping the whole checkout rather than reading lab titles. **I3 cited Lab 06 as a "dynamic-blocks section": there are zero `dynamic "…"` blocks in the entire repo.** The lab's title, "Making Code Dynamic and Reusable", uses *dynamic* in the plain-English sense — its steps are variables, data sources and string interpolation, and its own wrap-up says "to see how dynamic our code is now, update the value of the `environment` variable". **I5 cited the same lab as a "reusable-module section": there are zero `module "…"` blocks anywhere, and the word *module* does not appear in that lab at all.** "Reusable" there means parameterized. Both slots now carry a ⚠️ saying plainly that KL does not cover the topic, which is more useful than a silently wrong link. Inventory and evidence in [[krausen-labs-facts]]. **A third, smaller mismatch:** lab 03's *directory* is `lab_03_working_with_variables_and_dependencies` while the lab is titled "Working with Variables and **Outputs**" and teaches variables, `tfvars`, outputs and precedence — dependencies are lab 04's subject. Both link labels relabelled, since the directory name is what the path had copied. **What KL actually is:** eight labs, each written three times for **AWS, Azure and GitHub**, with 01–05 cumulative and 06–08 standalone; 01–05 are instructions only, 06–08 ship starter `.tf`. Lab 08 is the pick of them — it keeps the `count` version of the resources beside the `for_each` version so the address difference is visible rather than asserted, which is exactly **I1**'s lesson. **Coverage measured by grep, and it stops early:** `count` appears in 25 files, `terraform state` in 4, `for_each` in 3, `depends_on` in 2 — while `backend "…"`, `terraform workspace`, `import {}`, `lifecycle`, `provisioner`, `terraform test`, `taint` and `terraform graph` appear in **none**. So KL is a strong companion for B1–B8 and I1 and nothing past it, which matters because the 004 exam does test backends and state management. **Two README claims that do not hold:** it promises a pre-configured dev container and tells you to create a Codespace "from this repo", but there is **no `.devcontainer`** — the only hidden directory is `.github` holding `FUNDING.yml`, and the Codespaces badge points at a *separate* `btkrausen/terraform-codespaces` repository; and its "Terraform 1.12.2 — Tested" badge sits beside a CI badge pointing at `terraform-testing`, which installs Latest, validates a **different** set of lab directories, and per [[krausen-lab-ci-facts]] has a plan gate that cannot fail. The B3 lab entry now carries the Codespaces caveat. No status changes.

> **2026-08-15** — **`btkrausen/terraform-testing` turned out not to be a testing-library repo at all, and is more useful than if it had been: it is a GitHub Actions harness, and it is a ready-made A3 case study — good pattern and cautionary tale in one file.** Captured as [[krausen-lab-ci-facts]]. Three workflows run a matrix over 16 lab directories per provider, on push, on PR, and on a **weekly cron** — and that cron is the transferable idea, since its purpose is not to test the author's code but to detect the day *someone else's* release breaks it. The pattern half is worth copying wholesale and is now step 4 of A3: **OIDC rather than static keys**, the third-party action **pinned by commit SHA**, an explicit least-privilege `permissions:` block, `TF_IN_AUTOMATION` plus `TF_CLI_ARGS: -no-color`, `terraform_wrapper: false` so step outputs are real exit codes, **`init -backend=false`** so validation never touches real state, and `fail-fast: false` with per-leg `mktemp -d` isolation. **The cautionary half is a matched pair of exit-code bugs in the same repository, failing in opposite directions**, which is why they earned a callout rather than a footnote. The workflow runs `terraform plan -detailed-exitcode || true` and then `echo $? > plan_exit_code.txt` — `$?` is the status of `true`, so the file always holds `0`, the checking step always reports success, and **the plan gate cannot fail**; a lab whose plan errors keeps the badge green. Present in all four jobs across all three workflows. The repo's standalone script captures the code correctly with `PLAN_EXIT_CODE=$?` but opens with `set -e`, under which the value `2` — "plan succeeded, changes present", the normal outcome — aborts the script before that line, so **the branch written to translate 2 into success is unreachable** and the gate cannot pass. `-detailed-exitcode` is genuinely awkward to consume: the value meaning "fine" is the value shell error handling treats as failure. This is the same lesson the path already draws about `terraform fmt -check`, with better evidence. **Two more, both silent:** the failure-notification job is gated on `needs.test-labs.result` while the jobs are named `test-tfb-labs` and `test-hol-labs`, so it never runs and the README's promise of an auto-filed issue does not happen — a skipped job looks like a passing one in a run summary, so alerting needs one deliberate failure to prove it — and the README says each module is run through `fmt`, which appears nowhere in the workflows. **Scope note recorded so the labs are not mis-trusted:** **KL** (`terraform-associate-labs`) is a different repository from the `modules/tfb/**` labs this harness checks, so nothing here changes KL's standing; what it changes is what the green badge supports — `init` and `validate` failures are caught for real, `plan` failures are not caught at all. Also noted: the AWS workflow's two jobs use two different credential models, OIDC in one and static access keys in the other, which is a partial migration frozen in place. **A3 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-15** — **The last unmined local repo is a course, not a tool, so the question it answers is different: what does citing TF2026 actually buy?** Read all thirteen lecture directories of `devopsforge2304/Terraform-Full-Course-2026` (`4f1928e`, 2026-03-25) and mapped each to a topic in [[tf2026-course-facts]]. The path cited three timestamps and never mentioned the companion repo at all; the resources table now carries it. **The finding that earned a callout is a direct conflict with A7.** Lecture 09 teaches environment management as CLI workspaces, with `lookup(var.instance_type, terraform.workspace)` over a `{ dev, staging, prod }` map — production separated from development by one string inside one backend, one credential set and one directory, which is exactly what A7's reference #2 exists to contradict. A7 already carries a ⚠️ for an *official tutorial* doing the same thing, so this is the second source teaching it; the warning now names both. The demo also has a real bug worth quoting, since it is the kind a learner hits before they can even see the lesson: a two-argument `lookup` **errors on a missing key**, and the map has no `default` entry, so the configuration fails in the `default` workspace — the one you are in before running `terraform workspace new`. **Three more aged idioms, each checked against current docs rather than assumed.** The backend lecture locks with `dynamodb_table`, which HashiCorp's S3 backend reference now deprecates in favour of `use_lockfile`, saying DynamoDB locking "will be removed in a future minor version". The debugging lecture recommends `terraform refresh`, deprecated since **v0.15.4** in favour of `-refresh-only` on `plan`/`apply`. And its import story is the CLI `terraform import` only — the declarative **`import` block** (1.5+) and `moved`/`removed` appear nowhere in the repository. **The gap that decides placement:** the README's section 10 is called "Terraform Security, Testing and Validation" and its testing table is `fmt`, `validate`, `tflint`, `checkov` — there is **no `terraform test`, no `.tftest.hcl`, no `precondition`/`postcondition`/`check` anywhere**, so the course cannot serve **A2** and the path is right not to cite it there. Its secrets material is sound, and matches **A6** on the point that matters: `sensitive = true` hides a value from CLI output while it stays in state, so the feature "alone is not sufficient for production security". **Also recorded:** the configurations are not runnable as written, since they hardcode one account's AMI, subnet, security-group and key-pair IDs plus globally unique bucket names, and the repo contains **no OpenTofu material at all** — the strings `tofu` and `opentofu` return zero hits. Nothing in it reaches the Advanced or Expert half. Verdict kept in the note so the course is not re-evaluated topic by topic: cite it for B1–B8 and the mechanical parts of Intermediate, not for testing, environment isolation, or state surgery. One unrelated tidy while in the file: **E5's reference list had two items numbered 5**. No status changes.

> **2026-08-15** — **Floci was the one local repo already written up, so this pass was a re-verification, and it moved three claims.** The checkout is a **fork**: `origin` is a personal one and **`upstream` is `floci-io/floci`**, with the working tree parked on the `1.6.0` release commit (2026-08-06) while `upstream/main` runs to 2026-08-15 with **no tag since** — so released and unreleased behaviour needed labelling separately, and [[floci-facts]] now says which is which. **The claim that changed shape:** "repeat `CreateBucket` in `us-east-1` is idempotent here, AWS is not" is narrower than recorded. `S3Service.createBucket` *does* raise `BucketAlreadyOwnedByYou` (409), guarded by a region check on both sides, so the plain repeat-create case matches AWS deliberately. What still diverges is the **creation-tags** case, where the controller re-runs `putBucketTagging` on the existing bucket — a repeat create silently *rewrites* the tags instead of failing. The second open item, missing `InvalidTag` validation, is unchanged: the string appears nowhere in the source. One item found on the good side — an explicit `<LocationConstraint>us-east-1</LocationConstraint>` is rejected with `InvalidLocationConstraint`, which is AWS's behaviour. **The authentication caveat is now confirmed in code and is starting to change.** `S3RequestAuthorizationParser` parses a signed request only far enough to pull the **access key ID** out of the `Authorization` header, so identity yes, signature no — which is exactly what the empirical test found. But a **per-service** `services.s3.enforce-auth` switch exists now, separate from the global `FLOCI_AUTH_VALIDATE_SIGNATURES`, and commit `3fe836aa` (2026-08-15) added genuine SigV4 verification **for pre-signed URLs** under it, with a test asserting a tampered signature is rejected. Unreleased, pre-signed only, and the flag itself is undocumented — so the undocumented-flag pattern moved rather than went away, while `FLOCI_SERVICES_IAM_ENFORCEMENT_ENABLED`, which the note had called undocumented, **is documented now**. **The find that changes how much to trust the labs:** Floci ships `compatibility-tests/` with `compat-terraform`, `compat-opentofu` and `compat-cdk` beside five SDK suites, running real clients against a live instance with no mocks — and the Terraform case drives an **S3 remote backend with a DynamoDB lock table**, so **I6**'s backend work is a path upstream tests. With one trap recorded: their `backend.hcl` uses `endpoint`, `dynamodb_endpoint`, `force_path_style` and `dynamodb_table`, all of which HashiCorp's current S3 backend reference marks deprecated in favour of an `endpoints {}` block, `use_path_style` and `use_lockfile` (DynamoDB locking "will be removed in a future minor version"), so a lab must not copy it verbatim. The **B2** emulator callout gains the compatibility-suite and IAM-enforcement facts, and its bucket-tag example is now past tense with the 1.6.0 fix named. Also recorded: `FLOCI_SERVICES_IAM_SEED_DEPLOYER_PRINCIPAL` for a real lab principal instead of the god-mode `test` key, and `FLOCI_PROTOCOLS_REJECT_UNKNOWN_SERVICE_SCOPE`, which is why an unimplemented service returns a clean error rather than a **misleading `NoSuchBucket`**. No status changes.

> **2026-08-15** — **Reading Terrakube's source rather than its docs overturned the A9 claim that mattered most: its permissions are no longer organization-wide.** The old callout said toggles apply across a whole organization with no project tier, so a tenant had to be an entire Terrakube organization — true of the pre-2.30 model, and wrong now. **RBAC v2** landed in **2.30.0** (2026-02-26, commit `5cfc9638`) with HCP-shaped roles (`admin`, `write`, `plan`, `read`, `custom`), and **project-level team permissions** in **2.32.0** (2026-05-17, commit `e99e40c9`), both gated by `git tag --contains`. Permissions now hang off three entities — `Team`, `ProjectAccess`, `Access` — so **a tenant can be a project**, which removes the main structural objection A9 raised against it. The verdict paragraph, which called the open-source permission surface uniformly coarse, now names Terrakube as the exception and keeps the charge against Atlantis. Captured as [[terrakube-facts]]. **Why the source was necessary:** unlike the other two checkouts, Terrakube's docs are a separate site, and the model is stated loosely there and precisely in the entity classes, which is a consequence of the API being built on **Elide** — authorization lives in annotations on the fields, so `Team.java` *is* the permission spec. Two behaviours only readable there: under a predefined role the fine-grained booleans are **ignored**, and a null or blank role normalizes to `custom`, which is what silently preserves pre-2.30 rows. The roles are also not a ladder — `write` plans and applies, but registry, VCS and template management are `admin`-only. **Three capabilities were missing from the path entirely.** `RemoteTfeController` implements the TFE v2 API at `/remote/tfe/v2/`, including workspace lock/unlock, state and configuration versions, run apply/discard, and `remote-state-consumers` — so the **`cloud {}` block works against a self-hosted Terrakube** and cross-workspace state reads are a modelled relationship rather than a bucket IAM condition, which was the specific gap A9 left open for the pure-CI design. There is an **HCP Terraform importer** carrying workspaces, variables, variable sets, tags and state versions, with a preview step for sensitive ones. And **federated credentials** (2.31.0) invert the dynamic-credentials pattern: a CI job authenticates *to* the platform with a workload-identity token matched on issuer, audience and claims, list-valued claims matching any element, which is exactly a GitHub Actions token's shape. **The run-isolation finding deserved its own callout**, since it is the constraint the whole topic turns on: the ephemeral executor runs **each job as its own Kubernetes Job**, and `EPHEMERAL_CONFIG_POD_ANNOTATIONS` annotates the generated pod so Vault Agent Injector or GKE Workload Identity can give that single run its own credentials. Recorded with its limit — the tenant's code still runs inside that pod, so plan-time code execution still reaches whatever identity the pod holds. Status unchanged: **A9** stays ⬜ — the topic gained accuracy, not completion.

> **2026-08-15** — **Terratest was three docs links and one wrong version fact; it is now source-derived, and the fact was wrong in a way that mattered.** [[terraform-testing]] recorded "Terratest latest published 2026-05-11," which is really **v1.0.0**, the first stable release — v1.0.1 followed 2026-06-27 and the v2 betas on 2026-07-20 and 2026-08-10. Corrected there and captured properly as [[terratest-facts]]. **A wrinkle in the checkout worth recording as method:** the working tree is a detached HEAD at v1.0.1-era code, thirty commits behind `origin/main`, and `main` is where the **v2 restructure** lives — so `ls modules/` locally contradicts the current package list. Everything v2 here was read with `git show origin/main:<path>`, which needs no checkout change and leaves the user's tree alone. **v2 is a multi-module split, not a rewrite:** sixteen independently versioned Go modules under `/v2` paths, so a Terraform-only test stops pulling in every cloud SDK; `random`/`retry`/`logger`/`shell`/`files` consolidate into `modules/core`; `http-helper`, `test-structure` and `dns-helper` lose their hyphens; and six packages — `collections`, `environment`, `git`, `version-checker`, `slack`, `oci` — were **deleted outright** in one commit, so those imports do not port by editing a path. The `go.work` file's own comment explains the release discipline, since tags are cut with `GOWORK=off` and a script enforces it. **The teachable API fact came from diffing tags, not from docs:** `modules/terraform/apply.go` at `v1.0.1` carries both `Apply` and `ApplyContext` with `// Deprecated:` on the bare form, and on `origin/main` only the `*Context` variants survive — so the four-variant convention (`Foo` / `FooE` / `FooContext` / `FooContextE`) is worth teaching as the thing that makes the whole API readable, and writing `*Context` calls today *is* the v2 migration. **Three hygiene rules got their own callout** because each one silently breaks a suite: `defer` the destroy or a failed assertion leaks paid resources; `-count=1` or Go's test cache returns a stale pass when only `.tf` files changed; `-timeout 30m` or Go kills the process at ten minutes and the deferred destroy never runs. Added `random.UniqueID()` namespacing and `ApplyAndIdempotentContext` to the A2 milestone. **One tie-in to the previous entry:** Terratest ships a full `terragrunt` helper module — `run --all`, `stack generate`, `stack run`, `stack output`, `dag graph` — so **E4**'s stack layout is testable end to end, noted there. Status unchanged: **A2** stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-15** — **Terragrunt was a name-drop in four places and a link to a docs root; it is now a source-derived reference, mined from the local checkout rather than the web.** The clone at `repos\terragrunt` (commit `54c43a44c`, tags to **v1.1.3**) ships its own documentation site under `docs/`, which is what renders at docs.terragrunt.com — so the checkout gives the docs, a per-release changelog as one file per change, and the code to settle anything the docs leave vague, all version-gated by tag. Captured as [[terragrunt-facts]]. **The framing correction is the biggest finding:** the path introduced Terragrunt as the DRY tool, and Terragrunt's own terminology page says that focus "has shifted to offering more tooling for *orchestrating* infrastructure," warning you "might miss the forest for the trees" by evaluating it on DRY alone. E4 and A7 now present it as a run orchestrator — discovery, run queue, runner pool, cross-unit DAG — with the vocabulary the docs use precisely, including the adage that **your current working directory is your blast radius**. **Three capabilities had no representation at all in the path:** `--filter`, a query language selecting units by name, path glob, config attribute, graph traversal or **Git diff**, which supersedes the whole `--queue-*-dir` flag pile and is the honest answer to "apply only what my PR changed" (dry-run it on `find`, reuse it verbatim on `run --all`); **explicit stacks**, a `terragrunt.stack.hcl` blueprint generating units into a gitignored `.terragrunt-stack/`; and the **content-addressable store**, on by default since 1.1.0, where each getter resolves through a cheap probe (`git ls-remote`, an HTTP `ETag`, `HeadObject`, an OCI manifest digest) and hard-links deduplicated blobs, which is why large estates stay fast for reasons beyond parallelism. **The CLI migration is worse than the `run-all` → `run --all` rename the path recorded:** the per-command `*-all` forms were removed outright rather than deprecated, env vars are `TG_`-prefixed, and several flag renames are not mechanical — `--terragrunt-debug` is `--inputs-debug`, and `--terragrunt-fail-on-state-bucket-creation` has no successor because backend provisioning became its own `backend bootstrap` command. **A naming collision now has a warning in E2:** a Terraform Stack (`.tfstack.hcl`, HCP-run) and a Terragrunt stack (`terragrunt.stack.hcl`, open CLI) share a word and nothing else. Two in-repo doc pages are themselves stale — one still calls Terragrunt pre-1.0, another still calls `terragrunt.stack.hcl` unshipped — so the note says to read the reference pages, not the narrative ones. Status unchanged: **E4** stays ⬜; this is reading material for it, not the topic done.

> **2026-08-15** — **The open-source half went from an architecture sketch to a buildable blueprint, and it answers the "is this doable" question with a yes.** The previous pass named where each control relocates but stopped before the mechanism, and "use CI OIDC" is not an architecture until the claim is named. Grounded it on GitHub's OIDC docs: the subject is `repo:octo-org/octo-repo:environment:prod`, conditioned in the cloud trust policy exactly as HCP's `organization:…:project:…:workspace:…` is, with the same wildcard failure mode, and the token is "only valid for a single job, and then automatically expires", which is what makes the shared-runner risk survivable. **Two timely findings.** Repositories "created after July 15, 2026 use an immutable default subject format that includes owner and repository IDs" rather than names, not available on GitHub Enterprise Server — that is a direct fix for the reuse hazard this topic flagged in offboarding, since an ID never repeats and a stale trust policy cannot be reactivated by recreating a same-named thing; trust policies written against the old format need re-checking. And **repository custom properties can be surfaced as OIDC claims**, enabling ABAC "driven by repository metadata rather than hard-coded allow lists", which is the open-source counterpart to HCP's project segment and the thing that makes fifty tenants tractable. Also wrote the state recipe (prefix per tenant, IAM condition, per-tenant KMS key, OpenTofu encryption on top), and the pipeline-ownership mechanism (CODEOWNERS on the workflow path, or a platform-owned reusable workflow), which is the structural equivalent of `Manage policies` being organization-level. **The finding worth carrying: open source is strictly stronger on modules.** HCP's private registry has no per-team read scope, so tenants who must not see each other's modules need separate organizations; with git sources, module visibility *is* repository visibility, so the capability HCP charges an organization split for is the VCS's default. Verdict recorded in the topic: achievable, stronger on module visibility and state encryption, harder on run isolation, coarse permission surfaces, and the absence of a single console answering "who can reach this tenant".

> **2026-08-15** — **A9 was an HCP topic wearing a general title; it now covers the open-source stack too.** The old treatment was a single callout saying OpenTofu has no user model and naming three products. Replaced with real coverage, and the framing that makes it teachable is that **the four controls relocate rather than disappear**: who may start a run becomes VCS branch protection, what the run may touch becomes CI OIDC federation, policy becomes a pipeline step the tenant cannot edit, and state access becomes backend IAM on bucket, prefix and key. Two properties transfer badly and are called out — there is no equivalent of Remote State Sharing, so cross-workspace reads must be an object-prefix IAM condition, and the audit trail becomes the CI's plus the cloud's, usually better than HCP's 14 days but requiring manual correlation. **The load-bearing finding came from Atlantis's own security page**, and it governs every self-hosted design: `apply` approval is not a boundary, because *"it is possible to run malicious code in a `terraform plan` using the external data source or by specifying a malicious provider"*, which "could then exfiltrate your credentials". A single runner holding every tenant's credentials therefore has no boundary regardless of approval rules, so the rule is one runner per trust domain with per-run OIDC credentials.
