Atlantis details recorded: authorization runs **twice** (repo, then per project after pre-workflow hooks, since affected projects are unknown at the first check), `--gh-team-allowlist` maps VCS teams to commands with the documented trap that omitting `policy_check` makes manual plans skip policy while autoplans still run it, and the **external command** in server-side config is the real per-tenant hook. **Terrakube** (Apache-2.0) is the closest workalike but scopes differently: teams are named after **Dex** groups so membership federates by construction, while permissions are **organization-wide booleans**, so a tenant is an organization rather than a project — though its separate **Manage State** toggle is better-grained than HCP, where all four fixed workspace roles read full state. **Digger** (MIT) sidesteps the runner problem by executing in the user's existing CI. **A rename worth catching:** `terrateamio/terrateam` now redirects to `stategraph/stategraph` (MPL-2.0, OCaml) — Terrateam's engine was open-sourced and the product is folding into Stategraph, which stores state as a graph in PostgreSQL; **A4**'s TID §8.7 citation now carries that warning, since the book's six-way comparison predates the move. Closed with **OpenTofu state encryption as the one tenant control HCP lacks**, per-tenant KMS keys making a bucket-wide read useless, alongside its documented limit that encryption "cannot protect the sensitive values in the state file from the person running the `tofu` command" — it defends a tenant against the operator and other tenants, never against itself. Plus a choosing-between-them callout, since the real decision is where the boundary lives rather than which vendor.

> **2026-08-15** — **The three "owned by another topic" areas now carry their multi-tenant halves, in both places.** A9 had deferred self-service, policy authoring and Stacks to E6, A5 and E2, which was right about ownership and wrong about completeness, since each behaves differently than its own topic implies once tenants exist. Each now has a multi-tenant note where it lives, plus a summarising callout in A9. **E6** turned out to hold the most useful finding: no-code provisioning is the one platform capability a tenant can run for itself, because it needs `Manage all projects`, `Manage all workspaces` **or project Admin**, and custom project permissions need only create-workspaces, write-variables and apply-runs. The mechanics fit the boundary exactly — provisioning picks a project, "teams with access to the specified project can view the workspace automatically", and "if the specified project contains any project-scoped variable sets, HCP Terraform automatically applies those sets", which is A9's per-tenant credential path arriving with no manual step. Two cautions recorded: **auto apply is the default** for no-code workspaces, and no-code readiness is a registry property, so the registry's missing read scope means every tenant can provision every no-code module. **A5** splits cleanly in two: policy *sets* scope to projects, workspaces or tags, but *authoring* needs organization-level `Manage policies`, which "implicitly gives permission to read runs on all workspaces", so no tenant can write its own policy without org-wide run visibility; the workable division is tenants choosing sets and the platform team owning the code. **E2** was the pleasant surprise — Stacks close the hole A9 spent a correction on, since the `store` block binds a variable set by name or ID and "variable precedence does not affect Stacks", so the workspace-variable credential override is impossible there. The cost is availability rather than confidentiality: removing a referenced variable set makes the Stack fail to deploy.

> **2026-08-15** — **Third coverage check on A9: onboarding was complete, the lifecycle was not.** Every control in the topic described standing a tenant up, and nothing described taking one down, which is where the sharpest docs sentence in this whole area lives: **"Terraform does not automatically destroy managed infrastructure when you delete a workspace."** Delete workspace and state, and the tenant's resources keep running unmanaged. The ordering is forced from below, since workspace deletion is guarded by default to unlocked workspaces managing no infrastructure (organization owners can **force delete** past it, or delegate that), while "you can only delete projects that do not contain Stacks or workspaces". Wrote the full teardown, because each step maps to something the topic had introduced separately and each is a leak if skipped: revoke team tokens, delete the team before a directory assertion repopulates it, unscope variable sets, policy sets and agent pools (recalling that unscoping a pool does not detach active users), remove the project from other workspaces' remote-state-sharing lists, and delete the cloud role, since **no HCP action touches a trust policy** and an orphaned one naming a deleted project goes live again if that project name is reused. Two edition facts folded in: ephemeral workspaces with project-inherited auto-destroy (Standard/Premium, and their runs skip apply approval), and **data retention policies being Terraform Enterprise only**, so "how long does a departed tenant's state persist" has a different answer per product. Second gap closed: an explicit **organization-versus-project decision rule**, built from the three things the topic found to be organization-wide and indivisible — registry visibility, the cost-estimation toggle, and the org permissions that widen read as a side effect — with the trade recorded that separate organizations lose cross-org registry sharing on HCP, which only Terraform Enterprise offers. Third: the **platform workspace** that holds the `tfe` provider token is the highest-privilege object in the estate and now says so, since the milestone asks for the access model as code and that workspace's state can rewrite every tenant's permissions.

> **2026-08-15** — **A9's five stated omissions closed, so the topic no longer defers anything.** The previous pass listed SSO mapping, audit trails, token attribution, non-AWS credentials and chargeback as deliberate exclusions; all five are now written from verbatim docs, and the exclusions callout is gone. **SAML mapping** turns out to be the one with teeth: membership is authoritative and re-evaluated at every login, "this automatic mapping overrides any manually set team memberships", so a directory edit moves a person between tenants and a manual UI fix survives only until their next sign-in. Two failure modes recorded, one silent and one fail-open — a team name not matching exactly is ignored with no team created, and a **missing attribute** drops users into a default `sso` team while "not removing them from any existing teams", so a broken assertion preserves stale access instead of revoking it. Also the permanent owner bypass: non-owners must use SSO, owners keep username-and-password access by design, hence the two break-glass accounts. **Audit trails** carry three constraints that shape any design: 14-day retention, Standard/Premium only and **not available in Terraform Enterprise**, and an endpoint that "cannot be accessed with a user token or team token", requiring an organization or audit-trail token, which is why the exporter is structurally a platform-team job. **Team tokens** trade attribution for durability, and the docs state the worst case plainly, that an owners-team token "will have all of the same permissions that an organization owner would" — one string crossing every boundary in the topic. **The per-cloud comparison** was the most useful find: the `sub` claim is identical everywhere but the matching primitive is not. AWS conditions on `StringEquals`/`StringLike`, GCP uses a workload identity pool attribute condition with `assertion.sub.startsWith(...)` so a project prefix is natural, and **Azure cannot wildcard at all** — the federated credential's subject "is a direct string match", requiring "two federated identity credentials … for each workspace", one per run phase. So Azure is safe by construction and heavy to automate, while AWS is flexible and one wildcard from being wrong. **Chargeback** closes on a constraint rather than a feature: cost estimation is a single organization-wide toggle with no per-project granularity, and gating on it forces the policy set onto **Legacy** policy execution mode capped at Sentinel 0.40.x, which is the strongest argument in the topic for doing cost governance with Infracost in the pipeline instead.

> **2026-08-15** — **Coverage audit of A9 found two controls missing entirely.** Asked whether the topic was complete, checked instead of answering, and it was not. **Remote State Sharing** was absent, which is a real hole under the state-boundary heading, because team permissions answer which *people* may read a state and this setting answers which *workspaces* may read it during a run. Three modes (organization, project, named list), defaulting to no sharing, with the project mode being the tenant-shaped one. It also carries a documented bypass worth knowing before trusting an audit: "Remote state access controls do not apply when using the `tfe_outputs` data source", so that path is governed by token permissions alone. Local execution mode moves the check to a user token as well. And a reviewer with scoped permissions cannot see the full list of workspaces that can reach a state, so the audit itself has a visibility floor. Second gap: **agent pools**, the compute half of the boundary, which scope to workspaces, projects or Stacks and then vanish from other workspaces' lists. Two catches recorded: `Manage agent pools` "implicitly grants access to read all workspaces and projects", making it the second org-level permission after `Manage policy overrides` that widens read access as a side effect; and scoping is not revocation, since it "does not remove it from workspaces, projects, or Stacks that are actively using it". Also added an explicit statement of what the topic stops short of — SSO/SAML team mapping, audit trails, team-token attribution, the non-AWS credential mechanics, and chargeback — so the boundary of the topic is stated rather than implied.

> **2026-08-15** — **Review of the A9 extension caught a claimed control that does not exist.** The extension asserted that putting per-tenant credential variables in a project-owned variable set stops a tenant repointing them, because project-owned sets need project Write or above. Checked against the variables pages, and it is wrong twice. **"Workspace-specific variables always overwrite variables from variable sets that have the same key"**, and the docs use credential swapping as the motivating example, so workspace **Write** alone is enough to repoint `TFC_AWS_RUN_ROLE_ARN`; the `OVERWRITTEN` badge is a notification, not a control. The actual control is marking the set **priority**, which inverts precedence so the set beats more specific scopes. The topic now says so, and says the thing that survives: repointing gains nothing anyway because the minted token still carries the attacker's own project in its `sub` claim, so **the trust policy is the control that holds and the variable placement is hygiene**. A design leaning on variable placement is one workspace variable from failing. Also found a **documentation conflict** worth recording rather than resolving: the project permission table marks `Manage variable sets` Admin-only, while the variables page grants project-owned variable set management to Write, Maintain, Admin, a `Manage variable sets` permission, or organization `Manage all projects`. Flagged in the topic with advice to verify per organization. Third fix: the registry callout said every organization registry permission is a publish permission, which overstated it, since two of the five govern attaching project tags and reserved keys; the point stands that none of them is a read scope.

> **2026-08-15** — **A9 extended from the permission tour into the four controls that actually make a tenant boundary.** The first draft covered HCP's access model well and gestured at everything else with cross-links. Four areas are now written properly, each from verbatim docs. **Per-tenant credentials:** the OIDC workload identity token's `sub` claim is `organization:ORG:project:PROJECT:workspace:WORKSPACE:run_phase:PHASE`, so the tenant boundary is expressible as a `StringEquals` condition in the AWS trust policy, which is the one place a tenant cannot edit its way around — the role ARN need not be secret. The hazard is the docs' own convenience variant, which relaxes to `StringLike` with `run_phase:*`; the same wildcard one segment left silently widens the role to the whole organization. HashiCorp's stated floor is checking audience and organization; the path records that as the floor and the *project* as the tenant boundary. Also folded in: split `TFC_AWS_PLAN_ROLE_ARN` / `TFC_AWS_APPLY_ROLE_ARN` roles as the credential-side analogue of the workspace Plan role, and **project-owned variable sets** as the delivery mechanism, since they need project Write or above, so a tenant cannot repoint its own credentials. **Cross-account:** the AWS provider's `assume_role` block, its `policy`/`policy_arns` session restrictions, and role chaining by repeating the block. Went to the AWS confused-deputy page rather than asserting from memory, and it corrected the draft: `external_id` is not "a secret the platform knows" — AWS's rule is that the value "must be unique among Example Corp's customers and controlled by Example Corp, not its customers", so a platform that lets tenants pick their own external ID has the field and none of the protection. **State as the boundary:** the four state levels read as three consumers rather than a ladder, with `Read outputs only` as the cross-tenant level that `terraform_remote_state` needs, and a CLI-versus-API asymmetry worth knowing, since the docs require read-and-write for the `terraform state` subcommands while plain Read already returns complete state files through the API. **Governance:** policy sets scope to projects, workspaces or tags (tags beta) and support exclusions, but the private registry has **no per-team read scope at all** — private modules are "only available to members of that organization", every registry permission in the org table is a publish permission, so tenants that must not see each other's modules need separate organizations, and consumption is controllable only through Sentinel. Two override traps recorded: organization **Manage policy overrides** "also gives teams read access to all workspaces in the organization" (use Delegate instead), and a policy set's own override switch beats even `Hard mandatory`. Closes with blast radius across tenants being an **identity** property where A7's is a **graph and state** property, and the four questions the four controls answer.

> **2026-08-15** — **New topic: A9 — Multitenancy, teams & RBAC.** The path covered environment isolation (**A7**), policy (**A5**), secrets (**A6**) and self-service (**E6**), but nothing about serving mutually distrusting *people* from one platform. A7 even quoted the docs line that workspaces "are not appropriate for … deployments requiring separate credentials and access controls" and then dropped the second half of it; A9 is that half, and A7 now points at it. Built from the four HCP permission pages plus the Teams overview, fetched verbatim with `fetch_page.py` rather than summarized, which was the right call: **the finding that anchors the topic only exists in the role table.** `Read state` is ✅ for **all four** fixed workspace roles, Read included, so the least-privileged preset still exposes every secret the configuration touched, and only a **custom role** can narrow it to `Read outputs only`. Project roles repeat the pattern. Four more facts folded in as callouts: permissions are **additive with the most permissive winning**, so an organization-level grant silently overrides a scoped workspace role while the reverse does not hold; the boundary leaks through **VCS merge rights, API bots and run tasks** (10-minute tokens), with HashiCorp's own line that "an integrated system is able to delegate any level of access that it has been granted"; **Sentinel mocks** are a state read by another name, downloadable at Write and "may contain unredacted sensitive information"; and **secret teams** are invisible to workspace and project admins who are not organization owners, so an access audit can be incomplete by design. Book coverage checked before citing: neither book covers HCP RBAC, so A9 cites **TUR Ch 10 §"A Workflow for Deploying Infrastructure Code" (pp. 390–411)** for the pre-HCP form (live repo vs modules repo, and the five mitigations for a CI server that must hold admin credentials), extracting the chapter to `pdf-cache` to verify rather than trusting the TOC, and carries an explicit no-book-covers-this callout. TID Ch 10 "Advanced Terraform topics" was checked and rejected — it is naming, networking, provisioners, external/local providers and checks, with nothing on access control. OpenTofu divergence recorded as an absence: the CLI has no user model, so the RBAC plane is Terrakube (Apache-2.0, verified via the GitHub API), Atlantis, or one of the commercial platforms TID §8.7 compares. Advanced re-estimated **60 → 65 hrs**; checkpoint gains a tenant-isolation bullet.

> **2026-08-15** — **Phase 4 review pass. Five things had drifted, and the worst of them was a number this path invented confidence about.** The path claimed the Associate 004 exam is "~57 multiple-choice". **HashiCorp publishes no question count** — the official exam-details table gives assessment type, format, duration (1 hr), price, language and expiry, and nothing else. The 57 comes from **ExamPro's free practice exam**, which circulates widely enough to be mistaken for the real thing, and the freeCodeCamp course this path cites is the ExamPro course, which is presumably how it got in. Replaced with a warning naming the provenance. **The other corrections:** OpenTofu stable is **1.12.5** (2026-07-21), not 1.12.4; the `Sources consulted` line still said Terraform's current patch was 1.15.7, now 1.15.8 — which is genuinely current, since `git ls-remote` shows **no `v1.16.0` tag** (rc1 only, with 1.17 alphas already on `main`), so every 1.16 row in this path stays provisional; the **Pro exam fee was unfilled** and is **$295 with a free retake included** against the Associate's $70.50 **without** one, plus it is **lab-based *and* multiple choice**, **4 hours including a 15-minute break**, US QWERTY only, and it still tests **Terraform 1.6** — three releases behind what the Associate tests; and **the Krausen hands-on-labs URL is dead**, redirecting to the course index, replaced with the current 004 exam-prep and 004 practice-exam pages. **Added rather than corrected:** HashiCorp's own 003→004 delta, which the path had summarised as "~90% the same" — the four new objectives are `4f` lifecycle rules (I1/I2), `4g` custom conditions (A2), `4h` ephemeral values and write-only arguments (A6), and `8c` HCP workspaces and projects (A4), so an older course leaves exactly those four holes. **A status-marker fix that mattered more than it looks:** the Intermediate, Advanced and Expert checkpoints were all marked ✅ while every topic under them is ⬜, which read as "levels complete". Only the Beginner checkpoint is genuinely passed. **Verified unchanged:** Terraform 1.15.8 stable, the 004 fee, duration, 2-year validity and its eight objective domains, the Pro exam's AWS-only scope with an Azure variant expected late 2026 (quoted verbatim from the page), TUR still at 3rd edition with no 4th announced, and the KodeKloud labs (26 labs, 20–30 hrs), its Coursera mirror, Collabnix, the `terraform-associate-labs` repo (still tested at TF 1.12.2) and the Rahul Oli video all live. Topic statuses match the book: 13 chapters, B1–B9 and I1–I4 ✅, next is **I5**.

> **2026-08-15** — **Observability with OpenTelemetry researched across all three layers, and the path's own claim about it was false.** E5 said "Terraform has no built-in OTel equivalent". It has one: `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp` enables an OTLP exporter in `telemetry.go`, and the reason nobody knows is that **it is undocumented** — a grep of the Terraform repo's `website/` for `opentelemetry` returns nothing, so the only public description is a source comment reading "BEWARE! This is not a committed external interface." The real divergence is depth. **OpenTofu 1.10+ traces the graph walk**: `Validation`/`Plan`/`Apply phase` spans, then one span per graph node (`Plan resource instance changes`, `Apply resource instance changes`, `Configure provider`) carrying `opentofu.resource_instance.address` and `opentofu.provider.source`, which is what makes "which resource is slow" answerable. **Terraform 1.15 has ~10 span sites**, all in `main`, `checkpoint.go`, and `internal/command` — module and provider install, backend init — and **no graph-walk spans at all**; the instrumented plan/apply code lives in `internal/stacks/stackruntime` and `internal/rpcapi`, which HCP Terraform drives over RPC, not the CLI. Three consequences worth carrying: OpenTofu reads **`TRACEPARENT`** (1.10) so a run nests inside a pipeline trace while Terraform ignores it ([#35444](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35444), open since 2024-07-10); Terraform exports spans through a **`SimpleSpanProcessor`** (synchronous, per span) where OpenTofu batches; and **Terraform's root span is named `terraform <full command line>`**, so `-var="password=…"` reaches the tracing backend, where OpenTofu's root span is just `tofu`. Version-gated with `git tag --contains`: init spans, node spans and `TRACEPARENT` all first ship in **v1.10.0**, `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME` in **v1.11.0**, and 1.13 (unreleased) adds `TRACEPARENT` propagation into `local-exec` children plus OTel library logs in `TF_LOG`. Two CI traps recorded in **A3**: a broken collector **fails the command** (both binaries exit 1 while the variable is set), and `autoexport`'s default protocol is `http/protobuf`/**4318**, so the OpenTofu docs' own `4317` example needs `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL=grpc` — the docs page contains both port numbers in different sections. **A4** gains the one layer that emits **metrics**: HCP Terraform agents (`-otlp-address`, `tfc-agent.*` metric families, per-run attributes like `workspace_name`/`run_id`), which neither CLI does. Two stale claims fixed in [[feature-history]] and [[opentofu-feature-history]] ("no OTel in Terraform" / "None"). Notes: [[ot-otel-tracing]], [[otel-tracing-facts]]. **E5 and E3 stay ⬜** — no chapter was written, so nothing flips.

> **2026-08-13** — **[[code-styling]] captured (Anton Babenko's *Terraform Best Practices*), a new source folder, and it closes a real gap rather than restating one.** The coverage check was unambiguous: **`pre-commit`, `terraform-docs` and `.editorconfig` appear nowhere in the path** — absent, not name-dropped. The official [[tf-style-guide]] (cited in **B4**) gives the *rules*; this page gives the **toolchain that enforces them**, and the two barely overlap. Folded into three topics. **B4** gains a callout on style being tool-enforced rather than discipline-enforced: the `terraform_fmt` pre-commit hook, **`terraform fmt -check`** as a CI gate (exit 0 when clean, non-zero listing offenders — the difference between a gate and a no-op), and **`.editorconfig`** as the layer `fmt` cannot reach, since `fmt` never touches non-`.tf` files and a repo with a `Makefile` still needs tabs enforced. **A3** step 2 now specifies `-check` over bare `fmt` and pairs it with the local hook so the pipeline stops being where formatting is discovered. **I5** gains `terraform-docs` as the way a module README's input/output tables stay current, plus the registry trap that *"all links in `README.md` should be absolute"* — the Registry renders the README off-repo, so a relative link that works on GitHub resolves to nothing there. **One divergence recorded rather than adopted silently:** both sources prefer `#` over `//` and block comments, but the `# -----` / `######` section-header convention is the community book's own, absent from HashiCorp's guide and unenforced by any tool. ⚠️ Source caveats now on the course index: it is **not** a HashiCorp publication, its pages date themselves only as *"Last updated N ago"* (this one reads "1 year ago", so its pinned `rev: v1.99.4` is roughly mid-2025 and should be re-checked), and the page carries a visible `@todo` for module versions, releases and GH Actions — so that half is unwritten, not deliberately omitted. Nav mirrored at **rung 3** from the GitBook DOM; the book's structure is flat, ten top-level pages, so no groups were invented. No topic page yet — style/formatting now has two sources ([[tf-style-guide]] + this), so it goes to the topics backlog as promotable. Statuses unchanged: **B4** stays ✅, **A3** and **I5** stay ⬜.

> **2026-08-13** — **Last unverified claim in the four reference pages settled: `-target` and `-exclude` really are mutually exclusive in OpenTofu, and the rule is wider than stated.** It had been carried from the docs, never checked. Source confirms it (`internal/command/arguments/extended.go`), and adds a detail no summary carried: in **1.9** the guard covered only the two direct flags, erroring with *"-target and -exclude flags cannot be used together"*; **1.10 widened it to the two families** on the same commit that introduced `-target-file`/`-exclude-file` (`556ba25638`, `git tag --contains` → first in v1.10.0), so any target-side option combined with any exclude-side option now fails with *"The target and exclude planning options are mutually-exclusive. Each plan must use either only the target options or only the exclude options."* The operational consequence worth carrying into **I7**: a plan is either allow-listed or deny-listed, never both, so there is no way to say "everything except X, but only within module Y" in one run. Both OpenTofu pages updated; the docs-sourced wording "mutually exclusive with it" is replaced by the behaviour and its version boundary.

And the sweep exposed a dating trap: **`action`, `action_trigger`, the `.tfquery.hcl` parser, the `list` block, and `terraform query` all exist in 1.13.0**, alpha-gated, and became stable in 1.14. The changelog is right about *availability* while the source shows *arrival*, so a claim like "actions landed in 1.14" needs to say which it means. New Part 3 on the page records all of it.

> **2026-07-17** — **Reference shapes are structural, and typed module outputs change what callers receive.** A contradiction inside Ch 7 (§2 "every resource reference is an object" vs §3 "references look like objects but aren't") turned out to be an instance-vs-container confusion: an *instance* is an object, a bare *type name* is not a value, and the container depends on the meta-argument. Chasing it found the shape line itself wrong — `count` gives a **tuple**, `for_each` an **object**, not the list/map the docs say (the docs [declare that conflation](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/expressions/types) openly; §2 is the section that breaks it). **Why structural:** a collection needs one element type and instances aren't guaranteed to share one — `for_each = { a = "str", b = 5 }` yields `a` with `input: string` and `b` with `input: number`, two different object types no map could hold. Resources take that path unconditionally (`evaluate.go:931/952`). **The find worth keeping:** modules *do* switch. `GetModule` branches on "does the module fully define all output types", so adding `type = string` to a module's output flips `module.c` from `tuple` → **`list`** and `module.f` from `object` → **`map`** (verified, TF 1.15.8). So output `type` (1.15+) does change the type callers receive — **but measuring that turned out to deflate it**: auto-conversion means callers can't tell, since a tuple splats, converts, and satisfies a `list(object(...))` constraint exactly as a list does. Ch 6's modest "improves matching across validation, plan, and apply" was right all along, and the B6 🔄 I raised against it was **my** over-claim, not the chapter's under-claim — retracted, along with a flatly false line in I5 saying callers "feel the difference". Noted in **B6**/**I5**/**E3** as a curiosity, not a reason. Finding a real mechanism is not the same as finding it matters. Also fixed: the splat section justified itself with "a `count` resource is a list" (it's a tuple — right conclusion, wrong reason). Two method failures recorded: `terraform_data` is the only provider-free resource *and* is atypical (dynamic attributes), and the `noDynamicTypes` branch that nearly reverted a correct fix lives in `GetModule`, not the resource path. Evidence: [[conditional-branch-evaluation]].
>
> **2026-08-11** — **TUR Ch 1 captured** ([[01-why-terraform]]), the third source on the [IaC fundamentals](topics/iac-fundamentals.md) topic page and the most opinionated. **What it adds to B1:** the **five-category taxonomy** (ad hoc scripts → configuration management → server templating → orchestration → provisioning), which reframes "Ansible vs Terraform" as a cross-category comparison rather than a head-to-head; the **10 → 15 servers** worked example, which *demonstrates* declarativeness where every other source asserts it (edit Ansible's `count` and you get 25 servers; edit Terraform's and you get `Plan: 5 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy`); and the two consequences that follow — procedural code doesn't capture state, and procedural code resists reuse. Also the seven benefits of IaC including *happiness*, the bus-factor argument, and the honest sidebar that **cross-cloud portability is a red herring** (Terraform gives you one language and toolchain, not one configuration). **Ch 1 of the book already answers B1's milestone**, so no 🔄 — this is enrichment, and the taxonomy plus the 10→15 example are the obvious candidates if that chapter is ever expanded. ⚠️ **The chapter is the most perishable in TUR**, being a June-2022 landscape survey, so the note carries a long *Version reckoning*, all of it verified 2026-08-11 into [[iac-tool-comparison]]: Terraform's **BSL relicensing (2023-08-10, all HashiCorp products incl. Packer)** makes Table 1-4's "Source: Open" row wrong and needs an OpenTofu column; **Terraform and Ansible now share an owner** (IBM closed HashiCorp 2025-02-27 and has owned Red Hat since 2019), so "independent commercial backing" no longer separates them; **Puppet repeated the pattern** — Perforce restricted packages Nov 2024, Vox Pupuli forked **OpenVox** 2025-01-21; **rkt** was archived by CNCF (2019) and **Apache Mesos** retired to the Attic (2025), so two named runtimes in the chapter are dead; **Terraform Cloud is HCP Terraform** since 2024-04-22; and Pulumi's paid-only state locking is gone (*"a basic file-based locking system is enabled by default for all DIY backends"*), though the non-transactional-checkpointing limitation stands. B1 stays ✅.

> **2026-08-08** — **TUR added as the site's second book, Ch 4 written** ([[04-reusable-modules]]; reading log, page map and nine placeholder chapters under `docs/books/tur/`). Brikman, *Terraform: Up & Running* 3rd ed., O'Reilly, first release 2022-09-19. Referenced in I4 and I5 since the path was written; now actually captured. **The [Modules](topics/modules.md) topic page gains its second book-length source** and a comparison table, since TID Ch3 and TUR Ch4 split cleanly: **read TUR for why each feature exists, TID for what the feature can do.** TUR arrives at inputs, locals and outputs because its running example broke without them; TID enumerates the type system, `validation`, `sensitive` and registry publishing, none of which TUR covers — it never mentions a module registry at all. **Three formulations worth keeping from TUR:** *"the input variables are the API of the module"*; **a variable is permission to change a value** (the port/protocol constants became `locals` precisely so callers could not override them accidentally — the author-side twin of [[tut-module-use]]'s NAT-gateway advice); and **prefer separate resources to inline blocks in a module**, argued from encapsulation rather than style, because a separate resource *"can be added anywhere"* while an inline block can only be added by the module that owns the resource. It is also the only source here with the **promotion workflow** end to end. **One correction filed against the book:** its *"Git tags are as stable as a commit"* is right against accidental drift and wrong against an adversary — a tag is a movable pointer, which is the supply-chain exposure I4 already records. ⚠️ **And a version finding big enough for the resources table:** TUR's running example is built on **`aws_launch_configuration`**, and AWS closed that path — no instance type released after 2023-01-01 works in one, and **accounts created on or after 2024-10-01 cannot create one by any method** ([[launch-configurations-eol]], new research-cache entry). Not a style nit; the example is unrunnable for a reader with a recent account, though none of the teaching depends on it. Its `aws_security_group_rule` advice also now points at a superseded resource. I4 and I5 both stay ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **The `module` block reference captured, closing the Modules docs section 8/8** ([[tf-block-module]]), inserted as I4 reference #5. It has absorbed the old *Module Sources* page, so it is the exhaustive `source` catalogue. **Things that live only here:** `//` marks a sub-directory within a package with query parameters placed *after* it, and since Terraform extracts the **whole** package to disk, sibling modules inside it can reference each other by local path — which is what makes A8's module-split shim resolve; **same `source`, different labels** is legal, and is the multi-region workaround forced by I8's static provider association; **absolute local paths are copied into the module cache** rather than referenced in place; **`localterraform.com`** resolves against whichever HCP/TFE instance runs the config; **HCP Terraform can authenticate to Git sources only with SSH keys**, repeated four times and prominent nowhere; and **`ignore_nested_deprecations`** (1.15) is the consumer's opt-out from an author's `deprecated` warnings. ⚠️ **The page contradicts itself on `source` ten lines apart** — *"You must specify a literal string… does not support template sequences or arbitrary expressions"* against *"The `source` attribute can reference constant input variables and local values"*. The second is current (1.15 dynamic sources); the first is stale text that survived a restructure, and the surrounding typo density fits a recently-rewritten page. Its `depth` *"default is 1"* also cannot be right — that would make every Git source shallow and break the SHA refs the same page documents, while the vendored `go-getter` takes its full-clone branch when `depth < 1`.
>
> **Correction to the 2026-08-08 `depth` entry below:** that entry called the `depth`-rejects-SHA restriction undocumented. **It is documented** — on this page, under GitHub, Git and BitBucket alike: *"you must specify a named branch or tag known to the remote repository. You cannot use raw commit IDs."* The two how-to pages I had read describe `depth` with no caveat, which is what I generalized from. The finding itself stands, and so does the I4 callout: **no page connects the restriction to the SHA-pinning advice**, so the cost still only surfaces when the pin fails. Corrected in [[tf-modules-configuration]], the I4 Git-sources callout, and `cache/search/git-module-shallow-clone-vs-sha-pin.md`.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **A8's primary reference captured** ([[tf-modules-refactoring]], HCDocs "Refactor modules"), folded into A8 reference #2 — the deep `moved` reference whose removal rules [[tut-move-config]] already quoted, with everything else new. **The governing rule is about addressing:** a bare address moves a resource *as a whole* and covers every instance, but an instance key on **either** side flips the block into instance mode. That single toggle is what makes `count` ↔ `for_each` ↔ neither migrations expressible, one `moved` block per index — and **neither [[tut-count]] nor [[tut-for-each]] mentions `moved` at all**, so the migration those tutorials walk through is a destroy-and-recreate as written. **One case is already free:** adding `count` to a resource that lacked it auto-proposes a move to index `0`, while `for_each` needs a key you pick and a rename is never free — which is exactly the "8 added, 4 destroyed" [[tut-count]] measured. **Two hard limits worth memorizing:** cross-*type* moves exist but are **provider-dependent** (ask the provider docs, not the language docs), and a managed resource can **never** become a `data` block — that path is `removed` with `lifecycle { destroy = false }` plus a separate `data` block. **The find worth keeping is the module-split shim**, which solves something nothing else in the docs addresses: splitting a *published* module without breaking consumers. The original module is kept as a shim that calls the new modules and carries the `moved` blocks, so existing users upgrade in place. HashiCorp flags it as violating the closed-box rule and attaches the precondition — *"all three of these modules are maintained by the same people and distributed together in a single module package"* — so this licence does **not** extend to writing cross-module `moved` blocks against someone else's module. Scoping bounds it: addresses resolve relative to the module they are written in, and *"a module may only make `moved` statements about its own objects and objects of its child modules"* — down, never up or sideways. Finally, **chaining** (`a→b`, `b→c`) is the mechanism that makes retention make sense: each block covers consumers arriving from a different starting version, so a module's `moved` blocks accumulate like database migrations rather than as a courtesy changelog. A8 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **"Publish modules" captured, mostly to record where it *isn't*** ([[tf-modules-publish]]), folded into I5 reference #2. Thinnest page in the Modules section and a third of it is ecosystem prose. **The find is a navigation trap:** the Configuration Language sidebar's **Publish modules** entry contains **none** of the publishing requirements — no repository-naming rule, no semver-tag rule, no structure requirement. Those live under the separate **Registry Publishing** top-level section, already captured in `cache/search/module-repo-naming-convention.md`, which now carries that pointer. A reader following this section's sidebar will not find them. Two things it does supply. **The cleanest one-line case for a registry source over a Git one** — *"The alternative sources do not support the first-class versioning mechanism"* — which is I4's registry-versus-Git argument compressed to a sentence. And **the public registry's curation model, stated plainly**: partner and community modules "both have their place… accessible to practitioners who can decide which modules best fit their requirements", i.e. **it hosts, it does not vet**, which is why [[tut-private-registry-add]]'s review checklist exists. ⚠️ Its own example omits the `version` argument on the very page selling version constraints. Also worth keeping: it recommends the standard module structure even for Git-distributed modules so they can *"be published on the registry at a later time"* — structure as a migration path, not just tidiness. I5 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **I5 gains its design reference** ([[tf-modules-composition]], HCDocs "Best practices for composing modules"), inserted as I5 reference #3 with the rest renumbered. The most substantial page in the Modules section, and **stricter than the enterprise guide it overlaps**: keep the tree **flat, one level of child modules**, wire them with expressions, because *"instead of a module embedding its dependencies… the module receives its dependencies from the root module, which can therefore connect the same modules in different ways to produce different results."* **The find worth keeping is that its headline pattern runs on the mechanism Ch 12 recorded as a footgun.** Its conditional-creation advice — never write a module that detects whether something exists; declare an `object(...)` input naming only the attributes you need and let the caller pass a `resource` **or** a `data` source — works because *"Terraform will allow any object that has at least these attributes"*, which is the same discard the Type Constraints page specifies and Ch 12 measured turning a caller's typo into a silent no-op. **So structural typing and typo detection are one knob turned two ways**: a *deliberately* partial object type is this pattern, an *accidentally* partial one is the footgun, and you cannot have both. OpenTofu's `Warning: Object attribute is ignored` makes it noisier for the pattern and safer against the typo. **Two more keepers.** *Assumptions versus guarantees* — an assumption is what must hold for a resource to be usable, a guarantee is what the rest of the configuration may rely on, and a `precondition` on an **output** is how a module asserts the latter (**A2**); an object type enforces shape while only a `validation` enforces content. And *data-only modules*, whose real trick is the last line of the page: **give a `join-network` module the same outputs as a `create-network` module and the two become swappable**, so promoting an environment from sharing infrastructure to owning it is a one-line `source` change — the most useful idea here for **A7**, and neither [[tut-organize-configuration]] nor [[tut-pattern-module-creation]] mentions it. Also a flat statement worth quoting back at people: Terraform *"intentionally does not attempt to abstract over similar services offered by different vendors"* because unification means a lowest common denominator; multi-cloud abstractions are yours to build from shared object types and matching output names. ⚠️ Two of its examples would fail `validate` — `aws_ami` takes **`owners`** (a list), not `owner`, and **`aws_subnet_ids` no longer exists** (docs 404 in the provider repo on `main`; `aws_subnets` returns 200) — plus its `recordsets` type requires `ttl` while the `for`-generated records omit it, which wants `optional(number)`. I5 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **I8 gains the module-boundary reference, and a rule cited five times finally gets its reason** ([[tf-modules-providers]], HCDocs "Providers Within Modules"), inserted as I8 reference #3 with the rest renumbered; it is the page [[tf-meta-providers]] explicitly defers all module-author guidance to, and that forward reference is now resolved. **The find that changes the story:** every tutorial in the Modules collection explains "no `provider` blocks in a module" by the `count`/`for_each`/`depends_on` conflict. That is the **later, mechanical** enforcement, added in v0.13. The actual reason is a lifecycle one — a provider configuration is required to *destroy* a resource as well as create it, and Terraform stores in state which configuration last applied each resource, so *"a provider configuration must always stay present in the overall Terraform configuration for longer than all of the resources it manages."* A module holding both would remove them simultaneously, leaving state pointing at a configuration that no longer exists, and planning errors until it is reintroduced. That generalizes past modules: **never delete a `provider` block while anything in state still references it.** **The inheritance asymmetry is the other keeper:** default configurations inherit, **aliased ones never do**, and **source/version requirements never do either** — *"especially important for non-HashiCorp providers"*, since a child omitting `required_providers` loses the source address and Terraform assumes `hashicorp/<name>`. **And it states plainly what [[tut-for-each]] could only infer:** the resource-to-provider association is *static*, so a `count`/`for_each` module call **cannot** vary providers per instance — multi-region fan-out is one `module` block per provider set, by design rather than omission. Also **closes an open docs discrepancy**: this page carries the corrected per-provider scoping (*"overrides the default inheritance behavior for that provider"*) while [[tf-meta-providers]] still carries the un-narrowed "cancels the default behavior" wording that issue [#35781](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35781) was filed against — the two reference pages disagree as they stand, and this one is right. Noted in both notes. Resolved in passing: [[tut-no-code-provisioning]] is not an exception to the rule, because a no-code module *is* the root module of the workspace HCP generates. I8 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **The standard module structure captured** ([[tf-modules-structure]]), folded into I5 reference #2 beside [[tf-modules-develop]]. The layout is a **machine-readable** convention, not a style preference — *"Terraform tooling is built to understand the standard module structure and use that structure to generate documentation, index modules for the module registry"* — which is why the registry's publishing bar requires it. **Four rules that cannot be guessed from the file tree.** A submodule's **`README.md` is its public/private marker**: *"Any nested module with a `README.md` is considered usable by an external user. If a README doesn't exist, it is considered for internal use only"*, and this is *"purely advisory"* — so a private submodule stays private by staying undocumented, which is an odd incentive worth knowing about. **The two source-address rules point opposite ways**: a root calling its own nested module uses a **relative** path so Terraform treats it as the same package rather than downloading it again, while a block in `examples/` must use **the external address**, because examples are expected to be copied into other repositories. **Resources may be split across files; `module` blocks may not** — nested calls stay in `main.tf`. And the root module *"is expected to be opinionated"* with nested modules as the escape hatch, which is the structural form of the 80%-of-use-cases rule. **One real conflict recorded:** this page says *"The README doesn't need to document inputs or outputs … because tooling will automatically generate this"*, where [[tut-pattern-module-creation]] instructs you to document required inputs, optional inputs and outputs in the README. Reconciled — the registry generates those tables from the `description` arguments, so writing descriptions *is* documenting the interface and a duplicated table only goes stale; what the guide adds on top is advertising default values and never defaulting a variable whose right value differs on every use. Smaller: use **commit-specific absolute URLs** in a module README or the registry renders them against the wrong version (the mutable-reference problem of I4's Git `ref` one layer up), and the page's own `minimal-module/` tree omits the `LICENSE` its prose says to always include. I5 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **I5's reference index captured** ([[tf-modules-develop]], HCDocs "Creating Modules" — the *Develop modules* overview), folded into I5 reference #2 and nested under the docs course's `Modules › Develop modules` group. Short page, kept for one section: **when *not* to write a module.** *"Over-using modules can make your overall Terraform configuration harder to understand and maintain"*; a good module *"raise[s] the level of abstraction by describing a new concept in your architecture"*; and the test worth memorizing — *"If you have trouble finding a name for your module that isn't the same as the main resource type inside it, that may be a sign that your module is not creating any new abstraction."* **Paired with [[tut-pattern-module-creation]]'s complexity test, the target gets sharp: a module should be nameable in a phrase that is neither a paragraph nor a resource type.** The two heuristics guard opposite failure modes, and together they qualify the tutorial's "aim for small and simple" — small is a scoping instruction, not a licence to wrap one resource. Noted honestly against it: `terraform-aws-modules/ec2-instance/aws`, which [[tut-module-use]] consumes, **fails the naming test but not the substance test** (at v6.4.0 it also creates an IAM instance profile, security group, EIP and EBS volumes), so the heuristic is a smell detector rather than a rule. Two smaller keeps: this is now the **third** official definition of "module" — container of resources *used together*, raising abstraction — and it is the only one that gives grounds to call a module a *bad* module, where [[tut-module]]'s is the mechanism and [[tf-modules]]'s is the unit. And its description of refactoring blocks, *"reinterpret existing objects as if they had been created at the corresponding new addresses"*, is the most precise one-liner on `moved` anywhere in the docs — and explains why [[tut-move-config]]'s plan still reported `3 to change`, since reinterpreting where an object came from says nothing about whether its desired configuration still matches. I5 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **I4's primary reference captured** ([[tf-modules-configuration]], HCDocs "Use modules in your configuration"), inserted as I4 reference #4 and filed under the docs course's new `Modules` group. It is the consumer-side reference behind [[tut-module-use]], and it covers five things that tutorial never mentions: Git `ref` selection, shallow clones, `const`-gated source expressions, `-replace` addressing inside modules, and removing a module without destroying it. **The find worth keeping is an undocumented incompatibility, and it sharpens I4's own Git-sources callout:** `?depth=1` — the documented way to avoid cloning a large repository's whole history — **rejects a SHA `ref` outright**. `go-getter` passes `--depth` together with `--branch <ref>` and `git clone --branch` accepts only a branch or tag, so a commit-shaped ref is caught by a regex and fails with *"note that setting 'depth' requires 'ref' to be a branch or tag name"*. Version-gated against `go-getter` **v1.8.6**, the release Terraform **1.15.8** vendors per `go.mod` in the local checkout at the release commit. So **immutable pin or shallow clone, not both** — a third cost of SHA-pinning alongside the two update-bot gaps already recorded, and neither docs page connects them. Marked as read from vendored source rather than reproduced. **Two smaller things:** the `const` requirement is a **timing** argument, not a safety one (modules install at `init`, before plan-time evaluation exists), and it constrains the whole dependency chain rather than the syntax — a `local` composed from const variables is legal in the source position; and **`init` honours what is already installed while `-upgrade` re-resolves the constraint**, which with no module lock file is precisely where an untested version lands. ⚠️ The page's removal section is garbled four ways, including a closing link to the **`moved`** block reference at the end of a section about **`removed`**. Also worth noting against the tutorials: its example pins the VPC module at **6.0.1**, a current major, where every page in the Modules tutorial collection still pins 3.x — the reference docs are maintained, the tutorials are not. Evidence: `cache/search/git-module-shallow-clone-vs-sha-pin.md`. I4 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **The language reference's Modules section index captured** ([[tf-modules]]), inserted as I4 reference #3 ahead of Module Sources and creating a new `Modules` nav group under Configuration Language in the docs course (sidebar rung 1, positioned after Outputs as the source puts it). A thin index page, kept for two things. **It defines a module differently from the tutorials, and both definitions are load-bearing:** *"a collection of resources that Terraform manages together"* here, against [[tut-module]]'s *"a set of Terraform configuration files in a single directory"*. The structural one makes "every configuration is already a module" true; the semantic one is what makes the scoping question in I5 answerable — if a module is just a directory, "what belongs in it?" has no answer. **And it supplies the Develop → Distribute → Provision frame** the whole docs section is built on, which maps cleanly onto the tutorial collection just captured: Develop is [[tut-module-create]] / [[tut-module-object-attributes]] / [[tut-pattern-module-creation]], Distribute is [[tut-module-private-registry-share]] / [[tut-private-registry-add]], Provision is [[tut-module-use]] — and [[tut-move-config]] belongs to none of them, because refactoring is what happens after all three. Recorded for later capture: the section has **eight** pages and this is one, with `Refactor modules` and `Publish modules` already quoted secondhand (in [[tut-move-config]] and `cache/search/module-repo-naming-convention.md`) but not yet captured directly. Also noted: the page says "Every Terraform workspace includes configuration files in its root directory" where it means *working directory* — a loose use of a word that already means two other things ([[tf-state-workspaces]]). I4 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **E6 gains its named hands-on, and the Modules collection is closed at 10/10** ([[tut-no-code-provisioning]], HCTut "Create and use no-code modules"). E6 reference #3 was already pointing at this tutorial by an alias URL with no notes behind it; it now has both, plus a worked-example callout in **A6**. **The design rule that defines no-code:** a workspace's entire change surface is *(variable values) × (module version)* — *"You cannot directly apply configuration changes to existing no-code workspaces"* — so anything the author did not expose is fixed until they ship a release, which turns [[tut-pattern-module-creation]]'s minimize-inputs advice into a hard requirement. **The find worth keeping is that it inverts the no-provider-blocks rule and, in doing so, explains why the rule ever existed:** provider blocks are forbidden in modules because they conflict with `count`/`for_each`/`depends_on` (the constraint [[tut-for-each]] measured), and *"Since users will not reference no-code modules in written configuration, there is no risk of this conflict."* The prohibition was never about providers. Its cost is that HCP spawns a workspace per provision, so credentials must arrive automatically — a project-scoped variable set in the tutorial, dynamic credentials in the page's own next steps. Governance lives in **Configure Settings**: pinning which version no-code deploys (pushing a tag publishes but does **not** roll out), and converting a free-text variable into a **dropdown of allowed values**, which is input validation at the UI layer rather than in HCL. **Also folded into A6:** its RDS module is the shortest complete `ephemeral` + write-only example available — one ephemeral password feeding `password_wo` and `value_wo`, with **both** `_version` companions pointed at a single local so the database and its SSM copy rotate together. ⚠️ Its own 1.0.1 upgrade adds a variable with **no default**, forcing every consuming workspace to answer a prompt — the breaking way to make an additive change, and a live counterexample to [[tut-module-object-attributes]], made worse by the fact that no-code users are by definition the people who don't write Terraform. Two things not to copy from the module: `publicly_accessible = true` on an RDS instance in a module meant to codify standards, and `cidr_blocks = ["192.80.0.0/16"]` described as an internal VPN when that is **public** address space (RFC 1918 is `10/8`, `172.16/12`, `192.168/16`). **One counter-observation:** this is the **only maintained page in the collection** — ephemeral resources (1.10), write-only arguments (1.11), `postgres16` — where the other nine carry 2022-era pins. E6 and A6 both stay ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **A8 gains its hands-on, and the Modules collection is now fully captured** ([[tut-move-config]], HCTut "Use configuration to move resources"), inserted as A8 reference #1 ahead of the reading, with a companion callout in **I7**. Ninth and last page of the collection, and the one that makes the other eight survivable on live infrastructure: extract a root resource into a local module *and* swap a hand-written module for a registry one, then reconcile with `moved`. **The find worth keeping is that the fixed plan is not empty** — `0 to add, **3 to change**, 0 to destroy. `moved` preserves **identity, not configuration**, so swapping implementations is a move *and* a config change landing in one apply (the registry module's defaults differ), while extracting your own code is a clean move that prints `has moved to` with no action symbol at all. **A zero-destroy plan is not a zero-impact plan** — read the `~` lines. Also recorded: the un-`moved` plan's tell-tale reason line *"because X is not in configuration"*, which is what an address in state with no matching block looks like whether you deleted it or renamed it; module-level `moved` cascading to every address beneath it but **requiring `terraform init`** because the install key changes; and `moved` blocks living in the **root** while naming in-module addresses on both sides, with `[0]` index keys taken from the destination module's internals. **Corrects the tutorial against its own reference docs on two points:** removing a `moved` block is safe *"when you are maintaining private modules within an organization and you are certain that all users have successfully run `terraform apply`"*, not never — the absolute phrasing is the page's, and the real rule is about whose state still holds the old address; and `moved` needs **v1.1**, not the v1.3 the page sets as a prerequisite. This is also the block [[tut-count]] needed and never named. A8 and I7 both stay ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **I5 gains its design theory, and the A4/A5 seam gets closed** ([[tut-pattern-module-creation]], HCTut "Module creation — recommended pattern"), inserted as I5 reference #5 with a new callout in **A5**. No code — it is an architecture guide from the *Recommended Enterprise Patterns* series, and it answers the question the rest of the collection skips: not which arguments should be variables, but **which resources belong in the same module at all**. **The three scoping axes are the most portable idea in the collection:** *Encapsulation* (group what always deploys together), *Privileges* (never span a segregation-of-duties boundary), *Volatility* (keep long-lived infrastructure out of a module that ships daily). The last two are blast-radius arguments, which is why they decide **state** boundaries as well as module boundaries — the same argument A7 makes one granularity up, and the principled version of what [[tut-organize-configuration]] demonstrates by destroying production from a dev file. **Also worth keeping:** its too-big heuristic (*"If a module's function or purpose is hard to explain, the module is probably too complex"*); the deliberate **minimize inputs, maximize outputs** asymmetry, since every input is a promise to maintain while an output is a value you already computed; the default test (`var.vpc_id` should never have one — *if the right value differs on every use, a default is a trap*); and the nesting trade, where an external child module can change behavior *"with no changes to the parent's calling code or version"* — an unpinned transitive dependency, I4's no-module-lock hazard one level deeper — while an embedded submodule is version-locked but unreusable. Its "avoid conditional expressions in an MVP" is **sequencing advice, not a contradiction** of [[tut-module-object-attributes]]: ship narrow, then add flexibility with `optional()` so the first consumer doesn't break. **The find that closes an open thread:** [[tut-private-registry-add]] left "approved module list" ambiguous, since curation enforces nothing; this page states the control outright — *"With Sentinel … only modules from the private Terraform registry can be used"* — so A4 publishes the set and **A5** makes it binding, now written into A5 as a worked-policy suggestion. ⚠️ Its naming table gives `terraform-azure-database`, but the provider is **`azurerm`** (verified against the Registry API; no `hashicorp/azure` exists), and the same three-row table mixes an underscore with hyphens. Third page in this collection to fumble that convention. Diagram captured. I5 and A5 both stay ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **A7 gains its hands-on** ([[tut-organize-configuration]], HCTut "Refactor monolithic Terraform configuration"), inserted as A7 reference #3. Seventh page of the Modules collection and it contains no modules — it makes the case *for* them by showing what a flat configuration costs. **The find worth keeping is its middle step, which demonstrates blast radius instead of describing it:** a shared `random_pet` feeds both bucket names, so changing its `length` in `dev.tf` — a file containing nothing but development resources — destroys and recreates production. So **splitting `main.tf` into `dev.tf` and `prod.tf` buys readability and nothing else**, because the coupling lives in the reference graph, not the filesystem. That is the sharpest available answer to "we separated our environments into files". Three more: **`-target` is named and dismissed** as "risky and not a sustainable way to manage distinct environments" (so it stays an I7 recovery tool, not a layout strategy); **converting one state into per-directory states needs `state mv` work**, which the exercise dodges by destroying everything first; and the local backend relocates non-`default` workspace state to `terraform.tfstate.d/<name>/terraform.tfstate`. ⚠️ **The page teaches CLI workspaces as environment separation, which A7 reference #2 exists to contradict** — the language reference marks *"Workspaces are not appropriate for system decomposition or deployments requiring separate credentials and access controls"* as **Important** ([[tf-state-workspaces]]), and dev/prod is the canonical case of the second clause. Its own mitigation is "be aware of the workspace you are working in", which is human vigilance standing in for an access boundary. Read its decision rule as being about configuration duplication, not isolation, and A7's existing position stands unchanged. Page defects: resource counts wrong twice over (says 5, its own prose describes 9, the repo defines 15), and it instructs you to comment out a `terraform` block the current repo does not contain — that repo has no `required_providers` anywhere, so nothing is version-pinned in a tutorial about growing a project safely. A7 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **A4 gains the registry-curation hands-on** ([[tut-private-registry-add]], HCTut "Add public providers and modules to your private registry"), inserted as A4 reference #4. A five-minute UI page with no configuration in it, but it forced a boundary worth drawing between **A4 and A5**. The tutorial says curation lets you *"define a list of approved components"*, which reads like a control; the docs say the registry *"stores a pointer"*, that the feature *"designate[s] which public providers and modules are recommended"*, and that after removal *"Users in the organization can still use the removed provider or module without changing their configurations."* **Two things follow that the page never states:** curation does not change the source address (which is exactly why removal breaks nothing), and it enforces nothing at all — so "approved" is a label, and the actual control is Sentinel/OPA policy in **A5**, or the exact-pin discipline already noted in I4. Four omissions from the page also folded in: the permission is **Manage Private Registry**, not org ownership, so curation is delegable to a platform team; it is drivable through the Registry Providers/Modules **APIs**, which makes the approved list a reviewable artifact instead of hand-maintained drift; **Terraform Enterprise must reach the Algolia search host as well as `registry.terraform.io`**, or the search box silently returns nothing on an egress-filtered install; and **no-code provisioning can be enabled on a curated *public* module** (Standard/Premium), which is the E6 self-service story without authoring or maintaining a module — the tutorial's own Tip points only at building your own. Evidence appended to `cache/search/hcp-private-registry-publishing.md`. A4 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **A4 gains the private-registry hands-on** ([[tut-module-private-registry-share]], HCTut "Share modules in the private registry"), inserted as A4 reference #3, with a companion callout in **I5** since publishing is the step after authoring. First page in the Modules collection with **no Community Edition path** — it is HCP-only and almost entirely UI. Facts worth holding: the private-module address is `<HOSTNAME>/<ORGANIZATION>/<MODULE_NAME>/<PROVIDER_NAME>`; **versions come from git release tags** (semver `x.y.z`, optional `v`, non-matching tags ignored, ≥1 tag required to publish), so versioning is a release-process decision in the repo rather than an HCL argument; and registry scope differs by product — HCP shares **within** an organization, Terraform Enterprise **within or across**. **The find worth keeping is the publishing-workflow split**, which the tutorial renders as a variant toggle and never explains: tag-based auto-imports on tag push, while branch-based needs a **Publish New Version** click and a commit SHA, cannot re-use a version number, and is **the only workflow supporting module testing** — which makes it the one to pick once `terraform test` (A2) is part of shipping a module. Branch-based versions still pin to a commit, so this is *not* the mutable-`ref` hazard from I4's Git-sources callout. ⚠️ Two errors on the page, both self-contradicting: it states the address format with a spurious literal `terraform/` segment, and it tells you to set the registry **Module Name** to the *repository* name (`terraform-aws-s3-webapp`), either of which would produce an address its own module block disagrees with. It does, however, state the `terraform-<PROVIDER>-<NAME>` convention **correctly as a repository rule** — so the collection contradicts itself, and [[tut-module]] is the page that has it wrong. **Both variants were then read in full, which turned up a content asymmetry rather than another typo:** the branch path removes the tag/release section outright, so a reader who picks that variant never meets the semver-tag rules at all, and the variant's own summary still claims they "created and versioned a GitHub repository" when the version was typed into HCP and the repo carries no tag. Read the tag variant first whichever workflow you intend to use. Evidence: `cache/search/hcp-private-registry-publishing.md`. A4 and I5 both stay ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **Ch 13 written, I4 → ✅.** Blends [[tf-modules]], [[tf-modules-configuration]], [[tf-block-module]], [[tf-expr-version-constraints]], [[tut-module]], [[tut-module-use]], [[tut-private-registry-add]] and TUR Ch 4 [[04-reusable-modules]]. **The chapter's spine is that `source` decides everything else** — whether `version` is legal, what pins the code, where it lands on disk, and whether an update bot can move it. **The headline measurement:** the same `main.tf` with `version = "~> 4.1"` ran `4.1.0` in one directory and `4.11.0` in a fresh one on the same machine, same day, no warning — because `.terraform.lock.hcl` records providers only and `modules.json` is never committed. Unpinned, the same module resolved **5.15.4**, a whole major away. **Second measured find:** the `init` / `init -upgrade` asymmetry holds for Git refs as well as registry versions, which makes the mutable-tag exposure demonstrable — `git tag -f -a v0.0.1 HEAD` was invisible to `plan`, then flipped the installed module on the next `init -upgrade` with the config byte-identical. **Third:** the `depth`+SHA restriction, which [[tf-block-module]] had only inferred from vendored `go-getter` source, is now **reproduced live** — `?ref=<sha>&depth=1` fails with `fatal: Remote branch <sha> not found in upstream origin (note that setting 'depth' requires 'ref' to be a branch or tag name)`, while the same address with a tag installs fine. **Fourth, and it corrects a note:** [[tut-module-use]] says local modules are **symlinked** into `.terraform/modules/`; on 1.15.8 that directory holds **only `modules.json`**, whose `Dir` points straight back at the relative source path. No copy, no symlink. The consequence the tutorial draws is still exactly right — a local-module edit is live with no re-`init`, and **both callers move together** (`0 to add, 2 to change`), which is the coupling versioned sources exist to break. **1.15 dynamic module sources measured end to end:** a `source` composed from two `const = true` variables through a `local` installs at `init`, accepts `-var` and `*.auto.tfvars`, and without `const` fails with `Only literal values and const variables can be evaluated during init.` **OpenTofu 1.12.4 diverges twice:** it installs the same variable-composed source **with or without** `const` (early evaluation since 1.8), and has **no `modules` command** at all (`OpenTofu has no command named "modules"`). Also captured: a child module contributes provider constraints to your resolution (`>= 5.83.0, ~> 6.0` from one `module` block), and `terraform get` installs modules without touching providers or the backend. Lab committed at `labs/chapter13/` (three parts, lab 3 self-contained via a local `git init` repo built by `setup.ps1`/`setup.sh`). ⚠️ Emulator fidelity note: Floci does not persist bucket tags, so a tagged bucket replans the same tag change forever — an emulator gap, not Terraform. Next: **I5 — Authoring modules**.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **I5 gains its interface-design hands-on** ([[tut-module-object-attributes]], HCTut "Customize modules with object attributes"), inserted as I5 reference #4; its CORS half also folded into **I3** reference #2, which had been an unsourced "build a security-group module" exercise. Nothing here contradicts Ch 12, so **I3 stays ✅** — the tutorial illustrates the chapter rather than revising it. The page's own argument for objects is API evolution: optional attributes *"make it easier for you to ship new module versions without changing the variables that module users need to define"*, which makes them the additive counterpart to the 1.15 `deprecated` mechanism already noted in I5. **Two transferable finds.** A shared prefix or suffix across flat variable names is **an object hiding in the interface** — `terraform_managed_files` becomes `files.terraform_managed`, the suffix having existed only because a flat namespace had nowhere to put the qualifier. And the module's type constraint should **mirror the wrapped resource's own required/optional split** (its `cors_rules` deliberately matches `aws_s3_bucket_cors_configuration`), or the module rejects valid configurations and defers errors to apply. **Ch 12's silent-discard footgun has no better illustration than this page, and the page never mentions it:** misspell `www_path` inside the `files` object and the object constraint drops the key, `optional(string)` fills `null`, the ternary falls back to the module's own `www` directory, and you ship a placeholder page with `validate` reporting `Success!`. ⚠️ Page defects beyond stale pins: its transcripts predate its own repo (`Plan: 6 to add` where a current clone gives 8, since the repo has since gained the ownership-controls and public-access-block resources), the "Clean up your infrastructure" section prints an apply that **adds** a resource under a `terraform destroy` heading, and the delete-these-four-variables snippet lists five. Its repo also omits the `depends_on` that `learn-terraform-modules-create` uses to order the bucket policy after the public-access block, leaving no graph edge between them — flagged as a graph reading, not a measured failure. One counter-observation worth keeping: **`hashicorp/dir/template` 1.0.2 is still the latest published version** (2020-07-29), so not every old pin is a stale pin. I5 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **I5 gains its first hands-on** ([[tut-module-create]], HCTut "Build and use a local module"), inserted as I5 reference #3. The authoring counterpart to I4's two consumption tutorials, and its real content is interface design, not syntax: **a value your module's purpose fixes is not a variable** (its example is the ACL, which must be `public-read` for website hosting — the author-side twin of [[tut-module-use]]'s NAT-gateway advice), and *"outputs are the only supported way for users to get information about resources configured by the module"*. Also confirms **no `provider` block in a module** — [[tut-for-each]] already showed that's a hard constraint once `count`/`for_each` is on the module block, not the recommendation this page calls it. ⚠️ **This one is not merely stale, it is broken.** Two AWS defaults changed underneath it: new buckets are Bucket-owner-enforced so ACLs are disabled (`400 AccessControlListNotSupported`), and Block Public Access is on, so `PutBucketPolicy` rejects a `Principal = "*"` policy. The page's four-resource module trips both. **Its example repo is already fixed** and the rendered page is not — the repo carries `aws_s3_bucket_ownership_controls`, `aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block` with all four settings `false`, and `depends_on` chains, for six resources. Third page-vs-repo divergence in this collection and the only one where following the page fails outright. **The find worth keeping beyond S3:** those `depends_on` blocks are the *legitimate* kind, and they're now the counterexample in I1's "prefer an attribute reference" callout — the ACL consumes no attribute from either resource yet must be applied after both, so ordering-without-data-flow is exactly what `depends_on` is for. Smaller: the module outputs `website_domain` (*"used to create Route 53 alias records"*) where `website_endpoint` is the browsable URL, so the tutorial's own destroy log prints a bare regional suffix with no bucket name in it — a live example of the output-choice judgment the page is teaching. Evidence: `cache/search/s3-acl-bpa-defaults.md`. I5 stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **I4 gains its hands-on** ([[tut-module-use]], HCTut "Use registry modules in configuration", Community Edition variant), rewriting I4 reference #2. Two registry modules produce **22 resources** — the encapsulation benefit and the review problem in the same number. **The constraint worth memorizing:** *"Terraform will not display module outputs by default"* — a child module's outputs are readable inside the configuration but invisible at the CLI until the root module re-exports them, so `module.MODULE_NAME.OUTPUT_NAME` is a config-level reference, not a CLI-level one. **The best line on the page is about variables, not modules:** you should *not* declare a variable for a value your organization has already decided (its example is `enable_nat_gateway`) — a variable is a permission to vary, and declaring one hands the decision to the caller. The tutorial then does exactly that. Also confirms `count` on a `module` block (the [[tut-for-each]] two-axis mechanism, seen here in its plain form) and that **local modules are symlinked** into `.terraform/modules/`, so edits land without re-`init` or `terraform get`. ⚠️ **Staleness checked rather than assumed, and the result cuts the other way from [[tut-count]]:** the pins are years old (provider `~> 4.49.0`, vpc **3.18.1** vs. current 6.6.1, ec2-instance **4.3.0** vs. current 6.4.0) but every argument and output the tutorial uses is still declared at vpc **v6.6.1** and ec2-instance **v6.4.0** — verified against the tagged source 2026-08-08. So this one is a version bump, not a rewrite; the three bumps are coupled, since ec2-instance 6.4.0 alone forces AWS provider `>= 6.37`. Its hardcoded `ami-0c5204531f799e0c6` is the part not to copy (`ami_ssm_parameter` exists at v6.4.0). Rendered page vs. example repo disagree on the instance `name`, and here the **page** is correct. Cache updated: `cache/search/aws-modules-current-versions.md` gains the ec2-instance row and the surviving-interface check. I4 stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **I4 gains its conceptual opener** ([[tut-module]], HCTut "Modules overview" — first page of the Modules tutorial collection, new `Modules` nav group under HashiCorp Terraform Tutorials). Inserted as I4 reference #1, renumbering the rest. It is a no-code page, so what it buys is vocabulary and motivation: **every configuration is already a module** (the directory you run commands in is the *root module*; one reached through a `module` block is a *child module*), Terraform reads exactly one directory unless a `module` block sends it elsewhere, and the strongest case for modules is **security, not tidiness** — object storage has too many options to get right repeatedly, so encode the correct settings once. **Found an error in the source page:** its best-practices list says *"Name your **provider** `terraform-<PROVIDER>-<NAME>`"*, but that three-part name is the **module repository** convention (`terraform-google-vault`, `terraform-aws-ec2-instance`); providers use `terraform-provider-<NAME>`. Verified against [Publishing Modules](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/registry/modules/publish) and folded into **I5** reference #2 with the rest of the registry's publishing bar (public GitHub repo, standard module structure, ≥1 semver tag). Cached at `cache/search/module-repo-naming-convention.md`. Glossary gains *root module*, *child module*, *no-code ready module*. Topic status unchanged — I4 stays ⬜ until a chapter exists.
